Feedback on Your Essay: The Exxon Valdez Oil Spill

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important topic with genuine passion, and your personal connection to the event—watching the spill unfold on television—gives the piece an authentic emotional core. However, the essay currently reads more as a personal reflection or emotional response than an academic argument. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, provide more concrete evidence, and revise for clarity and precision throughout. Below are specific areas to address.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Your opening claims that "money is the root of all evil" and that you'll "take the side of the everyday person... and the issues of big business," but it's unclear what specific claim you're arguing. Are you arguing that:
Corporate negligence, driven by profit motives, caused the spill?
Exxon should be held more accountable than it was?
Regulatory changes are needed to prevent future disasters?

Pick one central, arguable claim and make it explicit in your introduction. Right now, the essay drifts between narrating events, expressing outrage, and offering policy recommendations without clearly connecting these threads to a unified argument.

Distinguish Fact from Assertion

Several claims need evidence or citation:
"The Exxon Valdez incident will go down in history as the biggest Public Relations blunder by any firm in the nation." This is a strong claim—can you cite sources (news coverage, PR analyses, academic studies) that support this ranking?
"The publishing of Exxon's notes and court transcriptions, prove that if Exxon would have maintained the ships in a better fashion this devastating nightmare may never have happened." What specific documents are you referencing? Quote or cite them directly.
The claim about double-hulled tankers becoming "common practice" is accurate and important—cite the source (this was actually mandated by the Oil Pollution Act of 1990) and use it as evidence of policy change resulting from the disaster.

Academic arguments require verifiable evidence, not just assertions of certainty ("still is unbelievable," "beyond belief"). Replace emotional intensifiers with specific facts, statistics, and sourced quotations.

Fix the Placeholder Text

You have an unfilled placeholder: "spokespersons said the same thing. ." This is a critical gap—what did Exxon actually say? This quote could be one of your most powerful pieces of evidence, showing the gap between corporate reassurances and actual preparedness. Research and insert Exxon's actual public statements here.

Strengthen the Personal Narrative's Purpose

Your personal anecdote about watching the spill on television is evocative, but consider how it serves your argument. Right now it demonstrates your emotional response, but for an academic essay, you might use it more strategically—perhaps as a brief frame (opening or closing) rather than the interpretive lens for the whole piece. The essay would benefit from moving beyond "I felt angry" to "the evidence suggests X, and here's why."

Address Counterarguments

A stronger essay would acknowledge the counter-view: perhaps Exxon's defenders would argue that human error (the captain's actions) was primarily to blame, not corporate policy, or that the settlement Exxon eventually paid was fair compensation. Engaging with these points—even briefly—and refuting them would strengthen your argument considerably. Right now, the essay is one-sided in a way that undermines its persuasiveness.

Revise for Precision and Repetition

Some specific line edits to consider:
"Prior to the grounding the Exxon" should be "Prior to the grounding of the Exxon Valdez" or similar.
You repeat "devastating" and its variants (devastating effects, devastating nightmare, devastation) frequently—vary your vocabulary.
"The carnage was and still is unbelievable" is vague—what carnage, specifically? Dead animals? Numbers? Species affected?
Check verb tense consistency, particularly in "The effects... were and still are devastating" constructions, which appear more than once.

Conclusion Needs Sharpening

Your final paragraph shifts into policy recommendations (funding research, buying land for sanctuaries) that feel disconnected from the narrative that preceded them. Consider explicitly transitioning: "Given Exxon's failures, as demonstrated above, three policy changes are necessary..." This will help the conclusion feel like a logical culmination rather than a new direction.

Suggested Next Steps

Draft a one-sentence thesis statement and revise your introduction around it.
Locate and integrate specific sources: Exxon's public statements before the spill, court documents, casualty statistics (barrels spilled, animals killed, economic impact).
Fill in the missing quotation.
Add a paragraph addressing a counterargument.
Do a line edit for repetitive phrasing and vague intensifiers.

With more specific evidence and a tighter argumentative structure, this essay could move from being a passionate personal account to a compelling piece of persuasive writing. Your instincts about corporate accountability are worth developing—now it's a matter of building the evidentiary case to match your convictions.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback Report: "Crime Does Not Pay"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a compelling topic and includes a genuinely effective illustrative example—the story of the businessman who scammed elderly couples. The narrative has real emotional weight, and you clearly understand the consequences you're trying to illustrate. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: paragraph structure, thesis clarity, argument development, and proofreading. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis appears to be "crime does not pay," but this claim needs to be stated clearly and *early*—ideally as the final sentence of your introduction. Right now, the thesis is buried in a bracketed placeholder  that seems to reference your title rather than stating the argument directly in the text. Make sure your thesis is a complete, explicit sentence within the essay itself, not something the reader must infer from your title.

Additionally, consider restructuring your essay along these lines:
Introduction: Briefly introduce the phenomenon of choosing crime over legal wealth-building, and end with your thesis statement.
Body Paragraph 1: Explain *why* people turn to crime (impatience, environment, rejection of societal rules).
Body Paragraph 2: Present your extended example (the businessman) as evidence.
Body Paragraph 3: Analyze the consequences and draw out the broader lesson.
Conclusion: Restate your thesis with a broader reflection.

Currently, your first paragraph tries to do too much—introducing the topic, categorizing criminals, and referencing your example—while the second paragraph shifts into narrative mode without a clear transition. A cleaner separation between *explanation* and *illustration* will make your argument easier to follow.

Argument Development

You assert that "crime does not pay," but the essay would be stronger if you explicitly analyzed the story to demonstrate *why* this is true, rather than simply narrating it. Right now, you tell us what happened to the man and his family, but you don't fully connect this back to your claim. Consider adding a paragraph (or expanding your conclusion) that explicitly argues:
What did the man "gain" temporarily, and how was it destroyed?
Was the temporary wealth ever *worth* the risk?
What does this suggest about the nature of illegally-obtained wealth in general?

This is also where you could acknowledge a counterargument: some criminals *do* get away with their crimes for years, or even permanently. Addressing this makes your argument more sophisticated—perhaps your claim is not that crime *never* pays financially, but that it carries risks and costs (legal, relational, psychological) that outweigh the benefits even when the money remains.

Use of Personal Example

The story about "someone you know" is your strongest asset, but it needs more integration:
Clarify the relationship earlier (Is this a family friend? An acquaintance?) so the reader understands your connection to the story.
The transition into the story is abrupt: "Just as the person knows, who I was referring to above" is confusing syntax—revise for clarity.
Consider adding a sentence reflecting on *how* this story shaped your own view on the topic. This is your evidence—use it to explicitly support your thesis.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring errors to address in revision:

Word choice/spelling: "societies" → "society's"; "expectable" → "acceptable" or "normal"; "catigory" → "category"; "beleive" → "believe"; "will" → "willing"; "way" → "was" (in "His family way very proud").
Homophone/word confusion: "an easier way" not "and easier way."
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "Crime does not pay, is a very true statement" should be restructured—perhaps "'Crime does not pay' is a very true statement" or "The saying 'crime does not pay' is very true."
Pronoun clarity: In the second paragraph, you shift between "he," "his family," and "they" in ways that occasionally create ambiguity about who is being discussed.
Awkward phrasing: "He was making good money and his family had all the things that they needed" is a bit flat—consider varying sentence structure and adding more specific, vivid detail (as you did earlier with "the house in which they lived").

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your introduction to clearly state your thesis in your own words (not through the title reference).
Reorganize paragraphs so explanation and narrative are distinct, with clear transitions between them.
Expand your analysis to explicitly connect the story to your central claim.
Consider briefly addressing a counterargument (some criminals succeed for a long time) to strengthen your position.
Proofread carefully for the spelling and grammar issues listed above—reading your essay aloud can help catch these.

Final Note

You have a strong instinct for using a real example to make an abstract claim concrete—this is a valuable skill. With more explicit analysis connecting your evidence to your thesis, and careful attention to structure and grammar, this essay could be quite persuasive. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to bring a draft to office hours for further discussion.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay engages with a genuinely interesting question—the relationship between cultural values and economic compensation—and the child care example is well-chosen because it makes an abstract argument concrete. However, several issues currently prevent the essay from making its full impact. Below is feedback organized by priority.

Missing Prompt Reference

Your essay opens with "," which appears to be a placeholder that was never filled in with the actual claim you're responding to. This is a significant oversight: readers need to know precisely what proposition you're evaluating (eg, "equal work deserves equal pay") before they can follow your argument. Please ensure the final draft states the idea you're examining explicitly and clearly in your first sentence.

Thesis Clarity

Your central claim seems to be that societal judgments of "value" are culturally constructed rather than objective, and that this construction unfairly disadvantages child care workers. This is a strong, debatable thesis—but it doesn't emerge until partway through paragraph one, and even then it's stated somewhat abstractly ("deeper cultural assumptions about values of various societal functions"). Consider stating your thesis in more concrete, assertive language early on, so readers know exactly what you intend to prove and how the child care example will serve that proof.

Logical Structure and the Ending

This is the most pressing issue in the essay. Your argument builds logically for three paragraphs: (1) equal pay for equal *work* isn't the real issue; (2) societal value is a cultural construct, illustrated by child care; (3) this undervaluing has real economic consequences for the nation. This progression is compelling.

However, your final sentences seem to *undercut* your own argument. You write that "the final irony is that, until the financial status of these workers becomes more equal, they will not be judged as making a valuable contribution"—but earlier in the essay, your whole point was that pay follows judgments of value, not the reverse (workers are underpaid *because* they're seen as low-value). The ending reverses this causal relationship without acknowledging the reversal, making the essay feel like it's arguing in a circle rather than reaching a resolution.

Revision suggestion: Decide which causal direction you actually want to argue (value → pay, or pay → value, or a mutually reinforcing cycle), and make that relationship explicit and consistent throughout. If you intend to argue it's a *cycle* — perception depresses pay, low pay reinforces perception — say so directly. That would actually be a sophisticated and defensible claim, but right now it reads as an unintended contradiction.

Evidence and Development

The essay currently relies almost entirely on assertion and abstract reasoning ("it is not difficult to conclude," "it is tempting to conclude"). To strengthen your argument, consider adding:
Concrete data on child care worker wages compared to other professions requiring similar credentials or working hours.
Historical or comparative examples (eg, how other countries structure and compensate child care, or how the perceived value of teaching has shifted over time).
A specific example of the "diversity of workers" you mention—perhaps a brief case study of how the lack of affordable, quality child care affects labor force participation, particularly for women.

Even one or two concrete statistics or examples would considerably strengthen your credibility and give readers something more substantial to evaluate.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

"man with family responsibilities" should be "men" (agreement error).
"pereniel" should be "perennial."
The sentence "It is not difficult to conclude that child care workers are underpaid, and the issue will be difficult to resolve without involving employers or the government" is logically awkward—you introduce a solution (employer/government involvement) and then immediately dismiss it ("which may not be the ideal solution") without explaining why. If you raise this as a possible remedy, briefly justify your skepticism toward it, or cut the aside if it's a tangent.
Several sentences are quite long and stack multiple qualifying clauses (eg, the sentence beginning "It is difficult to imagine, however, a society..."). Consider breaking these into shorter sentences for clarity, especially in a paragraph where you're building a chain of conditional reasoning.

Paragraph Transitions

Your paragraphs each contain a solid idea, but the transitions between them could be more explicit. For instance, the jump from paragraph 2 (child care as undervalued) to paragraph 3 (economic consequences for the nation) would benefit from a transitional sentence that signals you're moving from *describing* the problem to *analyzing its consequences*.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in the placeholder and clarify your opening claim.
Resolve the contradiction in your conclusion regarding the direction of causality between pay and perceived value.
Add concrete evidence (data, examples) to support your claims.
Correct minor grammar errors and tighten long sentences.
Strengthen paragraph transitions to make your logical progression more visible to the reader.

Overall, you have the makings of a thoughtful, original argument here. The core insight—that economic value is culturally constructed rather than objectively determined—is worth developing further and could be even more persuasive with clearer logic and stronger evidence.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay tackles a genuinely important and complex issue—how society values (or fails to value) unpaid domestic labor—and you write with conviction and clarity of voice. The prose is engaging, and your rhetorical questions are effective at drawing the reader into the problem. Below are suggestions organized by argumentative structure, evidence, and style to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis is somewhat diffuse. In your introduction, you propose "some sort of compensation should be made for 'home-making service.'" But by the conclusion, you've shifted to a different, more specific proposal: paycheck made out to "the couple" or "family" rather than the individual. These are not the same argument. The first suggests direct payment for homemaking labor (from whom? the state? a spouse?); the second suggests restructuring *existing* wages to be shared within a household. Readers will experience whiplash if the essay's central claim changes shape between the opening and closing paragraphs. Decide which claim you actually want to defend, then make sure every paragraph builds toward that single, specific proposal. If you want to end with the "joint paycheck" idea, foreshadow it in your introduction rather than introducing it only in the final paragraph.

Development of Key Terms

You raise the crucial question "But what is 'equal work'?" but then don't fully answer it. You establish that comparing homemaking to a neurosurgeon's work is nearly impossible—but this cuts against your own argument. If the labor is "immeasurable," how do we assign it a dollar value? This is the central logical problem of your essay, and right now you name it without resolving it. Consider engaging directly with the various ways economists and policymakers *have* tried to quantify this (eg, studies estimating the "market value" of a stay-at-home parent's labor by pricing out equivalent services—childcare, cooking, cleaning, tutoring). Even if you ultimately argue that dollar equivalence is impossible or reductive, showing that you've considered the counterargument will make your final proposal more persuasive.

Evidence and Specificity

The essay relies heavily on assertion ("many of the ills of society... have been traced by study after study") without citing any actual studies, statistics, or named sources. For an argumentative essay, unsupported appeals to authority ("studies show") weaken your credibility rather than strengthen it. Even brief, specific references would help: Which studies? What countries currently offer caregiver stipends or "parental wage" policies (some Nordic countries, for instance, have experimented with related policies)? Concrete comparative examples—other nations' approaches to valuing domestic labor, historical debates around "Wages for Housework" movements in the 1970s—would ground your abstract claims in something the reader can verify and engage with.

Addressing Counterarguments

Your essay would be more persuasive if it anticipated objections. For example: Who would pay for home-making compensation—the state, through taxes? A spouse, from shared income? What about single parents, or people who choose not to have children but still keep house? Does compensating homemaking risk *reinforcing* traditional gender roles by formalizing the domestic sphere as separate from the "real" economy, rather than encouraging more equitable sharing of both paid and unpaid labor between partners? A brief paragraph considering one or two of these complications—and responding to them—would demonstrate more rigorous thinking and preempt a skeptical reader's objections.

Structural Suggestions

Paragraph 2 (on "career with a capital C") does useful work establishing what marketplace jobs already receive recognition, but its final line about "equal pay for equal work" could transition more explicitly into paragraph 3's central question, rather than functioning as a stand-alone platitude.
Consider adding a short paragraph near the end that explicitly weighs the two solutions you gesture toward (direct compensation vs. shared paycheck) against each other, rather than presenting the joint-paycheck idea as an afterthought in your final sentences. Right now the ending feels tentative ("Perhaps this is a solution worth its salt")—for an argumentative essay, you want your conclusion to land with more conviction, even if you're advocating for further societal reflection rather than a single fixed policy.

Style Notes

Watch for a few typos/grammatical slips: "an neurosurgeon" should be "a neurosurgeon"; "so immense" should be "is immense"; "it, of course, the worker is married" is garbled and needs revision for clarity.
The phrase "doctors, lawyers and Indian chiefs" borrows a colloquial idiom that may read as flippant or (given the reference to "Indian chiefs") potentially insensitive to some readers; consider replacing it with a more direct list of professions.

Overall: You have a compelling, socially relevant argument at the core of this essay. Tightening your thesis, supporting your claims with concrete evidence, and directly grappling with the "immeasurability" problem you yourself raise will transform this from a passionate op-ed into a rigorous piece of argumentative writing.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback on "Money, Desire, and the Limits of Moral Aphorism"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting philosophical puzzle—testing the logical coherence of "money is the root of all evil"—and your opening line is genuinely striking. The essay shows real analytical ambition: you're not simply agreeing or disagreeing with the saying but interrogating its internal logic. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: clarity of definitions, structural completeness, and concrete support.

Argument and Content

Your core move—testing the saying as if it were a testable correlation—is clever, but you introduce and manipulate key terms (*desire*, *happiness*, *virtue*) too quickly and without stable definitions. For instance:

You claim desire "cannot be measured on some utilitarian scale of pleasure," then immediately assert it "increases and decreases with the acquirement of wealth." These two claims sit in tension: if desire can't be measured, how do you know it increases/decreases with wealth? Clarify what kind of claim you're making—empirical, conceptual, or purely logical.
You equate "happiness" with "a lack of desire for further money" and then equate *that* with virtue. This is a big philosophical leap (Stoic/Buddhist in flavor) that needs to be argued for, not asserted. A skeptical reader will ask: why should contentment with wealth count as virtuous, rather than merely comfortable or complacent?

The essay's most serious problem is structural: the placeholder  appears where your key transitional claim should be. As it stands, the final paragraph opens with "A truer statement, I think" with no antecedent statement given. This leaves your conclusion floating—readers cannot tell what alternative claim you're proposing to replace the original aphorism. This needs to be filled in explicitly: what *is* the truer statement? Right now the essay ends with a broad historical generalization ("those with power... are just as often the oppressors as saviors") that feels asserted rather than earned by the preceding argument.

Structure

Right now the essay moves: (1) logical critique of the saying via correlation, (2) a psychological argument about happiness and virtue, (3) a demonstration that this creates a contradiction, (4) an incomplete resolution. This is a reasonable skeleton, but the transitions between sections are abrupt. Paragraph 2 pivots to "happiness" without explaining why this concept is relevant to testing the original saying. Paragraph 3 announces "the problem becomes immediately clear," but the problem needs one more sentence of scaffolding for readers unfamiliar with your reasoning.

Consider adding a brief roadmap sentence near the beginning (after your excellent opening line) that tells readers the shape of your argument: something like, "I will argue that the saying fails not because it is false, but because it treats two immeasurable things—desire and virtue—as if they could be objectively weighed against each other."

Evidence

The essay is entirely abstract—no historical examples, no philosophical authorities (Aristotle on virtue, Marx on wealth, Franklin's original phrasing of the aphorism), no concrete cases of wealthy people behaving virtuously or viciously. Your final paragraph gestures at "history" but offers no example. Even one or two brief illustrations (a philanthropist heir vs. a robber baron, for instance) would ground your abstract logic and make the argument more persuasive and readable.

Style and Mechanics

The prose is intelligent but needs cleaning up:

Spelling/typos: "privelaged" → *privileged*; "acruement" → *accumulation* or *accrual*; "wheighed" → *weighed*; "serrendipitously" → *serendipitously*. Please proofread carefully.
Awkward phrasing: "the wealthy would be immediately privelaged in such a formula" is unclear—privileged in what sense? Rephrase for precision.
Redundant negation: "meaningless—or a[t] least impractical" is a good hedge, but the sentence around it ("since desire—much less evil—cannot be appropriated objectively") should read "apportioned" rather than "appropriated," which has a different connotation (seizing/taking).
Fragment/awkward construction: "called 'compatible' since... they are mutually exclusive" is confusing—do you mean they *cannot* be called compatible? Revise for clarity.
Watch overuse of italics/quotation marks around key terms (*happiness*, *virtue*)—use them consistently only at first definition, not every subsequent use.

Priorities for Revision

Fill in the missing  section—this is essential; the essay currently cannot be evaluated as complete.
Define your key terms early and consistently (desire, virtue, happiness).
Add at least one concrete historical or literary example to ground the abstraction.
Proofread for spelling and clarify a few awkward sentences as noted above.
Write a clearer thesis statement near the opening that previews your full argument.

You have a genuinely interesting philosophical instinct here—now it needs the scaffolding to let readers follow you all the way to your conclusion.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You're grappling with an ambitious and interesting question—whether Marx's egalitarian ideal is achievable in a Western, individualist context—and you bring some genuine insight to the family and religion sections. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of loosely connected reflections than a tightly argued essay. The revision should focus on sharpening your thesis, strengthening the logical connections between paragraphs, and restoring/citing the quotations that are currently missing.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your central claim doesn't emerge clearly until the final sentence: that the egalitarian ideal is "admirable" but "unlikely to be achieved" in Western culture. This is a reasonable thesis, but it needs to appear early—ideally at the end of your introduction—so readers know what claim you're building toward and can evaluate each paragraph's contribution to that claim.

Right now the essay's structure is: (1) family exhibits Marx's principle naturally, (2) religion reinforces it, (3) but monetary reward systems raise problems, (4) individualism conflicts with collective good, (5) moral relativism undermines shared ethics, (6) therefore the ideal won't be realized. This is a workable skeleton, but you need explicit transitions that tell the reader *why* each new point follows from the last. For instance, after establishing that family and religion instill egalitarian norms, you pivot to critiquing monetary contribution—but you never explain why the argument moves from private/religious spheres to public economic ones. A sentence bridging these ("While family and religion may successfully instill egalitarian values on a small scale, the same principle becomes far more contested when applied to society's distribution of wealth") would help readers follow your logic.

Development of Ideas

Your analogy between family nurturing and Marxist egalitarianism is intriguing, but it needs more scrutiny. Marx's principle is fundamentally about the relationship between production and distribution in a *society*, not about parental instinct. You should either justify the analogy more carefully (why does familial altruism count as evidence for a broader social principle?) or acknowledge its limits before using it as your first example.

The paragraph on monetary reward asks strong rhetorical questions ("who is to define what contribution merits what reward?") but doesn't fully answer them or draw out their implications for your thesis. Consider stating explicitly what these questions reveal—perhaps that egalitarian ideals falter when they require an external authority to judge individual contribution, which conflicts with Western respect for autonomy.

The discussion of moral relativism at the end feels somewhat disconnected from the rest of the essay. You assert that relativism makes it "difficult to impose such an ethic," but you don't explain the connection between individualism (discussed in the previous paragraph) and relativism (discussed here). Are you arguing that individualism *causes* relativism? If so, say so directly and give a brief example.

Missing Quotations

You have three placeholder marks () where quotations should appear—presumably Marx's "From each according to his ability, to each according to his needs," a New Testament passage (perhaps Acts 2:44-45 or the Golden Rule), and the phrase about "giving back to society." These quotations are essential to your argument and must be restored, properly cited, and briefly glossed for readers unfamiliar with them. As it stands, a reader cannot fully evaluate your argument without knowing what these quotes actually say.

Style and Mechanics

There are several typos and grammatical slips that need correcting:
"on e would understand" → "one would understand"
"who it to define" → "who is to define"
"learned primarily form the family" → "from the family"
"society or culture is the first to apply" is awkward; consider "is often the first site where..."

Additionally, some sentences are quite short and declarative in a way that makes the prose feel choppy rather than analytical (eg, "Society also has social institutions & practices in order which preserve the egalitarian concept"). Avoid ampersands (&) in formal prose—write out "and."

Conclusion

Your conclusion is only one sentence and doesn't synthesize the essay's various threads (family, religion, economics, individualism, relativism) into a final, memorable statement. Expand this into a short paragraph that briefly recaps your main line of reasoning and explains *why* this conclusion matters—what does it tell us about Western society, or about the limits of imported ideological systems?

Summary of Priority Revisions

Restore and cite all quotations.
State your thesis explicitly at the end of the introduction.
Add transitional sentences explaining how each paragraph builds on the last.
Justify or qualify the family/Marxism analogy.
Strengthen the connection between individualism and moral relativism.
Proofread for grammar and typos.
Expand the conclusion to synthesize your argument.

With these revisions, the essay's genuine insights—particularly on the tension between individual freedom and collective good—will come through much more persuasively.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback on "Does Crime Pay?"

Overall Impression

This essay demonstrates real conceptual creativity—framing criminal activity through the lens of cost-benefit business analysis is an effective way to complicate a seemingly simple question. Your instinct to distinguish between literal dollar accounting and a "deeper" calculation of gains versus losses shows genuine analytical sophistication. However, the essay currently reads more as a thoughtful exploration than as an argumentative essay with a defensible thesis. Below are suggestions for strengthening the piece.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim is elusive. The essay's opening promises to determine whether crime pays, but the conclusion offers only "In some cases it may"—a hedge that leaves the reader without a clear answer to the question you posed. For an argumentative essay, you need a thesis that takes a defensible position, even if that position is nuanced (eg, "Crime rarely pays when calculated as a genuine cost-benefit analysis, because criminals systematically underestimate hidden costs" or "Whether crime pays depends less on individual crimes than on structural factors like education and social capital").

Right now, the essay functions more as a taxonomy of costs than as a sustained argument. Consider revising your introduction to forecast a specific claim, and then use each body paragraph to build toward that claim rather than simply cataloging types of loss.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's most significant weakness. The entire argument rests on hypothetical scenarios and reasoning by analogy (crime as small business) rather than actual evidence. While the jewelry store example is a useful illustration, illustrations are not evidence—they demonstrate a concept but don't prove it applies broadly.

To strengthen your argument, consider incorporating:
Criminology or economics research on the actual earnings of criminals relative to legal alternatives (economists like Gary Becker and later researchers such as Steven Levitt have written specifically on this question).
Statistics on recidivism, incarceration rates, or injury rates among people involved in criminal enterprises.
Case studies of specific criminal operations (drug trafficking organizations, for instance) where cost structures have been documented.

Even citing a few sources would transform this from a speculative essay into a substantiated argument, and it would also give you material to test your business analogy against real-world complexity.

Organization

The paragraph structure is logical—you move from capital costs to health costs to legal costs, each building on the last. However, transitions between paragraphs are abrupt; each new paragraph announces "another type of loss" without explaining how it relates to what preceded it or advances your overall argument. Consider adding a sentence at the end of each paragraph that ties the specific cost back to your central claim.

Additionally, some potential counterarguments are missing. What about criminals motivated by something other than profit maximization—thrill-seeking, ideology, desperation? What about crimes with very low capital investment and low risk (eg, certain white-collar crimes) where the cost-benefit calculation might favor the criminal more clearly than in your violent, high-risk examples? Addressing these complications would make your analysis more complete and your eventual thesis more credible.

Style and Tone

Your prose is generally clear and readable, and the business metaphor is sustained effectively throughout. A few specific issues:

The closing line ("no taxes") is clever but undercuts the essay's argumentative seriousness at exactly the moment you should be delivering your strongest, most conclusive statement. Save the wit for elsewhere and use your conclusion to drive home your thesis.
Vague references need unpacking. "Mafia and police pay-offs" is mentioned without explanation—what does this cost look like, and how significant is it?
Awkward phrasing: "crimes can earn less to justify time spent" is unclear—consider rewriting for precision.
Spelling: "equiptment" should be "equipment."

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into a specific, defensible claim.
Add at least two or three pieces of concrete evidence (statistics, research findings, or documented cases) to ground your cost categories in reality rather than hypothetical reasoning.
Address at least one counterargument or complicating factor.
Revise your conclusion so that it resolves the question you raised in the introduction, saving any clever final flourish for after you've delivered a substantive answer.
Proofread for the spelling error and awkward phrasings noted above.

This essay has a strong conceptual foundation—your job now is to give it the evidentiary backbone and argumentative clarity it needs to fully persuade your reader.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Love of Money"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting philosophical distinction—between money itself and the *love* of money—and you clearly have a well-formed opinion on the matter. The core insight (that money is a neutral tool, but an excessive attachment to it is corrosive) is a strong thesis. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of related reflections than a tightly organized argument. With some revision to structure, evidence, and precision of language, this could become a much more persuasive piece.

Establishing Context

The essay opens with "This idea is completely erroneous," but the reader has no idea what "idea" you are referring to. If this essay responds to a specific prompt or quotation (likely "money is the root of all evil"), you need to state that claim explicitly in your first sentence or two before refuting it. Right now, the reader is dropped into the middle of an argument without being told what the argument is about. Consider opening with something like: "The popular saying that 'money is the root of all evil' is a common misreading of a much older idea. In fact, it is not money itself but the *love* of money that leads people astray." This gives your essay a clear target and lets your thesis emerge naturally.

Additionally, if you are drawing on a specific source (this phrasing closely echoes 1 Timothy 6:10 in the Bible), you should cite it explicitly rather than let it float as an unattributed proverb. This will strengthen your credibility and clarify that you are engaging in a real interpretive debate, not just asserting an opinion.

Structure and Development

The essay's structure is repetitive: you state that money is neutral, then that love of money is dangerous, then you illustrate this with a list of consequences (broken families, failed companies, crumbling nations), then you restate the neutral/dangerous distinction again in the conclusion. Because the same point is made three or four times in slightly different words, the essay feels circular rather than progressive.

Consider organizing your paragraphs around a clear logical sequence:
State the claim you're responding to and your thesis (money vs. love of money).
Explain what money is *for* — its legitimate, necessary functions (trade, security, generosity).
Explain what happens when the desire for money becomes distorted — give one or two developed examples rather than a broad list.
Explain the psychological/social mechanism — why this desire takes hold (this is where your point about children learning the "power" of money is genuinely interesting and could be expanded).
Conclude by returning to your refined thesis with a fresh insight, not just a repetition.

Evidence and Support

This is the area needing the most attention. Right now, your evidence is vague and hypothetical: "How many times have we seen on the evening news…" This rhetorical question assumes shared knowledge but doesn't actually supply any information. Real essays need real support. You don't need statistics, but even a single concrete example—a historical case, a well-known corporate scandal, a specific psychological study on materialism and family conflict—would make your argument far more convincing. Right now, a skeptical reader could reasonably ask, "Where is this happening? To whom? How often?"

Your example of the 4- or 5-year-old child discovering the "power" of money is a nice, original observation—more of this kind of specific, small-scale illustration would serve the essay better than broad generalizations about "society."

Style and Word Choice

Your sentences are often eloquent, but some phrasing is imprecise or clichéd: "wreaks havoc," "wooed their hearts" (capitivates should be "captivates"), "wealth... pushed to an extreme" is used twice almost verbatim. Watch for repeated phrases across paragraphs—this creates a sense that the essay is circling rather than advancing.

There are also a few word-choice and typo issues to fix:
"capitivates" → captivates
"sued for good" → used for good
"it's needs met" → its needs met (possessive, not contraction)
"all together" → altogether (when meaning "entirely")

Grammar and Mechanics

Watch subject-verb agreement and sentence fragments, eg, "is money the root of all evil? No." should begin with a capital "Is." Also check comma placement in "Money perhaps, is a necessary evil"—the commas around "perhaps" are used inconsistently.

Summary of Priorities

Clarify what claim you're responding to at the very start.
Cut repetition; each paragraph should add something new.
Replace vague appeals ("we've all seen on the news") with one or two specific, developed examples.
Proofread carefully for typos and word confusion (its/it's, capitivates/captivates).
End with a conclusion that adds nuance rather than restating the thesis verbatim.

With tighter structure and concrete evidence, your genuinely interesting central idea will come through much more powerfully.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Crime Doesn't Pay"

Overall Impression

This is a lively, well-organized essay with a clear and defensible thesis: that "crime doesn't pay" functions more as comforting wishful thinking than as an empirical truth. You build your case through a logical progression—individual criminals, then systemic incentives—and your writing voice is engaging, with nice touches of irony ("a more cold-blooded statistician than I am"). The essay's real strength is its move from anecdote to a more structural argument about the incarceration industry, which elevates the piece above a simple list of counterexamples. Below are suggestions to sharpen the argument and tighten the prose.

Structural and Logical Issues

The placeholder for your title phrase. You've left "" in the first sentence rather than stating the actual phrase ("Crime doesn't pay"). Even if this was a formatting error, make sure the phrase appears explicitly and early, since your whole essay depends on the reader knowing exactly what claim you're testing.

Clarify the logical backbone. Your key premise—"Behavior that is not reinforced in some way will not persist"—is doing a lot of work here, essentially importing a behaviorist psychological principle to structure your whole argument. This is a smart move, but it needs to be flagged as a premise, not stated as self-evident fact. Consider explicitly naming it as an assumption drawn from behavioral psychology, and perhaps acknowledge that human motivation is more complex than simple reinforcement (people commit crimes for reasons beyond calculated payoff—passion, desperation, addiction). Addressing this makes your argument more sophisticated rather than weakening it.

Distinguish between different senses of "paying." Your essay moves through at least three different meanings of "crime pays": (1) it pays for individual criminals who profit despite punishment (Milken, Nixon), (2) it pays in a cost-benefit sense for calculating criminals (the murder statistic), and (3) it pays for *institutions* that profit from incarceration itself. This last point is your most original and interesting move, but the transition needs to be made explicit. Right now you write, "In addition to having crime pay for some well-known criminals, I think it could also be argued that crime pays for an industry that thrives on it"—but you might slow down here and explain that you're shifting from asking "does crime pay for the criminal?" to "does crime pay for the system?" These are related but distinct claims, and naming the shift will help readers follow your reasoning.

Consider the counterargument more directly. Your examples (Milken, Nixon, a hypothetical calculating murderer) are compelling, but they are also unusual, high-profile cases. A skeptical reader might argue that most crime does *not* pay—that these are outliers, and the saying holds true on average even if it fails in dramatic individual cases. Your essay would be stronger if you anticipated this objection. You don't need to concede the point, but showing you've considered it (perhaps by acknowledging that the phrase may function as a *statistical* truth even while failing as a *moral certainty*) would make your conclusion more persuasive rather than simply asserting "wishful thinking."

Evidence and Documentation

Several of your claims need sourcing or more precision:

The "15 years average" statistic for murder convictions should be cited. Where does this come from? Is it a national average, and does "murder" include manslaughter?
The Michael Milken example needs a name correction (you've written "Milliken"), and the claim about his earnings versus restitution should be supported with a specific figure or source.
The Indiana CHINS example is effective and concrete, but a citation or specific cost figure (rather than "several thousand dollars") would strengthen it.

As it stands, the essay reads as informed opinion; a few citations would convert it into something closer to persuasive argument backed by evidence, which matters especially in an academic context.

Style Notes

Your prose is generally strong, but watch for tonal consistency. Phrases like "miscreants" and "cold-blooded statistician" have a wry, slightly formal humor that works well—keep this voice, but make sure factual claims (dates, figures, names) are precise enough to match the confidence of your tone.
The Nixon paragraph could use one more sentence connecting his post-scandal rehabilitation more explicitly back to your thesis—right now the final clause ("honored as a 'statesman' rather than a criminal") is a strong ironic point, but you might spell out *why* this counts as "crime paying" (reputation and legacy as a form of payoff, distinct from money).

Suggested Revisions Going Forward

Restore the actual phrase in your opening sentence.
Add a sentence naming your reinforcement premise explicitly as an assumption.
Insert a transition sentence marking your shift from individual to institutional "payoff."
Address the outlier-versus-average counterargument, even briefly.
Correct "Milliken" to "Milken" and add citations for statistics.

This essay has a genuinely interesting thesis and good instincts for using irony and concrete example. With tighter logical signposting and stronger sourcing, it will be quite persuasive.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Financial Reward and Effort

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable question: should financial reward be tied to individual effort? You clearly have opinions on this topic, and your writing shows some genuine engagement with the ideas. However, the essay currently reads more as a series of related musings than a tightly constructed argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen for your revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis appears in the first paragraph: "men and women should be financially rewarded for their individual efforts." This is a reasonable claim, but it needs sharpening. Ask yourself: what specifically are you arguing *against*? Is this a response to socialism, universal basic income, minimum wage policies, or something else? Right now, the essay argues against an unspecified alternative (a hypothetical equal society), which makes your position feel abstract rather than engaged with real debates. Consider naming the counter-position explicitly and early on.

Structure and Logical Development

The essay's structure follows an intuitive chronological pattern (past → present → hypothetical future), but this organization doesn't necessarily serve your *argument*. Right now, the paragraphs feel more like separate reflections than building blocks of a single case. Consider organizing around your reasons instead:

Reason 1: Historical/logical connection between effort and reward
Reason 2: Reward-based systems drive innovation and technological progress
Reason 3: Removing reward incentives would create negative social consequences

This would let you develop each claim with more depth rather than moving quickly through time periods.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's greatest area for growth. Currently, your support consists almost entirely of hypothetical scenarios and personal assertions ("I would venture to guess," "I feel," "I don't think so"). For an argumentative essay, you need:

Concrete examples: Historical or contemporary cases where reward-based systems produced measurable benefits (economic growth, innovation, productivity data).
Counterarguments addressed: What about people who work hard but aren't rewarded fairly (eg, systemic inequality, discrimination, luck, inherited wealth)? Acknowledging this complicates but strengthens your argument—ignoring it weakens your credibility.
Specific rather than general claims: "Fights would occur" and "the world would become divided or even destroyed" are dramatic but unsupported. What historical examples of collectivist experiments could you point to instead? What do economists or sociologists say about this?

Paragraph-by-Paragraph Notes

Paragraph 1: The opening is vague ("many standards," "the past") and doesn't preview your argument. Consider starting with a clearer claim or a provocative question that signals your stance.

Paragraph 2: The "In order to..." repetition is a nice rhetorical touch, but the paragraph's final claim—"the harder you worked, the more you gained"—needs a caveat. This wasn't universally true even historically (weather, health, social status affected outcomes independent of effort). Precision here would strengthen your credibility.

Paragraph 3: This paragraph's logic is unclear. You note that some people "pay someone to do their labor," which could actually *undermine* your argument (wealthy people benefiting from others' labor, not just their own effort). This tension needs to be addressed, not glossed over.

Paragraph 4: This is your most developed paragraph, but it relies heavily on speculation and hyperbole. The transition from "competitiveness and jealousy" to "the world would become divided or even destroyed" is a significant logical leap. Scale back the claims to what you can actually support, or provide evidence for why you believe this escalation would occur.

Paragraph 5: This concluding paragraph raises an interesting question—"is it really about money?"—that feels like it belongs *earlier* in the essay, as it complicates your thesis in an interesting way. Consider integrating this idea into your body paragraphs rather than introducing it in the conclusion.

Style and Mechanics

Watch for grammatical slips: "should be financially reward" (should be "rewarded"), "took up of majority" (remove "of"), "jealously" (should be "jealousy"), "held us meet" (should be "help us meet").
Some sentences are quite short and choppy (paragraph 2's "In order to..." sequence); consider varying sentence length for better flow.
The exclamation points ("much more!", "even wants!") feel more appropriate for informal writing than academic argument—consider removing them for a more measured tone.

Suggested Next Steps

Identify a specific counter-position you're arguing against.
Reorganize paragraphs around reasons, not chronology.
Add at least two concrete, researched examples (historical, economic, or sociological).
Directly address the strongest counterargument (unequal starting points, luck, inherited advantage).
Proofread for the grammatical issues noted above.

This essay has a clear point of view and some genuine passion behind it—your task now is to build an evidentiary structure that supports the ideas you're already expressing.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a genuinely interesting and difficult question—whether compensation *should* correspond to a person's "contribution to society"—but the argument doesn't yet fully cohere. You have several promising threads (the 1920s critique of inherited wealth, the migrant worker example, the doctors example), but they aren't woven into a single, sustained line of reasoning. Right now, the essay reads more like a set of observations orbiting a topic than an argument building toward a conclusion. Revision should focus on clarifying your thesis, tightening the logical connections between paragraphs, and strengthening your evidence.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening paragraph asserts that "contribution to society" is subjective and impossible to compensate equally, but this claim is somewhat buried and phrased tentatively ("never so simple," "certainly," "some employers are sure to"). Since this appears to be your actual thesis (confirmed by the essay's final line), it needs to be stated more directly and prominently—ideally as the last sentence of your introduction, phrased as a clear, arguable claim.

More importantly, the body paragraphs don't consistently support this thesis. The 1920s paragraph criticizes people who live off investment income as parasitic; the migrant worker paragraph highlights unfair pay for hard labor; the "Democracy" paragraph introduces competing philosophical views (meritocracy vs. structural luck) but doesn't resolve them. By the time you reach the doctors example, the essay pivots to a fairly different point—that market prices reflect a kind of consensus about fairness. These are all interesting angles, but as written they read like separate essays on the general theme of pay and fairness, rather than steps in one argument. Ask yourself: what is the *single* claim I want to prove, and how does each paragraph move the reader toward it? You might restructure around this logic: (1) people disagree fundamentally about what "fair contribution" means; (2) historical and contemporary examples show this disagreement can't be resolved through outrage or moral judgment alone; (3) therefore, pay cannot be tied to contribution without imposing one contestable value system on everyone.

Organization and Transitions

Several paragraphs shift topics abruptly. The move from 1920s speculators to "people who inherit money" is reasonable, but the transition into migrant workers ("What about people who work very hard...") arrives without a signal that you're now introducing a contrasting case. Similarly, the paragraph on "Democracy" (note: this should not be capitalized) introduces two opposing views on desert and luck, but never explicitly connects them back to your thesis about subjectivity. Consider adding transitional sentences that state explicitly why each new example matters to your overall claim—eg, "This same tension appears at the other end of the economic spectrum" before the migrant worker paragraph.

Evidence and Development

The historical reference to 1920s American authors is vague and would benefit from specificity: which authors? Fitzgerald's *The Great Gatsby* seems implicitly present here, but naming and briefly quoting or summarizing a specific text would strengthen your credibility and give readers something concrete to evaluate.

The doctors example is clever but underdeveloped, and the logic deserves scrutiny: you infer that current fees must be "fair" simply because doctors haven't lowered them. This is a weak inference (people often charge what the market allows, not what is "fair"), and a sharp reader will notice the gap. Either qualify this claim or use it more explicitly as an example of how "fairness" gets defined by social consensus rather than objective merit—which would actually reinforce your subjectivity thesis nicely.

Sentence-Level and Mechanics

"In the 1920s in American" → "in America"
"Democracry" → "democracy" (and lowercase)
"The answer this question lies" → "The answer to this question lies"
"none of the doctors are lowering" → subject-verb agreement: "none of the doctors is lowering," or rephrase as "no doctors are lowering"
"much too money" → "far too much money"
"responsibly" → "responsibility"
"A migrant work" → "A migrant worker"
Inconsistent/incorrect quotation mark placement (eg, "equally'.")

A careful proofreading pass will catch these; reading the essay aloud often helps.

Priorities for Revision

State your thesis explicitly and early.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one visibly advances that thesis.
Add a specific literary reference for the 1920s section.
Reconsider and clarify the logic of the doctors example.
Proofread carefully for grammar and mechanics.

You have real substance here—now the task is architecture.

Feedback on "Crime Pays" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a provocative, counterintuitive thesis, and your examples are lively and varied. The essay has real potential, but it needs significant revision in terms of organization, argumentative rigor, and precision. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your thesis appears in the first paragraph: crime can be profitable when we consider "historical context, current material gain, and the consequences of crime in America." This is a reasonable three-part structure, but the categories overlap confusingly. "Historical context" and "consequences" both end up discussing similar phenomena (celebrity criminals, media profit), making paragraphs 2 and 4 feel repetitive rather than building toward a cumulative point. Consider reorganizing around clearer, non-overlapping categories—for example: (1) direct financial gain from criminal acts themselves, (2) indirect profit through media/fame after the fact, and (3) systemic failures that allow criminals to escape real punishment.

Logical Weaknesses to Address

Several claims need more careful reasoning:

The Boston Tea Party and Benedict Arnold examples work against you. These are cases where the *legitimacy* of the law itself is in question (revolutionary defiance of unjust rule), not examples of "crime paying" in the sense you mean elsewhere (financial profit despite guilt). Using them alongside Prohibition bootleggers muddies your argument—are you arguing crime pays financially, or that criminality is often just a matter of historical perspective? These are different claims and need to be separated or clarified.

"No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money, if it were not why would people commit them" is a circular argument and overstates your case. People commit crimes for many reasons (desperation, passion, addiction, ideology)—not all crime is motivated by profit-seeking, and this sentence undermines your credibility by ignoring obvious counterexamples.

The O.J. Simpson example is worth expanding, but you should clarify *what* he profited from—is fame itself the "profit," even amid tarnished reputation? This distinction (fame vs. actual financial security vs. moral cost) deserves more nuanced treatment throughout the essay.

Consider explicitly addressing the counterargument: what would opponents say about the *majority* of criminals who do face real consequences (prison, poverty, stigma)? Right now your essay uses only success stories, which weakens its persuasive force. Acknowledging that these are exceptions—but *revealing* exceptions—would strengthen rather than weaken your argument.

Evidence and Development

Your historical and cultural references (Divine Brown, Joey Buttafuco, Las Vegas, Prohibition) are engaging, but most are asserted rather than developed. For each example, add a sentence explaining *how much* profit was gained, or *what specifically* enabled it. This will move you from anecdote-dropping to genuine argument-building.

Style and Tone

The essay's voice is confident and often witty ("Apparently crime can be quite profitable if one has the right agent"), which suits an argumentative piece. However, watch for:

Sarcasm undermining precision. Phrases like "the ever present laws" and "learning new ways to break" are effective rhetorically but can read as glib rather than analytical. Balance wit with more substantive claims.
Sentence fragments and run-ons, especially in the conclusion ("tes." appears to be a stray fragment/typo—please proofread carefully before submitting).
Word choice: "avow" in the opening is oddly formal; "Los Vegas" should be "Las Vegas."

Grammar and Mechanics

Proofread carefully for:
Subject-verb agreement: "crime is a recurrent theme" (fine) but "the vents shaping" should be "events"
"convicted killers autobiographies" needs an apostrophe: "killers' autobiographies"
The stray "tes." in your conclusion suggests a copy-paste error—review the final paragraph closely
Comma splices in the second-to-last paragraph ("At best criminals learn... at worst they receive...")

Suggestions for Revision

Tighten your thesis to focus on one clear, defensible claim (eg, financial/reputational profit specifically, not moral relativism about lawbreaking).
Reorganize paragraphs so each covers genuinely distinct territory.
Add a counterargument paragraph acknowledging that most criminals don't profit, then explain why your examples matter *despite* this.
Develop each example with more specific detail (dates, figures, outcomes).
Proofread thoroughly for the mechanical errors noted above.

You have a strong, opinionated voice and interesting source material—the next revision should focus on sharpening the logic and ensuring each paragraph builds systematically toward your conclusion. This essay could be genuinely persuasive with more precision and structural clarity.

Feedback on Your Essay: Women's Roles and Feminism

Thank you for tackling a complex and contested topic. You clearly have opinions and are willing to take a stance, which is a good starting point for argumentative writing. However, the essay currently has significant issues with thesis clarity, logical consistency, evidence, and tone that need to be addressed before this argument will be persuasive to an academic audience.

Unclear and Contradictory Thesis

Your central claim shifts throughout the essay. You begin by acknowledging that women faced real discrimination, then argue that feminists have overreached, then suggest women's roles are determined by "nature," and finally conclude that "women should be created equal, but they can only do what nature intended." This is a direct contradiction—if women can only do what "nature intended," in what sense are they "equal"? Before revising further, identify one clear, defensible claim you want to argue and build every paragraph toward supporting it. Right now, the essay reads as several conflicting opinions rather than a unified argument.

Unsupported Generalizations

Several claims are stated as fact without any evidence:
"Women just can't fight in a war very well. They are not strong enough."
"Now many employers are the victims [of sexual harassment claims]."
"Women are having more heart attacks, being more stressful, and kids are being more neglected" [because of modernization].

These are exactly the kinds of claims that require statistical or scholarly support in an argumentative essay. Without evidence, they come across as personal bias rather than reasoned argument, and readers who disagree will dismiss them immediately. Consider: what research exists on women in combat roles? What data supports claims about divorce rates and their causes? Find sources, and be prepared to acknowledge complexity (eg, correlation vs. causation regarding divorce and women's employment).

Use of Religious Belief as Evidence

The line "God would have created us all the same if this was not true" assumes a shared religious framework that not all readers hold, and it is not a logical proof—it's a statement of faith. In secular academic argument, this won't function as evidence. If you want to make a claim about "natural" differences between men and women, you need to draw on biological, sociological, or psychological research instead.

Overgeneralizing "Feminists"

You treat "feminists" as a monolithic, rule-imposing group ("all women have to go along with these feminists rules"). This is a common but weak rhetorical move—it's easier to critique a caricature than the actual range of feminist thought, which includes many different positions on work, family, combat roles, and equality. Try to engage with specific arguments or writers rather than a generalized "they." This will make your critique feel more informed and fair, even if you ultimately disagree.

Tone and Word Choice

Phrases like "I felt that these people were crazy" and "piers" (should be "peers") weaken your credibility. Academic argument benefits from measured language even when you disagree strongly with a position. Instead of "crazy," you might write "I find this position unconvincing because..." and then explain why with evidence.

Structure and Paragraph Focus

Each paragraph currently touches on multiple loosely related ideas (combat roles, promotions, harassment, divorce rates, "God," presidency). Consider organizing around 2-3 focused sub-arguments, each with its own paragraph:
Historical discrimination against women (acknowledge and give specific examples/dates)
Where you think feminist advocacy has succeeded
Where you think it has gone too far or ignored some women's preferences (with evidence, not assertion)
Your nuanced conclusion

Proofreading

There are several mechanical errors to fix: "societies role" → "society's role"; "verses" → "versus"; "piers" → "peers"; "The are not strong enough" → "They are not strong enough"; "did they intended" → "intended them to."

Suggested Next Steps

Write a single-sentence thesis statement and check that every paragraph supports it.
Replace assertions with cited evidence wherever possible (look for peer-reviewed sources or credible journalism on women in combat, wage gaps, harassment claims, divorce statistics).
Remove or reframe the "God" argument in secular terms.
Revise emotionally charged language into measured, analytical prose.
Resolve the contradiction between "equal" and "nature intended" by clarifying what kind of equality you're arguing for (equal opportunity? equal outcomes? equal legal rights?).

This essay has the seeds of an interesting argument about balancing individual choice with collective advocacy movements—but it needs significant revision in logic, evidence, and tone to make that argument convincingly. I'd be glad to discuss a revision plan in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting angle on why "crime does not pay," and your hypothetical scenario in the second half is vivid and engaging—you clearly have a talent for narrative detail. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection paired with a short story than a structured argumentative essay. Below are some key areas to focus on for revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Your opening paragraph raises multiple ideas—crime not paying, secrets coming to light, escalation from minor to major offenses—but it's not clear which of these is your central claim. Is your argument:
That crime inevitably leads to punishment?
That criminal behavior escalates over time (a "slippery slope" argument)?
That punishments increase in severity based on harm caused?

Right now, the essay touches on all three but doesn't commit to one clear, defensible thesis. Revise your introduction to state one specific claim you will argue and support throughout the essay. For instance, your strongest and most original point seems to be the *escalation* idea—that minor unpunished crimes embolden offenders toward more serious ones. If that's your real focus, foreground it explicitly.

Strengthen the Logical Connection Between Sections

Your essay has three loosely connected parts: (1) the escalation theory, (2) a general point about punishment severity, and (3) an extended hypothetical narrative. The narrative is compelling, but you need explicit *analysis* connecting it back to your thesis. Right now, the story does a lot of implicit work that the reader has to interpret. Ask yourself: does this story prove that "crime does not pay," or does it actually illustrate something different—that crime *escalates in severity* once weapons and unpredictable circumstances enter the picture? Be precise about what the example demonstrates, and say so directly rather than trusting the story to speak for itself.

Use Evidence, Not Just Anecdote and Hypothesis

An academic argumentative essay needs more than personal anecdote ("I was always told...") and invented scenarios. Consider incorporating:
Criminological research on recidivism or escalation patterns
Sentencing guidelines or actual case law showing how weapon use and harm to victims increase charges
Statistics on crime "success" rates (are most crimes actually solved/punished?)

Your hypothetical convenience store scenario is illustrative, but it cannot serve as your *only* evidence. Real-world data would make your argument far more persuasive and academically credible.

Address Counterarguments

Right now, your essay assumes its thesis is self-evidently true. A stronger argumentative essay would acknowledge that plenty of crimes *do* go unpunished, or that some criminals never escalate. Engaging with this complexity—and then explaining why your argument still holds—will make your essay more sophisticated and convincing.

Revise for Tone and Register

Your writing has personality, which is good, but the informal tone (eg, "run like hell," "guess what happens... You know what happens," "Sounds like trouble to me") undermines the academic register expected in argumentative writing. Aim to preserve your voice while tightening the diction. You can still be vivid without slipping into casual asides that address the reader directly.

Proofread Carefully

There are several typos and grammatical issues to fix in revision:
"committs," "convenient store" (should be "convenience store"), "whrn," "strats," "hurridly," "persistant," "individuals" spelling, "deterent"
Subject-verb agreement: "a person that commits a crime will probably reap the benefits" — reap the benefits of what, exactly? This sentence needs clarification since it seems to contradict your "crime does not pay" thesis.
"With this attitude someone could definitely headed for destruction" — verb form error, should be "could definitely be headed."
The transition "Besides the fact that you will eventually get caught doing a crime your punishment varies..." is a run-on and unclear; break it into two clear sentences.

Overall Suggestion

Consider restructuring your essay as follows:
Introduction: State one clear thesis (likely the escalation argument).
Body Paragraph 1: Support escalation claim with research/data on recidivism.
Body Paragraph 2: Use your hypothetical narrative, but frame it explicitly as an illustration of escalation and consequence severity—not just a story.
Body Paragraph 3: Address a counterargument (eg, some crimes go unpunished) and rebut it.
Conclusion: Return to your thesis and explain its broader implications (eg, for criminal justice policy or personal decision-making).

Your storytelling instinct is a genuine strength—use it in service of a tighter, more clearly argued thesis, and this essay will be much stronger.

Feedback on "Money is the Root of All Evil" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting philosophical question, and your central insight—that money itself is morally neutral and it's human attitude that determines its effect—is a genuinely thoughtful claim. However, the essay needs significant development in three areas: logical consistency of the thesis, paragraph development with concrete evidence, and grammar/mechanics. Right now the essay reads as a set of assertions rather than a supported argument, and it ends abruptly without a conclusion.

Thesis and Argument Consistency

This is the most important issue to address. Your opening line asserts that the ancient saying "money is the root of all evil" is *true* ("its truth applies to all generations"). But by the second paragraph, you argue the opposite: "Money of itself is neither good nor bad." These two claims contradict each other. If money is neutral and only human attitude matters, then the proverb is *not* true as stated—the root of evil is greed or desire, not money itself. You need to decide which claim you're actually defending and revise your opening to match. A stronger thesis might be: "The old saying blames money itself, but it is not money that is evil—it is the human desire behind it." This would unify your essay's two competing claims into one coherent argument.

Development and Evidence

Right now, nearly every claim in this essay is asserted rather than demonstrated. For example:

You claim money can buy "social status, acceptance, prestige" but never show *how* or give an example.
You list addictions (drugs, gambling, prostitution) but don't explain how money-seeking behavior connects to each—these are stated as a list, not analyzed.
The final paragraph introduces "greed" as a key term but defines it only abstractly, with no illustration of what a greedy person's choices actually look like.

Suggestion: Choose one or two concrete examples—a historical figure, a corporate scandal, a hypothetical scenario, or even a personal observation—and walk through it in detail. Showing *one* well-developed example (eg, how a specific person's pursuit of wealth led them to compromise ethics) will do more for your argument than five abstract lists. Academic essays are strengthened far more by depth than by breadth.

Organization

The essay currently has three paragraphs and no formal introduction or conclusion. Consider this structure:

Introduction: State the proverb, acknowledge its common interpretation, and present your actual thesis (money is a neutral tool; the real root of evil is human desire/greed).
Body Paragraph 1: Explain what money represents (power, survival, status) with a concrete example.
Body Paragraph 2: Explain how *attitude* transforms this power into harm—use the addiction/greed examples here, developed with specifics.
Body Paragraph 3: Consider a counterargument—can money be an active corrupting force even for someone without pre-existing greed? Engaging with this complicates and strengthens your position.
Conclusion: Return to the proverb and explicitly restate how your argument revises or refines it.

The essay currently just stops after the greed paragraph—add a concluding paragraph that ties your ideas back to the opening claim.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address in revision:

Capitalization after periods: "when the need for money..." should be "When the need..."
Apostrophe errors: "Ones's attitude" should be "One's attitude"; "greedy persons life" should be "greedy person's life."
Comma splices/run-ons: "Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive." This should be two sentences or joined with a semicolon.
Fragment/missing punctuation: "Money does good or evil--depending on who has the money" needs a period, and this final sentence is too abrupt to serve as a conclusion—it should be developed into a full concluding paragraph.
Consistent term usage: You introduce the term "money" awkwardly ("the definition of term money"—missing "the"). Revise for smoother phrasing: "The key lies in how we define money."

I'd recommend reading the essay aloud slowly—many of these errors will become audible as places where the sentence doesn't flow naturally.

Priority for Revision

If you only have time to fix a few things, prioritize in this order:
Resolve the contradiction between your opening claim and your actual argument.
Add one fully developed example per body paragraph.
Write a conclusion that returns to and resolves the proverb.
Proofread for capitalization, apostrophes, and sentence boundaries.

This essay has a promising core idea—now it needs the structural and evidentiary support to make that idea persuasive. I look forward to seeing the revised version.
