Feedback Report

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting question—how feminism has changed dating norms—and you write with an authentic, conversational voice that will engage readers. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection than an academic argument. Below are suggestions organized by priority to help you strengthen it for a more formal context.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim seems to be: *feminism has improved women's professional and civic lives, but has damaged traditional romantic courtship.* This is a compelling, nuanced thesis, but it doesn't emerge clearly until late in the essay. Consider stating this claim explicitly in your introduction so readers know where you're heading. Right now, the essay opens with autobiography ("I have been hanging around this continent...") rather than argument, which delays your point and can make the piece feel meandering.

Suggestion: Try a two-sentence thesis early on: something like, "While the feminist movement has rightly advanced economic and civic equality for women, it has inadvertently eroded the traditions of courtship and chivalry that once gave dating relationships structure and meaning." Then use the rest of the essay to support this.

Distinguish Between Personal Experience and Evidence

Your strongest tool here is personal experience, and that's valuable—but for an argumentative essay, personal anecdotes work best as *illustrations* of a broader claim, not as the claim's only support. Right now, statements like "men are confused whether to open a door" are presented as general truths, but your evidence is a few friends and a "significant other." This is worth acknowledging.

Suggestion: Be explicit about the limits of your evidence: "Admittedly, my evidence here is anecdotal, drawn from conversations with friends and my own dating history spanning the 1960s to the 1990s—but this pattern seems worth examining further." This kind of qualification actually *strengthens* your credibility, because it shows critical self-awareness rather than overreach.

Address the Counterargument More Directly

You gesture toward feminist counterarguments (eg, that opening doors can feel patronizing, that unwanted advances get labeled harassment for good reason) but you dismiss them quickly with "this is bunk!" A stronger essay would take these counterpoints seriously before responding to them.

Suggestion: Ask yourself: *why* might a woman feel that having a door opened is patronizing rather than kind? Is there a difference between chivalry offered as a genuine gesture versus chivalry as an assumption of female weakness? Exploring this distinction would make your argument more persuasive to skeptical readers, rather than alienating them.

Tighten the Structure

The essay currently loops between several ideas—dating norms, workplace equality, division of household labor, voting rights—without clear transitions explaining how they connect to your main point. The paragraph about hiring a housekeeper and sharing chores, for instance, is interesting but feels like a tangent from your core argument about dating and chivalry.

Suggestion: Consider organizing with clear signposting:
Para 1: Thesis + roadmap
Para 2: Acknowledge feminism's legitimate achievements (pay equity, career choice, suffrage) — briefly
Para 3-4: Focus your main argument on dating/courtship specifically, using your personal experience as illustration
Para 5: Address the counterargument (why some women resist chivalry) and respond to it
Para 6: Conclusion that returns to your thesis with a call to action or invitation for balance

This would let you keep your rich personal material while giving the reader a clearer sense of logical progression.

Polish Language and Tone

A few smaller issues:
"Stigmatism" should be "stigma."
The shift into second person at the end ("I'm not asking for the world from you men out there") is emotionally effective but tonally inconsistent with the rest of the essay, which uses third person ("men," "these feminist groups"). Decide whether you want a personal, direct-address tone throughout, or a more analytical one—mixing both can feel jarring.
Phrases like "this is bunk!" are vivid but casual for academic writing. You could keep the passion while phrasing it more precisely: "This reasoning does not hold up under scrutiny" or similar.

Final Thought

You have real insight here, particularly the idea that professional equality and romantic courtship may operate by different rules—that's a genuinely thought-provoking distinction most casual conversations about feminism miss. Sharpening your thesis, taking the counterargument seriously, and tightening your structure will help this insight land with the force it deserves. Your voice is engaging—now it needs a clearer argumentative backbone to match.

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've chosen an interesting angle on a familiar phrase, and you clearly have several distinct ideas about how crime "costs" more than money alone. Below are observations to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your opening raises a good question and hints at a thesis: crime costs money. However, as the essay develops, you actually introduce *multiple* costs—financial, moral/emotional, and social (real estate values, stigma/employment). This is a strength in terms of content, but your thesis statement needs to reflect this broader scope. Right now, the thesis promises an essay about *financial* cost, but two of your four body paragraphs are really about non-financial costs (guilt/remorse, and the lasting social stigma of a criminal record). Consider revising your thesis to explicitly signal that crime costs are financial, emotional, *and* social—or, alternatively, narrow the essay to focus only on financial costs and cut or refocus the paragraphs that drift into moral and reputational territory.

Paragraph-by-Paragraph Issues

Paragraph 2 (incarceration costs): This paragraph works well because it stays tightly focused on financial cost and explains a clear causal chain—crime leads to incarceration, incarceration is paid for by taxpayers. Consider adding a statistic or estimated cost of incarceration to make this concrete and persuasive; right now the claims are asserted without evidence.

Paragraph 3 (moral aspect): This paragraph is the least connected to your thesis about money. Remorse and emotional pain are important, but they don't obviously support "crime costs money." Ask yourself: is this paragraph serving your argument, or is it a separate argument entirely? You could either (a) explicitly link emotional cost to a financial one (eg, therapy costs, lost wages due to psychological distress) or (b) revise your thesis to include emotional/moral cost as a category of "cost" alongside financial cost.

Paragraph 4 (real estate values): This is a strong, concrete example and returns clearly to the financial theme. Good instinct here—consider expanding it with a specific example or figure (eg, average % drop in home values in high-crime areas) to strengthen your evidence.

Paragraph 5 (criminal record/stigma): Like paragraph 3, this drifts from pure financial cost toward social consequence. It could be tied back to money by emphasizing *lost income* from being denied jobs—you gesture at this ("denied the opportunity for a job") but don't develop the financial angle. Make that connection explicit.

Evidence and Development

Currently, every paragraph relies on general assertions ("in most cases," "for the most part," "the result is a drop") without any supporting evidence—no statistics, studies, examples, or even a hypothetical scenario worked through in detail. This is the area most in need of revision. Academic argumentative writing needs evidence to be persuasive, not just logical assertions. Consider:
Citing crime/incarceration cost statistics (many public sources exist)
Referencing a study on property values and crime rates
Using a specific, illustrative example (a named case, or a detailed hypothetical) to humanize the incarceration or stigma paragraphs

Conclusion

There is currently no concluding paragraph. The essay ends abruptly on the stigma point. You need a conclusion that returns to your opening phrase and question, synthesizes the different "costs" you've discussed, and leaves the reader with a clear final statement of your argument's significance.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Missing citation: you refer to "" as a placeholder for a phrase—make sure the actual quoted phrase appears in the final draft.
"have you ever actually thought" — check subject-verb agreement with "has been repeated" earlier; consider consistent tense/formality.
"to often overlooked" → "too often overlooked"
"freinds and acquantances" → "friends and acquaintances" (proofread carefully for spelling)
"as the number of criminals increases so to will" → "so too will"
"their is no escape" → "there is no escape"
Watch pronoun agreement: "the individual... his or her... they" — pick a consistent approach (singular they is fine, but be consistent)
Several sentences run on or lack punctuation (eg, paragraph 4's "value for that area. so even though...")

Priority for Revision

Clarify and broaden your thesis to match your actual content, or narrow your content to match a financial-only thesis.
Add evidence—statistics, examples, or research—to every paragraph.
Write a conclusion.

Feedback Report: Draft Essay on Organized and Unorganized Crime

Overall Impression

You clearly have real interest and some specialized knowledge in this subject, and that enthusiasm comes through. However, as it stands, this draft reads more like an informal reflection than an argumentative essay. To meet the expectations of academic argumentative writing, the piece needs a clear, stated thesis; more rigorous evidence; and a structure that builds toward a defensible conclusion. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argument

The single biggest issue is that your central claim is never explicitly stated. You open by referencing "this statement" without telling the reader what the statement is, and you never convert it into a clear question or claim that the essay will answer. Are you arguing that organized crime is more profitable than unorganized crime? That organized crime is more socially tolerated because of institutional corruption? That the categories themselves are misunderstood? Right now, the essay gestures at several of these ideas without committing to one.

Action step: Write a one-sentence thesis and place it in your introduction. For example: "While popular culture treats organized crime as more dangerous than unorganized crime, in practice organized crime is more profitable and more socially entrenched because it depends on complicity from law enforcement and government officials." Every paragraph should then work to support that claim.

Structure and Organization

The essay moves from unorganized crime, to organized crime, to a general reflection on money and power, and then—unexpectedly—into a paragraph of personal biographical information (name, age, major). This last paragraph does not belong in an argumentative essay and should be removed entirely, or moved to a separate cover page/introduction if your assignment requires author identification.

The body paragraphs would also benefit from clearer topic sentences that preview the paragraph's argument, rather than starting with a definition or observation and drifting into commentary. Consider restructuring as:
Introduction: State the debate/statement, then your thesis.
Body 1: Define and characterize unorganized crime, using your thesis lens.
Body 2: Define and characterize organized crime, again tying back to your thesis.
Body 3: Directly compare the two using evidence (profitability, risk, social tolerance).
Conclusion: Restate thesis with a synthesized insight, not a new tangent about "money is power."

Evidence and Support

Several claims need support:
The assertion that unorganized criminals are "usually somewhat less educated" is a generalization that could be seen as classist or unsupported. If you have data from your course, cite it. If not, soften or remove the claim.
The "$30 per person" statistic on illegal gambling is presented without a source. Since you mention this comes from your Organized Crime & American Politics course, cite the course materials, textbook, or lecture (even informally: "According to [Author/Textbook], the average American spends approximately $30 annually on illegal gambling").
The claim that police "make two or three fold" their salary from protecting organized crime is a strong, specific claim that needs a citation or should be qualified as an estimate/hypothesis rather than stated as fact.

Action step: Go through the essay and flag every specific numerical or factual claim. For each, either add a citation or rephrase as a more cautious, attributed statement.

Tone and Style

The essay's tone is conversational, which works for a personal blog but not for academic argument. Some specific fixes:
Remove direct address to the reader ("Now that you have read this and may be trying to figure me out...").
Avoid all-caps for emphasis (MONEY!, BIG, ANYTHING). Use precise language instead: "money is the central mechanism enabling organized crime to function."
The phrase "save the best for last" and "both sides of the track" are casual idioms; replace with more formal phrasing appropriate to academic writing.
Contractions and rhetorical asides ("I cannot comment on whether...") should be reconsidered—if you can't take a position, question why that point is included.

Clarity and Precision

The distinction between "unorganized" and "organized" crime needs a clearer definition early in the essay, ideally with a citation from your course materials, since you're using specialized terminology.
The claim "organized crime involves two or more individuals working together for the same cause" is vague—"cause" implies a moral or political motive, when you likely mean "criminal enterprise" or "shared financial objective." Precision here matters.

Summary of Priority Revisions
Write and insert an explicit thesis statement.
Delete the biographical paragraph.
Add citations for all statistics and specific claims.
Revise tone to remove casual asides and all-caps emphasis.
Strengthen topic sentences and paragraph transitions to reflect the thesis.

You have strong raw material here—sharpening the argument and grounding your claims in evidence will make this a much more persuasive piece.

Feedback on Your Narrative Essay

Thank you for sharing this deeply personal and emotionally resonant story. Your essay does an excellent job of conveying the fear, frustration, and eventual sense of justice you experienced. Below is feedback organized by area, along with suggestions for revision.

Strengths

Your narrative has a clear emotional arc—from childhood belief, to violation, to doubt, to vindication—that gives the essay coherence. The specific details (the cold January day, the trunk of the old car, the list of stolen items) ground the story in reality and help readers visualize your experience. Your voice is sincere, and the personal stakes are evident throughout.

Areas for Revision

Clarify the Thesis and Argumentative Purpose

You've labeled this an "argumentative essay," but currently it reads as a personal narrative. Ask yourself: what is the *claim* you want to prove? Right now, the essay circles around the idea that "crime doesn't pay," but this needs to be stated as a clear, debatable thesis early on—perhaps in your introduction—and then supported with reasoning, not just anecdote. Consider: Are you arguing that the justice system generally works? That victim advocacy matters? That plea bargains often fail victims? Pick one specific claim and build your argument around it.

Move Beyond a Single Anecdote

A strong argumentative essay uses evidence beyond personal experience—statistics on plea bargains, restitution rates, or victim impact, for example, or discussion of how the justice system is *supposed* to work versus how it often does. Your story is compelling, but as it stands, it's an example, not an argument. Consider using your experience as a launching point, then broadening the discussion to make a case that others should find persuasive, not just moving.

Address the Counterargument More Directly

You mention that the prosecutor initially suggested a plea bargain—this is actually a great entry point for argumentative tension. Why do plea bargains happen? What are the systemic reasons (case loads, cost of trials, evidence issues)? By explaining the "other side" and then explaining why your intervention changed the outcome, you strengthen your argument that victims' voices matter in the process. Right now this section is compressed; expand it.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

There are several recurring errors to address:
Verb tense and agreement: "my mother and father always taught me the stealing was wrong" → "that stealing was wrong." Also "they saying that" is unclear—there seems to be a missing phrase (the  marker suggests something was left out here intentionally or by accident; make sure this is filled in).
Word choice: "I did not think that they would have to pay for their crime" — did you mean you *didn't think they would get away with it*, or that you *doubted* they'd be punished? The sentence contradicts the essay's later resolution, so clarify your meaning here, as it currently confuses the reader about your emotional state.
Awkward phrasing: "that it was our home they stolen property came from" should be revised to "that the stolen property had come from our home."
Pronoun errors: "I kept remembering what my father told be" → "told me."
Watch for run-on sentences and comma splices, especially in the second and fifth paragraphs. Reading your essay aloud will help you catch these.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your ending reiterates "crime doesn't pay," which is a nice thematic bookend, but for an argumentative essay, you want your conclusion to restate your *thesis* and briefly show why it matters *beyond your own life*. What do you want the reader to think or do differently after reading this? Make that explicit.

Suggested Next Steps

Draft a one-sentence thesis statement that makes an arguable claim.
Reorganize your body paragraphs so each one supports a specific point tied to that thesis (rather than simply following chronological order).
Add at least one outside source or broader contextual point (eg, about plea bargaining or victims' rights) to elevate this from narrative to argument.
Proofread carefully for the grammar issues noted above—reading aloud or using a grammar-checking tool will help.

You have a powerful personal story here; with sharper argumentative framing, it could be both moving *and* persuasive. Well done on a strong first draft—revise with your thesis in mind, and this will be very effective.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an important and contested topic—the evolving meaning of "feminism"—with genuine conviction. Your central claim (that the term has been distorted from its original meaning of equality into something associated with dominance or man-hating) is clear and consistently argued. However, the essay would benefit significantly from more rigorous evidence, more precise language, and closer attention to logical structure. Below are specific areas for revision.

Argumentation and Evidence

Your strongest asset is your passion for the topic, but the essay currently relies heavily on assertion rather than evidence. Claims like "many radical women have used it as an umbrella" or "some feminist groups will take cases to an extremes" need concrete support. Who are these groups? What specific incidents or statements are you referring to? Without citations, examples, or named figures/organizations, these claims read as generalizations that weaken your credibility rather than strengthen your argument.

Similarly, the historical claims about Susan B. Anthony are accurate in spirit but underdeveloped. Consider incorporating a brief quotation from Anthony or another suffragist to ground your historical argument in primary source material. This would also help you transition more smoothly into your discussion of contemporary feminism.

Problematic Language and Tone

The description of the "concept many people today have of a feminist" as "a big chunky lesbian who hates all men" is a significant weak point. Even though you go on to say "this is hardly the case," introducing this stereotype—especially with the word "chunky," which adds an unnecessary and demeaning physical judgment—risks alienating readers and undermining your credibility. You do not need to voice the stereotype in such vivid, insulting terms to critique it. Consider replacing this with a more analytical description of the stereotype (eg, "many people picture feminists as man-hating extremists") that doesn't reproduce the insult itself. Revising this passage will make your argument more persuasive to a broader audience, including readers who might otherwise sympathize with your critique of stereotyping.

Logical Consistency

There's a tension in your essay that needs addressing directly: you argue that the *stereotype* of feminists is inaccurate, but then you also argue that *some* feminists genuinely have become extreme or anti-male. These are two different claims—one about public perception versus reality, and one about actual internal problems within the movement. Right now the essay blurs these together. Clarify whether your argument is (a) the stereotype is wrong and unfair, (b) the stereotype has some basis in the actions of a vocal minority, or (c) both, and if so, how these two claims relate to each other logically. This distinction is crucial to making your argument coherent.

Structure

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph-level organization. Right now, several paragraphs contain multiple ideas that could be separated or better connected with topic sentences. For example, the final paragraph introduces the idea that "groups grow and change" but doesn't fully develop this as a general principle before moving to the feminism-specific application. Consider adding a sentence or two of transition, or even reordering some material, so each paragraph has one clear governing idea.

Additionally, consider adding a paragraph near the end that more explicitly and directly answers the original prompt/statement you are responding to. Right now, we can infer what that statement was (something like "feminism has become more harmful than helpful"), but stating it explicitly and then directly refuting or qualifying it would sharpen your conclusion.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

"by this I do not mean" should be capitalized: "By this I do not mean."
"A women is just as much a citizen" should be "A woman."
"The equality of woman is no such an absurd notion" is grammatically unclear—likely you mean "is not such an absurd notion" or "is no longer such an absurd notion." Please clarify and correct.
"to an extremes" (used twice) should be "to extremes" or "to an extreme."
Watch for comma splices, particularly in the second paragraph ("The ideas behind 'feminism' were rooted...").
The essay would benefit from a proofreading pass focused specifically on subject-verb agreement and article usage (a/an/the).

Suggestions for Revision

Revise the stereotype description to remove insulting/judgmental language while still making your point about media distortion.
Add specific examples or citations (news stories, quotes from feminist theorists or critics, named controversies) to support claims about "radical" feminism.
Clarify the logical relationship between "stereotype vs. reality" and "some feminists really are extreme."
Tighten paragraph structure so each paragraph has one controlling idea.
Proofread carefully for the grammatical issues noted above.

With these revisions, your essay's core argument—that feminism's original egalitarian spirit has been obscured by both stereotype and a vocal minority—will be considerably more persuasive and polished.

Feedback on Draft Essay: The Perception of Radicalism in Women's Liberation

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting question: why has the Women's Liberation Movement struggled for acceptance despite the moderate goals of most of its members? You have a clear point of view and some good historical instincts. However, the essay needs stronger organization, sharper argumentation, and more careful use of evidence to be fully persuasive. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your central claim—that public perception of radicals, rather than the radicals themselves, has damaged the movement's reputation—doesn't fully emerge until the fourth paragraph ("I contend that the fault does not lie with the radical members..."). Consider stating this thesis explicitly in your introduction so readers know where the essay is heading from the start.

More importantly, there's a logical tension you should resolve. In paragraph two, you write that Anthony and Sand "were noticed and ridiculed, thus defeating their cause, *because of their aggressive action*." This implies the radicals' own behavior caused the failure. But your thesis later argues the opposite: that the fault lies with observers' perception, not with the radicals themselves. You need to reconcile these two claims. Perhaps your point is that the radicals' actions were reasonable or justified, but observers *chose* to focus on those actions rather than on quieter, more "acceptable" feminists. If so, say this directly—right now the essay seems to blame the radicals in one place and exonerate them in another.

Historical Evidence and Accuracy

A few of your examples need reconsideration:

Emily Dickinson is an unusual choice as a "feminist" figure. She's known for her poetry and reclusive life, not political activism or advocacy for women's rights. If you mean to use her as an example of someone whose *work* was later read through a feminist lens, you should explain that connection rather than simply listing her alongside Anthony and Sand.
"Stein" in your 1960s paragraph is ambiguous—do you mean Gertrude Stein (who died in 1946 and predates this era) or Gloria Steinem? This is likely a typo, but it undermines your credibility on a factual point.
You move between the 19th-century suffrage movement (Anthony) and the 20th-century "Women's Lib" of the 1960s-70s as though they are one continuous movement with the same dynamics. Historically, these are distinct movements with different goals, leaders, and cultural contexts. If you want to argue they share a common pattern, make that argument explicitly rather than assuming it.

Strengthening your examples—perhaps replacing Dickinson with someone more clearly tied to organizing or advocacy—would make your historical argument more convincing.

Organization

The essay's structure would benefit from clearer signposting. Right now, paragraphs sometimes repeat the same point (radicals overshadow moderates) without advancing new evidence or complicating the idea. Consider organizing chronologically and explicitly building toward your thesis:
19th century (Anthony/Sand vs. quieter figures)
1960s-70s (radicals vs. the "housewife" example)
Present day (25 years later)

Each section should add something new—new evidence, a nuance, or a complication—rather than restating the same claim in different words.

Tone and Register

The line "So what am I getting at?" is too informal for academic argumentative writing and creates an abrupt tonal shift. It reads as though you're thinking aloud rather than guiding the reader through a structured argument. Consider replacing this transition with a topic sentence that restates and sharpens your claim, such as: "This pattern reveals that the failure of feminism to gain broader acceptance stems not from the actions of its advocates, but from selective public memory."

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several errors to correct in revision:
"Whenever a movement is began" → "is begun"
"oppostion" → "opposition"
"therfore" → "therefore"
"readiliy" → "readily"
"the good that feminists have promote" → "promoted"
"and and other" (repeated word)
"the public it was being aimed" → "the public for whom it was being aimed" or "the public at which it was aimed"

A careful proofreading pass will catch these and similar small errors.

Summary

Your core insight—that movements are often judged by their most visible extremes rather than their typical members—is a strong and defensible idea. To strengthen the essay, state your thesis earlier, resolve the tension between blaming radicals' actions and blaming public perception, verify your historical examples, and tighten your organization so each paragraph builds the argument rather than repeating it. With revision, this essay could make a compelling case.

Feedback on "Feminism and the Beijing Conference" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely and significant occasion—the 1995 UN World Conference on Women in Beijing—as a frame for your essay, and your prose is confident and readable. However, the essay's central problem is a mismatch between its stated scope and its actual content: you open by invoking a *global* gathering of women but then discuss only American history. This creates a structural and argumentative gap that needs addressing before the essay can succeed.

Structure and Focus

The introduction sets up an expectation you never fulfill. You write that because women "of the entire world" are gathering, you'll discuss feminism's impact "on countries around the world," then immediately narrow to "what impact the cause has had on American culture." This narrowing might be a legitimate rhetorical choice (perhaps you intend to use the U.S. as a case study or comparison point), but you need to *signal* that choice explicitly and explain why the American example matters to a discussion of global feminism. As it stands, the international frame in paragraph one feels vestigial—present only to justify the topic's timeliness, then abandoned.

The essay would benefit from one of two revisions:
Narrow the thesis honestly. State from the outset that you will examine American feminism specifically, perhaps as a lens through which to consider what the Beijing conference might mean, or as one national story among many being compared at the conference.
Broaden the essay. If you want to make good on the global framing, you need at least a paragraph addressing how American feminism compares to, influences, or contrasts with women's movements elsewhere. Otherwise the final line—"America continues to set an example for women around the world"—is an unsupported leap. You haven't shown us anything about the rest of the world, so this claim, which is really the essay's thesis, arrives without evidence.

Argument Development

Your argument moves chronologically (suffrage → 1960s feminism → present-day gains), which is a sensible structure, but each stage needs more analytical weight:

Suffrage paragraph: You assert that winning the vote made women "equal in stature" to men, then almost immediately complicate this by noting the "battle" continues. Consider tightening this logic—perhaps: winning suffrage was a necessary but not sufficient step toward equality, and the vote alone didn't equalize other domains (economic, professional, social). This would set up your later paragraphs more clearly.

The Friedan/Steinem paragraph contains a bracketed placeholder——where a quotation or summary of their message was clearly meant to go. This needs to be filled in before the essay can be evaluated on its merits; right now the sentence "They have always preached the same sermon, from the same pulpit" promises content that never arrives. Choose a specific, well-known statement or idea (eg, Friedan's critique of domesticity in *The Feminine Mystique*, or a concrete equal-rights principle) and use it as evidence.

The final substantive paragraph (on daughters having more opportunities) is your strongest, because it's concrete—law schools, medical schools, military academies. Push further here: cite a statistic, a date, or a specific institutional change (eg, VMI's admission of women, which was decided by the Supreme Court in 1996—very close to your Beijing timeframe and would strengthen your historical grounding).

Evidence and Specificity

Throughout, claims are asserted rather than demonstrated. "No one today would say that women are not equal to men in the voting booths of America" is a strong, quotable line, but it needs a supporting fact or two (voter turnout statistics by gender, the number of women in Congress in 1995, etc). Similarly, "corporate boardrooms have expanded greatly" would benefit from even a rough figure or named example. Academic argumentative writing is strengthened by verifiable specifics, not just confident assertion.

Conclusion

The conclusion is only one sentence and doesn't do the work a conclusion should do: synthesizing your argument and returning to the stakes you raised in the introduction (the Beijing conference, global feminism). Right now it reads as an afterthought rather than a culmination. Consider expanding it to explicitly connect your American case study back to the conference's international context—what lesson, if any, does the American experience offer to the women gathered in Beijing? Is it a model, a cautionary tale, one perspective among many? Right now you assert American exceptionalism without earning it.

Line-Level Notes

"suffragates" should be "suffragists" or "suffragettes."
"Gloria Steinam" should be "Gloria Steinem."
Watch redundant phrasing: "equal opportunity, equal recognition, equal pay, and equal rights" risks feeling like a list rather than an argument—choose the two or three terms most central to your point and develop them.
The placeholder  must be resolved before resubmission.

Priority Revisions

Fill in the missing quotation/content.
Either narrow your thesis to explicitly be about American feminism, or add global comparison to justify the Beijing frame.
Add concrete evidence (dates, statistics, named events) to support key claims.
Expand the conclusion to tie back to the international context you opened with.

Feedback on "The Love of Money Is the Root of All Evil" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting philosophical distinction—between money itself and the *love* of money—and you clearly have a defensible position. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: clarifying your thesis and structure, strengthening the logic of your arguments, and cleaning up mechanical errors. Below are specific suggestions organized by these categories.

Thesis and Structure

The most pressing issue is in your opening paragraph. You write: "since the original subject is  I will have to disagree with this statement." This looks like a placeholder that was never filled in—it leaves your reader unsure what claim you're actually responding to. Before you submit this essay, make sure this sentence is complete and clearly states what the original, uncorrected claim is (presumably "money is the root of all evil," as opposed to the corrected version, "the *love* of money is the root of all evil").

More importantly, your thesis is not fully articulated. You seem to be defending the idea that money itself is *not* inherently evil, but that the *love* of money—along with other vices like greed—can lead to evil. This is a nuanced and interesting position, but you should state it explicitly and early, ideally at the end of your introduction. Right now, readers have to piece your argument together across paragraphs. A clear thesis sentence such as "While money itself is morally neutral, the love of money—like greed, dishonesty, or a lack of morals—can lead individuals to commit evil acts" would give your essay a stronger backbone.

Your conclusion introduces a new claim—that evil is ultimately a matter of individual choice—that has not been developed anywhere else in the essay. This is a compelling idea, but it needs its own space earlier in the essay, perhaps as a paragraph connecting the "love of money" discussion to individual agency and decision-making. As it stands, the conclusion feels like a new argument rather than a synthesis of what came before.

Logical Development

Several of your points need tightening:

Paragraph 2: The claim "If money were the root of all evil, we all would have to be evil because we all have money" is a good logical move, but it doesn't yet distinguish between *having* money and *loving* money—which is really the crux of your argument. Consider revising this paragraph to explicitly separate these two concepts, since that distinction is what makes your whole essay work.

Paragraph 3: You state that "It is true to say that money is the root of some evil," which seems to contradict your overall thesis (that money itself is *not* evil, but the *love* of it can produce evil behavior). Clarify whether you mean people's *desire for* money, rather than money as an object, causes these actions.

Paragraph 4: This paragraph does good work broadening the discussion to other sources of evil (attitudes, morals, greed), but it would benefit from at least one concrete example—historical, literary, or personal—to illustrate how greed or dishonesty (rather than money itself) produces evil outcomes. Right now the essay is entirely abstract; a specific case would make your argument more persuasive.

Paragraph 5: The point about charitable organizations is a nice piece of evidence, but you undercut it somewhat by immediately conceding that some funds are misused. Consider whether this paragraph strengthens or weakens your argument, and revise the transition sentences so the concession doesn't overshadow your main point.

Style and Mechanics

A few sentence-level issues to address in your next draft:

"I wold like" → should be "I would like."
"how can honestly be associated with evil?" → you likely mean "honesty," not "honestly."
The semicolon usage in "attitudes, morals (lack of), greed; there is an endless list" is incorrect—semicolons should separate independent clauses or items in a complex list. Here, a colon or simple comma-separated list would work better: "attitudes, a lack of morals, and greed are just a few examples on an endless list."
"worth-while" should be one word: "worthwhile."
Watch subject-verb agreement and pronoun consistency (eg, "will he choose" assumes a male subject as default—consider "will a person choose" or "will they choose" for more inclusive phrasing).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fix the placeholder text in your introduction and state your thesis clearly.
Strengthen the distinction between *having* money and *loving* money throughout.
Add at least one concrete example to illustrate your claims.
Integrate your concluding idea about individual choice earlier in the essay.
Proofread carefully for grammar and punctuation errors.

With these revisions, your essay will present a much clearer, more persuasive argument. You have a genuinely interesting philosophical point here—now it just needs sharper structure and support.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0025.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: Money, Power, and Ethics

Thank you for sharing this draft. You're clearly thinking through some interesting philosophical territory here—the relationship between money, power, and personal values is rich material. However, the essay currently needs significant revision to communicate your ideas as clearly and persuasively as possible. Below are the key areas to focus on.

Clarify Your Central Argument

The essay opens with "It's difficult to justify this saying," but you never state what "this saying" is. Since I don't have access to your assignment prompt, I can only guess you're responding to something like "money is the root of all evil." This is a critical problem: your reader needs to know exactly what claim you're evaluating from the very first sentence. Quote the saying directly, even if your instructor already knows it—your essay should stand on its own.

More importantly, by the second paragraph you pivot to a *different* saying ("absolute power corrupts absolutely") and spend the rest of the essay discussing power rather than money. This creates a structural problem: is your essay about money, or about power? Right now it reads as though you started arguing one thing and abandoned it mid-way for another. If your real thesis is that money and power are entangled—that money is often a vehicle for or a stepping-stone to power—you need to state that connection explicitly and use it to unify the essay. Otherwise, the essay feels like two loosely related short pieces stapled together.

Strengthen the Logical Connections Between Paragraphs

Right now, ideas are asserted rather than developed. For example:
Paragraph 1 asserts money is necessary for survival.
Paragraph 2 asserts power corrupts.
Paragraph 3 asserts effort creates appreciation of money.
Paragraph 4 asserts there are two types of people (those who use money vs. those used by it).

Each of these could be a strong claim, but you don't explain *why* one leads to the next. Ask yourself: what is the throughline? A revision should include transitional sentences that show causation or contrast—eg, "Because money grants access to power, the corrupting potential of power discussed above is really an extension of money's own danger." Right now the essay reads as a list of loosely connected observations rather than a built argument.

Develop Your Claims with Evidence or Examples

Several strong claims currently stand alone without support:
"Anything that comes easy most probably won't be as appreciated..." — This is a common claim, but it needs an example (a study, a historical figure, a personal anecdote, even a hypothetical scenario) to be persuasive rather than assumed common sense.
"Many people rate the quality of life and success by a dollar figure..." — Who? Can you point to a specific example (a celebrity, a cultural trend, a statistic about wealth and self-reported happiness)?

Argumentative essays are strongest when abstract claims are anchored in concrete illustration. Even one well-chosen example per paragraph would substantially strengthen your credibility.

Tighten the Conclusion

Your final paragraph repeats ideas from earlier ("money is for us to use, not for it to use us" echoes paragraph 4's "slave" metaphor) without adding new insight. A strong conclusion should synthesize your argument and perhaps gesture toward broader implications—why does this distinction matter? What should a reader *do* with this insight? Consider ending with a memorable, forward-looking statement rather than restating the master/slave metaphor a third time.

Proofread Carefully

There are a number of spelling and word-choice errors that distract from your ideas and should be corrected in revision:
"currupts" → corrupts
"sence" → sense
"cicle" → cycle
"begats" → begets
"sucess" → success
"indespencable" → indispensable
"one heir more substantive accomplishments" — this phrase is unclear; I suspect you mean "on more substantive accomplishments." Please revise for clarity.
"Once ones basic..." needs an apostrophe: "one's"
Watch comma splices, eg, "Money makes things happen, and quickly" — consider whether this fragment works grammatically or needs revision.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State the exact saying you are responding to in your opening sentence.
Decide whether your essay is about money, power, or their relationship—and make that choice explicit and consistent throughout.
Add transitional reasoning between paragraphs so each idea visibly builds on the last.
Support your generalizations with concrete examples.
Proofread for spelling and grammar before final submission.

You have the seeds of a thoughtful essay here—the master/slave metaphor for money, and the distinction between people who chase dollar figures versus substantive accomplishment, are both compelling ideas. With a clearer thesis and more deliberate structure, this essay could be quite strong. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0026.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting philosophical question, and I can see you have a clear position: money itself is neutral, but the pursuit of it can lead people toward harmful behavior. This is a solid, defensible thesis. However, the essay needs revision in several areas to make it more academically rigorous and persuasive.

Structural Issues

The opening needs work. The  placeholder suggests something went wrong in submission, but even assuming the phrase is "money is the root of all evil," you should state this explicitly in your introduction rather than relying on a vague reference. Your opening paragraph is also just a string of questions—while questions can be rhetorically effective, four in a row without any framing leaves the reader unsure of your direction. Consider condensing to one or two purposeful questions, then pivoting to your thesis.

Your thesis is buried and split. You seem to be arguing two related but distinct claims: (1) the *desire* for money can corrupt people, and (2) money itself is a neutral object incapable of moral qualities. These are both good points, but they appear disconnected—the essay reads like two mini-essays glued together rather than one unified argument. I'd recommend stating both parts of your thesis clearly in the introduction (eg, "While money itself is morally neutral, the excessive desire for it can corrupt human character") so readers know where you're headed.

The religious framing needs justification or broadening. You pivot into a specifically Christian framework in paragraphs 2-3 without explaining why this lens is relevant to the broader question. If the essay is meant to explore a general cultural saying, you might either (a) explicitly justify why Christian ethics are your chosen framework, or (b) broaden your argument to draw on other ethical traditions or secular reasoning, which would make your argument accessible to a wider audience and feel less like an assumption.

Argument Development

Paragraph 3 relies on generalization without evidence. You describe "the christian way of life" in fairly stereotyped terms (subservient, without material things) without citing scripture, theology, or scholarship. If you're going to invoke a religious ethical framework, ground it in specific sources—a Bible verse, a theologian's argument, or even a well-known teaching—rather than asserting it as common knowledge.

Paragraph 4 needs a stronger logical bridge. You jump from "searching for money" to "gambling" rather abruptly. Walk the reader through *why* this progression happens—what psychological or social mechanism causes someone chasing money to end up gambling or abandoning relationships? A brief explanation or example (real or hypothetical) would strengthen the causal claim.

Your strongest paragraph is the fifth one. The argument that money is an inanimate object incapable of moral agency is clear, logical, and effectively stated. This is your best philosophical move in the essay—consider expanding it. You might address a counterargument here: some would say money's *effects* make it evil even if it lacks agency. Anticipating this objection would strengthen your position.

The conclusion paragraph (money helping the poor) needs a transition. This point about money's positive uses is compelling, but it currently reads as a new topic rather than a natural extension of your argument. Connect it explicitly back to your "neutral object" thesis: since money is neutral, it can be directed toward good ends just as easily as bad ones.

Style and Mechanics

"mear" should be "mere"
"subservant" should be "subservient"
"christian" should be capitalized as "Christian" throughout
"educated the uneducated" is awkward phrasing—consider "educate those who lack access to education"
The final line is a nice rhetorical flourish, but "I think not" is informal for academic writing; consider "This claim is unconvincing" or similar

Overall Recommendation

Your core argument is genuinely strong: money is a neutral tool, and moral failing lies in human choices, not the object itself. To elevate this essay, tighten your structure around this central claim, provide concrete evidence or examples (rather than general assertions), and proofread carefully for spelling and word choice. Consider also addressing the counterargument more directly—since your title question implies debate, engaging with the *strongest* version of the opposing view will make your ultimate rejection of it more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0027.1.txt =====
Feedback Report: "Crime Does Pay"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a provocative, attention-grabbing thesis, and the essay is organized around a clear structure (job security, wages, retirement). However, the argument currently rests on unverified assumptions, contains a critical mathematical error, and doesn't acknowledge any counterarguments. To make this a persuasive piece of academic writing, you'll need to address these issues substantially.

Content and Argument

Unsupported Claims
The opening claim—"Many criminals live far better than the average American"—needs evidence. Do you have statistics on criminal income, recidivism, or lifestyle? Without data, this reads as an assertion rather than an argument. Academic writing requires you to substantiate claims with research, even in an essay that takes a deliberately contrarian stance.

The Math Doesn't Add Up
This is the most serious problem in the essay. In paragraph 3, you establish that a criminal earns "$300.00 without... deductions" for one hour of work per day—implying $300/day. But in the retirement paragraph, you suddenly calculate "$300.00 per hour" for five hours per week. These are wildly different figures ($300/day vs. $1,500/week if $300/hour × 5 hours), and the final total of $1,170,000 doesn't correspond to either rate over 15 years. You need to recalculate this carefully and use consistent numbers throughout, or your central piece of "evidence" collapses.

Ignoring the Obvious Counterargument
The essay's biggest structural weakness is the absence of any engagement with the risks of criminal life: incarceration, violence, injury, death, loss of freedom, damaged relationships, and the harm done to victims. A reader will immediately think, "But what about prison?" A strong argumentative essay anticipates this objection and responds to it—even if your response is "the financial upside outweighs these risks" or "many criminals evade these consequences longer than assumed." Right now, the essay only shows the upside of one column in the ledger, which undermines its credibility rather than making it persuasive.

Defining "Crime"
The essay treats "crime" as a monolithic category, but stealing $300/hour is a very different activity than, say, low-level drug dealing or property theft, both statistically more common and far less lucrative. Grounding your argument in a specific type of crime (eg, white-collar fraud, organized theft) would make your numbers more credible and your argument more focused.

Structure and Paragraphing

The transition "And finally, the one thing the criminal does have in common with the average worker" is a strong sentence, but it appears as a paragraph opener with no topic sentence framing the retirement comparison. Consider revising for clarity.
Paragraph 3 mixes too many types of information (hourly wage, weekly totals, deductions) without clear signposting. Consider breaking the wage comparison and the criminal's "hourly wage" into separate, clearly labeled steps.
The essay would benefit from a paragraph (before the conclusion) addressing the counterargument and explaining why, despite risks, you still believe crime "pays." This is the paragraph that would elevate the essay from a one-sided comparison to genuine argumentation.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few recurring errors need correction:

"it's advantages" → should be *its* (possessive, not contraction).
"rather or not" → should be *whether or not*.
"There is never a lack of work are a means" → this sentence is garbled; likely intended as "There is never a lack of work or a means for continuing employment." Revise for clarity.
"costs them" → should be *cost* (subject-verb agreement with "deductions").
"numbers" → should be *number* ("a specific number of hours").
"invest" → should be *invests* or *invest* depending on subject agreement; check "he/she invest" for consistency (should be "invests").

Tone

The opening line, "I beg to differ," works well as a hook, but a few other phrasings (eg, "Let's take time to compare") are conversational in a way that may not suit a formal argumentative essay. Consider whether your assignment calls for a more analytical register throughout, even while maintaining a provocative stance.

Suggestions for Revision

Fix the math. Recalculate the retirement figures so they're internally consistent, and clearly show your work.
Add a counterargument paragraph. Address risks of criminal life directly, then explain why your thesis still holds (or qualify your thesis to something more defensible, like "certain types of crime can be more financially rewarding than average wage labor, despite serious risks").
Narrow your definition of "crime." Specify what kind of criminal activity you're discussing to strengthen your numerical comparisons.
Proofread for grammar, particularly homophone errors (it's/its) and subject-verb agreement.
Consider your source of statistics. Where do the wage and interest figures come from? Citing even informal sources (BLS data, criminal justice studies) would strengthen credibility.

This essay has a clear voice and an interesting angle—sharpening the logic and evidence will make it much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0028.1.txt =====
Feedback on "Crime Pays" Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a provocative and socially significant claim with confidence and a clear voice. You've chosen vivid, concrete imagery (luxury cars, designer clothes) to ground an abstract argument, which is a strong rhetorical instinct. However, the essay currently reads more like an assertion repeated with different examples than an argument supported by evidence and reasoning. Below are specific areas to strengthen for revision.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim—that crime pays for inner-city youth—is stated boldly but never rigorously defined or defended. Consider these questions:

What does "pays" mean? Are you arguing that crime is *more profitable* than legal work, that it's *rational* given the alternatives, or that society's response *incentivizes* it? These are related but distinct claims, and the essay drifts between them.
What is your actual claim about causation? You suggest poverty, lack of policing, and lenient sentencing all contribute, but you don't establish how these factors interact. Right now the essay reads as a list of grievances rather than a structured argument.

I'd recommend building a clear thesis statement early on that specifies your claim's scope (eg, "For many low-income urban youth, the economic logic of crime—combined with inadequate policing and lenient sentencing for nonviolent offenses—creates a rational incentive structure that legal employment cannot match"). Then each paragraph should visibly support one piece of that claim.

Use of Evidence

This is the essay's most significant weakness. Every claim is presented as self-evident ("The proof exists on any street in urban America") rather than substantiated. Ask yourself:

What percentage of criminals actually avoid jail? You assert this is "shockingly low" but provide no figures.
What is the actual recidivism rate for nonviolent offenders, and how does it compare to your claims about "short" jail time?
Is there data comparing the earnings of youth involved in illegal economies versus minimum-wage work? Studies exist (eg, research on drug economies by sociologists like Sudhir Venkatesh) that complicate the picture—some show that street-level drug dealing pays *less* than minimum wage when time is factored in.

Without data, your argument is vulnerable to the objection that you're generalizing from visual impressions ("driving down any street") rather than demonstrating a pattern. Incorporating even a few statistics or scholarly sources would transform this from opinion to argument.

Logical Gaps and Oversimplification

A few claims need more nuance:

You attribute luxury goods ownership broadly to "black and white" youth but then frame the entire argument around "inner-city" poverty, which creates some inconsistency. Are you talking about urban poverty specifically, or something broader?
The claim that police are "no threat" because they're "outnumbered" is an assertion, not an argument. Why are they outnumbered? Is this about funding, strategy, or something else?
The essay assumes readers will accept that most nonviolent offenders return to "wealth and comfortable living"—but this ignores criminal records' impact on future legal employment, which actually *supports* your argument (crime becomes even more necessary after a conviction) but is never explored.

Tone and Ethical Framing

Your essay risks sounding like it endorses or excuses criminal behavior without examining consequences to victims or communities. Even if your goal is provocative social commentary, consider explicitly acknowledging the human cost of crime (theft, violence, community destabilization) even as you explain its economic logic. This would make your argument more persuasive to skeptical readers, since it demonstrates you've considered counterarguments rather than dismissed them.

Style and Mechanics

Watch for typos: "wether" (whether), "en in sight" (end in sight), extra periods.
The rhetorical questions ("Where do the funds come from?") are effective early on but become repetitive—vary your sentence structures.
"Rolling wads of money into their designer jeans" is vivid but slightly awkward; consider revising for clarity.
Avoid absolute language ("It is obvious," "few alternatives") unless you can back it up—these phrases invite skepticism rather than persuasion.

Suggested Revision Priorities

Narrow and clarify your thesis to specify exactly what causal or economic claim you're making.
Add concrete evidence—statistics, studies, or expert testimony—to support your claims about incarceration rates, police funding, and criminal earnings.
Acknowledge counterarguments (eg, the long-term costs of a criminal record, the risks of violence, community harm) to strengthen your credibility.
Tighten paragraph focus so each section builds logically toward your thesis rather than repeating the same claim with new imagery.

This essay has a compelling premise and confident voice—the next step is grounding that confidence in evidence and logical rigor.

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback on "The Modern Microcomputer" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an ambitious and genuinely interesting angle—the "catch-22" of computing—and there's real intellectual energy in this draft. However, the essay needs significant revision to make its central argument clear, logically consistent, and well-supported. Below are the main areas to address.

Clarify and Refine the Thesis

Your thesis—"The computer has made life easier from a physical standpoint and harder from a mental standpoint"—is a promising organizing principle, but the categories don't hold up under scrutiny. What you actually describe in the "physical" paragraph (office efficiency, automation) isn't really *physical* ease; it's more about *practical* or *functional* convenience. Meanwhile, your "mental" section blends two very different phenomena: (1) reduced mathematical understanding leading to errors, and (2) job displacement causing psychological distress. These aren't really two aspects of the same "mental" category—one is about *cognition/competence*, the other about *emotional/social consequences*. Consider renaming your categories more precisely, or narrowing your thesis to focus on just one clear tension (eg, "computers increase efficiency but decrease human understanding and job security").

Strengthen the Logical Structure of the "Errors" Argument

The paragraph on computational errors is currently circular and hard to follow. You write that errors occur because "that person... told the computer what to do," but then say if the person "knew what he should do... he would not have erroneous answers." This seems to blame human ignorance for computer error, but then you also say models are "riddled with errors... because there are many things people don't know or can't predict." These are two different claims:
Claim A: Computers make errors due to human programming mistakes.
Claim B: Computers make errors because reality is too complex to model perfectly.

These need to be separated and each fully developed, or one should be dropped. Right now the paragraph talks in a circle without landing on a clear point.

Avoid Redundancy Between Sections

Job displacement is mentioned in paragraph 2 (factory automation) and again in paragraph 5 (computers replacing workers). Consider consolidating these into a single, more developed discussion rather than repeating the point in two different sections under two different headings.

Support Your Claims with Evidence

This essay relies entirely on general assertions ("many people," "everyone already knows," "computers make errors all the time") without any concrete examples, statistics, or cited sources. For an argumentative essay, you need actual evidence: a documented case of computer-driven job loss, a specific example of an erroneous mathematical model, or data on automation's economic impact. Concrete examples will make your abstract claims about "anger, frustration, fear" and "erroneous predictions" much more persuasive.

Reconsider Tone and Word Choice

A few phrases undercut the essay's credibility:
The parenthetical "(except for people in 2nd & 3rd world countries)" is dismissive and reads as an aside rather than a serious acknowledgment of global inequality in technology access—this deserves either real development or removal.
Phrases like "another perhaps impossible computation" and "surplus of 'needless' computations" are vague; clarify what you mean concretely.
The term "catch-22" is used loosely; make sure you're using it accurately (a catch-22 is a paradoxical situation where you can't win either way—clarify how computers exemplify this rather than simply having both pros and cons).

Develop the Conclusion More Fully

Your proposed solutions (better math education, curtailing "theoretical programming," teaching philosophy/psychology) are interesting but appear suddenly, without being set up earlier in the essay. If solutions are part of your argument, foreshadow them in the introduction and connect them explicitly back to the specific problems you identified (error-proneness, job loss, etc). Right now they feel like a new essay topic rather than a natural conclusion.

Proofread for Clarity and Precision

Watch for run-on constructions and vague referents: "The effects of the computer have been tremendous" is repeated almost verbatim from the first paragraph. Also check verb agreement and punctuation (eg, "Also almost everyone" needs a comma; "if programmed 100% correctly" needs tighter phrasing).

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis to name a single, precise tension.
Separate the two different "error" arguments into distinct, well-developed points.
Add at least 2–3 concrete, specific examples (with sources if possible).
Cut the parenthetical aside or expand it into a real point about global inequality.
Connect your proposed solutions explicitly to the problems raised earlier.

This essay has a strong core idea—just needs sharper definitions and firmer support to make the argument land.

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback on "The Computer's Impact on Modern Life"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic, and your instinct to use a concrete workplace example is a good one. However, the essay in its current form reads more like a rough outline than a developed argument. Each paragraph introduces an idea but doesn't fully explore it, and the connections between your points aren't always clear. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction states that "having knowledge of how the computer operates is considered a necessary component of leading a successful life." This is a reasonable thesis, but it's quite broad and not fully argued in the body of the essay. Ask yourself: what specifically do you want to prove? Is your claim that (1) computer literacy is now essential for job security, (2) computers have created new risks (like hacking) that require public awareness, or (3) both, as part of a broader claim about adaptability in a changing world? Right now the essay gestures at all of these without committing fully to any one line of argument. A stronger thesis would forecast the specific reasons you'll discuss, eg, "As computers become central to employment and finance, individuals who fail to develop basic computer literacy risk both professional and financial harm." This would give your reader a roadmap and help you stay focused.

Structure and Development

The essay has four short paragraphs, and each one needs more development:

Paragraph 2 (the manager example) is your strongest section because it's concrete, but it ends abruptly. Consider adding a sentence of analysis explaining *why* this example matters—what lesson should the reader draw beyond the literal story? Right now you tell the story but don't fully connect it back to your thesis.

Paragraph 3 (banking) introduces two different ideas—hacking and personal financial vigilance—but doesn't develop either one. Hacking, in particular, deserves more explanation: how does it relate to your thesis about individual computer literacy? Is your point that hackers exploit people who don't understand computer security? If so, say that explicitly and perhaps provide a brief example or statistic.

Paragraph 4 (conclusion) is only one sentence. A conclusion should synthesize your argument, not just restate the general topic. Try revisiting your specific examples (the manager, banking) and showing how they support your final claim.

You might also consider whether two examples (workplace and banking) are enough to support a broad claim about "every aspect of our lives," as your introduction suggests. Either narrow your thesis to match your evidence, or add a third example (eg, education, healthcare, communication) to strengthen the case.

Evidence and Specificity

Your strongest tool in this essay is the manager anecdote, but it would benefit from more specific, verifiable detail. Is this a real case you researched, a story you heard, or a hypothetical? If real, cite where you learned about it. If hypothetical, consider signaling that ("Imagine a scenario in which...") or replacing it with documented data (eg, statistics on job displacement due to automation) to strengthen your credibility. Similarly, your claim about hacking needs support—a statistic, a news reference, or a specific case would make this section far more persuasive than the current vague statement that "hacking has become quite a problem."

Coherence and Transitions

Right now the paragraphs feel like separate mini-essays rather than parts of one continuous argument. Work on transition sentences that explicitly link each paragraph back to your thesis and to the previous paragraph. For example, before moving to banking, you might write something like: "Beyond individual employment, computer literacy also affects our financial security, as the banking industry demonstrates." This kind of transition helps the reader see the logical structure of your argument.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are several errors to correct in revision:
"twenthieth" → twentieth
"auto-pasts company" → likely "auto-parts company"
"succesful" → successful
"defecit" → deficit (and note: "defecit" as used here is also the wrong word choice—you likely mean the manager was seen as a *liability* or *cost*, not literally a financial deficit)
"internet working" → probably meant "networking"
Watch subject-verb agreement and article use throughout (eg, "put all of their inventory on computer" should be "onto computers" or "into a computer system")

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, which often catches these small errors more effectively than silent proofreading.

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to state a specific, arguable claim.
Expand each body paragraph with more analysis, not just narrative.
Add at least one more well-developed example if you want to support a broad claim.
Write a real conclusion that synthesizes your argument rather than simply restating the topic.
Proofread carefully for spelling and word-choice errors.

You have the seed of a solid argument here—now it needs room to grow through more development, clearer organization, and careful editing.

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback on "The Impact of Computers on Society"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic with real substance, and your instinct to explore both the benefits and drawbacks of computer technology gives the essay a balanced, thoughtful shape. The bones of a good argumentative essay are here—a clear stance, two contrasting bodies of evidence, and a personal, engaged voice. To strengthen the essay, focus on tightening your thesis, adding concrete evidence, smoothing out mechanical issues, and building a proper conclusion.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—that computers have been "both bad and good"—is serviceable, but it's quite general. Consider sharpening it into a claim that previews *how* you'll organize the essay and what your ultimate judgment is. For example: "While computers have made daily tasks faster and more efficient, they have also eroded human connection and fostered a dangerous over-reliance on technology." This gives your reader a roadmap and makes your final position clearer.

Structurally, you have one paragraph for the "good" and one for the "bad," which is a reasonable approach for this length. However, the essay ends without a real conclusion—it trails off into a hopeful wish rather than synthesizing your argument. Add a short concluding paragraph that restates your thesis in light of the evidence you've presented and leaves the reader with a final, memorable thought (rather than introducing a new idea, like the "slow" comment, at the very end).

Development and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Right now, your claims are frequently broad and undersupported:

"Basically every facet of life has been affected by computers bringing about positive results" — this is a sweeping generalization. Which facets, specifically? Education? Healthcare? Communication? Choose two or three concrete domains and develop them with detail.
The ATM example is good—it's concrete and relatable. Try to find one or two more examples like this in *each* paragraph (eg, specific job sectors displaced by automation, or a real instance of technology failure) to make your argument more persuasive and less impressionistic.
Consider incorporating an outside source (a statistic, a news story, an expert opinion) to lend credibility. Right now the essay relies entirely on personal feelings ("I feel," "in my opinion," "I fear"). These phrases are fine occasionally to mark your stance, but overuse them and the essay reads as a series of opinions rather than an argument built on evidence.

Addressing Counterarguments

You do good work acknowledging tension within your own position—for instance, admitting that computer-assisted cars are "safe" before pivoting to the danger of over-reliance. Push this further: actually name a counterargument someone might raise ("Proponents of automation argue that computers reduce human error") and then explain why your concern outweighs it. This will make your argument feel more rigorous.

Style and Word Choice

A few issues to revise:

Repetition: Words like "significantly," "quite," and "I feel" appear often. Vary your language and cut phrases that don't add new information (eg, the first two sentences essentially repeat each other).
Tone: The essay shifts between formal argument and casual, conversational asides ("EVERYTHING," exclamation points, quotation marks around "impersonalized" and "slow" used as scare quotes). For an academic essay, aim for a consistent, measured tone. Save emphasis for well-chosen words and strong sentence construction rather than capitalization or punctuation.
Wordiness/Awkward phrasing: Sentences like "Computers have also made live transpire much quicker than before" could be tightened and clarified (also, "live" should be "life"). Look for places where a simpler verb or restructured sentence would communicate the idea more directly.

Mechanics

There are several errors worth correcting in revision:

"life style" should be "lifestyle" (one word) or "life styles" consistently.
"Interraction" → "interaction" (appears twice).
"obstical" → "obstacle."
"proceed with" → "process" (word choice: "process the same transactions").
Subject-verb agreement: "It seems these days EVERYTHING is run by computers" is fine, but check other sentences like "Personal computers are becoming quite common, and therefore less expensive" — the causality here is backwards (computers became common *because* they became less expensive, or the two happened together, not "therefore").
Comma splices and run-ons appear in a few places (eg, "These changes resulting from the invention of the computer, have been, I feel both bad and good" — the commas here are inconsistent and interrupt the sentence oddly).

A careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud, will catch many of these.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to be more specific and analytical.
Add concrete, specific evidence and examples to support each claim.
Write a genuine concluding paragraph that synthesizes your argument.
Reduce reliance on personal-opinion phrasing; let evidence carry more weight.
Proofread carefully for spelling, word choice, and punctuation.

You have a genuine, engaged perspective on this topic—the revision process is about giving that perspective more evidentiary weight and polish.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic, and your passion for cultural equality and human dignity comes through clearly. You've chosen a subject that matters, and your conviction is evident throughout. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen both your argument and its presentation.

Unsupported claims: Statements like "racism is almost inherent in today's society" and "people are understanding that prejudice and discrimination are not consciously learned but they must be unlearned" are presented as established fact without evidence. Since this is an argumentative essay, you need specific examples, historical references, or scholarly sources to support these assertions. Consider: What specific movements, legislation, or social changes exemplify this "new direction" you describe? Naming particular events (Civil Rights Movement, specific organizations, key figures) would ground your abstract claims in concrete reality.

Underdeveloped conclusion: Your final paragraph introduces new ideas (the "opportunity to learn" and "avalibility to study cultural difference") rather than synthesizing your argument. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis and demonstrate how your evidence supports it, not introduce fresh points.

Logical gaps: The essay moves quickly between broad generalizations ("As a society," "as a much larger world") without explaining the mechanisms of this change. How exactly are we "unlearning" prejudice? What does that process look like?

Structure Suggestions

Consider reorganizing with this structure:
Introduction with a clear, specific thesis
Historical context: How discrimination has functioned in the past (with specific examples)
Evidence of change: Concrete examples of progress (organizations, laws, cultural shifts)
Ongoing challenges: What work remains
Conclusion that ties back to your thesis about significance

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Your essay would benefit significantly from careful proofreading. Here are patterns to watch for:

Spelling errors: "discriminitive" (discriminatory), "excepted" (accepted), "enought" (enough), "sterotypes" (stereotypes), "burried" (buried), "avalibility" (availability), "existance" (existence)
Word choice: "deeply breed" should likely be "deeply bred" or "deeply ingrained"
Subject-verb agreement: "Organizations and forms are presently being held" — did you mean "forums"?
Punctuation: The comma splice in "As individuals we are constantly surrounded..." needs a comma after "individuals." Also check comma usage throughout for clarity.
Apostrophe error: "peoples lives" should be "people's lives"
Awkward phrasing: "which includes more then blacks African Americans" is unclear—do you mean "more than just African Americans"? Also, "then" should be "than" here and elsewhere.

I'd strongly recommend reading your essay aloud, which often catches these errors, and using spell-check as a first pass before deeper revision.

Voice and Tone

Your personal investment in this topic is a strength, but be mindful of the line between passionate argument and unsupported opinion. Phrases like "I greatly value all of humanity" are sincere but don't advance an academic argument. Try channeling that passion into more specific, evidence-based claims: *why* does cultural understanding matter, and *what evidence* demonstrates its importance or its progress?

Revision Priorities

For your next draft, I suggest focusing on:

Sharpen your thesis into one clear, arguable sentence
Add concrete evidence—specific examples, dates, organizations, or scholarly sources
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar
Revise your conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new ideas
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs to show logical progression

This essay has a solid foundation and an important message. With more specific evidence and careful editing, it will be considerably stronger. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further or look at your revision.
