Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay engages with an important and timely topic—the gap between America's foundational ideals of equality and opportunity, and the lived reality of many Americans in a fast-paced, media-driven, individualistic society. There's a genuine critical instinct here, and the closing image of "parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated" suggests real rhetorical ambition. However, the essay in its current form reads more like a set of connected observations than a developed argument. Below are some areas to focus on as you revise.

Clarify and Sharpen Your Thesis

Your opening sentence tries to do a lot of work at once: it names an "ideology," criticizes the myth of equal opportunity, and gestures toward media's role in reinforcing that ideology—all in one long, grammatically tangled sentence. Before revising anything else, isolate the single claim you want to defend. Are you arguing that:
(a) the American ideology of meritocracy is a myth that media perpetuates, or
(b) modern technology and individualism have eroded empathy and made inequality invisible, or
(c) both, as cause and effect?

Right now the essay drifts between these related but distinct claims. A clear thesis sentence at the end of your first paragraph—one that states *what* you're arguing and *why it matters*—will give your reader (and you) a roadmap for what follows.

Develop Paragraphs with Evidence and Reasoning

Each paragraph currently makes assertions without supporting them. For example:
"One can see these ideas in popular media everyday. T.V. commercials & T.V. programs project models of how one should be." *How?* Give a specific example—an ad, a show, a slogan—and explain how it reinforces the ideology you're critiquing.
"In the 20th cen, the values... have changed in meaning... nothing was ever equal." This is a strong claim that needs historical support. What values? Whose experience shows that "nothing was ever equal"? A brief historical reference (eg, to segregation, labor history, or wealth disparity) would ground this claim.
The second paragraph's claims about technology, mobility, and individualism are compelling but asserted rather than argued. Consider: What evidence shows people "buy more things" or substitute family time for TV? Even a single statistic, scholarly source, or well-chosen anecdote would strengthen your credibility.

Tighten the Logical Connections

There are causal leaps that need explicit reasoning. For instance, you move from "life moves at a much faster pace" to "one no longer sees the relative humanness between people" without explaining the mechanism connecting speed and empathy loss. Ask yourself: *why* would a faster pace of life cause people to stop recognizing shared humanity? Spelling out this logic (even in one or two sentences) will make your argument more persuasive and harder to dismiss.

Revise for Clarity and Grammar

The essay contains frequent errors that obscure meaning and will distract academic readers:
Spelling/typos: "comercials," "cen" (century), "nolonger," "realitive," "unsuccessfull," "thier," "substitued."
Sentence structure: The opening sentence is a run-on that needs to be broken into two or three clearer sentences. Consider: "A dominant ideology in the United States holds that success depends on following society's rules and that everyone has equal opportunity to succeed. When someone fails, this ideology attributes the failure to personal shortcoming rather than structural inequality."
Word choice/logic: "when one does not do this one fails" is a bit reductive—make sure you're accurately describing the ideology you're critiquing, not oversimplifying it into a strawman.
Punctuation: Watch comma splices, especially in the second paragraph ("People are more mobile, can work more, and buy more things, but time for relaxation and family are often substituted with T.V." is fine, but several other sentences run multiple independent ideas together without clear connectors).

I'd strongly recommend reading the essay aloud—this is one of the fastest ways to catch run-ons, missing words, and awkward phrasing.

Strengthen the Ending

Your final image—"the voices of the mass media easily replace those voices that struggle to be heard. The parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated"—is evocative but arrives abruptly, almost as a sentence fragment, without unpacking the metaphor. Who are these unheard voices? What is "the machine"? If this metaphor is meant to crystallize your argument, give it room to breathe—perhaps make it the culminating idea of a concluding paragraph rather than the final, truncated thought of the essay.

Suggested Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis stating your central claim.
Outline 2–3 body paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence, one or two pieces of evidence, and explicit reasoning connecting evidence to your thesis.
Draft a conclusion that returns to your thesis and expands on your "machine" metaphor.
Proofread carefully for spelling and sentence-boundary errors.

You have a strong critical perspective—the revision task now is to organize and support it so readers can follow your reasoning from start to finish.

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You're clearly grappling with a subject that matters to you personally, and the personal stakes come through—the story about your grandmother's cancer treatment and the "Spain in Michigan" anecdote are memorable and human. That said, the essay's central argument becomes unclear as it progresses, and by the final paragraph, the connection to your opening claim about technology has largely disappeared. My feedback focuses on strengthening the logical thread that should run through the whole piece.

The Core Problem: What Is Your Argument About?

Your first two paragraphs promise an essay about technology's dual nature—friend and "Nemesis." But paragraph three pivots to a discussion of American educational insularity (lack of geography and foreign language instruction), and this pivot happens without explanation. Ask yourself: how, specifically, does technology cause the ignorance you describe?

Right now, the essay implies a connection but never states it. Is your claim that:
Technology (eg, the internet, translation apps) has made Americans *feel* self-sufficient, so they no longer see the need to learn languages or geography?
Technology has given Americans access to entertainment and information that displaces genuine curiosity about the world?
Something else entirely?

Without this causal link made explicit, the essay reads as two separate arguments stitched together: one about technology's ambivalence, and one about American educational failures. You need a sentence—ideally in your introduction and echoed in your topic sentences—that names the mechanism connecting these two things.

Structural Suggestions

Revise your thesis. Right now it's abstract ("closest friend and greatest Nemesis"). Try narrowing it to the specific claim you actually develop: something like, "While technology has connected the world materially, it has allowed Americans to substitute technological self-sufficiency for genuine cultural and geographic literacy."

Add a bridge paragraph or bridging sentences. Between the "technology is good and bad" paragraph and the "American youth are geographically ignorant" paragraph, you need a sentence that explicitly connects the two ideas. Otherwise the reader has to guess at your logic.

Return to technology explicitly in your language-learning example. The paragraph about not being able to study Spanish until age 20 is compelling, but it reads as an indictment of *school curricula and policy*, not technology. If you want this to support your thesis, you need to explain how technology fits into this story—did technology give you the tools to learn Spanish independently later? Did the absence of a language requirement reflect an "I don't need help" mentality that you're linking to a technological mindset?

Write a conclusion. The essay currently ends on a confusing, incomplete-feeling note ("our minds to be heard, and a world that is growing while my country lags behind"). This sentence needs to be rewritten for clarity, and more importantly, you need an actual concluding paragraph that returns to your opening metaphor (friend/Nemesis) and states what you want the reader to take away.

Sentence-Level Issues

"The effort of the United States has caused our minds to be heard" — This sentence is not grammatically or logically clear. What does "the effort of the United States" refer to? What does it mean for minds to "be heard"? Revise for precision.
"Science and technology have had effects on the U.S. that cannot be described" — This is vague. If effects can't be described, don't mention them; instead, name at least one specific effect.
Capitalizing "Nemesis" and other dramatic flourishes (eg, "the most dangerous things that we hold in our hands") suit a personal essay or speech more than an academic argument. Consider a more measured tone, or use such language more sparingly for emphasis.
"the less overt corners of our lives" is imprecise—give an example instead of gesturing vaguely.

Evidence and Development

Your two anecdotes (the "Spain in Michigan" story and your own delayed language learning) are your strongest material. Consider giving them more space and specificity rather than moving quickly past them. For instance, what was the children's reaction when you corrected them? Why did you have only German as an option—was this a resource issue, a curricular choice, a reflection of some larger value system? Slow down and analyze these moments rather than just reporting them.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify the precise causal claim linking technology to educational insularity.
Add transitional sentences/paragraphs to bridge your two main ideas.
Rewrite the confusing final sentence and add a real conclusion.
Tighten vague, dramatic language in favor of precise claims.
Develop your anecdotes with more analytical depth.

You have real material and genuine insight here—the revision task is mainly about building the logical scaffolding that lets your reader follow your reasoning from claim to claim.

Feedback on Your Essay: Computers and Everyday Life

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've chosen a topic that's relevant and relatable, and your writing shows a clear structure with an introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion. However, the essay would benefit from significant revision to strengthen its argument, deepen its analysis, and tighten its prose. Below are specific areas to address.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Your essay's title asks a question ("How could people live without the invention of the computer?"), and your conclusion circles back to this same question. This creates a sense of repetition rather than progression. More importantly, it's unclear what specific *argument* you're making. Is your claim that computers have improved life? That dependence on computers is dangerous? That both are true simultaneously? Right now, the essay reads as a general description of computer use rather than an argument that takes a position and defends it.

Revision suggestion: Write a clear thesis statement in your introduction that states a specific, debatable claim. For example: "While computers have made daily tasks more efficient, society's growing dependence on them has created a vulnerability that outweighs their benefits" or "Computers have transformed daily life so thoroughly that functioning without them is no longer realistic for most people." Choose one direction and build your paragraphs around defending it.

Develop Your Evidence and Analysis

Currently, your paragraphs list examples (schools, businesses, homes) without developing them. Each claim needs specific evidence, examples, or reasoning to support it. For instance, you write that "schools use computers to teach children because it is fun and exciting"—but *why* does fun and excitement matter to learning? What does research or your own observation show? Similarly, "businesses use computers because they can be more productive" needs elaboration: productive in what way? What happens when they aren't productive?

Revision suggestion: For each body paragraph, ask "so what?" after every claim. Push yourself to explain the significance of each example rather than simply naming it. Consider adding concrete details—statistics, a specific scenario, or a hypothetical example—to make your points vivid and convincing.

Address the Counterargument More Substantively

Your final paragraph raises an important and interesting point: dependence on computers becomes a problem when they break down. This is actually one of the most original ideas in your essay, but it's introduced only in the last paragraph and not fully explored. This point deserves its own body paragraph with specific examples (eg, what happens when hospital systems crash, when a business loses data, when students can't access online resources).

Revision suggestion: Consider restructuring so that this counterpoint becomes a substantial part of your argument, not an afterthought. This would also give your essay a more nuanced, balanced feel rather than reading as one-sided praise of computers until the very end.

Revise for Redundancy and Repetition

Several ideas are repeated across paragraphs. For instance, "people have come to rely on computers" or similar variations appear at the end of nearly every paragraph. This repetition weakens the essay's momentum, as each paragraph doesn't feel like it's building new ground.

Revision suggestion: After drafting, go through and highlight sentences that restate previous points. Cut or consolidate these, and use the freed-up space to add new evidence or deeper analysis.

Proofread for Grammar and Word Choice

There are a few errors to correct:
"calculators as usually found" should likely be "calculators are usually found"
"laboreous" should be "laborious"
The sentence "Computers have significantly changed people's lives" appears almost verbatim in both the introduction and paragraph 3—revise for variety.
Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences, such as: "People have come to rely on computers because of these reasons." Consider what "these reasons" specifically refers to and state it directly.

Reconsider Your Historical Claim

You state that the calculator was "the first computer." This claim is questionable and could distract a knowledgeable reader. If you want to include historical context, verify this claim with a source, or simply omit it if it's not central to your argument.

Summary

Your essay has a workable structure and an interesting final point about dependency, but it needs a clearer central argument, more developed evidence, and tighter prose. I'd recommend choosing a specific angle (benefits vs. drawbacks, or a nuanced view of both) and revising each paragraph to serve that argument directly. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report: Einstein and the Impact of Relativity

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious and genuinely interesting topic—the philosophical and social impact of Einstein's theory of relativity. You have real intellectual curiosity here, and your instinct to connect abstract physics to human psychology and geopolitics is compelling. However, the essay currently reads more like exploratory notes than a developed argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Clarify Your Thesis

Right now, your central claim is difficult to pin down. Are you arguing that:
Relativity hasn't been philosophically absorbed by humanity yet?
Relativity's true significance has been overshadowed by its weaponized application (atomic bombs)?
Humanity should shift from "fear" to "peace" as an organizing principle?

These are three different (though related) claims. Choose ONE as your primary thesis and make it explicit in your opening paragraph. Right now you spend the introduction describing Einstein's genius rather than stating what *you* will argue about his legacy.

Strengthen the Logical Connections

Several key moves in your argument need more explanation:

The Socratic connection: You assert that relativity "shattered" the Socratic idea that knowledge could be fully attained through inward revelation. This is a striking claim, but you need to explain *why* relativity does this. What specifically about "every point being a point of departure" undermines the possibility of fixed, complete knowledge? Right now the connection is asserted, not demonstrated.

From philosophy to atomic bombs: The transition from "humanity's philosophy" to "atomic bombs" is abrupt. You call the bomb an application of Einstein's ideas "not worthy of the genius," but you don't explain the actual scientific link (E=mc²) or why this application betrays the *philosophical* implications you just described. Right now these feel like two separate essays stapled together.

Fear to peace: This concluding call for a shift from fear to peace is admirable but underdeveloped. How does understanding relativity specifically produce peace rather than fear? You gesture at Hawking as an example, but don't explain what he understood that others didn't, or how that understanding translated into a peaceful (rather than fearful) worldview.

Address Factual and Conceptual Precision

The parenthetical "[his and his wife's]" needs clarification—if you're referencing scholarly debate about Mileva Marić's contributions to Einstein's work, this deserves a full sentence and perhaps a citation, not a passing bracket.
"USA-USSR; with Japan and Germany as the 'winners'" is confusing—Japan and Germany lost WWII. Clarify what you mean (perhaps that they benefited economically in the postwar order?).
The "sidphantha and his rock" reference is unclear to readers unfamiliar with this allusion. If it's central to your point about "indwelling" a topic, explain it.

Revise for Clarity and Precision of Language

Some phrases are evocative but imprecise:
"Relatively speaking, fear has been the main point of departure for the global community construction" — this sentence tries to cleverly echo relativity's vocabulary, but the wordplay obscures rather than illuminates your meaning. Aim for clarity first; clever phrasing should serve the argument, not replace it.
"Philosopher's stone was pulverized into utopic and nostalgic dust" — vivid image, but unpack what you mean. Utopian in what sense? Nostalgic for what?

Proofreading Issues

Please correct throughout:
"Einstien" → "Einstein" (multiple instances)
"histories" → "history's"
"nostolgic" → "nostalgic"
"Hawkings" → "Hawking"
"Socratic" should be capitalized consistently
Semicolons are used where periods or commas would be more appropriate (eg, "Cold wars; north-south relations")
Sentence fragments like "A shift in focus if you will." should be integrated into fuller sentences or used more sparingly for deliberate rhetorical effect

Structural Suggestions for Revision

Consider this structure:
Introduction: State your specific thesis about relativity's *unrealized* philosophical impact.
Body ¶1: Explain the philosophical shift relativity demands (the end of Socratic "closure"), with more explanation of *why*.
Body ¶2: Explain how this philosophical potential was diverted into the atomic bomb—make the E=mc² connection explicit.
Body ¶3: Develop your fear-to-peace argument with a specific example (perhaps expand on Hawking, or offer another).
Conclusion: Return to your thesis with a clear statement of what humanity stands to gain by "catching up" to Einstein's insight.

Final Thoughts

Your essay has an ambitious, almost philosophical style that echoes the scope of your subject—but ambition needs scaffolding. Each big claim you make deserves at least a few sentences of explanation and support. Focus your revision on explaining your reasoning steps, not just asserting your conclusions. You clearly have interesting ideas; now show your reader *how* you arrived at them.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled an interesting and locally-grounded topic: the economic and social effects of suburban shopping malls on downtown Kalamazoo. Your instinct to root an abstract argument in your own community is a strong choice—it gives you access to specific, concrete detail that many student writers lack. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of structure, argumentation, and mechanics before it fully delivers on its potential. Below are the key areas to focus on.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis—that malls are "more of a detriment than a blessing"—is stated early, which is good. However, the essay spends much of its length narrating the *history* of urban flight rather than building a sustained *argument*. Ask yourself: what is the specific claim I want readers to accept, and what evidence most directly supports it? Right now, the essay reads more like a historical summary with an argumentative frame loosely attached. Consider revising your thesis to be more precise—for example, arguing specifically that malls exacerbate economic inequality by stranding poorer residents in disinvested downtowns. This would give you a sharper analytical thread to follow throughout.

Structure and Organization

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph-level organization. Right now, ideas about geography, wealth, transportation, and downtown decline are somewhat tangled together within paragraphs. Consider organizing around discrete claims:

Historical background – how cities like Kalamazoo were originally organized around transportation and shared space.
The mechanism of decline – how wealth and mobility allowed some residents to leave, while others could not.
Consequences – what this has meant for those left behind (reduced services, disinvestment, etc).
Counter-evidence/complications – the role of colleges and festivals in partially revitalizing downtown.
Conclusion – restating the stakes and perhaps suggesting what should be done.

Right now, the colleges/festivals paragraph feels like a stray thought rather than a considered complication or counterargument. If you want to include it, explain *why* it matters: Does it undermine your thesis? Does it suggest a path forward? Right now it's disconnected from your central claim.

Development and Evidence

Your argument relies heavily on general historical narrative and assumption ("Throughout history, the trend is for the rich to get richer and the poor remain poor") rather than specific evidence. Since you're writing about your own hometown, you have an opportunity to strengthen your credibility with concrete details: What stores or industries closed? What data exists on downtown vacancy rates, poverty levels, or population shifts in Kalamazoo? Even a couple of specific facts, dates, or named businesses would make your claims far more persuasive than broad generalizations.

Additionally, the rhetorical question ("Why should we, say those with cars, go downtown...") is an interesting technique—it's meant to voice the mentality of suburban shoppers—but it appears without warning and shifts the essay's voice unexpectedly into first person plural. Consider setting this up more deliberately, perhaps as an explicit hypothetical: "One might imagine a suburban shopper reasoning: 'Why should I...'" This preserves the technique while keeping your authorial voice consistent.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are a number of issues to clean up in revision:

Fragment/formatting errors: "and still could be!" appears as an isolated line—this looks like a stray note left over from drafting rather than an intentional stylistic choice. Either integrate it into a sentence or cut it.
Homophone errors: "There neighborhoods don't get financial support" should be "Their neighborhoods." Similarly, "clientel" should be "clientele," and "cafés" appears with an encoding error (�) that should be fixed before submission.
Word choice/logic slip: "the flu from the inner cities" almost certainly should be "the flight from the inner cities"—this is an important correction since it currently creates a confusing image.
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences string clauses together with commas where periods or semicolons are needed (eg, "Businesses that could move out of the downtown district did, and still do, and relocate...").
Inconsistent verb tense: The essay shifts between past and present tense when describing ongoing processes; decide whether you're describing a historical process or a continuing one, and keep tense consistent accordingly.

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into a single, precise, arguable claim.
Reorganize paragraphs around discrete stages of the argument.
Add specific, local evidence (data, examples, named institutions) to replace generalizations.
Clarify the function of the colleges/festivals paragraph—counterargument or complication?
Proofread carefully for homophones, fragments, and encoding errors.

This essay has a genuine, locally-grounded argument at its core. With tighter organization and more specific evidence, it could be quite compelling.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic—the dual nature of television's impact on society. You have a clear stance and some good instincts about structuring an argument with a concession-and-rebuttal pattern. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen your argument significantly.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your thesis is somewhat buried and inconsistent. In your introduction, you claim television "has made the most impact on world views and lifestyle" and then pivot to how it "gave society an easy way to waste time." By the conclusion, you're making a more nuanced claim: society has become "more informed but too lazy to think about this new information." This final formulation is actually more interesting and specific than your opening thesis—consider leading with this idea instead. Right now, readers must wait until the last paragraph to discover your most sophisticated point.

Suggestion: Revise your introduction to state clearly: television has increased access to information while simultaneously discouraging critical engagement with it. This gives you a sharper, more arguable claim to build toward.

Development and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay makes assertions but rarely supports them with evidence. For example:
"Millions of Americans can be found to be staring at the T.V. at any one time" — What data supports this? Even an approximate statistic (eg, "the average American watches X hours of TV per day") would strengthen your credibility.
"T.V. is a major cause of lack of togetherness found in many American families" — This is a strong causal claim. Do you have a study, survey, or even a specific example to support it? Correlation and causation are easily confused here.
"television has lost much of its integrity" — Compared to what specific earlier era or programming? A brief historical example (eg, referencing early educational programming vs. contemporary reality TV) would make this claim concrete.

Suggestion: For each major claim, ask yourself "How do I know this?" and try to include at least one piece of specific evidence—a statistic, a named study, or a concrete example—per paragraph.

Paragraph Organization

Your second and third paragraphs both discuss television's negative effects, creating some redundancy. Consider whether they could be combined and reorganized: one paragraph presenting television's *potential* benefits (paragraph 2's opening), and a second, more developed paragraph presenting the *reality* (combining material from paragraphs 2 and 3) with your evidence integrated throughout.

Sentence-Level Issues

You have several run-on sentences and comma splices that need correction:

"Television has potential to offer wonderful things to its viewers, such as educational programming for children, cultural programs about far away places, and a closer look at current events than cannot be given by newspaper." — "than cannot" should be "than can"; also check this sentence for clarity, as the comparison is awkwardly phrased.
"T.V. is a major cause of lack of togetherness found in many American families, and has also resulted in an increase in laziness." — This is grammatically fine, but note the shift from a claim about families to a claim about individual behavior; make sure the connection between these two ideas is explicit.
"society has becomed more informed" — should be "become"

Proofreading

There are several typos and errors that need correction before this could be considered a polished draft:
"00 years ago" (paragraph 4) — this appears to be a placeholder or typo; fill in the actual number
"integrety" → "integrity"

"becomed" → "become"

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting and historically significant topic—the impact of the polio vaccine—and you clearly have some genuine enthusiasm for the subject. However, the essay needs substantial revision in several areas to strengthen its academic rigor and persuasive power. Below are key areas to focus on.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your thesis suggests two main claims: that the polio vaccine (1) changed the population and (2) acted as a foundation for further medical advances. However, the essay spends almost all of its time on the first claim and barely touches the second. If you want to argue both points, you need a paragraph dedicated to explaining *how* the vaccine served as a "foundation for further medical advances." What specific innovations or approaches did Salk's work make possible? Right now, the final paragraph gestures at this idea ("The process of vaccination also opened new doors") but doesn't develop it with evidence or examples.

Consider narrowing your thesis to one focused, defensible claim. Trying to prove both population impact *and* foundational scientific influence in a short essay spreads your argument too thin.

Historical Precision and Evidence

This is the essay's most significant weakness. You make several claims that need factual support:

When exactly was the vaccine introduced? You say "the middle of the century" multiple times—but the Salk vaccine was introduced in 1955. Precision matters in argumentative writing; vague temporal references weaken your credibility.
What are the actual statistics? How many children were affected by polio annually before the vaccine? How did those numbers change afterward? Without concrete data, your claims about the vaccine's population-level effects remain assertions rather than arguments.
The Baby Boom connection is unclear and underdeveloped. You correctly hedge by saying the vaccine "did not CAUSE" the boom, but then the explanation of what relationship *does* exist is muddled. The Baby Boom actually began around 1946—nearly a decade *before* the Salk vaccine (1955). This chronological problem undermines your argument as currently written. You need to either correct this timeline or clarify that you're discussing the vaccine's effect on children born *during* the boom, not the boom's origins.

Logical Development

The claim that "disease plays a role of population control" and that "every few years a new one comes along to normalize an overpopulated world" is a sweeping generalization presented without evidence. This kind of claim needs either support or removal. As written, it also sits awkwardly with your main argument—are you saying the vaccine disrupted a "natural" population control mechanism? If so, what are the implications of that claim? This deserves more careful thought and explicit reasoning, not just a passing assertion.

Structure and Paragraph Focus

Your second paragraph tries to do too much: it moves from personal generational comparison, to vaccine timeline, to Baby Boom causation, to disease as population control, and back to the vaccine's effects—all without clear transitions. Consider breaking this into two or three focused paragraphs, each with a single clear claim supported by evidence.

Tone and Register

The personal, conversational tone (eg, "I'm sure in the past there were 'booms'... but the population of children at that time would gradually level off due to disease") reads more like a spoken reflection than formal academic argument. Phrases like "For sure there was an effect" or "Why was it so great?" as a rhetorical question are casual choices that may undercut the academic register expected in this genre. Consider revising toward more formal phrasing: "This raises the question of why the vaccine had such significant impact" rather than "Why was it so great?"

Mechanics

There are several typos to fix:
"century before" (redundant with "for centuries")
"mamed" → "maimed" (appears twice)
"never never" → "never"
Capitalized "CAUSE" and "AIDS" should follow consistent formatting conventions—italics or no emphasis at all, rather than all caps, in formal prose.

Suggested Next Steps

Narrow your thesis to one clear, arguable claim.
Research and integrate specific statistics on polio incidence before and after 1955.
Correct the Baby Boom timeline or reframe that section to avoid factual inconsistency.
Develop the "foundation for future advances" claim with concrete examples (eg, how Salk's approach influenced later vaccine development, such as the oral polio vaccine or approaches to other viral diseases).
Proofread carefully for typos and revise casual phrasing into more formal academic language.

With more precise historical grounding and a tighter argumentative focus, this essay has strong potential. The topic matters, and your instinct to explore both immediate and long-term consequences of the vaccine is a good one—it just needs more rigorous development.

Feedback Report: The Impact of the Airplane

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious topic—the wide-ranging effects of air travel—and you've organized it clearly around four distinct domains: personal travel, business, entertainment, and international relations. This structure gives readers a clear roadmap, and each paragraph stays focused on its designated topic. However, the essay currently reads more like a list of examples than a developed argument. To strengthen this piece, you'll want to work on thesis clarity, analytical depth, and mechanical precision.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening thesis states that the airplane has "drastically changed the lives of many people," but this claim is broad enough that almost no one would disagree with it. Consider what specific *argument* you want to make. Are you claiming the airplane has been primarily beneficial? That its effects are more complex than commonly recognized? That it has fundamentally altered human relationships across distance? Your concluding sentence hints at a more interesting claim—that the airplane brought both positive and negative changes—but this idea appears only in your final lines, when it should shape your entire essay.

Suggestion: Revise your thesis to make an actual claim that requires defense, then ensure your body paragraphs work together to support that specific claim.

Paragraph Development

Each paragraph currently follows a similar pattern: a general claim followed by one or two brief examples. This creates a "list" feeling rather than sustained analysis. For instance, in your business paragraph, you assert that countries "can work together much easier" but don't explain *how* or *why* this matters. What does easier international business actually change about the world? Does it increase prosperity? Create new dependencies between nations? Your personal anecdote about noticing "the increase in International Business" needs elaboration—what have you specifically observed?

Suggestion: For each paragraph, ask yourself "So what?" after each claim. Push your analysis one or two steps further than your first instinct.

Use of Evidence

Your examples are relevant but underdeveloped. The Michigan-to-California comparison is effective and concrete—more paragraphs would benefit from this kind of specific detail. However, examples like "Michael Jackson just gave a concert in Madrid" need context: why does this matter? What would touring have looked like without air travel, and what does the current ease of travel enable for artists and audiences?

The final paragraph's treatment of Pearl Harbor and the atomic bombings is particularly thin. These are enormously significant historical events, and reducing them to a single sentence each feels rushed, especially given that this "negative" side of your argument could be your most compelling material. If the negative consequences of aviation interest you, consider giving this dimension more space—perhaps even reorganizing the essay so this complexity is explored throughout, not just tacked on at the end.

Structure and Balance

Right now, three of your four paragraphs focus on unambiguous benefits, while complexity and critique appear only in the final paragraph. This imbalance makes your concluding acknowledgment of "negative changes" feel like an afterthought rather than a genuine part of your argument. If you want to make a nuanced claim about mixed effects, that nuance needs to appear earlier and more substantially.

Grammar and Mechanics

You should proofread carefully for the following recurring issues:

Spelling errors: "effeciently" (efficiently), "musicans" (musicians), "Habor" (Harbor), "choosen" (chosen)
Word choice confusion: "effected" should be "affected" (affect = verb, effect = noun) in multiple places
Subject-verb agreement: "these would not take place as often as the do" (should be "they do")
Comma splices/run-ons: The first sentence and others combine independent clauses awkwardly with "&" — spell out "and" in formal writing, and consider breaking some of these into separate sentences
Punctuation: "The invention of the airplane has had many positive effects-, unfortunately, however, it was brought about some negative changes, also." This sentence needs revision—it's unclear and grammatically tangled. Consider: "While the airplane has had many positive effects, it has also brought about troubling changes."
Ampersands: Avoid "&" in formal academic writing; always write "and"

Revision Priorities

Sharpen your thesis to make a specific, arguable claim
Deepen your analysis in each paragraph—move beyond examples to explain their significance
Rebalance the essay if you want to seriously engage with both positive and negative consequences
Proofread carefully for spelling, word choice, and sentence structure

You have good instincts for organization and relevant examples—now focus on developing your ideas with the same care you've shown in structuring them.

Feedback on Your Essay: AIDS and Its Impact on Society

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic with real significance, and your essay shows genuine engagement with the social consequences of AIDS. However, the essay would benefit substantially from revision in three key areas: thesis clarity, argumentative development, and mechanics. Below, I offer specific suggestions organized by priority.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Argument

Right now, your essay reads more like a list of observations about AIDS than an argument. What is the specific claim you want readers to accept? Consider these possible directions:

AIDS fundamentally changed American social behavior (regarding sex, relationships, and prejudice)
AIDS-related fear revealed and amplified existing prejudices (homophobia especially)
Misinformation about AIDS caused more social harm than the disease itself

Your final paragraph gestures toward an interesting claim—that fear of AIDS made people more cautious and *more relationship-oriented*—but this idea appears only briefly and isn't fully argued. This could actually be your most original point. Consider making it your central thesis and building the whole essay around it.

Address Internal Tensions in Your Argument

Your essay contains an unresolved contradiction worth examining: you argue that fear of AIDS increased *prejudice and social division* (the Ryan White example, stigma against gay men) but also that it increased *caution and valuing of relationships* (less casual sex, more careful choices). These aren't necessarily incompatible, but you need to explicitly connect them. Are you arguing that fear produces both destructive and constructive social responses? That would be a compelling, nuanced thesis—but you need to state it directly and explore *why* fear leads to both outcomes.

Strengthen Your Evidence

The Ryan White example is your strongest piece of evidence—it's specific and illustrates prejudice concretely. Expand it. When did this happen? What exactly did the school do? How did White respond? A sentence or two more of detail would make this vivid and persuasive.

Your claims about "casual sex" declining and relationships being "valued more" are broad generalizations without support. Do you have any statistics, historical sources, or even anecdotal evidence to back this up? As it stands, this reads as speculation. Even one supporting source would strengthen this significantly.

"Undetectable strands of AIDS"—this is presented as established fact but needs clarification. What do you mean by this? Are you referring to asymptomatic HIV transmission, or something else? Precision matters here, both for credibility and clarity.

Reconsider Some Claims

You state AIDS "always leads to death and there is no cure." Depending on when this essay was written, you may want to acknowledge treatment advances (antiretroviral therapy) that have changed AIDS from a certain death sentence to a manageable chronic condition for many. This wouldn't undermine your argument about social impact—if anything, it could *strengthen* it, since you could discuss how perception lagged behind medical reality.

The phrase "this kind of ignorant thinking is breaking us apart" is a strong claim—identify *who* "us" is, and be specific about the social costs beyond stigma (job loss, family rejection, healthcare avoidance, etc).

Proofread Carefully

There are multiple spelling and grammar issues that need correction before this essay is ready for submission:

"peoples" → people's
"awhile" → a while
"untill" → until
"it's presence" → its presence (its = possessive; it's = "it is")
"it's consequences" → its consequences
"definately" → definitely
"devistating" → devastating
"there is more of stigma" → there is more stigma (remove "of")
"strands of AIDS" → likely strains (a strand is a thread; a strain is a genetic variant)

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using grammar-checking software, to catch these errors—they're distracting to readers and undermine your credibility.

Suggested Revision Priorities

Write a clear, arguable thesis statement for your introduction that captures your central claim.
Expand your best example (Ryan White) with more specific detail.
Support your claims about behavioral change with actual evidence or sources.
Explicitly connect the prejudice angle and the "increased caution" angle—show me how they relate.
Proofread thoroughly for spelling and grammar.

A Final Thought

Your instinct that fear and crisis can produce *both* social division and social reflection is genuinely interesting. If you build your revision around exploring that tension—rather than just listing effects—you'll have a much stronger essay. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You're tackling an ambitious and genuinely interesting thesis: that nuclear weapons/energy represent the most significant force of change in modern history. There's real passion here, and your personal reflections on growing up during the Cold War add valuable texture. However, the essay needs substantial revision in terms of structure, argumentation, and clarity to fully deliver on its premise.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your thesis claims nuclear weapons are the "most significant factor of change" in recent history—but the essay doesn't fully argue for this comparative claim. To support a "most significant" argument, you need to at least gesture toward *other* candidates for change (globalization, decolonization, the internet, civil rights movements) and explain why nuclear weapons surpass them. Right now, the essay describes the *effects* of the nuclear age (fear, militarization, the end of the Cold War) without making the comparative case explicit. Consider either narrowing your thesis to something more provable ("nuclear weapons fundamentally reshaped American culture and psychology during the Cold War") or dedicating a paragraph to briefly addressing counter-candidates for "most significant change."

Paragraph Organization

Each paragraph tries to do too much. For example, paragraph two moves from fear replacing nationalism, to your childhood associations, to government spending, to a list of Cold War artifacts (bomb shelters, command posts)—all in one paragraph. Consider breaking this into two paragraphs: one on the psychological/cultural climate of fear, and one on the material/political costs (spending, militarization, government structures). This will let each idea breathe and be developed with more specific evidence.

Evidence and Specificity

The essay relies heavily on general impressions ("uncountable trillions," "hundreds of smaller symbols of freedom") rather than concrete evidence. Since you're making a historical argument, even approximate figures or specific events would strengthen your credibility. For instance:
How much *did* the U.S. spend on nuclear weapons during the Cold War? (There are actual estimates you could cite.)
Which "hundreds of smaller symbols of freedom" are you referring to? Name a few (Checkpoint Charlie, the Iron Curtain more broadly, specific Eastern Bloc regimes).
"Reduced by several thousand troops"—compared to what baseline? This claim needs a number to be persuasive.

Clarity and Precision of Language

Some phrases are vague or imprecise and would benefit from revision:
"the race was on to create the most devastating..." — whose race? Be specific: this is really about the U.S. and its Cold War adversary.
"Soviet citizens with anti-Christ" — this is a striking personal detail, but the phrasing is unclear. Do you mean you associated Soviet citizens *with* the concept of the anti-Christ, or that you literally believed this? Clarify the sentence.
"arrived a standstill" should be "reached a standstill" or "come to a standstill."
"chief Executive commandpost from the air" is unclear—are you referring to Air Force One or the "Doomsday Plane" (the National Airborne Operations Center)? Naming it specifically will help readers understand your reference.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several errors to address in your next draft:
"Though advances" should likely be "Through advances" (paragraph 1).
"chief Executive" is capitalized inconsistently and "commandpost" should be two words ("command post").
"Although the old habits die hard, they are indeed dying" — the tense shift here (die hard vs. are dying) is a bit awkward; consider revising for smoother flow.
Watch comma splices, particularly in paragraph one: "the world as a body of people living in close proximity, has rapidly shrunk" — the comma before "has" is unnecessary and disrupts the sentence.
"9 presidential administrations" — spell out numbers under 100 in formal prose ("nine").

Tone and Voice

Your personal voice comes through most strongly in paragraph two, where you describe your own associations and fears as a child of the Cold War. This is your strongest material—consider expanding it. Personal testimony about Cold War psychology (bomb drills, cultural paranoia, specific memories) would make your abstract claims about "fear replacing nationalism" much more vivid and convincing.

Conclusion and Forward Look

Your final paragraph pivots optimistically toward the post-Cold War era and the potential of nuclear technology for peaceful purposes. This is a nice rhetorical move, but it happens quickly and could use more development. What specific humanitarian or medical applications are you envisioning? A stronger conclusion might also circle back explicitly to your opening thesis about "most significant change," reminding the reader how the essay has demonstrated that claim.

Priority Revisions

Sharpen or qualify your thesis to make it more provable.
Reorganize paragraph two into two focused paragraphs.
Add concrete evidence—dates, figures, named events—throughout.
Expand your personal narrative, which is your most compelling material.
Proofread carefully for the grammar issues noted above.

This essay has real potential—the ideas are ambitious and your personal connection to the topic is compelling. With more precise evidence and tighter structure, it will be considerably stronger.

Feedback Report: Penicillin Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay offers a clear, competently written historical overview of penicillin's discovery and its impact on the pharmaceutical industry. However, as an argumentative essay, it currently reads more as an informative summary than an argument. Below I outline the main areas for revision, organized by priority.

Establish a Clear Argumentative Thesis

This is the most important issue to address. Right now, your essay traces a chronological narrative (discovery → synthesis → industry growth) but never stakes out a claim that could be debated or that requires defense. Ask yourself: what is the *argument* here? Possible directions include:

Penicillin's discovery fundamentally *transformed* the pharmaceutical industry's approach to drug development (from natural discovery to synthetic design).
The economic incentives created by antibiotic synthesis had both positive and negative consequences (eg, overuse of antibiotics for minor illnesses like the common cold, foreshadowing resistance issues).
The shift from natural to synthetic antibiotic production reflects a broader turning point in how science and industry became intertwined.

Any of these would give you a debatable claim to build toward, rather than simply reporting "what happened." Your introduction should state this thesis explicitly, and each paragraph should work to support it.

Support Claims with Concrete Evidence

The essay currently relies on general, unsupported statements: "many uses," "a variety of other naturally-occurring antibiotics," "some were found to be medically useful." For an argumentative essay, you need specific, cited evidence:

Who discovered penicillin, and when? (Fleming, 1928; mass production during WWII)
What are examples of other antibiotics discovered afterward (streptomycin, tetracycline)?
What specific companies or research programs (you mention Upjohn — expand with a sentence on what they did, dates, figures)?
Statistics on survival rates, production volume, or market growth would strengthen your claims considerably.

Without citations, historical and scientific claims (especially about company names and outcomes) will not be credible to an academic reader. Consult secondary sources on the history of antibiotics and cite them properly.

Correct a Factual Error

Your conclusion refers to penicillin as coming from "a small bacteria." Penicillin is produced by the mold (fungus) *Penicillium notatum/chrysogenum*, not a bacterium. This is a meaningful error in an essay about medicinal chemistry and should be corrected — accuracy here is essential to your credibility.

Reduce Passive Voice and Vague Subjects

The essay is heavily dependent on passive constructions with unclear agents: "It was discovered that...," "It was also attempted to increase...," "New drugs were also synthesized." These constructions obscure *who* is doing the discovering or attempting, which weakens the historical specificity of your argument. Consider revising to name actors directly:

> Weak: "It was also attempted to increase the production of the naturally-occurring antibiotics through synthesis."
> Stronger: "Chemists at [company/university] sought to increase production of natural antibiotics through synthetic methods."

This shift will also make your prose more dynamic and analytical rather than descriptive.

Strengthen Structure and Transitions

Currently, the essay's three paragraphs follow a loose chronological pattern (discovery → synthesis boom → conclusion), but the connections between them are additive rather than analytical ("This...also led to...This...also caused..."). Consider organizing around your argument's logic instead of strict chronology. For example, if your thesis is about the transformation from natural to synthetic drug development, one paragraph could focus on the natural discovery phase, another on the synthetic/industrial phase, and a third on the implications or consequences of this shift (positive and/or negative).

Revise the Conclusion

Right now, the conclusion mostly restates the body paragraphs. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis and explain its broader significance — why does this history matter today? You might gesture toward antibiotic resistance, the pharmaceutical industry's current reliance on synthetic drug design, or global health implications, connecting the historical narrative to a stake for present-day readers.

Minor Editing

"attempted" is misspelled ("attemped").
"in vitro" should probably be "in vitro" (correct) but check that the concept (culturing organisms/compounds) is used precisely — antibiotics themselves aren't "cultured," the producing organisms are.
Watch comma splices and repetitive sentence openers ("This... This... It was...").

Summary

Your writing is clear and grammatically solid, but the essay needs a genuine argumentative claim, more specific and cited evidence, correction of the fungus/bacteria error, and revision of passive constructions to sharpen agency and analysis. Focus your revision on identifying *what you want to argue* about penicillin's legacy, then rebuild the paragraphs to support that claim with specific historical detail.

Feedback on "The Invention of the Television" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging topic and touched on several genuinely interesting dimensions of television's impact—entertainment, communication, and family life. However, the essay currently reads more as a list of observations than a sustained argument. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

This is the most significant issue. An argumentative essay needs a clear claim that you defend, not just a survey of "pros and cons." Right now, your thesis statement announces *topics* ("enhanced communication," "altered family life") rather than an *argument* about them. Then, within each section, you present both sides evenhandedly and never resolve or evaluate them. Your conclusion even states "it is anyone's guess if it will ever be resolved"—which essentially tells the reader you haven't taken a position.

Revision suggestion: Decide what you actually think. For example: "While television has undeniably improved access to information, its net effect on family life and cultural values has been more harmful than beneficial." A thesis like this gives you something to prove, and you can still acknowledge counterarguments (eg, "some argue television brings families together") before refuting them.

Structure and Organization

Your three body areas (entertainment, communication, family) are reasonable categories, but they're compressed into two paragraphs, which makes the essay feel rushed. Each topic deserves room to breathe:

Consider giving entertainment, communication, and family/values each their own paragraph.
Within paragraphs, follow a consistent pattern: claim → evidence/example → analysis → transition. Currently, several ideas are stated but not developed. For instance, "it has been helpful in warning people of dangers" is asserted without an example (a hurricane warning? a public health announcement?).

Development and Evidence

The essay relies heavily on generalizations ("many believe," "some argue," "others say") without naming who these people are or offering concrete support. For an argumentative essay, even informal evidence—specific examples, statistics, or references to studies—would strengthen your claims considerably.

Revision suggestion: Choose one or two vivid, specific examples per section. For instance, instead of general references to "violence and sex," you might cite a specific study on children's television viewing and aggression, or reference a well-known case (eg, debates about V-chips or the FCC's content ratings) to ground your claim in something concrete.

Paragraph Focus

The entertainment paragraph starts strong but drifts into a mini-debate about whether TV is "beneficial or not" that isn't fully connected to the paragraph's main point. Similarly, the family paragraph raises the dinner-table disruption issue and the values/violence issue as if they were separate topics crammed together. Consider splitting these into distinct paragraphs, each with its own clear controlling idea.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors worth addressing systematically:

their/there confusion: "Of course their have been..." should be "there have been." Also "As television programing improves their is an increasing number" → "there is."
Verb tense/agreement: "the become desensitized" → "they become desensitized."
Word choice: "yonkies" should be "junkies." Also "invention of the 20 century" is missing "-th" (20th century) throughout.
Comma splices/run-ons: "This invention not only introduced a new form of entertainment, it has enhanced communication..." needs a semicolon or "but" ("not only introduced...but also enhanced...").
Redundant conjunctions: "and and diminished communication" — likely a typo; remove the duplicate "and."
Ampersands: Avoid "&" in formal writing; spell out "and."
Parallelism: "created news forms" should be "new forms."
Punctuation with quotation marks: "'family dinner'" is followed by a line break before the verb—check formatting/spacing throughout the manuscript, as there seem to be stray line breaks mid-sentence (possibly a formatting artifact from copying).

I'd recommend running a careful read-aloud pass specifically hunting for *there/their/they're*, subject-verb agreement, and run-on sentences, since these recur multiple times.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates the essay's structure rather than reinforcing an argument. Since your ending claims the issue is unresolved, it undercuts the persuasive power of the essay. Once you sharpen your thesis (see #1), revise the conclusion to reaffirm your specific stance and perhaps gesture toward why it matters (eg, what should readers or policymakers do in light of your argument?).

Priority for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to state a clear, debatable claim.
Reorganize into distinct paragraphs, each with a single controlling idea and concrete evidence.
Revise the conclusion to align with your strengthened thesis.
Proofread carefully for the grammar patterns noted above.

You have good instincts about what makes this topic interesting—now it's a matter of taking a stand and giving your reader specific, well-organized reasons to agree with you. I'm happy to look at a revised draft once you've reworked the thesis and structure.

Feedback on "The Washing Machine" Essay

Overall Impression

This essay has an engaging, conversational voice and some genuinely funny moments (the image of someone clutching a washing machine while fleeing a house fire is memorable!). However, the essay struggles to establish a clear argumentative position and relies almost entirely on hyperbole and rhetorical questions rather than actual evidence or reasoning. Let's work through the key areas for revision.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis is unclear. The essay opens as if it will *praise* the washing machine ("millions and millions of lives were made simpler"), but by the end you're arguing that such conveniences make us lazy and contribute to weight problems. This is a legitimate and interesting argument, but you need to signal it from the beginning. Right now, the reader doesn't know what claim you're defending until the final paragraph, and even then it arrives somewhat suddenly with the Weight Watchers reference.

Revision suggestion: Decide on your actual claim. Are you arguing that convenience technology has hidden costs to health and self-reliance? If so, state this directly in your introduction so readers know what to expect and can evaluate your evidence as they read.

Evidence and Logic

This is the essay's biggest weakness. Every claim is asserted through exaggeration rather than supported with evidence. Consider these unsupported leaps:

The claim that people would run into the street clutching a washing machine during a fire is a joke, not evidence of the machine's necessity.
The connection between washing machines specifically and the rise of diet culture is asserted, not demonstrated. Many factors contribute to changes in health trends (diet, food processing, sedentary jobs, car culture); isolating "modern gadgets" as *the* cause needs support.
"It is no small wonder that..." and "it is easy to see that..." are phrases that assert conclusions without actually showing the reasoning that gets you there.

Revision suggestion: For an argumentative essay, you need at least some concrete support—historical data on physical activity levels, studies on labor-saving technology and health, or even just a more developed logical chain (eg, "Hand-washing clothes required X calories of physical exertion per week; machine washing requires none; multiply this loss across all labor-saving devices, and the cumulative effect on activity levels becomes significant"). Even one or two real statistics would strengthen your credibility considerably.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay meanders between describing the washing machine's convenience, mocking people's fear of hand-washing, and finally arriving at a critique of modern gadgets generally. Each paragraph could be tightened around a single controlling idea:

Paragraph 1: Currently a broad history of invention. Consider trimming this and moving directly toward your actual argument.
Paragraphs 2–3: Both make essentially the same point (people are afraid of hand-washing) through similar hyperbolic examples. Consider combining these or differentiating their purposes—perhaps one focuses on emotional reaction, the other on practical consequence.
Paragraph 4: This is where your real argument lives. It deserves to be expanded and probably split into two paragraphs: one developing the "convenience causes inactivity" claim, another addressing the counterargument (the housewife's benefit) more fully before your rebuttal.

Tone

The sarcastic, humorous tone is enjoyable but inconsistent with the more serious claim you're making in the conclusion about health and modern life. Ask yourself: do you want this essay to be a comedic riff, or a serious argument with humor as seasoning? Right now it's unclear which effect you're aiming for, and the abrupt shift into the Weight Watchers line at the end feels like it belongs to a different, more serious essay than the one about clutching washing machines during a fire.

Revision suggestion: Keep some humor, but let it serve the argument rather than distract from it. For instance, the fire anecdote could be repurposed to *illustrate* dependency (a serious point) rather than simply provoking a laugh.

Mechanical Issues

There are several errors to correct in your next draft:
Spelling: "continously" → continuously; "necesity" → necessity
"with out" should be "without" (appears twice)
Avoid using "&" in formal academic writing—write out "and"
"hand clothes washing" is awkward; consider "hand-washing clothes"
The ellipsis after "But is that all that bad?...." is overused and should be replaced with a period or dash
"his/her" is acceptable but consider whether a plural subject ("people") might read more smoothly throughout

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis clearly and early.
Add real evidence, not just hyperbolic anecdotes.
Tighten paragraph focus so each one advances the argument rather than repeating the same point.
Decide on a consistent tone and use humor purposefully.
Proofread carefully for spelling and punctuation.

You have a strong voice and a genuinely interesting angle here—now it needs the scaffolding of a rigorous argument to support it.

Overall Impression

This essay has an engaging, conversational voice and a clear enthusiasm for its subject. The personal anecdotes (especially the smelly locker story) are vivid and effective. However, the essay currently reads more like an informal list of anecdotes than a structured argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, reorganize for logical flow, and elevate the diction and tone to match academic expectations.

Strengths to Build On

Personal voice and authenticity: The anecdotes make your claims concrete and believable rather than abstract.
Specific examples: You avoid vague generalizations by naming actual uses (roast beef sandwiches, shampoo bottles, ice for a swollen lip).
Enthusiasm: Your genuine interest in the topic comes through, which is a good foundation for persuasive writing.

Areas for Revision

Thesis and Structure

Right now, the essay lacks a clear, arguable thesis statement. "The zip-lock bag was definitely a good invention" is your closing line, but it should appear (in a more developed form) in your introduction to guide the reader. Consider crafting a thesis that specifies *why* this invention matters—for instance, that its versatility across food preservation, odor control, and travel makes it an indispensable household tool. Also, the essay would benefit from clear topic sentences that preview each paragraph's focus, rather than transitions like "There are also times when..." or "But, there are other uses..." which feel loose and conversational.

Paragraph Organization

Your paragraphs would be stronger if each focused on one clear category of use rather than blending examples. For instance, paragraphs 3 and 4 both deal with odor control and could be merged or more clearly distinguished (one is about odors escaping, the other about odors from food combining). The final paragraph introduces travel, wet/dry storage, and injury care all at once—consider whether these deserve their own paragraph or a clearer subdivision.

Tone and Register

Since this is an *argumentative* essay, aim for a more formal register throughout. Some phrases to reconsider:

"It is funny that we are writing on this subject" — this opening is casual and delays your actual argument. Consider starting with your claim or a compelling image instead.
"onlies???" — this aside is fine for a draft, but in a final version, either commit to a phrasing (eg, "dry-clean-only garments") or omit the parenthetical uncertainty.
"What more could you ask for?" — rhetorical questions can work in argumentative writing, but use them sparingly and purposefully.

Sentence-Level Issues

Watch for stray punctuation errors, such as the extra period after "locker" in paragraph 2 ("even opened my locker. .").
"A third solution would be to bag everything!" feels like a throwaway joke that undercuts the logical seriousness of your two prior solutions. Either develop this idea meaningfully or cut it.
Some sentences run together ideas awkwardly—eg, "Anything can be put in zip-lock bag, and not get freezer burn" has a grammar slip ("in zip-lock bag" needs an article) and could be tightened.

Developing the Argument Further

An argumentative essay benefits from acknowledging counterarguments or limitations. Are there downsides to zip-lock bags (environmental impact, cost, single-use plastic waste) that you could address and then refute or contextualize? Engaging with a potential objection—even briefly—would strengthen your credibility and show critical thinking, not just enthusiasm.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently just restates examples rather than synthesizing your argument or leaving the reader with a broader insight. Consider ending with a reflection on why small, practical inventions like this matter in everyday life, or a nod to how such an "unglamorous" invention deserves more recognition than it gets.

Suggested Next Steps

Draft a clear thesis statement for your introduction that states your claim and previews your main categories of support (freshness, odor control, versatility/travel).
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has one clear focus with a topic sentence.
Revise the tone to be more consistently formal while preserving your personal examples.
Consider adding a brief counterargument paragraph.
Proofread for grammar and punctuation errors.

You have great material here—now it needs a stronger frame to support it.

Feedback on "The Computer: The Most Influential Invention of the 20th Century"

Overall Impression

Your essay identifies an interesting and defensible claim—that the computer is the most transformative invention of the 20th century—but as it stands, the essay reads more like a general description of computers than an *argumentative* essay. To strengthen this piece, you need to sharpen your thesis, provide concrete evidence, address counterarguments, and tighten your prose. Below are specific areas to focus on in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your opening paragraph states that the computer is "the most influential" invention, but this claim is asserted rather than argued. An argumentative essay needs to acknowledge that other candidates exist (the telephone, the automobile, antibiotics, the airplane, television, the internet as distinct from the computer itself) and explain *why* the computer surpasses them. Consider revising your thesis to something more specific and defensible, such as: "While inventions like the automobile and telephone reshaped daily life, the computer's unique capacity to process, store, and transmit information has made it the most transformative invention of the 20th century because it fundamentally altered how humans think, work, and connect."

A strong thesis should also preview your supporting reasons, giving the reader a roadmap for the essay.

Structure and Paragraph Development

Currently, your three paragraphs function as: (1) introduction, (2) history of computer hardware, (3) impact and speculation about the future. This structure spends too much time on *description* (how computers evolved physically) and not enough on *argumentation* (why this evolution matters more than other inventions). Consider restructuring as follows:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly and preview 2–3 main reasons.
Body Paragraph 1: One major impact of the computer (eg, business/productivity), with specific evidence.
Body Paragraph 2: A second major impact (eg, communication or scientific research).
Body Paragraph 3: Address a counterargument—why might someone argue another invention was more influential? Then rebut it.
Conclusion: Synthesize your argument without introducing excessive new speculation.

Evidence and Specificity

This is the essay's biggest weakness. Claims like "productivity of people has increased ten-fold" and "every human being in the developed world will have their own PC" are asserted without any supporting evidence, statistics, examples, or citations. Since this is an argumentative essay, you need concrete support:

Where does the "ten-fold" figure come from? If you don't have a source, remove or qualify the claim.
Use specific historical examples: the ENIAC, the IBM PC (1981), the rise of spreadsheet software transforming accounting, email replacing postal correspondence, etc
If you don't have access to research sources, at least use more precise and defensible language rather than sweeping generalizations.

Tone and Speculation

The final paragraph drifts from argument into prediction and hyperbole ("small digital assistants that will replace the printed page," "the future of this invention looks 'bright' indeed"). While some forward-looking commentary can strengthen a conclusion, extensive speculation weakens your credibility as it isn't grounded in evidence. I'd also avoid the pun on "bright"—it feels overly casual for an academic essay and risks undercutting your argument's seriousness. Consider ending instead by returning to your thesis and summarizing *why* the computer's impact has been unmatched, perhaps with one forward-looking sentence at most.

Style and Word Choice

"expensive monsters" is a vivid but informal phrase; consider more precise language, such as "room-sized, costly machines."
"20th century miracle" is another informal/hyperbolic phrase to reconsider.
Watch for redundancy: the first paragraph essentially repeats the same claim (the computer changed how people live) three times. Trim repetition.

Grammar and Mechanics

"occured" → "occurred" (double check spelling throughout).
"2st century" → "21st century."
"is being dealt with" — check subject-verb agreement: "data and information *are* dealt with" (or treat "data" as singular collective, but be consistent).
Watch inconsistent spacing (eg, extra space before "predecessors").
The sentence "We are limited only by our imaginations / and the future..." has an odd line break—check formatting before submission.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to make a clear, arguable claim.
Restructure paragraphs around *arguments*, not just description.
Add concrete evidence, examples, and (if required) citations.
Include and rebut a counterargument to strengthen your position.
Reduce speculative and hyperbolic language, especially in the conclusion.
Proofread carefully for spelling, agreement, and formatting errors.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a general appreciation of computers to a persuasive, well-supported argument. I look forward to seeing your next draft!

Feedback on "AIDS and Society" Essay

Overall Impression

You're tackling an important and complex topic, and your passion for the subject—particularly your concern about stigma, education, and compassion—comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a tightly argued essay. To strengthen this piece, you'll want to sharpen your thesis, reorganize around a clear line of reasoning, and revise at the sentence level for precision and correctness.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening claims that AIDS is "one of the most important discoveries of the 20th Century," but this framing creates a problem: AIDS is a disease, not a discovery (the discovery would be identifying the disease or its cause, HIV). This may seem like a small point, but it signals to your reader that the essay's central claim isn't yet precise.

More importantly, by the end of the essay, you're no longer really arguing that AIDS was an important "discovery"—you're arguing that society's response to AIDS reveals patterns of prejudice, followed by grudging acceptance, and that we must now prioritize compassion and education over blame. This is actually a more interesting and defensible thesis than your opening sentence suggests. I'd encourage you to revise your introduction to state this argument directly: something like, "The history of the AIDS epidemic reveals a troubling pattern: society only extends compassion to victims of disease once it can no longer distance itself from them." Then make sure every paragraph serves that claim.

Organization

Right now the essay moves chronologically and somewhat associatively—from gay men, to IV drug users, to children, to Ryan White, to sex education, to Magic Johnson—without clear signposting of *why* each example matters to your argument. Consider:

Paragraph 1: Establish the pattern of selective empathy (gay men, drug users ignored).
Paragraph 2: Ryan White and children as the turning point—but also introduce how awareness bred *new* discrimination, since this is a key claim you make but don't fully develop.
Paragraph 3: The sex-education debate feels like a tangent as written. If you keep it, you need to explicitly connect it back to your thesis—perhaps as another example of society reacting to fear rather than facts.
Paragraph 4: Return to Magic Johnson as an echo of Ryan White (another case of a "sympathetic" figure needed to shift public attention), then close with your call for acceptance and education.

Adding these connective threads—explicitly telling the reader *why* you're including each example—will make the essay feel unified rather than episodic.

Development and Evidence

Several claims need more support or explanation:
You assert that "awareness also came discrimination" but never illustrate this discrimination with a specific example (school exclusion? job loss? violence?). This is a missed opportunity, since it's one of your strongest potential points.
The statistic that "children are becoming sexually active at the age of eleven" needs a source or at least softer, more defensible phrasing.
The claim that "abstinence is the only safe way to prevent AIDS" is true only in the narrowest sense and could use nuance (eg, safe sex practices reduce risk significantly).

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Fragment: "Even if a cure is never found." This should be attached to the following sentence.
Ampersands: Avoid "&" in formal academic writing; spell out "and."
"Effect" vs. "affect": "they thought AIDS would never effect them" should be *affect*.
Comma splices: "As the 80's came to a close there was a greater awareness of AIDS; however, with the awareness also came discrimination" — this one is fine, but check others like "Parents are still fighting the distribution of condoms in schools and they want their children to learn abstinence" (needs a comma before "and," or restructuring).
Word choice: "How wrong they were" as a standalone sentence is dramatic but underdeveloped—wrong about what, specifically? Tie it back to a concrete consequence.
Tone: Some phrasing ("no one really cared that gays would die") is powerful but could be strengthened by acknowledging *why* this indifference existed (homophobia, lack of information) rather than stating it flatly.

Revision Priorities

Rewrite the introduction with a precise, arguable thesis.
Add a concrete example of discrimination following AIDS awareness.
Clarify the connection between the sex-education paragraph and your main argument, or consider cutting it if it can't be tightly integrated.
Proofread for fragments, ampersands, and affect/effect errors.
Strengthen the conclusion by circling back explicitly to the thesis about societal patterns of empathy and prejudice.

You have the raw materials for a compelling essay about how fear and prejudice shape public health responses—now it's a matter of organizing and sharpening those ideas into a clear argument.
