Feedback on Your Essay: Technology, Nature, and Human Motivation

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely interesting and ambitious thesis: that only medical inventions have improved life in the twentieth century, because all other technology stems from humanity's attempt to dominate nature and other people. This is a bold, philosophically rich claim, and your personal investment in the topic is evident in your writing. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of assertions than a developed argument. To strengthen it, you'll need to add concrete evidence, address obvious counterarguments, and tighten your logic. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim is intriguing, but it rests on a psychological premise—that all invention stems from fear of death and insecurity—that you assert rather than support. This is a big claim about human nature, and readers will want to know why they should accept it. Consider: Is *all* technology motivated by fear and domination? What about inventions driven by curiosity, creativity, play, or the desire to connect with others (musical instruments, the telephone, writing tools)? Addressing this complexity, even briefly, would make your argument more credible rather than weakening it.

Similarly, your claim that medical technology *cannot* be used to dominate others deserves scrutiny. Historically, medicine has been implicated in eugenics, unequal access to healthcare, and even weaponization (biological warfare, for instance). Acknowledging these complications and explaining why you still find your distinction meaningful would make your argument more sophisticated and harder to dismiss.

Development and Evidence

This is the area that needs the most attention. Right now, each paragraph makes a claim but offers little to no concrete support:

Paragraph 2 claims inventions lead us "away from a lifestyle peacefully coexistent with nature" and that we're "destroying ourselves," but gives no specific examples. Which twentieth-century inventions do you have in mind? The automobile? Industrial agriculture? Nuclear power? Naming specific technologies and explaining their environmental consequences would transform this from assertion into argument.

Paragraph 3 is only two sentences long and simply repeats the claim that inventions are used "to dominate one another" without elaboration. What weapons or economic systems are you referring to? How do they function to "deprive" people? This paragraph needs substantial expansion.

Paragraph 4 asserts that medical inventions help "people of all colors" and are "kind," but again offers no examples (penicillin? vaccines? the polio vaccine?) and no acknowledgment of medicine's complicated history (unequal access, exploitation in medical research, etc).

As you revise, aim to support each major claim with at least one specific, concrete example. This will not only make your argument more persuasive but will also help you test whether your claims actually hold up.

Structure and Organization

Your essay follows a clear structure: introduction with thesis, two paragraphs on "bad" technology, one paragraph on "good" (medical) technology. This is a reasonable skeleton, but the paragraphs are uneven in length and depth—paragraph 3 in particular feels like a placeholder rather than a developed section. Consider whether the argument would benefit from:

A clearer transition explaining *why* domination of nature and domination of other people are two versions of the same underlying impulse (you gesture at this in the introduction, but the body paragraphs treat them as separate, disconnected points).
A paragraph addressing counterarguments or complications (see above) before your conclusion, which would strengthen your credibility.
A concluding paragraph that goes beyond restating your thesis—perhaps reflecting on the implications of your argument, or on what it would mean to invent more "kindly."

Style and Mechanics

Your prose is clear and direct, which is a strength. However, watch for:

Typos and word errors: "occured" → occurred; "0the century" → 20th century; "truely" → truly; "affect" → effect (as a noun, you want "effect").
Comma splices/spacing: There's an extra space before some commas ("dwell on the characteristics," "For these reasons ,"). Proofread carefully for spacing and punctuation.
Repetition: Words like "dominate" and "insecurities" appear frequently; varying your vocabulary would make the prose more engaging.
Tone: The opening sentence ("Interestingly enough, this topic is one that is very important to me") is a bit informal for an argumentative essay. Consider opening with a claim or question that draws the reader into the stakes of your argument more directly.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add concrete historical examples to support every major claim.
Address at least one counterargument or complication to your thesis.
Expand paragraph 3 substantially.
Proofread carefully for typos and punctuation.
Consider revising your opening to establish stakes more formally.

You have a genuinely thought-provoking idea here—the revision work now is about giving it the evidentiary and logical scaffolding it deserves.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Satellite Dish and Global Communication

Overall Impression

Your topic—the satellite dish's impact on global communication—is genuinely interesting and timely. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of loosely related observations than a cohesive argument. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to clarify your thesis, provide evidence for your claims, and revise for basic accuracy and clarity. Below are specific areas to address.

Establish a Clear, Arguable Thesis

Right now, your essay doesn't take a clear stance. Is the satellite dish's impact primarily positive, negative, or mixed? Your conclusion suggests "pros and cons," but this is too vague to guide a reader through an *argumentative* essay. Consider narrowing your focus: Are you arguing that satellite technology has fundamentally reshaped geopolitical power? That it has degraded media quality? That it has created economic inequality? Pick one clear claim and build your paragraphs around defending it.

Support Claims with Evidence

Several assertions in this essay need factual backing:

"40% of U.S. millionaires are entertainers" — Where does this statistic come from? Unsupported numbers undermine your credibility. Cite a source or remove the claim.
"The intelligence of an average teenager is lowering itself due to cable viewing" — This is a strong claim that requires research support (studies on media consumption and cognitive development, for instance). As written, it sounds like speculation.
"Naval programs have wasted exceeding amounts of money" — What programs? What amounts? This sentence is too vague to be persuasive.
CNN's influence on government decisions — This is a compelling idea (sometimes called the "CNN effect" in political science), but you should reference specific examples or scholarship rather than asserting it in general terms.

Fix Factual and Technical Errors

The opening paragraph contains confusing errors: "1/2 egg" and "in the last 0 years" appear to be typos that obscure your meaning. Please proofread carefully—these errors make the introduction hard to follow.
STARWARS should likely be capitalized/formatted as "Star Wars" (referring to the Strategic Defense Initiative), and you should clarify this reference for readers unfamiliar with the term.
Check company names for proper capitalization ("Panasonic," not "panasonic").
MTV's launch date (1980) should be verified—MTV actually launched in 1981.

Improve Paragraph Organization and Transitions

Each paragraph introduces a new topic (news media, business, entertainment, cable/military) without clear transitions or a unifying thread. Consider:

Adding topic sentences that explicitly connect back to your thesis
Using transitional phrases to show how each example builds your argument (eg, "Just as CNN transformed political discourse, the entertainment industry experienced a parallel transformation...")
The paragraph on "Panasonic and CORE" feels underdeveloped—only two sentences long. Either expand this point with specific examples of how these companies use satellite-driven information, or integrate it into another paragraph.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates that there are "pros and cons" without committing to an interpretation. A strong argumentative essay should end by reinforcing your specific claim, not deferring the judgment entirely to "the average person." Consider ending with a more assertive synthesis: What do *you* conclude, based on the evidence you've presented?

Proofread for Clarity and Correctness

There are several grammatical and spelling issues to address:
"recieve" → "receive"
"affect" vs. "effect" (paragraph 4—profits "have had a strong affect" should likely be "effect," since "affect" is a verb but you need a noun/verb agreement check here)
"it's" → "its" (referring to possession in the first sentence)
"pro's and con's" → "pros and cons" (no apostrophe needed for plural nouns)
"ctry" appears to be an abbreviation for "century"—spell this out
"millionares" → "millionaires"

Suggested Next Steps

Draft a one-sentence thesis statement that takes a clear position.
For each paragraph, ask: "Does this directly support my thesis?" If not, revise or cut it.
Find at least 2-3 credible sources (news articles, academic sources) to support your statistical claims.
Do a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch awkward phrasing and errors.

You have interesting raw material here—particularly the idea of the "CNN effect" on global politics. With a clearer argument and stronger evidence, this could be a compelling essay. I'd be happy to discuss your revision plan further.

Feedback Report: "The Television Revolution"

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an ambitious and genuinely interesting question—how television reshaped American culture—and it offers a clear three-part structure (mass culturalization, commercialization, speed/reach of information) that gives readers a roadmap for your argument. Your examples are vivid and well-chosen (The Cleavers, *Green Acres*, the Challenger explosion, Vietnam War coverage), and you clearly have a strong intuitive grasp of the subject. The essay needs revision primarily in three areas: sharpening the thesis and argumentative claims, strengthening evidence and analysis, and cleaning up mechanics and style. Below are specific suggestions.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening line contains a typo that undercuts your credibility from the first sentence: "The 0th Century" should read "The 20th Century." Proofread openings especially carefully, since first impressions matter.

More substantively, your thesis promises three effects, but by the conclusion you pivot to a fourth, larger claim: that television is a "manipulator" we must watch critically. This is actually your most interesting insight, but it appears only in the final paragraph, almost as an afterthought. Consider foregrounding this argument earlier. Right now the essay reads as descriptive (television caused X, Y, and Z) rather than argumentative (television's cultural power is dangerous because...). Ask yourself: what is the *stakes* of your claim? Why should a reader care that TV homogenized culture or sped up information? If the danger of manipulation is your real point, restructure the thesis to state this explicitly, and make each body paragraph build toward it rather than simply listing effects.

Evidence and Analysis

Each paragraph offers a good example but often stops short of analysis. For instance, in paragraph 2, you assert that TV created a false, uniform picture of "the ideal family" and that this fostered "mass culturalization." This is a strong claim, but you move from example (The Cleavers) to conclusion (mass culturalization) without explaining the *mechanism*. How exactly does watching the same sitcom translate into cultural homogenization? Does it erase regional differences, create false expectations, or something else? Spell out the logical steps.

Similarly, the commercialization paragraph is thin compared to the others—only three sentences. This is your shortest paragraph but arguably contains a rich topic (the transformation of ads into "an art form," the sale of "15 second blocks of time"). Expand this section with a concrete example (a famous ad campaign, a statistic about ad spending) and explain *how* advertising changed values or behavior, not just that it did.

The Vietnam War paragraph is your strongest because you connect the visual immediacy of television ("bloody children under the American flag") directly to a psychological/political effect (increased opposition to the war). Use this paragraph as a model for the others: concrete image + explicit causal claim.

Also consider: you are making broad historical and sociological claims (about cultural homogenization, advertising's evolution, media effects on protest movements) without any citations. Even in an argumentative essay, evidence beyond your own assertions—historical facts, statistics, or scholarly claims about media effects—will make your argument far more persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Style, Tone, and Mechanics

The essay mixes casual and academic registers in ways that weaken it. Specific issues:

Abbreviations: "w/," "w/o," "&," and "N.Y." are appropriate for notes but not polished prose. Spell these out.
Capitalization: "vietnam war," "united States," and "Ghetto" should be capitalized/formatted correctly (Vietnam War, United States, ghetto life—lowercase unless part of a proper name).
Spelling: "grupes" (groups), "breath" (breadth), "livingrooms" (living rooms), "inhance" (enhance), "Populuxe" (verify spelling and define this term for readers unfamiliar with it).
Sentence fragments: "The television a powerful and dangerous tool" is missing a verb ("is"). Read your final paragraph aloud—it's your weakest sentence-level writing in the essay, ironic given it delivers your most important idea.
Comma splices: "Rather than several small, isolated cultures, the united States is moving..." — check punctuation throughout for run-ons, especially in paragraph 2.
Word choice: "omniscient picture" is imprecise—omniscient means "all-knowing," not "uniform" or "singular." Consider "monolithic" or "uniform" instead.

A careful proofreading pass—reading the essay backward, sentence by sentence, or aloud—will catch most of these errors.

Conclusion

Your conclusion is abrupt. "We must watch what we watch" is a clever line, but it needs development: what does critical viewing actually look like? What should readers *do* differently? Consider expanding this final paragraph into a fuller synthesis that returns to your three main points and explains how awareness of manipulation might change the way we engage with each.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify and foreground your central argument (likely: TV's power is double-edged, and danger lies in unconscious consumption).
Deepen the "commercialization" paragraph with a concrete example.
Add explicit causal reasoning connecting examples to claims throughout.
Proofread thoroughly for spelling, capitalization, and informal abbreviations.
Strengthen the conclusion into genuine synthesis, not just a closing gesture.

You have real analytical instincts here—the Vietnam paragraph proves it. Apply that same rigor throughout, and this essay will be significantly stronger.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Fall of the Berlin Wall

Overall Impression

You've written a vivid, personal reflection on a genuinely significant historical moment, and your emotional investment in the topic comes through clearly. The opening paragraph in particular does excellent work drawing the reader into the atmosphere of that day. However, as an *argumentative* essay, the piece currently reads more like a personal narrative or reflection. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your central claim, build a clearer logical structure, and address some issues with clarity and mechanics.

Identifying Your Argument

Your thesis seems to be that the fall of the Berlin Wall was the catalyst for the collapse of communism across Eastern Europe. This is a strong, defensible claim—but it doesn't emerge clearly until your final paragraph. Consider stating this thesis explicitly near the end of your first paragraph or the start of your second, so readers know from the outset what you're arguing and why the personal anecdotes matter.

Additionally, you make a striking concession midway through: "Eventually, I think that things would have turned out as they did." This is a significant admission that seems to undercut your catalyst argument. If you believe communism's collapse was inevitable regardless of the Wall's fall, you need to reconcile this with your claim that the Wall was "the catalyst force." Was the Wall's fall causally necessary, or did it simply accelerate an inevitable process? This distinction matters and should be addressed directly rather than left as a passing aside.

Structure and Development

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph organization tied to your argument:

Paragraph 1 effectively sets the scene but could end with a clearer thesis statement.
Paragraph 2 shifts to historical context (40 years of communist oppression) and a personal anecdote about your classmate's father. This anecdote is powerful, but its argumentative function isn't fully developed—how does it support your claim about the Wall being a catalyst? Consider drawing out the connection explicitly: perhaps his story illustrates the human cost that made the momentum after 1989 so unstoppable.
Paragraph 3 attempts to make your causal argument but moves quickly through complex claims (Hungary, Czechoslovakia, Poland, Romania rising up) without evidence or elaboration. This is where your argument needs the most development. What evidence supports the idea that the Berlin Wall's fall specifically inspired these other uprisings, rather than shared underlying conditions?

Suggestion: Consider adding a paragraph with concrete historical evidence—dates, specific events, perhaps brief mention of Solidarity in Poland or the Velvet Revolution in Czechoslovakia—to substantiate your causal claim rather than asserting it.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Dashes: You use dashes (--) extensively where periods, semicolons, or colons would be more appropriate. This creates run-on sentences and makes the prose feel breathless throughout. Revise these into proper sentence boundaries.

Spelling/Typos: "mesmirized" → mesmerized; "Tchekoslovaquia" → Czechoslovakia; "Roumania" → Romania.

Pronoun inconsistency: You refer to your classmate as "he" and then "her father" in the same paragraph. Clarify the gender throughout.

Awkward phrasing: "Without the 'events of Berlin Wall' history probably would not have taken a very different course" is confusingly worded—likely a typo, but as written, it says the opposite of what you probably mean (that history *would* have taken a different course). Revise for clarity.

"our now as-President Bush": This phrasing is unclear. If you mean Bush was president *then*, that needs rewording; if you're distinguishing from a later Bush presidency, clarify further.

Strengthening the Argument

To move this from a reflective narrative toward a persuasive essay, consider:

Explicitly stating your thesis early.
Providing historical evidence (specific events, dates, quotations from historians or leaders) to support the causal claim that the Wall's fall triggered the broader collapse of communism.
Addressing counterarguments—your own concession that the outcome was "eventually" inevitable is essentially a counterargument. Engage with it directly: perhaps argue that even if collapse was inevitable, the *timing and speed* were significantly shaped by the Wall's fall, which matters historically.
Balancing personal narrative with historical analysis. Your anecdotes are compelling and give the essay heart, but they should serve your argument rather than stand alone. Consider trimming or reframing them so each ties directly back to your central claim.

Final Thoughts

You have real material here—an evocative memory, a clear stance, and a meaningful historical claim. Revising for structural clarity, resolving the tension in your causal argument, and cleaning up sentence mechanics will transform this into a much stronger piece of academic argumentation. I'd encourage you to draft a one-sentence thesis statement now, and then check whether every paragraph explicitly serves that sentence.

Feedback on "The Television" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an engaging topic—arguing that television is the most transformative invention of the twentieth century—and you clearly have ideas worth developing. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of loosely connected observations than a sustained argument. The feedback below focuses on strengthening your thesis, tightening your structure, and cleaning up language issues so your argument lands with the force it deserves.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction promises a comparative claim: television "stands out" among many twentieth-century inventions. This is a strong, debatable thesis—but you undercut it with hedging language ("Perhaps one does stand out *a little* more among its competition"). Academic argument requires confidence. Compare:

Weak: "Perhaps one does stand out a little more among its competition, however; that is the television."
Stronger: "Among these inventions, television stands out as the single most transformative, because it fundamentally altered how humans experience information, culture, and one another."

Once you commit to this claim, you need to actually defend it *comparatively*. You mention the garbage disposal and the A-bomb in your opening line, but you never return to them. Why is television more significant than the atomic bomb, an invention that reshaped global politics and warfare? Even a brief acknowledgment of this counterargument would make your case more persuasive and show critical thinking rather than assertion.

Structure and Development

Your second paragraph spends considerable space praising radio and newspapers as an effective "system that... worked." This is useful as a baseline, but it should more explicitly set up the *contrast* you draw in paragraph three. Right now, the transition into television's advantages ("giving the public a 'peek' at what radio wasn't able to give them") is clear, but the argument itself—that images "enhance absorption"—needs development. *How* does seeing an event change understanding, versus hearing it? Give a concrete example (a specific broadcast, a specific historical moment) rather than general claims.

Paragraph four offers good examples (a president's speech, a mother watching her son's Olympic race), but these examples are asserted rather than analyzed. After each example, ask yourself: *why does this matter?* What does it reveal about human connection, democracy, or globalization that wouldn't be possible otherwise? Right now you say "the examples could be endless," which reads as a shortcut around deeper analysis rather than an invitation to more evidence.

Paragraph five introduces a significant shift: from celebrating television to warning about its power to "persuade" and needing "control." This is a legitimate and interesting complication, but it arrives without warning and isn't tied back to your thesis. Is this a caveat to your argument, or a separate point? Consider signaling this shift explicitly ("Yet this same power that unites the world also carries risks...") so readers understand how it fits your overall claim.

Conclusion

The final line—"it is necessary to remember to keep the vision in television"—is a clever wordplay, but it's too vague to serve as a real conclusion. What does "keep the vision" mean concretely? Restate your thesis with the weight of everything you've argued, and give the reader a clear sense of what's at stake if we don't "control" this technology.

Style and Language

A few stylistic habits weaken your prose:

Hedging and filler phrases: "Perhaps," "for the most part," "one way or another" soften your claims. Cut these where you actually mean what you're saying.
Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "A system that seemed to work, many households throughout the nation owned or had access to a radio..." This sentence needs a subject and verb, or should be joined with a semicolon or conjunction.
Word choice: "peak" should be "peek"; consider replacing informal touches like scare-quotes around "understand" with a clearer explanation of what you mean.

Mechanics and Proofreading

There are several spelling and typographical errors that need correction before submission:

"vertually" → virtually
"boarders" → borders
"absorbtion" → absorption
"spening" → spending
"necesary" → necessary
"olympic" → Olympic (capitalize)
"one lives" → "one's life" or "our lives"
"television strength" → "television's strength" (missing possessive apostrophe)

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a spell-checker plus a careful manual read-through, since several of these are the kind of errors that spell-check alone won't catch (eg, "boarders" vs. "borders").

Priorities for Revision

Strengthen your thesis—commit to the claim and briefly address why television outranks other inventions.
Deepen your examples—move from listing instances to analyzing their significance.
Integrate the counterargument in paragraph five more smoothly into your overall structure.
Proofread carefully for spelling, punctuation, and sentence boundaries.

With these revisions, this essay has real potential to make a compelling, well-supported argument.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on the Computer's Impact

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious and relevant topic—the transformative role of the computer in the information age—and you demonstrate genuine enthusiasm for the subject. However, the essay currently reads more as a descriptive history of computer development than an argumentative essay. To meet the expectations of the assignment, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, strengthen your argumentative structure, and address counterarguments. Below are specific areas for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction states that the computer "has had a great impact" on how society and business handle information. This is a reasonable starting point, but it is *descriptive* rather than *argumentative*—it doesn't stake out a debatable position. An argumentative essay should assert a claim that a reasonable person might dispute. Consider revising your thesis to something more pointed, such as: "The personal computer has fundamentally restructured social and economic life by decentralizing access to information—a shift as significant as the printing press." This gives you something to *defend*, rather than simply *describe*.

Ask yourself: what is actually at stake in your argument? Is it that the computer has been beneficial overall? That it has increased inequality? That it has changed human cognition? Choose a specific, defensible claim and build every paragraph around supporting it.

Structure and Paragraph Organization

Currently, your essay follows a chronological narrative (history of computing → invention of the PC → modern developments → conclusion). This structure suits a historical overview but doesn't allow you to build a sustained argument. Consider reorganizing around claims rather than chronology. For example:
Paragraph 2: Claim about accessibility/democratization of information
Paragraph 3: Claim about efficiency/productivity in business
Paragraph 4: Claim about the acceleration of daily life
Paragraph 5: Counterargument and rebuttal (see below)

Each paragraph should open with a clear topic sentence stating the claim, followed by evidence and analysis—not just narrative description of "what happened next" in computing history.

Evidence and Specificity

Much of your essay relies on generalizations ("many advances," "more sophisticated," "much more quickly") without concrete evidence. An argumentative essay is strengthened by specific, verifiable support:
Statistics: How many households owned computers in 1980 versus 2000?
Named examples: Which specific software or applications changed business practices (eg, spreadsheets like VisiCalc or Lotus 1-2-3)?
Expert testimony: Do any scholars, historians of technology, or business analysts support your claims?
Fact-check: Note that IBM's PC was released in 1981, not 1980, and personal computers existed before IBM's entry (eg, Apple II, 1977). Verify all historical claims before submission, as factual errors undermine your credibility.

Counterargument

A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections. Right now, your essay presents an entirely one-sided, celebratory view of computing. Consider addressing potential counterpoints: Has the computer increased inequality between those with and without access? Has it created information overload rather than clarity? Does constant connectivity (laptops, modems, fax machines) blur healthy boundaries between work and personal life? Even a brief paragraph acknowledging and responding to such concerns will make your argument more persuasive and intellectually honest.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Several grammatical issues need attention:
"There has been many inventions" → should be "there have been many inventions" (subject-verb agreement with plural noun).
"recieve" → "receive" (spelling).
"with the use of Modem" → "modem" should not be capitalized unless part of a proper name.
Comma splices and inconsistent punctuation appear throughout (eg, "everyday information. The computer however in this..."—consider restructuring for clarity).
You shift between second person ("you can sit...") and third person ("society," "one's own home"). Choose a consistent voice—third person is generally preferred in formal academic writing.

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool to catch these issues, then doing a manual review afterward, as automated tools don't catch everything.

Conclusion

Your conclusion reiterates your introduction without significantly advancing new insight or a call to reflection. Consider ending with a stronger synthesis: what should the reader *do* with this argument? Should they be excited about future computing developments, or wary of overdependence? A conclusion should feel like a culmination of your argument, not a restatement of the introduction.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis into a debatable, specific claim.
Reorganize paragraphs around arguments, not chronology.
Add concrete evidence—statistics, named technologies, expert sources.
Fact-check historical details (eg, IBM PC release date).
Include a counterargument and rebuttal.
Proofread for grammar, spelling, and consistent point of view.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a well-intentioned narrative into a compelling, well-supported argument. You have good instincts about the subject's importance—now it's time to make a specific, defensible case for *why* it matters.

Feedback on "The Century of Democracy" Essay

Overall Impression

This essay captures a genuine, personal enthusiasm for a historical moment, and that voice is one of its strengths. However, as an *argumentative* essay, it currently reads more like a reflective journal entry. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen the thesis, deepen the evidence, and address the counterarguments that a claim this large inevitably invites.

Thesis and Scope of the Argument

Your title and final line of the introduction promise a defense of the entire 20th century as "The Century of Democracy." But the body of your essay focuses almost exclusively on events from 1989–1992 (the fall of the Berlin Wall, Soviet transition, recent U.S. elections). This creates a mismatch between your claim and your evidence.

Consider one of two revisions:
Narrow the thesis to focus specifically on the *late* 20th century or the post-Cold War moment, which is what you actually discuss.
Broaden the evidence to trace democracy's development across the century—perhaps referencing decolonization movements, the expansion of suffrage, post-WWII democratization in Germany and Japan, or the civil rights movement—so that your claim about the "century" is actually earned.

Right now, the essay's strongest material (fall of the Wall, Soviet collapse) supports a much more specific and interesting claim than the one you've stated.

Addressing Counterevidence

A claim this sweeping needs to reckon with the century's darker chapters: two world wars, the Holocaust, Stalinist and Maoist totalitarianism, apartheid, colonial violence, and the Cold War's proxy conflicts. You don't need to dwell on these at length, but acknowledging them—and explaining why the *trajectory* toward democracy still outweighs them, or why the century's end matters more than its middle—would make your argument far more persuasive and intellectually honest. Right now, the essay risks seeming one-sided because it only looks at recent, positive developments.

Development of Evidence

Each of your three body paragraphs (Berlin Wall, Soviet Union, end of Cold War) is quite short and largely descriptive rather than analytical. You tell us *what* happened, but not enough about *why* it matters to your thesis or *how* it connects to a broader pattern. For instance:

The Berlin Wall paragraph is mostly personal anecdote (buying a piece of the wall). This is a nice detail, but it should serve the argument, not replace it. What did the wall's fall *mean* for democracy globally, not just symbolically for you?
The Soviet Union paragraph raises "Marxism" as a foil to democracy, but doesn't explain what specifically changed in the "many unhappy people['s]" political life, or what challenges remain in that transition (this is worth acknowledging, since Soviet/Russian democratization was far from complete or guaranteed in 1992).
The final paragraph on arms sales and nuclear war is important but underdeveloped—only three sentences for a significant claim about global security.

Try expanding each paragraph with more specific evidence (dates, figures, named leaders or movements) and more explicit reasoning connecting the evidence to your thesis.

Structure

The introduction is long and drifts through several decades before arriving at your thesis. The "capricious" tour through the 1920s–1970s is engaging but doesn't set up your argument about democracy—flappers and automobiles aren't obviously related to political freedom. Consider trimming this survey or replacing it with an opening that gestures more directly toward *political* change across the century, so the introduction earns its way to your thesis rather than arriving at it somewhat abruptly.

The three "Perhaps..." sentences are a nice rhetorical device, but the personal-patriotism paragraph that follows them (the "ethnocentric idealism" section) interrupts the momentum toward your main argument. Consider whether this material could be tightened or moved.

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates the essay's sentiment but doesn't add new insight or resolve the tension between the recent, celebratory tone and the complexity of "democracy" as a global project. Consider ending with a more concrete forward-looking claim: what specifically should readers watch for as evidence that democracy continues to spread, or what obstacles most threaten it?

Style and Word Choice

"A very capricious decade" — reconsider whether "capricious" is the right word for the 1920s; it usually describes unpredictable *people* or moods, not eras.
"Marxism" should be capitalized.
The phrase "the superpower among superpowers" is vivid but slightly redundant—consider a sharper phrase.
Watch for comma splices and slightly informal transitions ("No, I'm not engulfed...").

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Align your thesis with your actual evidence (narrow or broaden).
Acknowledge and respond to counterevidence from earlier in the century.
Deepen analysis in each body paragraph—move beyond narration to argument.
Tighten the introduction so it builds more directly toward your claim.
Proofread for word choice and capitalization.

You have real passion and a compelling personal vantage point (writing from abroad, during a historic moment)—use that energy to drive a more rigorously argued essay.

Feedback Report: Atomic Energy Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an important and complex topic with genuine passion, and that passion comes through clearly. However, the essay would benefit significantly from tighter argumentation, more precision in its claims, and a clearer distinction between its key terms. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Content and Argument

Conflating nuclear weapons and nuclear energy. This is the essay's most significant conceptual problem. The essay opens with the atomic bomb (a weapon) but then pivots to discussing nuclear power plants (energy generation). These are related but distinct technologies with different risks, benefits, and policy implications. A reader can't tell whether your argument is "nuclear weapons are too dangerous to exist" or "nuclear power plants are too dangerous to use" — and the evidence you'd need for each claim is different. Choose one focus, or explicitly justify treating them together.

Factual accuracy. Oppenheimer did not "unveil" the bomb to the world in 1945 in the sense implied here — the Manhattan Project was secret, the bomb was developed by a large team (not a solo "invention" of Oppenheimer's), and it was used on Hiroshima and Nagasaki by military decision, not revealed as a public demonstration. This historical framing needs correction, as it undermines your credibility on the topic.

Unsupported claims. Several strong assertions are made without evidence:
"the world's need for this dangerous energy augments" — augments compared to what? Cite data on global nuclear energy usage or growth.
"we have many other power source alternatives" — which ones? Are they currently sufficient to replace nuclear power at scale? This claim needs support, since it's central to your argument that continued nuclear use is a *choice* rather than a necessity.
The link between nuclear energy and "holes in our ozone layer" is scientifically inaccurate — ozone depletion is caused by chemicals like CFCs, not nuclear plants. This kind of error will seriously undermine your argument's credibility with an informed reader.

The conclusion doesn't match the argument. The essay builds toward an environmental catastrophe narrative but ends with "write your local congressman" — a vague, low-stakes call to action that doesn't correspond to the scale of the crisis you've described. If the problem is as urgent as you claim, what should readers actually ask their representatives to do? Support a specific policy? Oppose a specific plant or bill? Specificity here would make your conclusion far more persuasive and actionable.

Structure

The essay currently moves: bomb → power plants → pollution/ozone → leaders' inaction → despair → call to action. Consider reorganizing around a clearer thesis stated in the introduction (eg, "Continued reliance on nuclear energy poses unacceptable environmental risks that world leaders have failed to address") and then organize body paragraphs around distinct, well-supported points: (1) the risks of nuclear energy specifically, (2) available alternatives, (3) the failure of political leadership, (4) a concrete call to action.

Style and Tone

The essay relies heavily on emotional appeals and hyperbole ("most awesome as well as terrible invention... of all time," "The saddest part of this tragedy," "almost appalling"). While emotional resonance has its place in argumentative writing, over-reliance on it without corresponding evidence can make the argument feel more like a rant than a persuasive case. Consider balancing emotional language with concrete facts, statistics, or expert testimony.

Also, watch for inconsistent register — the essay shifts between formal analysis and casual, almost sermon-like address ("what is to become of our children," "It starts with you"). Decide on a consistent voice for your intended audience.

Grammar and Mechanics

Several issues need cleaning up:
"the the most awesome" — repeated word.
"Atomic energy" and "Atomic bomb" are inconsistently and unnecessarily capitalized (these are not proper nouns).
"routed in" should be "rooted in."
"It is considered" — the capital "It" mid-sentence after a comma is an error; check punctuation here (this may need to be a separate sentence or lowercase "it").
"irreversable" should be "irreversible."
Several sentences run long or use comma splices; reading each paragraph aloud will help you catch these.
"what is to become of our children" should end with a question mark.

Suggestions for Revision

Narrow your focus to either nuclear weapons or nuclear energy (or clearly justify addressing both).
Fact-check historical and scientific claims, especially about Oppenheimer and ozone depletion.
Add concrete evidence: statistics on nuclear energy use, specific alternative energy sources, examples of leadership failures or successes.
Strengthen the conclusion with a specific, proportionate call to action.
Proofread carefully for capitalization, spelling, and punctuation errors.

With these revisions, your essay's evident passion can be matched by the rigor needed to persuade a skeptical reader.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Television

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and defensible topic—the argument that television is the most significant communication invention of the 20th century—and you clearly have real insight into its effects (the Vietnam War example, the "shrinking the globe" idea). However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of related observations than a tightly argued case. With some revision to structure, development, and clarity of claims, this could become a much stronger piece. Below are the main areas to work on.

Sharpen and Clarify Your Thesis

Your central claim is buried and stated ambiguously. You write that television is "the most frequently used and influential invention of the field," but you never clearly define what "the field" is (communication technology in general? visual media specifically?). You also introduce a concession in the first paragraph ("There are, however, several downsides...") without telling the reader how that concession relates to your overall argument. Does the existence of downsides weaken your claim that television is the most significant invention, or do you believe its significance outweighs its costs? Right now, the reader isn't sure what you're actually trying to prove.

Revision suggestion: Write a single, clear thesis sentence near the end of your introduction that states your claim and previews your reasons. For example: "Despite its potential for misuse, television has done more than any other 20th-century invention to transform how people understand and respond to world events, because it compresses distance, time, and emotional immediacy into a single image." This gives your reader a roadmap and gives you a standard to organize each paragraph around.

Improve Paragraph Organization and Topic Sentences

Each paragraph currently drifts between multiple ideas. Paragraph 2, for instance, starts with a strange qualifying clause ("Not even truly dealing with...") and then moves through television-in-war, protests, Somalia, and radio versus television—all without a clear topic sentence to unify them. Paragraph 3 similarly shifts from "shrinking the globe" to "communication power" to "desensitization" to a restated conclusion, all in one paragraph.

Revision suggestion: Give each paragraph one clear job. For example:
Paragraph A: Television's power to bring distant events into immediate, visual reality (with the Vietnam and Somalia examples fully developed).
Paragraph B: The concern about desensitization or "unreality" of the image (developed as a real counterargument, not just mentioned in passing).
Paragraph C: Why, despite this drawback, television's benefits outweigh the risk.

This kind of one-idea-per-paragraph structure will make your argument much easier to follow.

Develop Your Evidence More Fully

Your examples (Vietnam, Somalia) are excellent choices—but you introduce them and then move on before explaining *how* they support your argument. For instance: *why* did seeing the war on television lead to protest? What specifically changed in public opinion? Similarly, what happened in Somalia that illustrates "public concern and effort"? Right now these are references rather than developed evidence. A reader unfamiliar with the specifics won't be persuaded.

Revision suggestion: For each example, add 2-3 sentences that explain the mechanism: what people saw, how it made them feel, what real-world action followed. This turns a passing reference into actual support for your claim.

Take the Counterargument Seriously

You mention that television "can be used in a useless way," that it "can be a harmful thing," and that it can "desensitize" viewers—but each of these is stated in a single vague sentence and dropped. If you want to write a strong argumentative essay, you should engage with the counterargument seriously: What specifically is the danger? (Overconsumption? Manipulation by broadcasters? Emotional numbness to violence?) Then explain why, despite this, your thesis still holds. Right now the concessions feel like hedges rather than genuine engagement, which weakens the essay's persuasive force.

Revise the Conclusion

Your final paragraph mostly repeats earlier claims (globe-shrinking, communication power, desensitization) without adding a new synthesis or insight. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis with fresh perspective—perhaps by considering implications for the future, or by directly answering the "so what" question: why does it matter that television has had this effect?

Editing and Style Notes

Capitalize "Vietnam."
Watch for wordy, roundabout phrasing: "has to do with the speed at which information is now communicated" could simply be "concerns the speed of global communication."
"made smaller the dimensions of the planet" is awkward; consider "has shrunk the planet" or similar direct phrasing.
Avoid vague qualifiers like "in a way," "of course," and "either way"—they weaken your authority as a writer.
Proofread for subject-verb agreement (eg, "television had changed" should be "has changed").

Summary

Your ideas are genuinely interesting, and you have good instincts about historical examples. Focus your revision on (1) stating a clear, arguable thesis, (2) organizing paragraphs around single controlling ideas, (3) developing your examples with real explanatory detail, and (4) treating the counterargument as a serious point to be argued against, not just acknowledged. These changes will transform this from a loose set of observations into a focused, persuasive essay.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of the Airplane

Overall Impression

You've chosen a strong, defensible topic, and your personal anecdote about visiting Spain adds a nice touch of authenticity. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of loosely connected observations than a tightly argued essay. Below are some specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction gestures toward three claims: the airplane enabled travel, broadened perceptions of the world, and introduced the concept of flight itself. This is a lot of ground to cover in a short essay, and the third claim (the "concept of flying") is never actually developed in your body paragraphs—you focus entirely on travel and international relations. For an argumentative essay, you need a single, clear thesis that previews exactly what your body paragraphs will prove. Consider narrowing your focus to one main claim (eg, "the airplane's greatest impact was cultural and economic globalization") and cutting the flying motif if you don't plan to address it.

Also, an argumentative essay typically requires you to take a position that could be contested and then defend it against objections. Right now, your essay reads as expository (explaining effects) rather than argumentative (persuading a skeptical reader). You might strengthen this by acknowledging a counterpoint—for instance, that critics point to air travel's environmental costs, or that globalization has homogenized cultures rather than simply broadening them—and then responding to it.

Structure and Development

The essay has only three paragraphs and no conclusion. This is a significant structural gap: after building an argument about international relations, you need a paragraph that synthesizes your points and returns to your thesis, ideally showing why this matters (What should the reader now believe or understand?).

Your second body paragraph (on international relations) is underdeveloped compared to the first. It contains several strong claims—that the airplane sped up postal communication and increased international business and diplomacy—but these are asserted rather than supported. Ask yourself: *what evidence or example would show this?* A concrete case (a trade agreement, a diplomatic summit, the growth of a specific industry) would make this paragraph much more persuasive.

Use of Evidence

Your strongest moment is the personal anecdote about traveling to Spain—it's vivid and specific. However, personal experience alone is a fairly thin foundation for an argumentative essay. Consider supplementing it with historical or statistical evidence: How long did transatlantic sea travel take compared to flight? How many international flights occur annually? How has global trade volume changed since commercial aviation began? Even one or two concrete facts would substantially strengthen your credibility.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

A few recurring issues to address in your revision:

Word choice: "The extend to which" should be "The extent to which."
Redundancy: "extremely time consuming" appears twice in close proximity (paragraph 2). Vary your language or cut repetition.
Awkward/unclear phrasing: "The frequency that which foreign countries are able to communicate" is grammatically tangled—likely you mean "The frequency *with* which foreign countries are able to communicate and negotiate face to face has drastically improved the establishment of international trade." Watch for misplaced modifiers like this.
Vague phrasing: "has basically speeded up the whole postal communication system also" is informal and imprecise. Consider: "The airplane also dramatically accelerated postal and shipping systems, reducing delivery times from weeks to days." This is a good place to add a concrete comparison for support.
Colloquialisms: Words like "basically" and "also" (used twice in one sentence) should be trimmed in formal academic writing.
Fragment/run-on tendencies: The sentence "Since the invention of the airplane the world has increased close contact between political systems, and international business endeavors" has an unnecessary comma and slightly unclear logic (does "the world" increase contact, or do airplanes enable it?). Revise for clarity: "The invention of the airplane enabled closer contact between political systems and international businesses."

Suggested Revision Priorities

Narrow and clarify your thesis to one focused claim you can fully support in the space you have.
Add a conclusion that returns to your thesis and explains its broader significance.
Develop the international relations paragraph with specific evidence or examples.
Consider a counterargument to make this feel more like a true argumentative essay rather than a description of benefits.
Proofread carefully for word choice, redundancy, and sentence clarity—reading your paragraphs aloud can help you catch awkward phrasing.

With a tighter focus and more concrete support, this essay has real potential—your instincts about the airplane's transformative impact are sound; now the evidence and structure need to catch up to the argument.

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic—the role of education in combating AIDS when medical cures remain elusive—but the argument needs significant development to reach its full potential. Right now, the essay reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a sustained, evidence-based argument. Below, I've outlined the main areas for revision.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your implicit thesis—that education, not medicine alone, is our best current defense against AIDS—is a strong and debatable claim worth making. However, it's never stated explicitly or developed with the specificity a strong argumentative essay requires. Ask yourself: What exactly are you claiming? Some options to consider:

Should education be prioritized over continued medical research funding?
What specific educational interventions are most effective (school programs, media campaigns, community centers)?
What evidence exists that education actually reduces transmission rates?

State this thesis clearly in your introduction, and make sure every paragraph works to support it.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

The essay currently jumps between ideas without clear transitions or logical progression. For example:
Paragraph 2 introduces support centers, but paragraph 3 shifts abruptly to school assemblies without connecting the two.
Paragraph 4 discusses various risk groups, but this list feels disconnected from your education argument—how does *knowing* about diverse risk groups translate into *educational strategy*?
Paragraph 5 introduces a new claim (people having sex with strangers) without preparation.
Paragraph 6 ("Obviously, our culture has begun to educate its population or I would be unable to write about this particular topic") is confusing and doesn't advance your argument. This sentence seems to suggest that the mere existence of your essay is evidence of societal education, which is a weak and somewhat circular claim.

Suggestion: Try outlining your essay before revising. Each paragraph should have one clear function: (1) introduce the problem, (2) present evidence that education works, (3) address a counterargument, (4) propose specific solutions, (5) conclude with implications.

Development and Evidence

This is the essay's greatest weakness. You make several claims without supporting evidence:
"Due to the ineffectiveness of medicines"—what evidence supports this?
"Many schools have begun inviting victims of AIDS to speak"—cite a specific program, study, or statistic.
The claim that dentists "forget to put on their plastic gloves" is presented as a real risk factor but feels anecdotal and slightly flippant given the seriousness of your topic.

Suggestion: Research actual statistics on AIDS transmission rates, the effectiveness of specific educational programs (eg, school-based sex education, needle-exchange programs), and quotes from public health experts. This will transform your essay from opinion into argument.

Logical Consistency

Some claims need clarification or reconsideration:
The suicide/drug injection comparison is underdeveloped and somewhat confusing—are you saying drug users don't consider AIDS risk as "suicide risk"? This needs more explanation to make sense.
The claim that strangers having sex are "selling their lives short" is moralizing rather than analytical. Consider reframing this in terms of risk awareness and informed consent rather than judgment.

Style and Tone

For an academic argumentative essay, the tone needs to be more formal and precise:

Avoid text-message abbreviations: Spell out "with," "and," "when," etc (w/, &, etc are inappropriate in academic writing).
Avoid casual asides: The quotation marks around "forget to put on their plastic gloves" read as sarcastic, which undermines the seriousness of your topic.
Watch for moralizing language: Phrases like "selling their lives short" shift from argument to judgment, which can alienate readers.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few specific errors to correct:
"The AIDS viruses was discovered" — subject-verb agreement error; should be "virus was" or "viruses were."
"many peoples lives" — should be "many people's lives" (possessive apostrophe needed).
"interact sexual w/" — should be "interact sexually with."
Semicolon usage is inconsistent (eg, "Due to the ineffectiveness of medicines; the population...") — a semicolon should connect two independent clauses, not separate a dependent clause from an independent one. A comma would work better here.

Revision Priorities

Write a clear, specific thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one builds logically on the last.
Add concrete evidence—statistics, expert opinions, or specific program examples.
Eliminate informal abbreviations and revise for a consistently academic tone.
Proofread carefully for grammar and punctuation errors.

This essay has a worthwhile argument at its core. With more structure, evidence, and precision, you can make a compelling case for the power of education in the fight against AIDS. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay has real strengths: it's personal, engaging, and you clearly have genuine enthusiasm for your topic. Your voice comes through clearly, and the anecdotes about basketball and swimming effectively illustrate why contact lenses matter to you. However, to strengthen this as an *argumentative* essay, there are several areas to address.

Argument and Thesis

Your central claim—that contact lenses are one of the 20th century's most important inventions—is a strong, debatable thesis. However, the essay reads more as a personal narrative about your relationship with contacts than as an argument for their broader importance. Ask yourself: what would convince a skeptical reader that contact lenses deserve this ranking among all 20th-century inventions (penicillin, the internet, vaccines, etc)? Right now, your evidence is entirely personal. Consider incorporating:
Brief historical/factual context (when were contacts invented, how many people use them today)
Broader impact beyond your own convenience (professional athletes, people whose careers require good vision, safety benefits)
Acknowledgment of counterarguments (Why might someone say contacts *aren't* among the most important inventions? Then refute this.)

Structure

The essay currently moves chronologically through your personal history with contacts (hard lenses → soft lenses → disposables) rather than building a persuasive case. This is a good organizing structure for a personal narrative, but for an argumentative essay, consider organizing around *reasons* your thesis is true:
Contacts solve practical/safety problems glasses can't (sports, swimming)
Contacts have continuously improved through innovation (hard → soft → disposable)

This reorganization would let your personal examples serve as *evidence* for claims rather than being the whole story.

The Colored Lenses Paragraph

This paragraph works against your argument. Calling colored contacts "ridiculous" undercuts your thesis that contacts are one of the century's great inventions. If you want to keep this point, either:
Cut it, since it doesn't support your main claim, or
Reframe it positively (eg, discuss how this shows the technology's versatility and cultural impact) if you want to argue it's still evidence of importance

Tone and Word Choice

The exclamation points (there are several) and phrases like "the 'lense craze' caught rapidly" create a conversational, almost diary-like tone. For academic argumentative writing, aim for more measured, precise language. Save exclamation points for genuine emphasis.
"Glasses are a thing of the past" is an overstatement (millions still wear glasses) that could undermine your credibility. Consider tempering this or reframing as your own experience.

Mechanics and Style

A few specific issues to address:
Spelling: "lense" should be "lens" (plural: "lenses")
Comma splices/run-ons: "if I wore my glasses, they would end up on the floor and if I didn't wear them, I'd be more or less 'blind'" needs a comma before "and"
Subject-verb agreement: "another type of lenses were made" should be "was made" (type is singular)
Ampersands: You use "&" throughout, which is inappropriate for formal academic writing—spell out "and"
Fragment: "If glasses were my only form of seeing better, I'd be blind most of the time!" — this works as a sentence, but check that all your conditional constructions are complete
Excessive scare quotes: You use quotation marks frequently for emphasis ("blind," "lense craze," "sting," "feel my way"). Reserve quotation marks for actual quotations or specific terminology; otherwise, choose stronger word choices to convey your meaning.

Revision Priorities

If you're revising, focus on these three things first:
Broaden your evidence beyond personal experience to strengthen your argumentative claim
Reorganize by argument/reason rather than chronology
Polish mechanics—particularly the ampersands, comma splices, and informal punctuation

Your personal investment in this topic is an asset—readers can tell you care. Channel that enthusiasm into building a more persuasive case that could convince someone who doesn't already wear contacts. You have good raw material here; the revision work is about argumentative structure and academic tone.

Feedback on "Feminism as Discovery" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and provocative idea—framing feminism as a "discovery" akin to technological breakthroughs. This is a creative angle, but the essay currently reads more like a set of loosely connected observations than a fully developed argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your central claim—that feminism should be understood as a "discovery" comparable to cars, telephones, and nuclear energy—is intriguing, but you don't do enough work to justify the analogy itself. Why is this framing useful or illuminating? What does thinking of feminism as a "discovery" help us understand that we wouldn't otherwise see? Right now, the comparison feels more like a clever hook for your introduction than a idea you're actually exploring throughout the essay. I'd encourage you to return to this analogy explicitly in your body paragraphs—what has been "discovered," specifically, and by whom? Is feminism a discovery of a truth that already existed (like nuclear fission) or more like an invention (like the telephone) that constructs something new? This distinction matters and could give your essay real intellectual traction.

Structure and Development

The essay is quite short, and each paragraph introduces an idea without fully developing it. For instance:

Paragraph 2 raises several important points (women's rights history, the ERA, the wage gap) but treats each as a passing fact rather than examining its significance. Why did the ERA fail? What does that failure tell us about the "discovery" of feminism being incomplete? The wage gap statistic needs context—when is this figure from, and how does it relate to your broader argument?

Paragraph 3 shifts to the "image of women" (pornography, language), which is a different dimension of feminism than the economic/legal focus of paragraph 2. The transition between these two areas of feminist critique feels abrupt. Consider adding a sentence that explicitly connects material equality (wages, rights) to representational equality (images, language)—are these two sides of the same coin, or fundamentally different battles?

Paragraph 4 (your conclusion) restates the "discovery in progress" framing but doesn't add new insight. Right now it's somewhat circular: feminism will be "discovered" when women achieve equality, and it will have achieved equality when discovered. Try to end with a sharper, more specific claim—perhaps about what remains to be "discovered," or why this framing matters for how we approach feminism going forward.

Suggestion: This essay would benefit from at least one more body paragraph with a concrete, well-developed example. Right now you're citing statistics and historical events in passing; choose one (the ERA, the wage gap, or media representation) and analyze it in depth. Show your reader *how* it functions as evidence for your argument, rather than just listing it.

Factual Accuracy

Please double-check your historical claims:
U.S. women won the right to vote in 1920 (the 19th Amendment), not "the 1800s." This is an important date to get right, especially in an essay about the history of feminism.
The wage gap statistic ("60 cents for every dollar") should be sourced and dated—labor statistics shift over time, and vague phrasing ("something like") weakens your credibility. If you can't verify the exact figure, consider softening the claim or finding a citable source.

Style and Mechanics

"0th century" is a typo—should be "20th century."
Watch for comma splices and slightly informal phrasing (eg, "It's only recently that we've 'discovered'..."). Since this is an argumentative essay, aim for a more consistently formal register.
The scare quotes around "discovered" and "discovery" are used inconsistently. Decide early on whether you're treating "discovery" as a genuine analytical category or an ironic/skeptical one, and use the quotation marks purposefully rather than scattering them throughout.

Revision Priorities

Deepen the "discovery" analogy—make it work harder throughout the essay, not just in the opening and closing.
Expand and support your evidence—choose fewer examples and analyze them more thoroughly.
Fix factual errors, especially the suffrage date.
Strengthen transitions between the economic/legal and cultural/representational dimensions of feminism.
Sharpen the conclusion so it moves beyond restating the thesis.

This essay has a strong conceptual seed—the challenge now is to let it grow into a fuller, more rigorously argued piece. I look forward to seeing the revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: "Caring for the Environment"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and relevant topic, and your essay shows genuine engagement with environmental issues. The structure—introduction, several body paragraphs each addressing a subtopic, and a conclusion—is logical and easy to follow. However, the essay currently reads more like a list of related environmental concerns than a sustained argument. To strengthen it, focus on developing a clearer thesis, providing concrete evidence, and deepening your analysis of *why* this idea has been significant. I'll address these issues in turn, along with some notes on style and mechanics.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening claim—that environmental awareness is the most significant idea to change lives in the 20th century—is a strong, debatable thesis, which is exactly what an argumentative essay needs. However, the essay never really argues for this claim; it primarily *describes* aspects of environmentalism (nuclear waste, deforestation, recycling, energy conservation, animal rights) without explaining why this idea, specifically, has been more transformative than alternatives (eg, civil rights, technology, globalization). 

To make this a true argumentative essay, consider:
Directly acknowledging why someone might choose a different idea, then explaining why environmentalism surpasses it.
Explaining the *mechanism* of change—how did this idea alter behavior, law, or consciousness? Right now you assert change occurred ("we realize," "we are now") but don't show the causal link between the idea and specific life changes.

Structure and Development

Each paragraph introduces a subtopic (nuclear waste, deforestation, recycling, energy, animals) but stays at the level of general assertion. For example:
> "Many countries realize that recycling is not only beneficial to their economy it also is beneficial to their environment."

This is a claim, not evidence. Strengthen your paragraphs by adding:
Specific examples: name a country, policy, or event (eg, the Kyoto Protocol, the EPA's founding in 1970, Germany's recycling programs).
Data or statistics: even approximate figures (percentage of waste recycled, number of species endangered) would substantially increase credibility.
Historical contrast: you gesture toward "before and after" comparisons ("we used to assume...now we realize"), which is a good rhetorical move—but develop it with actual historical detail rather than vague generalization.

Also consider consolidating: five body paragraphs covering nuclear weapons, waste, deforestation, recycling, energy, and animal rights is a lot of ground for a short essay. You might combine related paragraphs (eg, recycling and energy conservation under "resource conservation") to allow deeper development of fewer points rather than shallow coverage of many.

Clarity and Logical Connections

Some sentences contain logical gaps or unclear connections. For instance:
> "We realize that conserving is not beneficial to our environment but it is also saves money."

This sentence seems to have a typo reversing your intended meaning (you likely meant "conserving *is* beneficial... but *also* saves money"). Double-check sentences like this for accidental negations, which can confuse readers about your actual argument.

Similarly, the aside about recycling helping "those w/o homes" is an interesting point but feels disconnected from your main argument about environmental awareness reshaping the century—clarify its relevance or consider removing it if it distracts from your central point.

Style and Register

For an academic essay, avoid text-message abbreviations such as "&," "w/o," "b/c," and "2 issues." Spell these out fully ("and," "without," "because," "two issues"). This is a simple fix but essential for maintaining a formal, credible tone.

Additionally, the essay would benefit from more precise word choice. "Considerable" in "we have also become more considerable of our animal life" should likely be "considerate." Watch for these near-miss word choices throughout.

Conclusion

Your conclusion admirably ties back to the introduction but ends on a vague, optimistic note ("hopeful by the 21st century we will have fixed everything we have destroyed!"). This slightly undercuts the seriousness of your argument. Consider ending with a more measured, analytical statement—perhaps a reflection on what remains to be done, or a final claim about *why* this cultural shift matters more than other 20th-century changes, tying back to your original thesis.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to explicitly argue why environmentalism is the most transformative idea, not merely describe its manifestations.
Add concrete evidence—dates, events, statistics, named policies—to support each claim.
Consolidate paragraphs to allow deeper analysis rather than a broad but shallow survey.
Eliminate informal abbreviations and revise for register consistency.
Revise the conclusion to reinforce your argument with more analytical weight rather than general optimism.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a descriptive overview to a persuasive, well-supported argument. You have good instincts about structure and relevant content—now it's a matter of deepening the analysis and evidence behind each claim.

Feedback on Your Essay on Computers

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic with real potential, and your personal examples (checkbook balancing, word processing, the study-abroad application) are relatable and concrete. However, this essay reads more like a personal reflection or descriptive narrative than an *argumentative* essay. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your central claim, tighten your organization, and clean up mechanical errors that undermine your credibility as a writer.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your thesis—"computers have definitely affected people's lives"—is not really arguable. Almost no reader would disagree with this claim, which means there's no real debate for you to engage in. An argumentative essay needs a claim that could be contested, one that requires evidence and reasoning to defend.

Ask yourself: What is the *specific* claim you want to make about computers? Some possibilities:
Computers have fundamentally changed how we manage personal responsibility and time (fewer errors, more efficiency).
Computers have made administrative/bureaucratic systems more reliable, but this reliability comes with hidden costs (privacy, dependency, etc).
Despite conveniences, our reliance on computers has created new vulnerabilities.

Notice that the second and third options invite complexity and even a counterargument—something your current draft lacks entirely. A stronger essay would acknowledge a potential drawback or objection (data loss, privacy concerns, digital divide, technical failures) and then explain why your claim still holds. This will make your argument feel earned rather than assumed.

Structure and Organization

Your essay currently has two paragraphs organized loosely around *chronology* (before I had a computer / now that I do) and *location* (my life at home / my life in Madrid), rather than around a logical argument. Consider restructuring around your *claim* and supporting *reasons*, each with its own paragraph. For example:

Introduction: State your specific, arguable thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: Computers increase personal efficiency (checkbook, word processing).
Body Paragraph 2: Computers streamline institutional processes (study abroad application, ticket purchasing).
Body Paragraph 3 (optional but recommended): Address a counterargument or complication (eg, dependency, error when systems fail, unequal access).
Conclusion: Return to your thesis with a fuller, more informed statement of its significance.

The Madrid paragraph, in particular, needs work. You *assume* your application was processed by a computer, but you don't know this for certain, and the reasoning here ("if it weren't for computers, things would be lost") is somewhat speculative. Try to find a moment where you can speak with more certainty about a specific technological process, rather than guessing at what "probably" happened behind the scenes.

Development and Evidence

Your best moments are your most concrete ones—the checkbook example and the word-processing example. These work because they contrast a *specific before-state* with a *specific after-state*. Apply this same precision to your other examples. The claim about your father buying tickets online, for instance, could be expanded: How long would this have taken by phone or in person? What did the computer allow him to see or compare that he couldn't before? Specificity is what makes an argument persuasive, not general statements like "the power of computers is incomprehensible."

Consider also using a source outside personal experience—even a brief historical fact about pre-digital record-keeping, or a statistic about processing speed—to give your claims more authority than they currently have. Right now the essay relies entirely on personal anecdote, which limits its persuasive reach.

Style and Mechanics

This draft needs a full proofreading pass before it's submission-ready. Specific issues to address:

"effected" should be "affected" in your first sentence (effect as a verb means to bring something about; affect means to influence).
Abbreviations like "w/" and "&" are appropriate for notes but not for formal academic writing. Spell out "with" and "and" throughout.
Apostrophe errors: "peoples lives" should be "people's lives"; "my life has change" should be "has changed."
Run-on sentences and comma splices appear frequently, especially in the first paragraph. Break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Inconsistent verb tense: you shift between past and present tense somewhat unpredictably (eg, "I had to balance" vs. "I'm now here in Madrid").
"form the US" should be "from the US."

Revision Priorities

If you revise this essay, focus first on narrowing your thesis into something genuinely arguable, then reorganize around that argument rather than chronology. Once the structural and logical bones are solid, do a careful line-edit pass to fix the mechanical issues. Your ideas and examples show promise—the task now is to give them a sharper argumentative frame and a more polished presentation.
