Feedback on Your Essay on Satellite Technology

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and specific angle—the "nowness" that satellites provide—and the Tiananmen Square example is a strong, evocative piece of evidence. However, the essay currently reads as a single unbroken paragraph with an underdeveloped argument, several grammatical errors, and shifts into informal language that undercut its persuasive power. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening claim—"the geo-centric satellite is the invention of the century"—is a bold, sweeping statement that needs more support to be credible. "Of the century" invites comparison to other transformative inventions (the internet, antibiotics, the automobile), and you don't address why satellites outrank these. Consider narrowing your claim: rather than arguing satellites are *the* most important invention, argue specifically that satellite technology's real-time global broadcasting capability has had a unique influence on political accountability and human rights awareness. This is closer to what your evidence (Tiananmen Square) actually supports, and it will make your argument more defensible.

Also, be careful with causal claims. You assert that "without the satellite, the movement for democracy... would have been lost due to the stagnation of time" and that global demands for China's government to change "would not have occurred." These are strong causal claims presented without evidence. Even if plausible, you need to acknowledge alternative explanations (print journalism, word of mouth, diplomatic channels) or soften the claim ("may not have gathered the same momentum").

Structure and Organization

Right now the essay is one long paragraph that tries to do too much: establish importance, explain "nowness," give the Tiananmen example, speculate about the future, and conclude. Break this into distinct paragraphs:

Introduction – State your thesis clearly and specifically.
Background paragraph – Explain what makes satellite broadcasting different from radio, TV, and videotape (the "nowness" point).
Evidence paragraph – Develop the Tiananmen Square example fully, with more concrete detail (what happened, how images spread, what effect this had internationally).
Implications paragraph – Discuss the argument about accountability and the future ("governments...will be less tempted to do wrong").
Conclusion – Synthesize your argument; avoid introducing a brand-new metaphor here (see below).

Right now these ideas are compressed into single sentences, which prevents you from fully developing your reasoning.

Evidence and Development

The Tiananmen Square reference is your strongest piece of evidence, but it needs more development. Right now you assert its significance rather than showing it. Consider adding:
A specific detail about how footage was transmitted and viewed internationally.
A concrete example of international response (a specific government statement, UN discussion, economic sanction) rather than the vague "instantaneous demands of the world community."
One additional example beyond Tiananmen Square would strengthen the argument that this is a pattern, not an isolated case.

You should also consider addressing a counterargument: satellite surveillance and constant global visibility can also enable government control, propaganda, or invasions of privacy. Even a brief acknowledgment would make your argument feel more balanced and credible.

Style and Tone

The essay shifts unpredictably between formal argumentation and casual asides. Notable examples:
"Freedom US. the tank of injustice" — this phrase is unclear (is "US." meant to be "vs."?) and the metaphor feels forced. Revise for clarity.
"Plus when China is placed down the block, who wouldn't want to help his neighbor?" — This final line is too colloquial for an academic essay and introduces a new, underdeveloped metaphor right at the conclusion, where you should be reinforcing your thesis, not introducing new ideas.

Aim for consistent academic register throughout. Rhetorical questions and colloquialisms can work in argumentative writing, but they need to be used deliberately and sparingly, not as last-minute flourishes.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are numerous errors to address in your next draft:
Spelling: "evention" → *invention*; "preceed" → *precede*; "openned" → *opened*; "litterally" → *literally*; "Teniman Square" → *Tiananmen Square*; "sensorship" → *censorship*; "mor" → *more*.
Subject-verb agreement: "other evention of great importance preceed" → *other inventions of great importance precede*; "none of these invention possess" → *inventions possess*.
Word choice/typo: "now the world functions" → *how the world functions*.
Fragment: "As it happens- the most profound byproduct of the satellite." This is not a complete sentence; integrate it into the previous sentence or rewrite.
Punctuation: Semicolons or periods, rather than dashes, would clarify sentences like "Truth before sensorship- this is the biggest advantage..."

Next Steps

Narrow and clarify your thesis.
Break the essay into clear paragraphs, each with a focused topic sentence.
Develop your Tiananmen Square evidence with more specific detail.
Address at least one counterargument.
Revise the conclusion to reinforce your thesis rather than introduce new metaphors.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

You have a promising core idea here—focus on making your evidence and reasoning as strong as your central insight about "nowness."

Feedback Report

Your essay tackles an important and relevant topic—the influence of computers on institutions and education—and you've organized it with a clear structure (introduction, body paragraphs on different themes, conclusion). However, the essay needs substantial revision in several areas: thesis clarity, argumentative depth, evidence, and editing. Below is detailed feedback organized by category.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction gestures toward a thesis but doesn't commit to one. You mention that computers affect "companies" and "students," but you don't state *what kind* of influence this is, or why it matters. Is your claim that computers have been an unambiguous good? That they've transformed specific practices? That they raise new questions worth considering?

Revision suggestion: State a clear, arguable thesis. For example: "Computers have fundamentally transformed how institutions manage information and how students learn, making processes faster and more accessible—though these benefits come with their own tradeoffs." Right now, your essay reads more like a descriptive list than an argument. Ask yourself: what am I trying to persuade my reader to believe or understand?

Development and Evidence

This is the essay's biggest weakness. Each paragraph makes claims but doesn't support them with real evidence, examples, or reasoning. Compare these two versions:

*Current:* "In hospitals and law firms where the paperwork was endless, much of it is now able to be filed into a neater, easily accessible, computer system."
*Improved:* "Hospitals now use electronic health records systems, which allow doctors to instantly access a patient's full medical history rather than waiting for paper files to be retrieved—reducing treatment delays and errors."

Notice how the second version gives a *specific* example and explains *why* it matters (the outcome). Throughout your essay, look for places where you assert something and ask: "How do I know this? Can I give a concrete example, statistic, or scenario?"

The paragraph on computers as "teaching instruments" is especially thin—it mentions Biology and math ("cosm graph," which I believe you mean "cosmology" or perhaps "graphing"?) but doesn't explain *how* computers aid learning in these areas. Give the reader something to visualize.

Counterargument

Right now, the essay only presents the positive side of computer technology. Strong argumentative essays acknowledge complexity. Consider: Are there downsides to this shift? Does reliance on spell-check reduce students' own spelling skills? Does digitized record-keeping raise privacy concerns? Even briefly addressing a counterpoint (and responding to it) would make your argument more persuasive and sophisticated.

Paragraph on Student Writing

This paragraph makes a claim that deserves more scrutiny: relying on computers to "fix" spelling errors isn't the same as learning to write well. You might complicate this point—yes, computers save time, but does that time savings come at any cost? This would also set up a nice opportunity for counterargument (see above).

Conclusion

Your conclusion is very short and ends with a vague, unearned flourish ("who knows where these machines will take us"). A strong conclusion should synthesize your argument, not just gesture at the future. Return to your thesis and show what you've demonstrated, then—if appropriate—suggest a forward-looking implication that's grounded in what you've actually argued.

Sentence-Level Issues

There are several errors that need correction:

"definantly" → definitely
"god send" → godsend
"accesible" → accessible
"previosly" → previously
"The extend" → The extent
"but they is clearly imp" → "but they are clearly important" (this sentence needs to be completed/fixed)
"cosm graph" → unclear; please clarify what term you mean
"w/o" → spell out "without" in formal writing
Comma splice: "computers are revolutionary. Students no longer..." — check punctuation throughout for comma splices and run-ons.

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, sentence by sentence, to catch these errors—many become obvious when heard rather than read silently.

Priority for Revision

If you only have time to address a few things, focus on:
Sharpening your thesis so readers know your specific argument.
Adding concrete evidence and examples to every paragraph.
Proofreading carefully for spelling and grammar.

You have a solid foundation and a topic with real potential—now it's a matter of deepening the analysis and tightening the prose. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay: Television as a Transformative Invention

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling topic and have clear enthusiasm for your argument about television's impact on global communication and culture. Your writing shows genuine engagement with the "global village" concept. However, the essay would benefit from tighter structural organization, more developed reasoning, and cleaner sentence mechanics. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis—that television has significantly changed lives—is clear, but it operates on two fairly distinct claims: (1) TV revolutionized news/information access, and (2) TV serves an important entertainment/educational function. Right now these feel like two separate essays loosely connected by the word "television." Consider:

Narrowing your focus to one claim and developing it more deeply, or
Explicitly connecting the two claims in your introduction so readers understand how they work together to support a single, unified argument (eg, "Television reshapes both what we know and how we understand ourselves and others").

Structure and Paragraphing

Your first paragraph is doing too much work—it introduces the invention, explains the news function, argues for the "global village" idea, AND gestures toward cultural exchange. This paragraph should likely be split. Consider this structure:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly.
Body Paragraph 1: TV and news/information (the "distortion vs. immediacy" argument).
Body Paragraph 2: TV and shared culture (the "global village" concept—give this its own space, since it's essentially your thesis in miniature).
Body Paragraph 3: TV and entertainment/moral education.
Conclusion: Synthesize these threads and restate significance.

Currently, the "global village" idea appears as an afterthought (a short, one-sentence paragraph) when it might actually be your most powerful concluding insight for the *first half* of the essay.

Development of Ideas

Several claims need more support or explanation:

The claim about reduced distortion: You argue that eyewitness viewing eliminates "interpretation & distortion," but this is debatable—television broadcasts are edited, framed, and often biased. Acknowledging this complexity (even briefly) would strengthen your credibility rather than weaken your argument.
The Spain/California/Japan example: This is a good start, but underdeveloped. *How* does watching people in other places lead to real cultural understanding, rather than superficial stereotypes? Consider a concrete example (a specific news event, or a specific show) to ground this claim.
The entertainment paragraph's counter-argument: You raise a strong point—that TV glorifies violence and unrealistic outcomes—but your rebuttal ("this sense of reality is not so distorted... that people are going around acting like Superman") is asserted rather than argued. Why isn't it distorted? What evidence or reasoning supports this? This is actually a rich area for development—perhaps citing research on media effects, or reasoning through *how* viewers develop critical distance from fiction.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are several run-on sentences and comma splices that obscure your ideas. For example:

> "While previously people had to wait days, or even weeks for news (which consisted of second-or third-hand accounts, which always involved much interpretation & distortion) now they can see it happening, for themselves, as it is happening, this allows them to make their own, informed judgment about the event..."

This sentence tries to hold too many clauses together. Break it into two or three sentences for clarity:

> *Previously, people waited days or even weeks for news, which often arrived as secondhand accounts full of interpretation and distortion. Now, people can watch events unfold in real time and form their own informed judgments.*

Also watch for:
Inconsistent punctuation with dashes and semicolons (eg, "such as the gathering of opposition to a certain law -- it can be made immediately")
The ampersand (&) should be spelled out as "and" in formal academic writing.
"Thru" should be "through."
Some sentences shift verb tense or lose subject-verb agreement in the rush to add clauses.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph currently functions as a second body paragraph rather than a true conclusion—it introduces a new counter-argument (violence in TV) without stepping back to unify your overall argument. Consider adding a genuine concluding paragraph that:

Briefly revisits both main threads (news/information and entertainment)
Explains their combined significance for your "global village" thesis
Perhaps gestures toward implications (eg, media literacy, critical viewing habits) rather than simply reassuring the reader that people aren't imitating Superman.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Split your first paragraph and give the "global village" concept its own developed space.
Strengthen your entertainment counter-argument with actual reasoning, not just assertion.
Fix run-on sentences and inconsistent punctuation throughout.
Write a conclusion that synthesizes rather than introduces new material.
Consider adding one concrete, specific example (a real broadcast, show, or event) to ground your abstract claims.

With these revisions, you'll have a tighter, more persuasive essay that fully delivers on your compelling central insight about television's transformative power.

Feedback on "The Microwave" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging topic and show genuine enthusiasm for your subject. The essay has a clear central claim—that the microwave represents a revolutionary break from culinary tradition—and you develop this through two main lines of reasoning: its technological novelty (no fire) and its practical benefit (time-saving). However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of argumentation, organization, and mechanics before it will be persuasive to an academic reader.

Argument and Development

Strengthen your claims with evidence. Right now, your essay relies heavily on assertion rather than support. You claim the microwave "revolutionized a science," but you don't define what you mean by "science" here, nor do you cite any sources—historians of technology, sociologists, or data on adoption rates—to substantiate claims like "our society has embraced this eliminator of wasted hours." Academic essays need evidence: statistics on time saved, historical sources on pre-microwave cooking, or scholarly commentary on domestic technology's social impact.

Address counterarguments. An essay arguing for revolutionary significance should anticipate objections. Some readers might argue the microwave hasn't fundamentally changed *what* we eat, only *how quickly* we heat it, or that stovetops and ovens still dominate actual cooking. Engaging with such objections would make your argument more sophisticated and credible.

Clarify your definition of "revolution." You link the microwave's significance to two things—the absence of fire and the reduction in cooking time—but you don't explain why these particular features matter more than, say, the invention of refrigeration or canning, which also transformed food preparation. Consider narrowing your claim: is the microwave revolutionary because of the *mechanism* (radiation vs. fire) or the *social effect* (freeing up time, especially for working parents)? These are related but distinct arguments, and separating them would sharpen your analysis.

Reconsider the child-safety point. The claim that children can "use this device without parental fear of fires" feels like a minor, somewhat tangential point compared to your larger argument about historical revolution. If you keep it, connect it explicitly back to your thesis about how the microwave reshapes domestic life.

Structure and Organization

Your extended metaphor of "culinary seas" and a "storm" is a creative opening, but it goes on for an entire paragraph before you state your actual subject. Consider trimming this significantly—one or two sentences of vivid imagery, then a clear thesis statement. As it stands, readers must wait too long to learn what the essay is about.

Your topic sentences are strong in principle (paragraph 2 focuses on technological break, paragraph 3 on time-saving), but paragraph 3 tries to do too much—covering working mothers, reheating leftovers, *and* child safety in quick succession without transitions or full development. Consider dedicating a full paragraph to each idea, or cutting the weaker points to keep your argument focused.

Your conclusion largely restates the introduction without adding new insight. Consider using it to reflect on broader implications: What does the microwave's history suggest about how future kitchen technologies might develop? Or how does this case study reveal something about technological "revolution" more generally?

Style and Mechanics

There are numerous spelling and grammatical errors that need correction before this essay is submission-ready:

"culunary" → culinary; "consistant" → consistent; "rythm" → rhythm; "twentyth" → twentieth; "comodity" → commodity; "dizzing" → dizzying; "differents" → different
"Progress has move slow" → has moved slowly
"cave men cooked animal flesh" — consider "early humans" for accuracy and inclusivity
"the fool of mankind" → likely "the food of mankind"
Random capitalization: "As History progressed" and "throughout History" should be lowercase unless personifying intentionally
Sentence fragment: "For As I have said before, there is no fire!" — needs revision; also, avoid exclamation points in academic prose
Inconsistent verb tense throughout (mixing past and present)

I'd strongly recommend using spell-check and reading the essay aloud before your next draft—many of these errors would be caught that way.

A Note on Historical Framing

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay raises an intriguing premise—that the machine gun and the radio were the two inventions that enabled modern repressive government—but it does not fully develop this into a sustained argument. Instead, the essay shifts into a general, largely descriptive account of how radio and television have shaped everyday life. As it stands, the piece reads more like a reflective narrative than an argumentative essay. Revising it will require sharpening your central claim and making sure every paragraph serves that claim.

Thesis and Argument

The opening paragraph promises a provocative thesis: radio (unlike the machine gun) has had an enormous impact on society, particularly through its connection to repressive governments and propaganda. This is a strong, specific, and debatable claim—exactly what an argumentative essay needs. However, you abandon this angle almost immediately. After the second paragraph, you never return to the idea of propaganda, dictatorship, or repression. Instead, the essay drifts into a much broader (and much less controversial) claim: "radio/TV has changed people's lives by informing and entertaining them." This second claim is not really an argument—it's a widely accepted observation that doesn't require defending.

Ask yourself: what do you actually want to persuade your reader of? If it's the propaganda/control angle from your teacher's comment, then the bulk of your essay should analyze *how* radio enabled political control—through state-run broadcasts, wartime propaganda, censorship, etc—and you'll need historical examples (Nazi Germany's use of radio, Cold War propaganda broadcasts, etc). If instead your real interest is the everyday transformation of life through broadcast media, then the opening paragraph about machine guns and repression is a misleading and unnecessary frame—cut it, and build a thesis around the more genuine claim you're making about media, information, and entertainment.

Structure and Organization

Right now, the essay's structure mirrors its confused argument. The introduction sets up a political/historical comparison; the body pivots to personal, everyday experience (weather forecasts, TV-watching children, your abuela). The conclusion doesn't synthesize these two threads—it simply restates that radio has "changed the way we see and do things," which is close to what you already said in paragraph two.

Consider organizing around one clear line of reasoning. For example:
Introduce your actual thesis (choose one of the two angles above).
Explain the mechanism of radio (how it works, why it reaches so many people).
Provide 2–3 well-developed pieces of evidence that support the thesis (historical examples, statistics, expert opinions).
Address a counterargument (Could newspapers or television have had similar effects? Why is radio specifically significant?).
Conclude by returning explicitly to the thesis and explaining its broader significance.

Evidence and Development

This is the area needing the most attention. Much of your support is anecdotal or asserted without evidence: "I don't think any people of my generation can even comprehend..." and "My señora... sits in front of it all day." These are vivid details, but in an argumentative essay they function as illustrations, not proof. For genuine persuasive power, you need historical facts, statistics, or expert commentary—especially since your central claim involves large-scale social and political change. For instance:
What percentage of households owned radios by a certain date?
How did specific governments use radio for propaganda?
What historians or media theorists (eg, Marshall McLuhan) have written about radio's societal impact?

The claim that "radio... gives people the option of becoming passive observers rather than active participants in the news making process" is a sophisticated and debatable idea—but you state it and move on without explaining or defending it. This sentence alone could anchor an entire paragraph of analysis.

Style and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to correct in your next draft:
Apostrophe misuse: "don' t," "it' s" should be "don't" and "it's" (or "its," depending on meaning—check whether you mean possession or contraction).
"Marshall Law" should be "martial law."
"forcast" should be "forecast."
The word "señora" appears to have an encoding error (renders as "se�ora")—make sure special characters transfer correctly, and clarify whether you mean "grandmother" (abuela) or literally "señora" (Mrs./madam), since "señora" doesn't typically mean grandmother.
Watch comma usage and sentence fragments (eg, "consists of going to school & coming home to watch T.V." uses "&" informally—spell out "and" in academic prose).
Some sentences run together as comma splices; consider breaking them into two sentences or using semicolons.

Suggestions for Revision

Decide on one clear, arguable thesis and delete material that doesn't serve it.
Replace anecdote-driven claims with researched evidence.
Develop your most interesting insight—the idea of "passive observers" of news—into a fully argued paragraph.
Proofread carefully for apostrophes, capitalization, and informal shorthand.
Revise your conclusion to explicitly tie back to your (new) thesis, showing why your argument matters.

You have a genuinely interesting idea buried in this draft. Focus your revision on selecting *one* strong claim and building the entire essay to support it.

Feedback Report: Draft on Technology and Modern Life

Overall Impression

Your essay has a clear, relatable premise and a nice personal touch, particularly in the anecdote about your great-uncle. However, in its current form, the piece reads more like a personal narrative than an argumentative essay. To meet the expectations of academic argumentation, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, broaden your evidence base beyond your own household, and tighten your structure. Below are specific areas to address as you revise.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, the essay's claim is vague. You open by saying technology has made "a significant impact" on people's lives—but this is not a debatable, arguable thesis; it's a statement most readers would accept without argument. An argumentative essay needs a claim that someone could reasonably disagree with. Are you arguing that the benefits of microwaves outweigh the drawbacks? That convenience technology has fundamentally changed family life and cooking culture? That resistance to such technology is rooted in a specific values conflict (efficiency vs. tradition)? Choose one clear, contestable claim and state it explicitly in your first paragraph.

Currently, the essay presents two anecdotes (your family's use of the microwave, and your great-uncle's resistance) but doesn't synthesize them into a coherent argument. The concluding line—"it has been a wonderful appliance for all of us"—undercuts the counterargument you raised about your great-uncle, but you don't explain *why* your view should prevail. Revise so that your evidence clearly supports your central claim.

Structure and Development

The essay is currently organized as a series of very short paragraphs, several of which are only one or two sentences long. While short paragraphs can be effective for emphasis, here they fragment your reasoning rather than build it. Combine related ideas: for example, your explanation of your family's cooking schedule and your microwave routine could be merged into a single, fuller paragraph that shows *how* the microwave solves a specific problem (time scarcity in dual-income households).

You also move from your personal example directly into the counterargument (your great-uncle) without transitioning or explaining the significance of the shift. Add a transitional sentence that frames the counterargument's purpose—eg, "Not everyone views convenience technology so favorably, however." Then, after the anecdote, add analysis: what does your great-uncle's comment reveal about generational attitudes toward efficiency, tradition, and the meaning of domestic labor? Right now you assert "a microwave isn't right for everyone" but don't unpack this claim.

Finally, the conclusion is abrupt. The detail that your great-uncle ultimately gave away the microwave is a great narrative beat, but you need to explain what it means for your argument. Did his eventual rejection of the microwave validate his position, or does the fact that *your* family uses it happily undercut his critique? Make the significance explicit.

Evidence

Right now, all your evidence is anecdotal and drawn from your own family. This is a reasonable starting point, but a stronger essay would incorporate at least some outside evidence—statistics on dual-income households, historical information about the invention and adoption of the microwave, or perspectives from articles/interviews about generational attitudes toward convenience technology. This would elevate the essay from a personal reflection to a more persuasive academic argument, and it would also help you address readers who might not find personal anecdotes fully convincing on their own.

Style and Mechanics

A few technical issues need attention:

Apostrophes/possessives: "peoples lives" should be "people's lives"; "they're" is misspelled as "they' re."
Spelling: "recieved" should be "received."
Redundant phrasing: "the point is, is that a microwave isn't right for everyone" repeats "is" unnecessarily. Revise to "The point is that a microwave isn't right for everyone."
Capitalization: "great Uncle and Aunt" should be lowercase ("great-uncle and great-aunt") unless used as direct address.
Sentence fragments/short paragraphs: As noted above, several paragraphs are only one sentence. Combine and develop these into fuller units of thought.
Informal phrasing: Expressions like "The long and short of the story" and "a little story to tell" are conversational and better suited to spoken narrative than formal argumentative writing. Consider more formal transitions appropriate to an academic essay.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a one-sentence, arguable thesis and place it clearly in your introduction.
Combine short paragraphs into fuller, well-developed ones organized around single main ideas.
Add a transition sentence introducing the counterargument.
Analyze the meaning of your great-uncle's anecdote rather than just narrating it.
Incorporate at least one outside source (statistic, article, or historical fact) to support your claim.
Proofread carefully for apostrophes, spelling, and redundant phrasing.
Revise the conclusion to directly restate and reinforce your thesis in light of the evidence presented.

With these revisions, your natural storytelling voice can be preserved while strengthening the essay's argumentative backbone.

Feedback on "The Impact of ATM Machines"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and relatable topic, and your enthusiasm for the convenience of ATMs comes through clearly. However, the essay would benefit from stronger organization, more developed argumentation, and more precise, error-free prose. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your opening paragraph introduces ATMs as a significant 20th-century invention, but the thesis statement itself is buried and underdeveloped. Right now, your main claim is essentially "ATMs are convenient," which is more of an observation than an arguable position. Consider sharpening your thesis to make a more specific claim—for example, that ATMs fundamentally changed consumer independence and financial behavior, or that their benefits to personal autonomy outweigh their costs to traditional banking employment. A stronger thesis gives your essay a clear argumentative spine that each paragraph can support.

Structure and Paragraph Development

The essay is quite short for the scope of its claims, and each paragraph introduces ideas without fully developing them. A few specific issues:

The counterargument is introduced but not addressed. You raise the objection that ATMs eliminate banking jobs, but you dismiss it in a single sentence ("we must also focus on the positive aspects") without actually explaining *why* the positives outweigh this real economic concern. This is a missed opportunity—a strong argumentative essay engages with counterarguments seriously before refuting them. Consider adding a few sentences acknowledging the job losses, then explaining why you believe the trade-off is worthwhile (eg, new jobs created in technology and maintenance, expanded access to banking, etc).

Personal anecdotes need more analytical framing. Your example about forgetting your wallet at the grocery store is relatable, but it functions more as a personal narrative than evidence supporting a broader claim. Try connecting your examples explicitly back to your thesis—what does this anecdote *prove* about the significance of ATMs on a societal level, not just your own convenience?

The essay would benefit from a fourth body paragraph or expanded existing ones. Right now you cover: (1) time-saving/access, (2) global availability and safety, and jump quickly to a conclusion. Consider dedicating a full paragraph to each major benefit (convenience/time, safety, accessibility) with more concrete evidence—perhaps statistics on ATM usage, banking hours before ATMs existed, or crime data related to cash-carrying.

Evidence and Support

Currently, your support is anecdotal and general ("ATM machines appear in all areas of the World"). To strengthen your argument, consider incorporating:
A brief historical comparison (what was banking like before ATMs?)
Any statistics about ATM usage or the reduction in in-person banking transactions
A specific example of how ATMs affect people beyond your own experience (eg, travelers, small business owners, people in rural areas)

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several errors to correct in revision:

"I can not comprehend" should be "I cannot comprehend."
"access at access to money" is awkward phrasing—revise to something like "access to money in emergencies."
"late 1900's" should be "late 1900s" (no apostrophe for plural).
The dash before your final sentence ("-- The development of computerized technology...") creates a fragment-like effect; this final claim about "computerized technology" broadens far beyond ATMs specifically and feels like a new idea introduced too late. Consider integrating this broader point into your introduction instead, then narrowing your conclusion back to ATMs specifically.
Watch subject-verb agreement and pronoun consistency—you shift between "I," "you," and "people," which creates an inconsistent voice. Choose one primary approach (perhaps blending personal example with more general third-person claims) and use it consistently.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates your appreciation for ATMs but doesn't synthesize the argument or leave the reader with a broader insight. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement: how might ATMs continue to evolve, or what do they suggest about the future relationship between technology and everyday convenience?

Summary of Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis into a clear, arguable claim.
Develop the counterargument about job loss more substantively before refuting it.
Add more varied evidence beyond personal anecdote.
Proofread for grammar and punctuation errors.
Ensure the conclusion ties back to a focused thesis rather than introducing new, broader claims.

With more developed paragraphs and a tighter argumentative focus, this essay has strong potential—the topic is engaging and your instincts about counterargument and real-world example are good starting points.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your topic—the cellular telephone as a 20th-century discovery—is engaging and relevant, and your personal anecdote about the international call is a genuine highlight. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a unified argument. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

This is labeled an "argumentative essay," but your thesis is unclear. Your first paragraph states that cell phones are "a complete luxury and not a necessity"—is *this* your argument? If so, the rest of the essay needs to consistently support it. Instead, your body paragraphs present a mix of benefits (emergency use, staying connected) and drawbacks (accidents, cost) without clearly tying these points back to the luxury/necessity claim. Ask yourself: What is the one claim I want readers to accept by the end of this essay? Then make sure every paragraph advances that claim.

Revision suggestion: Consider revising your thesis to something more precise, such as: "While cellular telephones offer real safety benefits, their costs—both financial and social—reveal them to be a luxury rather than a necessity." This gives you a roadmap: benefits, then costs, leading to your conclusion.

Paragraph Organization and Unity

Your second paragraph mixes emergency use (a *practical* benefit) with people's *desire* for constant access (which you call "ridiculous"). These are somewhat contradictory ideas placed in the same paragraph without acknowledgment. Separate them, or explicitly discuss the tension between genuine need and perceived need.

Your third paragraph tries to do too much: it covers accident rates *and* cost, which are quite different issues. Each deserves its own paragraph with a clear topic sentence and supporting evidence.

Evidence and Development

Currently, your claims are asserted rather than supported. For example:
"People are able to use their car phones in case of an emergency" — Can you find a statistic or example illustrating this?
"When someone is concentrating more on their telephone conversation... they are more likely to get into an accident" — This is a common claim; strengthen it with data (eg, studies on distracted driving) rather than presenting it as self-evident.
"Cellular telephones are also very expensive" — Compared to what? Give approximate costs or a comparison to landline costs to make this concrete.

Academic argumentative writing benefits from evidence beyond personal impression. Even brief research citations would substantially strengthen your credibility.

The Anecdote and Its Placement

Your fourth paragraph (the four-way call story) is vivid and effective, but it currently reads as a positive counterpoint that isn't integrated into your argument. If your thesis is that cell phones are a luxury, this anecdote could actually support that claim—it demonstrates impressive but non-essential capability. Make that connection explicit: something like, "This kind of connection, while extraordinary, is a convenience—not something previous generations required to live full lives."

Conclusion

Your final paragraph shifts to a new idea (robots) that isn't set up anywhere earlier in the essay. This can work as a broader reflection on technology, but right now it feels disconnected from your central argument about cell phones. Consider either:
Cutting this and instead synthesizing your luxury/necessity argument, or
Briefly connecting it: technology continues to blur the line between luxury and necessity, and cell phones are just the beginning.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Notes

Punctuation: Watch spacing around punctuation ("it' s" should be "it's"). Also, avoid using the ampersand (&) in formal writing—write out "and."
Contractions and informal language: Phrases like "a bit ridiculous" and "truly amazing" are conversational. In academic writing, aim for more precise, analytical language (eg, "an unnecessary indulgence" or "a significant technological advancement").
Punctuation for asides: The dash after "Madrid" and the double exclamation points are informal; consider standard punctuation (colons, commas, or periods) for a more academic tone.
Verb tense consistency: Check for consistency, particularly in the anecdote paragraph.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify and commit to a single, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear focus.
Add concrete evidence (statistics, expert opinions, or specific examples) to support your claims.
Integrate your anecdote and conclusion so they clearly serve your argument.
Revise for more formal, analytical language and consistent punctuation.

You have compelling ideas and a strong personal voice—the goal now is to channel that into a tighter, more persuasive argument. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on "The Creation/Evolution Controversy" Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely difficult and controversial topic, and your essay shows real effort to organize a multi-part argument with reasoning, quotations, and rebuttals. However, the essay currently reads more as advocacy than as academic argumentation, and several structural and logical issues need attention before this meets university-level standards.

Argument and Logic

The central weakness is that your "counter-arguments" are not genuine. You state early on that you'll show how "the opposition's argument becomes increasingly weak," but this conclusion is asserted rather than earned. In your rebuttal section, you summarize the evolutionist position in a single sentence and then immediately have the two-model side respond—without ever letting the strongest version of the opposing argument breathe. This is a straw man structure. For real persuasive power, you need to represent the evolutionist position with the same nuance and detail you give the creationist position (for example: the distinction scientists draw between *scientific theory* as empirically testable and *religious claims* as not falsifiable in the same way; the Establishment Clause concerns in *Edwards v. Aguillard*, 1987, which specifically struck down mandated creationism teaching).

The Newton/Einstein analogy is logically shaky. Einstein didn't "study creation" or a competing religious framework—he extended and revised an existing scientific paradigm using the same methodological tools (evidence, testable prediction, mathematics). Using this example to argue that evolution should be paired with creationism conflates two very different kinds of "questioning": scientific revision within a discipline versus introducing a non-scientific framework into a science class. This analogy needs to be either qualified carefully or replaced.

The legal argument is incomplete and one-sided. You mention a Supreme Court case permitting "objective study of religion" and note that Mississippi banned teaching evolution—but you don't mention the more directly relevant case law (*Edwards v. Aguillard*) that addresses this exact controversy, nor do you explain why an old, unconstitutional Mississippi law supports your point. This weakens rather than strengthens your credibility with an informed reader.

Use of Evidence

Several placeholders () remain unfilled where quotations and evidence should be. Before submitting, every one of these needs a specific, cited quotation with a full source (author, publication, date). Right now, claims like "some argue yes, saying " leave your reader unable to evaluate your evidence at all—this is the single most urgent fix needed.

Additionally, when you cite the Bliss study via Bergman's book, you should clarify: Is this a peer-reviewed study? What was the sample size and methodology? "Learned very much about evolution" and "argued significantly better" are vague; specify what was measured. Secondhand summaries of studies (a study *about* a study) are weaker evidence than direct citation of primary research, so track down Bliss's original work if possible.

Structure

The essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap early on. Consider explicitly outlining your three or four main arguments in the introduction so readers can follow your reasoning. Right now the reasoning is presented cumulatively (methods → evidence → studies → rebuttals) but the transitions between sections don't always signal *why* one argument follows another.

Also reconsider your thesis. An academic essay defending the two-model approach should acknowledge from the outset that this is your persuasive position—not present it as though you're neutrally "showing" that one side is objectively weaker. Own your stance transparently; this actually strengthens credibility rather than weakening it.

Style and Mechanics

"where as" should be "whereas" (one word)
"fro ma" is a typo for "from a"
E=MC² needs proper superscript formatting
Watch comma splices and run-on sentences, eg, "The more attempts to answer these questions, the more ways in which it is debated" is grammatically unclear—revise for clarity
Rhetorical questions ("isn't the classroom the most effective place...") work in casual persuasive writing but should be used sparingly in formal academic argument; consider converting some to declarative thesis statements
"a little more widely accepted" understates the scientific consensus on evolution significantly; be careful not to inadvertently undermine your credibility by minimizing well-established facts

Priority Revisions

Fill in all placeholder quotations with full citations
Strengthen and fairly represent the evolutionist counterargument before rebutting it
Reconsider or qualify the Newton/Einstein analogy
Add relevant case law (*Edwards v. Aguillard*) to the legal discussion
Proofread for grammar/typos
Clarify your thesis as an explicitly argued position rather than an "objective" demonstration

This essay has a solid skeleton—sharpening the logic and evidence will make it considerably more persuasive to a skeptical academic reader.

Feedback on "Mercy Killing" Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult ethical issue, and you clearly did some research into different perspectives on euthanasia. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of loosely connected observations than a unified argument. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Clarify and Sustain Your Thesis

Your introduction states that individuals should have the "freedom" to choose euthanasia as a God-given right. Yet your later paragraph on God's gifts argues the opposite—that life is not ours to end because "it is not society's choice to decide when he or she has had enough of life." These two positions contradict each other, and the essay never resolves the tension. Your conclusion further complicates this by introducing a *conditional* position (acceptable if suffering is severe and the person has no chance of recovery; unacceptable if recovery is possible).

This is actually a nuanced and defensible stance—but it needs to be your explicit thesis from the start, not something that emerges accidentally in your final paragraph. Revise your introduction so it clearly states this conditional position, and make sure every subsequent paragraph builds toward it rather than presenting values (religious freedom vs. divine ownership of life) that pull in opposite directions without being reconciled.

Fix the Missing Source Material

There are several bracketed placeholders  where quotations or paraphrased evidence should be. These need to be filled in with actual content and proper citations before this essay can be evaluated on the strength of its evidence. Right now, sentences like "Some say that in cases of euthanasia they [ ]" and "The person suffering should remember [ ]" are incomplete thoughts that break the reader's understanding of your argument. This is the most urgent fix.

Strengthen the Values vs. Consequences Framework

Your most interesting analytical move is the distinction between euthanasia as a *values* issue versus a *consequences* issue (paragraph 5). This is a promising angle, but it's underdeveloped. Consider making this distinction the organizing structure of the whole essay:
Explain what a "values-based" argument looks like (tone, focus on morality, personal/emotional framing)
Explain what a "consequences-based" argument would look like (what are the actual societal/legal/medical consequences of legalizing euthanasia?)
Then argue which framework you find more persuasive and why

Right now this insight appears, then gets abandoned. Bring it back in your analysis of the Kevorkian case and the Cruzan article, and use it to organize your conclusion.

Integrate the Kevorkian and Cruzan Cases More Analytically

You reference two real cases (Kevorkian, Cruzan) but mostly *describe* them rather than *analyze* them in relation to your thesis. For each case, ask explicitly: does this case support a values-based or consequences-based argument? Does it support or complicate your own conditional stance on euthanasia? For example, you could use the Cruzan case to test your own criterion (was recovery possible?) rather than simply disagreeing with prolife activists in general terms ("I personally don't agree with that... I believe the U.S. lacks certain morals and values but to allow mercy killing to be a phenomenon would be outrageous"). This paragraph currently just states opinion without connecting it back to your criteria or evidence.

Improve Transitions Between Paragraphs

Several paragraphs shift topic abruptly: from the values/consequences discussion to a paragraph on religious upbringing, then to the Kevorkian case, then back to a discussion of article tone. Add transitional sentences that explicitly link each new paragraph to the argument you are building, so the reader isn't left to guess how, say, the discussion of church-taught values connects to the Kevorkian case that follows it.

Address Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are recurring errors to proofread for:
"an complex issue" → "a complex issue"
"no one has the right to take someone else life" → "someone else's life"
"patients personal decision" → "patient's personal decision"
"en a painful life" appears to be a typo (perhaps "end")
"sym pathetic" — stray space, likely a line-break artifact from copying
Subject-verb agreement issues: "Individual articles expressed the fact that..." is awkward phrasing throughout; look for places where noun/verb pairs don't match cleanly (eg, "This meant that there was some obvious debate")
"Coinciding with values, 'I believe God gave all people the gift of life'" — this sentence is unclear; a quotation appears mid-sentence without a clear introduction of its source or its grammatical role in the sentence.

A careful proofreading pass, ideally after you've revised the content and structure, will catch these.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Resolve the contradiction between the "freedom to choose" argument and the "life belongs to God" argument—decide on one coherent position.
Fill in the missing quotations/evidence where the placeholders appear.
Develop the values-vs-consequences framework as the essay's organizing structure.
Analyze, don't just summarize, the Kevorkian and Cruzan cases in light of your thesis.
Proofread for grammar and sentence-level clarity.

You have a strong foundation of ideas here—particularly the values/consequences distinction—but the essay needs a clearer, more consistent argumentative through-line to bring those ideas together persuasively.

Feedback Report: Capital Punishment Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely difficult topic and taken a clear position, which is essential for argumentative writing. Your essay demonstrates that you understand both sides of the debate and have gathered relevant sources. However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of arguments than a persuasive case for your thesis. Below are specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—that the "anti-death" argument is "stronger and more sensible"—is clear, but the essay's structure doesn't fully serve this claim. You spend substantial space presenting the pro-death penalty arguments (retribution, deterrence) almost as strongly as the counter-arguments, without enough direct comparative analysis. Ask yourself after each paragraph: *Have I shown why this specific point favors my side?* Right now, the essay alternates between "here's their argument" and "here's my argument" without enough direct engagement between the two. Consider restructuring so that each pro-death-penalty point is immediately followed by your rebuttal in the same paragraph, rather than in separate sections pages apart.

Use of Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. You have numerous placeholder citations (marked as ) throughout the essay—for Hoyler, Nygaard, Conway, and Reiman. These quotations are essential to your argument's credibility, and right now the essay cannot be fully evaluated without them. As you revise:

Make sure each quotation is followed by your own analysis explaining *why* it matters and *how* it supports your specific claim.
Avoid simply dropping in a quote and moving on. After Nygaard's quote, for instance, explain precisely how his words support your claim about "lowering the value of human life."
Vary your integration—not every source needs the same "X states that <quote>" format. Try paraphrasing some material and reserving direct quotes for especially powerful language.

Logical Development

Several claims need more support or more careful reasoning:

"It is a known fact that for most people, the biggest fear in life is the fear of death." This is asserted without evidence. Either support it or qualify it.
The deterrence rebuttal is one of your strongest sections because you use statistics (crime rates, death row populations) rather than just assertion. Consider bringing this same rigor to your other rebuttals.
"The fact is that society simply craves violence" — this is a strong claim in your conclusion that appears nowhere earlier in the essay. If you believe this is central to your argument, it needs to be introduced and developed earlier, not introduced for the first time in your final paragraph.
The claim that killing a criminal "sets the value of his life equal to that of the victim" is an interesting and original point—arguably your most sophisticated observation. Consider developing this idea further and giving it more space, as it's more compelling than some of the more familiar arguments (eg, "two wrongs don't make a right").

Tone and Rhetorical Choices

Watch for moments where emotional appeals substitute for argument. For example, "Why should we be concerned with the life of a violent criminal?" is a good rhetorical question, but your answer ("The violent criminal is still a member of society") is underdeveloped for such an important point—critics would push back hard here, and your essay should anticipate that pushback.

Similarly, calling capital punishment "heinous" and "barbaric" is fine as persuasive language, but make sure these characterizations follow from your evidence and reasoning rather than substituting for it. Academic argumentation is most persuasive when strong claims are paired with careful support.

Fairness to the Opposing View

You do a reasonable job acknowledging why people support the death penalty (eg, understanding victims' families' rage), which is good practice. However, make sure you're representing the strongest version of the pro-death-penalty argument, not just the most emotional one. Are there any pro-death-penalty scholars who address the deterrence question with data, rather than intuition? Engaging with the best opposing evidence—not just the weakest—will make your eventual rebuttal more convincing.

Sentence-Level Issues

Watch for absolute claims ("It is a known fact," "simply impossible") that are difficult to defend and can undercut your credibility.
The repeated phrase "the value of human life" is central to your argument—consider defining it explicitly early on so your later uses of it feel more precise and less like a slogan.

Priority Revisions

Insert and fully integrate your quotations, then add analysis after each one.
Restructure for direct engagement—pair each opposing argument with an immediate rebuttal.
Develop the "equal value" idea from paragraph 6—it's your most original contribution.
Move your conclusion's claims about societal "violence-craving" earlier, or cut them if unsupported.

Feedback on "New Age Teaching" Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done real research here, and your engagement with the topic comes through—especially in the personal anecdote about running to ask your neighbors about tobacco statistics. That kind of critical, skeptical reading of a source is exactly what strong argumentative writing requires. However, the essay currently has several structural and logical issues that weaken its persuasive power. Below are the main areas to focus on for revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is buried in a long, run-on sentence in paragraph three ("the case against the teaching of new age beliefs is stronger than the case for it because..."). Pull this out, sharpen it, and consider placing a clearer, more concise version at the very end of your introduction. Right now, readers have to work hard to find your central claim.

The essay's structure is also uneven. You spend a great deal of time defining "New Age" and explaining its origins before you get to your actual argument. Consider tightening the definitional section significantly—one paragraph should suffice—so you can devote more space to comparing the two sides' evidence, which is your real contribution.

Logical Fallacies to Address

This is the most important area for revision. You make a hasty generalization in the tobacco/alcohol passage: because you found *one* factual error in *one* source supporting New Age teaching, you conclude that the entire pro-New Age case "doubted it as a true argument." One error, however significant, doesn't invalidate an entire body of literature. To strengthen this argument, you'd need to show a pattern of unreliability across multiple pro-New Age sources, not just one flawed claim.

Similarly, you dismiss the entire "question of values" approach for the pro-New Age side because the authors express personal opinions—but then you build your own argument partly on personal conviction ("I feel the Bible is very real and true"). This is an inconsistency a careful reader will notice immediately. If personal belief disqualifies an argument on one side, it should be held to the same standard on the other. I'd recommend removing your personal declaration of faith in the Bible's truth altogether. You don't need to endorse the Bible's literal truth to argue that it functions as an authoritative, widely-recognized text that lends credibility to an argument. Frame this as a rhetorical strength (broad cultural authority, longevity, wide recognition) rather than a theological claim—this will make your argument far more persuasive to readers who don't share your religious views, and it will keep your paper in the realm of argumentation rather than personal testimony.

Missing Evidence

There are several places marked with  where quoted material was clearly meant to be inserted—the four themes of New Age belief, the Andrew Weil quote, and the testimony from the Moody article. These are crucial pieces of evidence for your argument, and without them, readers can't evaluate your claims. Make sure these are filled in before your final draft, with proper citations.

Tone and Voice

The opening ("Yoda, that little green guy from Star Wars?") is a fun hook, but the essay's tone shifts unevenly between casual, conversational asides ("I honestly ran out of my room...") and more formal academic argumentation. This isn't necessarily a flaw—personal anecdotes can be effective in argumentative essays—but you should make sure the transitions between register feel intentional rather than accidental. Consider revising some of the more casual phrasing ("stupendous job," "really strong argument") to elevate the overall academic tone, while keeping your personal anecdotes as deliberate rhetorical moments.

Treatment of the Opposing Side

Your essay would be much stronger if you engaged more seriously with the *best* arguments in favor of teaching New Age ideas rather than focusing on their weakest example. Right now, it reads as though you searched for the easiest target to knock down. A more persuasive essay would acknowledge a legitimate, well-supported point from the pro-New Age side and then explain specifically why the anti-New Age case still outweighs it. This adds credibility and shows you've engaged with the topic fairly.

Conclusion

Your conclusion largely repeats what you've already said rather than synthesizing it. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement—what should be done with this conclusion? Should schools adopt policies? Should readers evaluate sources differently? This would give your essay more purpose beyond summary.

Priority Revisions

Fill in missing quotations/citations.
Sharpen and relocate your thesis.
Remove the personal faith claim about the Bible's truth; reframe its authority in rhetorical terms.
Address the hasty generalization in your tobacco example.
Engage with a stronger pro-New Age argument, not just a flawed one.

With these revisions, you'll have a much more balanced, logically consistent, and persuasive essay.

Feedback on Your Nuclear Power Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled an ambitious topic and demonstrated genuine research effort, particularly in the sections on cost comparisons and waste management. Your essay shows a clear structural logic—establishing the opponents' emotional appeals before systematically countering them with the proponents' factual claims. However, the essay's effectiveness is undermined by factual errors, unclear argumentative stance, and prose that needs significant polishing. Let's work through these issues.

Factual Accuracy (Critical Issue)

Your opening sentence contains a serious factual error: nuclear bombs have explosive power measured in kilotons or megatons of TNT equivalent, not three hundred tons. The Hiroshima bomb, for instance, was approximately 15 kilotons (15,000 tons). This error appears in your very first sentence and undermines your credibility from the start. Since accuracy is paramount when discussing a technical, controversial topic, please verify all statistics before revision—including your claims about cost percentages and lives saved, which need citations.

Argumentative Stance: Analysis or Advocacy?

This is the most significant structural problem. Your thesis promises a rhetorical analysis—examining *how* each side argues (values vs. facts). Indeed, your second and third paragraphs set this up nicely. However, by the fourth paragraph, you abandon analytical distance and begin arguing FOR nuclear power yourself ("these supporters use the fact that..." shifts to sentences that assert these claims as true rather than examining them as rhetorical strategies).

Your conclusion confirms this shift, explicitly advocating for nuclear power ("nuclear power will truly be an asset to our revolutionary country").

You need to decide: Is this a rhetorical analysis of both sides' persuasive techniques, or an argumentative essay advocating for nuclear power? Right now it's an uncomfortable hybrid. If you want analysis, you must maintain analytical distance throughout and give equal, serious treatment to the opponents' claims (which you currently reduce to fear-mongering). If you want advocacy, restructure your thesis to state your position clearly, and treat opposing evidence more substantively rather than characterizing it merely as manipulative.

Treatment of Opponents

Currently, you frame opponents' views as based on ignorance and media manipulation ("taking advantage of their unfamiliarity") while framing proponents as purely factual and rational. This creates an unbalanced, biased analysis. Even if you ultimately favor nuclear power, a strong essay acknowledges the *legitimate* substance behind safety concerns, waste disposal challenges, and accident risks (Chernobyl, Fukushima, Three Mile Island deserve mention). Dismissing opposition as merely "beating waste issues to death" weakens your credibility as an analyst.

Organization

Your body paragraphs (cost, safety, cleanliness, waste) are logically sequenced and each has a clear focus—this is a strength. However:

The transition sentences are formulaic ("Another big factor is...")
You never return to systematically analyze the *opponents'* four points with the same depth you give proponents' points. This imbalance reinforces the one-sidedness noted above.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

You have frequent errors that need careful proofreading:

"do to" should be "due to"
"three hundred tons" — factual/numerical error (see above)
"analyzation" is not a standard word; use "analysis"
"counter act" should be "counteract"
"meggawatts" should be "megawatts"
"took" should be "taken" ("precaution taken")
"then" vs. "than" — you consistently confuse these (eg, "more cost-efficient then," "cheaper then coal")
There's a broken sentence: "though simple, easy-to-understand facts such as:  the average American can easily relate..." — this appears to be an incomplete example (missing a specific fact/statistic)
"our revolutionary country of life, liberty, property" — the Declaration of Independence references "life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness," not "property"; this misquote in your conclusion is jarring

Please read your essay aloud slowly, or use grammar-checking software, to catch these issues systematically.

Citations

You cite no sources. Claims like "50 percent more expensive," "20 percent cheaper," and "twenty and one hundred lives saved" require attribution. Without citations, readers cannot verify your claims, and the essay reads as opinion rather than researched argument.

Revision Priorities

Clarify your purpose: rhetorical analysis or advocacy essay? Revise thesis and conclusion accordingly.
Fact-check all statistics, especially your opening claim.
Balance your treatment of both sides' arguments with equal depth and fairness.
Add citations for all statistical claims.
Proofread carefully for the recurring "then/than" error and other mechanical issues.

You have solid raw material here—strengthen the analytical framework and precision, and this could be a compelling essay.

Overall Impression

Complete the quotations from *Desegregation and Beyond*.

Beyond simply filling gaps, every source needs a clear, specific citation—not just "a study done by two researchers from New York" or "a writer for the New York Times Magazine." Your introduction promises an examination of integration through "questions of value and consequence," but this framework doesn't clearly organize the rest of the essay. Are you arguing that integration succeeded or failed? That it succeeded academically but failed socially? That success depends on implementation details (age of transfer, teacher attitudes, community support)? Your conclusion suggests the latter—"there are both values and consequences... it needs support from venues other than the school systems themselves"—but this idea should be stated as your thesis *up front*, not just asserted at the end. Explicitly returning to "value vs. consequence" language at the start of each section so the reader can track your argument.
Combining the personal narrative section more tightly with the community-resistance section, since both deal with race relations. Right now they read as separate topics.
Strengthening your conclusion. Right now it restates rather than synthesizes. Given how strong your personal experience is, consider ending with a reflection on what your own experience *taught you* about the conditions necessary for successful integration, tying back to your thesis.

Your account of the Chapter 220 program is the most original and compelling material in the essay—don't undersell it. Right now, it's mostly narrated rather than analyzed. Ask yourself: what does your experience *prove* about integration more broadly? You note that younger students adapted better and that increasing numbers of transfer students changed group dynamics—this actually supports your earlier academic-research point about age of placement, but you don't draw that connection explicitly. Make these links visible to the reader. Also, be cautious with generalizations like "many black students took advantage of the white community's reluctance to speak out"—this is a strong claim based on personal impression; consider tempering it, acknowledging your limited perspective as a member of the majority group, and/or supporting it with your source material.

Evidentiary Balance

You use statistics on integration rates in the first paragraph, but you never return to hard data on outcomes at the end—the essay becomes anecdotal and impressionistic in the second half. Where possible, pair your personal narrative with pointing back to any research from earlier in the essay (Fuller, the NY-based study) to reinforce, or complicate, what you experienced personally.

Concrete Next Steps

Fill in all  gaps with complete, cited information.
Write a clearer thesis sentence stating your actual claim.
Reorganize so that "value" and "consequence" (or your revised framework) structure each paragraph.
Add analytical sentences connecting your personal narrative back to the academic sources.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize rather than restate.

This essay has strong bones—a genuine argument, real evidence, and a valuable personal perspective. With revision focused on clarity and connection, it will be considerably stronger.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You're tackling a genuinely difficult and important topic—racial dynamics on predominantly white campuses—and your central thesis (that separation, however understandable, ultimately reinforces the problem it seeks to address) is a defensible and interesting position. You also do good work acknowledging counterarguments before responding to them, which shows analytical maturity. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: evidentiary support, precision of argument, and mechanical execution.

Major Issues

Missing Evidence (Critical)
The bracketed placeholders  appear at crucial argumentative junctures—including your opening hook, key statistics from the Penn State study, and your engagement with Steele's essay. As it stands, the essay is essentially a skeleton of citations you haven't yet filled in. Before this essay can be evaluated on its merits, you need to:
Actually quote Alberta's statement and explain why it's significant
Provide the specific findings from the Penn State study (what percentage of students reported what experiences?)
Quote Steele accurately and represent his argument fairly—right now you assert "his reasoning was" without telling us what it was
Fill in your concluding thesis restatement

This isn't optional polish—an argumentative essay lives or dies on its evidence, and right now the argument is unsupported assertion.

Conceptual Confusion: Race vs. Class
Your essay repeatably conflates racial discrimination with poverty as the driver of stereotypes (eg, "whites and black alike, who live in poverty, must deal with a higher crime rate"). This is an important point, but you introduce it and then drop it without integrating it into your thesis about campus segregation. Are you arguing that stereotypes stem from race, class, or the intersection of both? Clarify this connection or cut the tangent—right now it reads as a digression that complicates rather than supports your argument.

Logical Gaps in the Core Argument
Your claim that black student organizations constitute "segregation" equivalent to historical racial segregation needs more careful argumentation. Historically, segregation was *imposed* by a dominant group to exclude and subordinate a minority; voluntary affinity groups formed by that minority operate under different power dynamics and serve different psychological/social functions (community, safety, cultural continuity). You gesture at this counterargument in paragraph 7 but don't fully grapple with the asymmetry. Strengthen this by directly addressing: *Is self-segregation by a marginalized group equivalent to segregation imposed by a majority?* Your essay would be stronger if you conceded more ground here before pivoting back to your integration argument.

Underdeveloped Claim About Administrators
You mention that administrators "allow" these organizations and that this is part of the problem, but never explain what you think administrators *should* do instead. This claim needs either development or removal.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Pronoun/verb errors: "e problem" (missing "th"), "a felling of inferiority," "in her essay" should possibly be "in his essay" (verify Steele's gender/pronoun)
Redundant phrasing: "the problems can the problems begin to be solved" (paragraph 8) — this needs to be corrected
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences string together independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, "Alberta, being of color, describes...")
Inconsistent tense: You shift between present and past tense when discussing historical events and ongoing conditions—decide on a consistent framework
Vague pronoun references: "It is true that this scenario is not always the case with a person of color" — unclear antecedent

Structural Suggestions

Strengthen your introduction: Right now it moves quickly from an anecdote to a broad claim. Consider explicitly stating your thesis in one clear sentence at the end of paragraph 1.

Reorganize the counterargument section: You raise two counterarguments (cultural pride, and "not the black students' job to fix this") in separate paragraphs. Consider whether these can be synthesized more efficiently, or whether they need clearer transitions to show how they build on each other.

Revise your conclusion: Since it currently begins with a placeholder, you have an opportunity to write a more powerful synthesis. Don't just restate your thesis—explain what's at stake if universities fail to pursue genuine integration.

Priority for Revision

Fill in all evidentiary gaps — this is non-negotiable before further feedback can be meaningful
Clarify the race/class relationship — either integrate or excise
Deepen your engagement with the self-segregation counterargument — acknowledge the asymmetry of power more explicitly
Proofread for grammar and mechanics — these errors currently undermine your credibility as a writer

Your instinct toward integration as a solution is a legitimate philosophical position, but the essay needs firmer evidentiary grounding and more nuanced argumentation to convince a skeptical reader. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Overall Impression

Before I can comment on the strength of your argument, these need to be filled in—right now, sentences like "Health services, however, argues that " and "Annas argues " leave the reader unable to follow what is actually being claimed or refuted. Please make sure your final draft has no placeholder gaps; every claim you attribute to a source needs the actual content included.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—that surrogacy is unethical primarily because of emotional harm to the surrogate mother—doesn't emerge clearly until quite late. The first paragraph opens with two different rationales (biological "only two parents" and "emotional strains"), and it's not obvious which is your main claim. Consider stating your thesis explicitly in the introduction, after you've defined surrogacy (the definition itself is missing—paragraph 1 begins "Surrogate Motherhood is when a "). A reader unfamiliar with the topic needs this definition to follow the rest of the essay.

The body paragraphs also tend to introduce a counterargument, gesture toward a rebuttal, and then drift into a related but distinct point before fully closing the loop. For example, the second paragraph moves from "women should control their own bodies" to "walking womb" to "no human being should be looked upon as a reproductive structure"—three related but not identical ideas presented without clear transitions showing how one leads to the next. I'd recommend restructuring so each paragraph follows a consistent pattern: (1) state the opposing claim, (2) cite the source's actual reasoning, (3) respond directly to that reasoning, (4) draw a mini-conclusion that connects back to your thesis.

Argumentation

Several of your claims need stronger logical support rather than assertion. For instance: "Surrogacy is unethical because biologically there are only two parents, excluding the adoptive mother." This is stated as self-evidently true, but it's actually the crux of the debate—you need to explain *why* biological parenthood should carry more ethical weight than intentional or contractual parenthood, since that's precisely what proponents of surrogacy would dispute.

Similarly, "there are many people in the world who's lives have not been altered because they are infertile" is unclear—do you mean infertile people who live fulfilling lives without children? If so, say this more precisely, and explain how it supports your point that the "longing" to have a biological child doesn't justify the surrogate's suffering. As written, the logical connection between the example and your claim is left for the reader to guess.

Also watch for repetition: the idea that the surrogate is treated as a "walking womb" or "reproductive structure" appears three times across two paragraphs. Consolidate this into one strong statement rather than repeating it, and use the space you free up to develop new supporting points.

Use of Sources

You reference several sources (Annas, "Health services," "the article above") but never fully name or cite them. For an argumentative essay, you need proper attribution—full names, publication information, and direct quotations or paraphrases that are accurate and specific. Right now the essay reads as though it's summarizing a source packet the reader doesn't have access to. Make your sources' arguments concrete and cited, and be sure to represent opposing views fairly and in detail before you rebut them—this will make your own rebuttal more persuasive.

Clarity and Mechanics

A few sentence-level issues to fix:
"Surrogate mother hood" should be one word: "surrogate motherhood" (check throughout).
"who's lives" should be "whose lives."
"substancial" should be "substantial."
"The couple ... do not see the surrogate mother as a person" – check subject/verb agreement ("couple" is singular; either "does not" or make it "the couple... do not" only if treating it as plural intentionally, but be consistent).
Some sentences make strong moral pronouncements ("No human being should be looked upon as a reproductive structure who has no feeling") that would benefit from softening into an argued claim rather than a bare assertion.

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all missing content—definitions, quotations, source names.
Write a clear one- or two-sentence thesis early in the introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs around a consistent claim–evidence–rebuttal structure.
Cut repetitive phrasing and use the space for deeper analysis.
Proofread carefully for the mechanical errors noted above.

With these revisions, your argument's emotional and ethical core—genuine concern for the surrogate mother's wellbeing—will come through far more persuasively.

Overall Impression
