However, the essay needs revision in several areas: structural organization, evidence integration, sentence-level clarity, and argumentative balance. Below are specific areas to address.

Missing Content and Placeholder Text

A skeptical reader will want this explained.

Tone and the Personal Appeal

Your paragraph about being a nursing student is emotionally effective and personalizes the issue well. However, academic arguments are generally strengthened, not weakened, by this kind of testimony *if* it's tied clearly back to evidence. Consider moving this to your conclusion and framing it as the real-world stakes of your data-driven argument, rather than a late addition.

Sentence-Level Issues (Proofreading)

You have several errors that need correcting in your final draft:
"argues" → "argue"; "agrues" → "argues"
"recup" → "recoup"; "recieveproper" → "receive proper"; "recieving" → "receiving"
"an other" → "another"; "senile dementia" (verify—Clozapril/clozapine is typically used for schizophrenia, not dementia; check this fact)
"morally ethical" is redundant—choose one
"peoples heads" needs an apostrophe: "people's heads"
The rhetorical question ending ("It is up to the American people to decide") feels abrupt—consider a stronger, more assertive conclusion that circles back to your thesis about federal regulation specifically, rather than leaving the decision open-ended after building such a strong case.

Priority Revision Steps

As it stands, a reader cannot evaluate your claims because the actual substance—the statistics, the quotations, the specific findings—is absent. This is not a minor citation issue; it's the difference between an argument and an assertion that an argument exists. Before revising anything else, you must locate your sources and fill in this missing content with accurate, properly cited information. If these are placeholders from an outline or draft, make sure the final version replaces every single one.

Structural and Argumentative Strengths

Your thesis is direct and previews your three main points effectively.
Each body paragraph has a clear topic and internal logic.
You appropriately acknowledge the counterargument (constitutional legality, "eye for an eye" ethics) before refuting it—this is strong argumentative practice.
Your conclusion accurately restates your three main points without simply repeating the introduction word-for-word.

Areas for Revision

Source Quality and Currency
Several of your sources are quite dated (Dann, 1935; Coleman case events from the early 1980s). For a contemporary argument about capital punishment, incorporate more recent studies (post-2000) alongside historical ones. This strengthens credibility and shows the issue's continued relevance. Death penalty research and statistics have evolved significantly—more recent meta-analyses exist that would bolster your deterrence argument.

Logical Gaps Requiring Attention

In the brutalization effect discussion, you state criminals "model their behavior after the state" and believe —but without the actual claim, this causal mechanism is asserted, not explained. When you fill this in, make sure you clearly explain *why* state killing would encourage private killing, since this is a counterintuitive claim that skeptical readers will resist.
The Coleman case is presented as evidence of wrongful conviction, but you should clarify: was Coleman later exonerated by DNA evidence, or does doubt remain? (Historically, DNA testing after his execution supported the conviction, complicating this as your primary example.) Verify this before using it, as using a contested case could undermine your credibility if a reader fact-checks it.
The transition "Yet another study was conducted by Derral Cheatwood" and similar phrases ("Further evidence of this is demonstrated by...") are somewhat mechanical. Consider varying your transitions to show *relationships* between studies (eg, "Corroborating this finding," "In contrast to earlier studies, Cheatwood found...").

Addressing the Strongest Counterargument
Your ethics paragraph raises the "protecting innocent people" argument but dismisses it fairly quickly. A stronger version of this counterargument is *incapacitation of specific dangerous individuals who might kill again even in prison* (eg, killing a guard or fellow inmate). Your life-imprisonment rebuttal addresses general deterrence but not this specific incapacitation concern. Consider strengthening this section.

Sentence-Level Issues
"should an individual state choose to employ it" — capitalization inconsistency ("constitution" should likely be capitalized if referring to the U.S. Constitution specifically).
"put a stop to it all together" → should be "altogether" (one word).
"It is reported that the case against Coleman . He was executed on May 20, 1982." — verify this date; Coleman was executed in 1992, not 1982. Double-check all dates and facts before submission.
Watch for repetitive phrasing: "the death penalty" appears extremely frequently; consider substituting "capital punishment," "this practice," or "executions" for variety.

Paragraph Length and Balance
Your statistics paragraph is quite dense with five separate studies, which risks feeling like a list rather than a developed argument. Consider whether all five are necessary, or whether you could develop two or three most compelling studies in greater depth with clearer analysis of *why* each matters.

Priority Action Items

Fill in all missing evidence — this is non-negotiable before further revision.
Fact-check the Coleman case — this could seriously undermine your credibility.
Update sources to include more recent research.
Strengthen the incapacitation counterargument in your ethics section.
Vary sentence transitions and word choice throughout.

Once the evidentiary gaps are filled, this essay will have a solid foundation. Focus your next revision pass on ensuring every piece of evidence is accurate, current, and clearly connected to your claims.

Your essay tackles an interesting and genuinely controversial topic, and you've done solid research to support your position. The most pressing problem is the numerous  placeholders where quotations should appear. These gaps appear at crucial argumentative moments—when doctors comment on Prozac's effects, when Dr. Kramer discusses suicide risk, when the rabbi describes his side effects, and in your concluding testimonials from Drs. Auerbach and Cass. Right now, readers cannot evaluate your evidence because it's literally absent. Before revising anything else, locate your sources and insert the actual quotations. This isn't just a formatting issue—your argument's credibility depends on this evidence being visible and properly cited (with page numbers or paragraph numbers for the Kramer article, publication information for others).

Strengthening the Argument

Acknowledge counterarguments more substantively. Your essay would be more persuasive if you engaged more seriously with the suicide concern rather than moving quickly to statistics. Consider: correlation doesn't prove causation, but neither does a low percentage disprove it entirely. Are there specific populations more at risk? Address this with more nuance rather than seeming to dismiss the concern.

The "numbing" side effect deserves more attention. You mention Sam losing his "quirkiness and sarcasm" and gesture toward the rabbi's incomplete quote, but then quickly minimize these as "slight obstacles." Is losing aspects of one's personality truly a minor issue? This connects to a deeper philosophical question your essay could explore: what does it mean to treat depression if the treatment changes personality traits unrelated to the illness itself? Grappling with this complexity would strengthen your credibility rather than weaken your argument.

Reconsider the rhetorical question ending. "What is wrong with making yourself better?" is compelling but assumes definitions that others contest. Some critics might argue Prozac isn't making people "better" but altering who they fundamentally are. A stronger conclusion would preemptively address this distinction rather than leave it open for skeptical readers to raise.

Organizational and Clarity Issues

Paragraph 2 conflates too many claims. You move from explaining serotonin mechanisms to listing other conditions Prozac treats (bulimia, OCD, panic disorder) to the Sam case study. Consider whether the list of other conditions belongs here or would work better integrated elsewhere, since your thesis focuses specifically on depression.

Transition between social and medical benefits could be smoother. The shift from "medically reliable" to "socially allows people to interact better" in paragraph 3 introduces a somewhat different argument (social benefit vs. medical safety) that could use a clearer bridge sentence explaining why both matter to your overall case.

Define "better than well" and similar loaded phrases. This phrase (borrowed from Kramer's famous framing) carries significant philosophical weight—it suggests Prozac might make people exceed normal functioning, not just treat illness. This is worth unpacking rather than using in passing, since critics seize on exactly this claim to argue Prozac is being misused as an enhancement drug rather than a treatment.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

"his pas social lives" should be "his past social life" (paragraph 4)
"banish ment" should be "banishment" (paragraph 1)
"chemical called serotonin is" is awkward; revise to "a chemical called serotonin is located" or similar
Check consistency in how you refer to sources on first mention (some get full titles/credentials, others don't)
"Says one physician" — vary your attribution phrases throughout; you rely heavily on Kramer without introducing other physician quotes with as much context

Suggestions for Revision Priority

First, insert your missing quotations and verify all citations
Second, add a paragraph or expand existing ones to more seriously engage the "numbing"/personality-change critique
Third, revise your conclusion to address the "better than well" tension rather than ending on an unexamined rhetorical question
Finally, proofread for the mechanical errors noted above

Your essay has a clear point of view and useful evidence—the revision work now is about making sure that evidence is fully present and that you engage as rigorously with counterarguments as you do with supporting evidence. This will make your ultimate defense of Prozac more convincing because readers will see you've genuinely wrestled with the complexity rather than dismissed it.

Feedback on Draft Essay: Homosexuals in the Military

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult and controversial topic, and you clearly understand both articles well enough to summarize their main claims. However, the essay needs significant revision in three key areas: thesis consistency, use of evidence, and the depth of your engagement with counterarguments. Right now, the essay reads more as a summary of two articles than as an original argument.

Thesis and Logical Consistency

Your opening paragraph creates a problem you never resolve. By invoking the civil rights movement and women's liberation as parallel struggles for equality, you set up an expectation that this essay will *extend* that trajectory of expanding rights. Instead, you pivot to argue that gay Americans should be *denied* the right to serve. This isn't necessarily a contradiction—but you need to explicitly address why the military ban is different from, say, racial segregation or gender discrimination. Right now, the comparison feels like a rhetorical flourish that undercuts rather than supports your argument. Either develop this comparison meaningfully (why is sexual orientation in the military a different category of claim than race or gender?) or cut it and open more directly with the debate itself.

Your conclusion also creates confusion. You write, "it was decided by the writer of this essay that there was not reason why homosexuals should not be prohibited from serving"—this double negative is nearly impossible to parse, and it's unclear whether your original position matched or opposed your final one. Since the "converted reader" move (I used to think X, but this article changed my mind) is a compelling rhetorical strategy, make sure the "before" and "after" positions are stated with total clarity.

Use of Evidence

This is the most significant issue in the draft. The essay is full of placeholder quotations (marked <\*>) that were never filled in. Without the actual language from Pattullo and Wall, a reader cannot evaluate whether your interpretations are fair or whether the quotes truly support your claims. This needs to be fixed before anything else—select precise, representative quotations, integrate them smoothly into your sentences, and follow each with your own analysis of *how* the wording works (or fails) rhetorically.

Relatedly, you tend to describe Wall's arguments in dismissive terms ("lighthearted attitude," "he does not see the point") rather than genuinely engaging with his reasoning. For a persuasive essay to be convincing, it needs to show that you've grappled with the strongest version of the opposing argument, not just an easily dismissed version of it. What is Wall's best evidence for treating sexual orientation as "irrelevant"? Why doesn't it hold up? Right now you assert that his analogy (comparing barracks assignment to musical taste) is weak, but you don't fully explain why—spend a sentence or two unpacking the disanalogy rather than simply declaring it invalid.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your paragraph organization (alternating between Pattullo and Wall on each sub-issue) is a reasonable structure, but transitions between paragraphs are abrupt. For example, the shift from discussing barracks-sharing to discussing "close-knit relationships" needs a clearer connective sentence explaining how these are related but distinct concerns. Consider adding brief topic sentences that preview the specific sub-argument each paragraph will address (eg, "A second consequence Pattullo identifies is the erosion of unit cohesion, which differs from the discomfort addressed in the previous section because...").

Style and Sentence-Level Issues

There are several grammatical errors that need correction:
"Pattullo's use of sound reasoning...will make on change their mind" — likely means "will make one change their mind," but needs rewording for clarity.
"James M. Wall's articles does not give" — subject-verb agreement (articles/does).
"there should be a ban gays serving" — missing preposition ("a ban *on* gays serving").
"This would be a very uncomfortable situation for whomever" — should be "whoever" (subject, not object).

I'd also encourage you to tighten the introduction. Sentences like "There are numerous debates surrounding this issue leaving people to wonder, what is the right thing to do?" are vague and could apply to almost any controversial topic. Try to be more specific about what exactly is at stake in this particular debate from the outset.

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all placeholder quotations with actual textual evidence, properly cited.
Clarify the relationship between your opening historical analogy and your thesis—either develop or remove it.
Deepen your engagement with Wall's argument rather than dismissing it quickly.
Proofread carefully for subject-verb agreement, missing prepositions, and confusing double negatives.
Add clearer topic sentences and transitions between paragraphs.

With these revisions, particularly the integration of real evidence, this essay has the potential to make a much more persuasive and polished argument.

Feedback Report: Steroids and Mandatory Drug Testing Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, well-defined topic and demonstrate genuine engagement with research on this issue. Your thesis is clear, and you've organized the essay around three distinct supporting reasons (fairness, health/safety, and role-modeling), which gives the essay a logical skeleton. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: citation integration, paragraph structure, source documentation, and proofreading. Below are specific areas to address.

Missing Quotations (Critical Issue)

Throughout the essay, you've placed  placeholders where quotations should appear (eg, "As John Chaplin... he stated, "). This is the most urgent issue to fix. As it stands, readers cannot evaluate your evidence at all—the essay currently makes claims that appear to be supported by quotes we cannot see. Before any other revision, insert the actual quotations you intended to use, and make sure each one is followed by your own analysis explaining *why* it supports your point. A quotation should never stand alone; always follow it with 1-2 sentences of interpretation.

You mention several articles by title ("A Game of Cat and Mouse," "OK to NCAA," "In Track and Field Time for Naked Truth," "Check Up or Check Out," "Test Case") but never provide full citations—no authors (for most), no publication dates, no page numbers. For an academic essay, you need:
A Works Cited/References page
In-text citations following whatever format your instructor requires (MLA, APA, etc)
Consistent formatting when introducing sources (currently you sometimes name the article, sometimes the author, sometimes both—choose a consistent method)

Paragraph Structure and Topic Sentences

Several paragraphs lack clear topic sentences that state their main point before diving into evidence. For example, paragraph 3 begins by referencing an article title rather than stating the paragraph's argument. Try restructuring so each paragraph opens with a claim (eg, "Dr. Paul Thompson's research reveals that performance-enhancing drugs compromise athletes' cognitive functioning") and *then* brings in supporting quotations.

Additionally, some paragraphs contain multiple loosely connected ideas. The paragraph beginning "Each year the field of sports becomes more and more competitive" reads like a conclusion but appears with body-paragraph content mixed in—clarify where your conclusion begins.

Logical Gaps and Underdeveloped Reasoning

There are several errors that need correction:
"Lial Alsado" — verify the correct spelling of this athlete's name
"lesson" should be "lessen"
"meathod" should be "method"
"strickter" should be "stricter"
"an nounced" should be "announced" (spacing error)
"role privacy" and similar phrases—recheck sentence construction in places like "It is wrong for athletes to cheat so they can win a gold medal, achieve a personal record or even win a championship" for smoother phrasing
The question "What are fans of great athletes supposed to think when they see that their role models have been using steroids." needs a question mark
Watch subject-verb agreement: "protect athletes health" should be "athletes' health" (possessive apostrophe needed throughout)

A careful line-by-line proofreading pass is essential before your final submission.

Repetition

Your thesis statement (ensuring fair competition, protecting health, preventing role-model drug use) is repeated almost verbatim in your conclusion. While restating your thesis is appropriate, try rephrasing it with new language or synthesizing your evidence rather than repeating the same sentence structure.

Priority Revision Steps

You have strong source material and a clear argumentative structure—now it's a matter of filling in the gaps and polishing the presentation. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Draft Essay: Prayer in Public Schools

Overall Impression

You've taken on an ambitious task—comparing three sources and arguing for a specific solution—and your organizational plan (evaluate Conservative Religious Group's articles, then Civil Liberties articles, then propose Lewis's solution) is logical and easy to follow. However, the essay needs significant revision in four areas: thesis clarity, quotation integration, depth of analysis, and grammar/mechanics.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis sentence is doing too much work and has become a run-on. Try breaking it into two or three sentences. Something like:

*"This essay compares two positions on school prayer represented in current journalism. While 'A Religious Argument Against School Prayer' offers stronger reasoning than 'Prayer in Public Schools, It's Nothing New to Many,' both articles present incomplete solutions. Anthony Lewis's proposal in 'Jefferson Still Lives'—a moment of silence before class—offers the most balanced resolution."*

This gives your reader a roadmap without forcing them to parse one overloaded sentence.

Missing Quotations

Your draft contains numerous  placeholders where quotations should be. This is the most urgent issue to fix before this essay can be evaluated on its merits. Without the actual quoted material, readers cannot judge whether your interpretations are accurate or whether the quotations truly support your claims. Please insert the real quotations with proper attribution (author's name, and page number if required by your citation style) before your next draft.

Depth of Analysis

Right now, many of your paragraphs follow this pattern: quote → brief restatement → "This is a good/weak argument." For example:

*"This is a good use by the author of debating the opposing view with factual statements."*

This phrase appears in slightly different forms throughout the essay. Readers need to know why it's good or weak. Push yourself to answer:
What specific evidence or reasoning makes an argument credible?
What logical fallacy, exactly, is being committed (you mention "fallacy" once—name it: hasty generalization? correlation-causation error?)?
How does the quotation connect logically to the claim it's meant to support?

For instance, in your discussion of rising crime/divorce/pregnancy rates, you correctly sense a problem—the author never establishes causation—but say so explicitly: *"The author commits a correlation-causation fallacy: even if these social problems increased alongside the removal of prayer from schools, this does not prove that the absence of prayer caused the increase."* This kind of explicit naming of the logical gap will strengthen your critical reasoning noticeably.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are frequent errors that need correction in revision. A partial list:

"arrise" → arise
"know as" → known as
"torwads / torward" → towards
"conatin" → contain
"stengthen" → strengthen
"mionorities" → minorities
"sustain from hurting" → refrain from harming (this is a significant word-choice error that changes meaning)
"ow" → how
"titles" → titled
"Gary Baur" — double check spelling of proper names against your source
Subject-verb agreement: "The second articles gave stronger reasoning" — should this be "second article" or "second group of articles"? Be consistent about whether you're discussing one text or several.

I'd recommend reading the essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, then cross-referencing each correction against the original texts to make sure names and terms are accurate.

Paragraph Transitions

Paragraphs currently begin somewhat abruptly ("A third reason argued by..."; "The second idea is the question..."). Consider adding brief transitional phrases that link back to your thesis or forward to your next point, eg, "Beyond the argument for religious education, the Conservative Religious Group also invokes..."

Conclusion

Your final paragraph restates your preference for Lewis's solution but doesn't fully synthesize *why* it resolves the shortcomings you identified in the other two positions. Consider explicitly returning to your thesis: how does a moment of silence address both the Civil Liberties Group's concern about state-imposed religion and the Conservative Religious Group's desire for spiritual expression? Making that synthesis explicit in your final paragraph will give the essay a stronger sense of closure.

Priority for Next Draft

Insert all missing quotations with proper citations.
Revise the thesis into 2–3 clear sentences.
Deepen the analysis in each "this is good/weak" moment—explain your reasoning.
Proofread carefully for spelling and word-choice errors.
Strengthen the conclusion by directly connecting Lewis's solution to the flaws you identified in both other positions.

You have a solid analytical framework here—now it needs a careful line-edit and more explicit critical reasoning to match.

Feedback on Death Penalty Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression
You've chosen an appropriately controversial topic and organized your essay around three clear reasons (ineffectiveness, immorality, unreliability of the justice system). This three-pronged structure is a strength. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of source integration, logical rigor, and tone before it will be persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Thesis and Structure
Your thesis is clear: the death penalty doesn't reduce crime, is immoral, and hinders society. However, your third body paragraph actually contains a mix of arguments—cost/appeals, backlogged courts, and wrongful convictions—that don't map neatly onto "society does not need a death penalty because it isn't foolproof." Consider splitting this into two paragraphs (one on systemic unreliability/wrongful convictions, one on cost/inequity) or tightening the topic sentence to cover all three sub-points explicitly.

Your conclusion introduces a *new* idea—education as a solution to crime—that is never developed earlier in the essay. Either remove this or add a paragraph developing it. Conclusions should synthesize existing arguments, not introduce new ones.

Evidence and Sourcing
This is the area that needs the most attention. Several bracketed placeholders  appear where quotations or data should go—these need to be filled with actual, cited evidence before submission. As it stands, readers cannot evaluate your claims.

Beyond the placeholders, be careful with how you present sources:
"A scientist named Forst" and "Solomon" need full citations (last name, publication, and ideally institutional affiliation) so readers can verify credibility. Simply naming someone and a year isn't sufficient support.
You use one study from 1975 and one from 1983 to support a claim about crime rates "today." Since crime rates and death penalty policy have changed substantially in 40+ years, you should either find more recent data or explicitly justify why these older studies remain relevant.

Logical Reasoning
Some of your reasoning needs tightening:
The "fear causes more murder" argument (Forst) is speculative and needs stronger support. You state you find it "logical," but simply asserting logic isn't the same as demonstrating it. Consider providing an example or additional evidence of criminals killing witnesses specifically to avoid capital punishment.
The claim about Liberal Democrats supporting the death penalty is a generalization that risks alienating readers and is factually questionable (historically, many Democrats have opposed capital punishment; support has cut across party lines). Cut this claim or replace it with accurate, sourced information—your credibility depends on accuracy here.
The moral argument ("no murder is right, not even the death penalty") is your strongest philosophical point, but it's asserted rather than argued. Address the strongest counterargument directly: retributivists would say state-sanctioned execution differs morally from murder because it follows due process and is enacted on behalf of society, not personal vengeance. Right now you dismiss this ("an eye for an eye") in one sentence without engaging it seriously.
In your final paragraph, the claim that death penalty supporters "only" support it for political gain is an unsupported generalization and borders on an ad hominem attack rather than engaging with their actual arguments (deterrence, justice for victims, closure).

Style and Tone
Several sentences begin with "Well," which reads as conversational rather than academic. Revise these openings (eg, "Well it has been proven..." → "Studies have shown...").
"Full-proof" should be "foolproof."
Watch for typos: "increases crime" should be "increased crime"; "so ciety" is a spacing error.
Several long sentences run together multiple ideas with commas rather than being broken up or using semicolons/conjunctions—for example, the second sentence of paragraph three needs restructuring for clarity.
Your closing line ("murder someone because they made a huge mistake") risks minimizing the severity of the crimes under discussion (eg, serial murder, as referenced with Dahmer). Reconsider this phrasing so it doesn't undercut your credibility by seeming to trivialize violent crime.

Priority Revisions
Fill in all placeholder citations with real, properly cited evidence.
Verify and correct the claim about political party alignment on this issue.
Strengthen the moral argument by directly engaging the retributive justice counterargument.
Either remove or develop the "education" idea introduced in the conclusion.
Proofread for typos and revise conversational sentence openers for a more academic register.

With more rigorous sourcing and engagement with counterarguments, this essay has strong potential—the organizational bones and moral clarity are there; now the evidence and reasoning need to catch up.

Overall Impression

This is a well-organized, ambitious essay that engages seriously with a genuinely controversial topic. Your structure—presenting the pro-prayer argument, then systematically refuting it—gives the essay a clear logical spine, and your vignette opening is a strong, concrete way to draw readers into an abstract debate. Below are specific areas to strengthen before your final revision.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your thesis is clear and appears in a good location (end of the introduction), but it's slightly buried in a long, complex sentence. Consider breaking it into two sentences: one stating your position, one previewing your method (ie, that you will show how opposing arguments dismantle the pro-prayer case). This will help readers track your argument from the start.

The essay's organizational logic—claim, then rebuttal, repeated for several sub-arguments—is effective, but it also makes the essay feel like a list of refutations rather than a cumulative argument building toward a larger insight. Consider adding brief transitional sentences between sections that explicitly connect each refutation back to your central thesis (eg, "This weakness in the polling data matters because it undermines the entire premise that prayer reflects genuine public consensus").

Evidence and Citation

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay contains numerous bracketed placeholders (<\*>) where quotations should appear. Before submitting a final draft, every one of these must be filled in with the actual quoted material, properly cited (author, source, page/paragraph number as required by your citation style). Right now, a reader cannot evaluate whether your paraphrases accurately represent the sources, and some of your strongest rhetorical moves ("As Klinghoffer writes," "She recalls") currently promise evidence you haven't yet delivered. This is likely the single most important revision to make.

Relatedly, double-check your source details. The Finke and Stark book is titled *The Churching of America, 1776–2005* (originally 1776–1990 in an earlier edition)—not 1776–1790, which would predate the nation's founding-era religious history the book actually covers. Verify all titles, dates, and statistics against your original sources; small errors like this can undermine your credibility on a topic where precision matters.

Your treatment of the "moral degeneracy" argument is your strongest section—you correctly identify the correlation/causation fallacy and press Reed to explain his causal mechanism. Apply this same analytical rigor elsewhere. For instance, when you discuss the Episcopalian and Jewish Orthodox examples, you present them as evidence that "bland" prayers cause religious decline, but you don't interrogate this causal claim the way you interrogated Reed's. Are there other explanations for declining church membership (secularization trends, demographic shifts, generational change)? Even a brief acknowledgment that this correlation, too, might not be pure causation would make your critique more intellectually consistent and preempt a skeptical reader's objection.

The "Mississippi Prays" Section

This paragraph currently ends abruptly and its logic is underdeveloped. You state that the writer believes prayer supporters "are not a threat" and wants "respect," but you don't fully explain what this writer's argument is or why it fails. Since this appears in your list of opposing claims, consider either expanding it with a proper claim-then-rebuttal treatment (matching your other sections) or trimming it if it's not central to your argument.

Style and Mechanics

Watch for typos: "many writes" (writers), "intrest" (interest), "oppotunity" (opportunity), "Sid dur" (Siddur), "parishoners" (parishioners).
"Anna Quindlen agrees as she remembers her experience" — check subject-verb and pronoun clarity throughout; some sentences shift focus mid-clause and could be tightened.
Be careful with word choice: "murmurs" and "mumble" both appear in your opening; varying vocabulary would sharpen the vignette.
The sentence "This is not a healthy attitude to be held by 25% of the student body" would be stronger as "Resentment and alienation are not healthy attitudes for a quarter of any student body to hold."

Conclusion

Your proposed solution (voluntary before/after-school prayer groups) is a genuinely useful contribution—it moves beyond pure refutation into constructive policy, which strengthens your essay considerably. Consider moving a preview of this solution into your introduction, so readers know from the start that you're not just opposing school prayer but offering an alternative. This would make the essay feel less purely negative/critical and more solution-oriented throughout.

Priority Revisions

Fill in all missing quotations and verify their accuracy.
Correct the Finke and Stark citation.
Add transitional sentences connecting each rebuttal to your thesis.
Apply the same causal-fallacy scrutiny to the religious-decline examples that you applied to Reed's claim.
Proofread for the typos noted above.

This essay has a strong foundation—clear position, logical structure, and a constructive proposal. Completing the evidentiary gaps and tightening the connective logic will make it publication-ready.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely important and timely debate, and you've clearly done research to support your position. Your thesis is clear, and you organize your argument into distinct sections that address different facets of the issue. However, the essay would benefit significantly from revision in several key areas: quotation integration, argument structure, logical consistency, and precision of language. Below, I offer specific feedback organized by category.

Quotation Integration

The most pressing technical issue is the placeholder  appearing throughout your essay in place of actual quotations. Before this essay is submission-ready, every one of these must be filled in with the actual source material. Beyond simply inserting quotes, you need to:

Introduce quotes properly. Phrases like "states, " are grammatically incomplete and don't guide the reader. Use signal phrases that indicate *why* you're citing this source and what work it's doing in your argument (eg, "Quindlen argues that mandated prayer trivializes genuine faith when she writes...").

Follow quotes with analysis. In several places, you quote a source and then move directly to your next point without explaining how the quote supports your claim. For instance, after citing the Wall Street Journal on "Church and State," you ask a rhetorical question but don't analyze the source's actual content. Readers need you to connect the dots explicitly.

Avoid awkward attribution. Phrases like "the author of the December 7, 1994 issue of the New York Times, she states" are redundant and imprecise—name the author directly and cite once, cleanly.

Logical Structure and Consistency

Your essay contains a significant logical tension that needs addressing: you argue *against* mandated prayer during school hours but then advocate *for* after-school religious activities on school grounds. While this is a defensible position, you don't adequately explain why government-sponsored after-school religious activities avoid the same establishment concerns you raise against morning prayer. If a school facilitates or sanctions religious clubs, does this not also involve government endorsement of religion? Anticipate this objection and address it directly, rather than letting it appear inconsistent.

Additionally, in your "counterargument" section, you seem to concede ground rapidly (agreeing that after-school prayer groups are a good idea) without fully representing the *strongest* version of the opposing view first. Strengthen this section by fully articulating the opponent's best argument before you respond to it.

Paragraph Focus and Transitions

Several paragraphs try to accomplish too much. The paragraph beginning "Some people state that the loss of prayer..." conflates the moral-decline/crime argument with a general point about academic stress; these are related but distinct claims and could be separated or more clearly connected.

Similarly, your final two paragraphs (on freedom of expression and on rights/tolerance) cover similar ground and could likely be combined and tightened into one stronger concluding argument, avoiding repetition of the phrase "freedom of religion/expression."

Work on transitions between paragraphs. Right now, paragraphs often begin with a new claim rather than a sentence that bridges from the previous paragraph's conclusion. This makes the essay feel like a list of points rather than a cumulative argument.

Sentence-Level and Word Choice Issues

"with little interest to actually pray" (thesis) is awkward; consider "with little genuine interest in praying" or similar.
"morals of children i" — there's a typo ("i" for "in").
"Even is there is a silent time" should be "Even if there is a silent time."
The phrase "decadent public school system" is a strong value judgment that appears without support—either justify this claim or remove it, as it risks undermining your credibility if unsupported.
Avoid rhetorical questions as substitutes for analysis (eg, "Does this means that..."). These can work rhetorically, but each one should be followed by your own reasoned answer, not left hanging.

Strengthening the Argument

Consider adding:
A clearer definition of what you mean by "moral values" early on, since your opponents (Bush, Gingrich) invoke this term as their justification—directly defining and then contesting their usage would sharpen your critique.
A more explicit engagement with the Establishment Clause or relevant constitutional language, since you invoke "freedom of religion" but never ground this in specific legal reasoning.
A brief acknowledgment of *why* prayer advocates believe school prayer matters (beyond crime reduction)—this would make your rebuttal more persuasive by showing you've engaged with their strongest reasoning, not just a weaker version of it.

Next Steps

Fill in all quotations with actual source material and integrate them properly.
Resolve the logical tension between opposing in-school prayer while endorsing after-school religious activities.
Tighten paragraph focus; consider merging redundant sections.
Proofread for typos and awkward phrasing.
Strengthen the counterargument section by giving the opposition its strongest voice before rebutting it.

With these revisions, your essay's argument will be considerably more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report: Animal Testing Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult ethical issue and you clearly have research to draw on (Traystman's epinephrine studies, the Draize test, thalidomide, etc). The essay's biggest problems are not a lack of material but a lack of *structure*, *sourcing*, and *consistent argumentative logic*. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Missing Citations

There are several places marked with  where a quotation or specific claim should appear but doesn't (the Bible reference on dominion, the Waddams quote, Traystman's claims, the Bromey quote). These gaps make it impossible for a reader to evaluate your evidence, and as it stands the essay cannot be submitted with these placeholders. This is your first priority: locate and insert the actual quotations/citations, and add a Works Cited list. Every statistic (eg, "seventeen to twenty-two million animals") also needs a source—unsourced numbers in an academic essay read as unsubstantiated assertion.

Thesis and Overall Argument

Your introduction promises a balanced *discussion* of a moral dilemma ("we must be careful to determine which side is morally correct"), but the essay actually argues a strong pro-testing position from paragraph two onward. Decide which essay you're writing: an even-handed exploration of the dilemma, or a persuasive argument for animal testing. Right now the framing (a careful, exploratory opening) doesn't match the execution (a one-sided argument). If you want to argue a position, say so directly in your thesis and restructure the introduction to preview that argument rather than presenting the essay as neutral.

Handling of the Opposing View

You do give space to animal rights activists' arguments (the Draize test, the dog-shock experiments), which is good practice. But the concessions are inconsistent and sometimes undercut your own credibility rather than strengthening your argument. For example, dismissing depression research by saying "this depression does not really classify as human pain and discomfort" is an unsupported and somewhat dismissive claim—depression is a serious condition, and asserting otherwise without evidence weakens your ethos. When you concede a point (as you do with the dog experiments), follow through on what that concession means for your overall thesis, rather than dropping it and moving on.

Logical Fallacies to Address

False dichotomy in the conclusion: "who's life is more sacred, the life of a three year old boy, or a pig?" This framing assumes testing is the *only* way to save the boy and ignores the actual complexity of the issue you spent the essay describing. Avoid ending on an oversimplified rhetorical question that erases the nuance you built up.
Appeal to authority: Using "the Bible says humans have dominion" as a decisive ethical argument will not persuade readers who don't share that religious framework, and it sits oddly next to your later, secular ethical analysis (the "morally correct = what the person concerned believes" definition). Consider whether you need this argument at all, or whether it should be explicitly framed as *one* perspective among several rather than the essay's foundation.
Anecdotal/emotional evidence: The Timmy Baker story is vivid, but it needs a citation—is this a real, documented case, or an illustrative composite? If invented, say so explicitly ("Consider a hypothetical patient..."), otherwise readers may feel misled. Also, a single dramatic anecdote proves a possibility, not a general rule; be careful not to let it stand in for statistical or systematic evidence.
Whataboutism: The paragraph comparing testing to hunting/bullfighting doesn't strengthen your argument—it deflects rather than justifies. Consider cutting it or reframing it to make a clearer point (eg, about inconsistency in moral outrage) rather than leaving it as a bare comparison.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay would benefit from clearer signposting between sections: introduction/dilemma → ethical definitions → the case for testing → the case against → rebuttals → conclusion. Right now sections blend together, and some paragraphs (the ethics definitions in paragraph 3) arrive after you've already made ethical claims in paragraph 2, so the reader needs the definition earlier. Consider moving your definition of "ethical/unethical" into your introduction or early second paragraph, before you start applying it.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are frequent typos and grammatical slips that need proofreading: "friends side," "Animal my not be conscious," "aides in the healing off birth defects," "One animal experiment" (written as "On animal experiment"), "Dr Raystman" vs. "Dr Traystman" (inconsistent spelling), "activly" (as "activity involved"), missing apostrophes ("humans"), and comma splices. A careful proofreading pass—ideally reading the essay aloud—will catch most of these.

Priority Revisions

Fill in all  citations and add sourcing for statistics.
Clarify whether your thesis is exploratory or persuasive, and align the introduction accordingly.
Strengthen or cut weak/fallacious moves (the dichotomy ending, the depression dismissal, the hunting comparison).
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

You have strong raw material here—tightening the logic and documentation will make the argument much more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and timely issue—gender inequality in college athletics—with genuine passion and conviction. The opening scenario is a strong hook that immediately engages readers and illustrates your thesis concretely. Your voice comes through clearly, and you clearly care about this topic. However, the essay currently has significant structural and mechanical issues that undermine its persuasive power. Below I outline the main areas for revision.

Missing Evidence (Critical Issue)

The most pressing problem is the numerous placeholder markers () throughout your draft where quotations, statistics, or supporting evidence should appear. In an argumentative essay, especially one invoking specific figures like Pat Summitt and Linnea Smith, your claims *depend* on this evidence. Right now, sentences like "Pat Summit says " or "According to women's rights advocates " leave your strongest points unsubstantiated. Before revising anything else, fill in these gaps with actual quotes, statistics, or citations. Without them, readers cannot evaluate whether your argument holds up—this is not a minor polishing issue but the foundation of your credibility.

Paragraph Organization and Topic Sentences

Several paragraphs introduce a claim and then pivot without fully developing it. For example, the paragraph on Title IX moves from popularity statistics to "the only improvement has been in the players themselves" without clearly stating what you mean. Similarly, the paragraph addressing counterarguments about money and prestige raises the objection but doesn't fully counter it before moving to new claims (recruitment spending, hotel stays) that feel like a list rather than connected reasoning. You do good work acknowledging opposing views (eg, "Some may argue that the women in the magazines are better to look at...," or the "added pressures" argument from coordinators). This is a strength—it shows awareness of the debate. However, your rebuttals sometimes rely on assertion rather than argument. For instance: "But this all seems to be just a big excuse" doesn't explain *why* it's an excuse. Strengthen these moments by explicitly connecting your rebuttal to evidence or logical reasoning. Ask yourself: what specific facts or reasoning show the counterargument is flawed?

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring errors worth addressing:

Homophone confusion: "Their lucky to hold a game" should be "They're lucky." "fell that" should likely be "feel that."
Word choice: "supposeively" should be "supposedly"; "otherhand" should be "other hand" (two words); "Their" for "There" appears elsewhere.
Subject-verb agreement: "Being a talented basketball player and being women does not seem to mix" — mixing singular/plural constructions creates confusion. Consider: "Being a talented basketball player and being a woman do not seem to mix."
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences string together independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "Money is also wasted on the recruitment process" transitions abruptly into unlisted evidence).

A careful proofreading pass, perhaps reading the essay aloud, will catch many of these.

Logical Clarity in the "Added Pressures" Section

This paragraph is currently confusing. You explain that some believe equal treatment would bring "added pressures," then argue women could avoid men's mistakes by using men's teams as "role models," but it's unclear whether you're saying this is a good or bad thing, or how it responds to the original objection about pressure. Clarify the causal chain: Are you arguing pressure is a myth, that it's worth it, or that women are better equipped to handle it? Make this explicit.

Strengthening the Conclusion

Your final paragraph gestures broadly to "sports, boats, and bats," which is a nice rhetorical move, but it could more directly synthesize your specific arguments (media coverage, funding, respect, Title IX enforcement) rather than staying abstract. Consider ending with a concrete call to action—what specifically should readers or institutions do?

Priority for Revision

Fill in all missing quotes/evidence (top priority—this affects everything else)
Clarify paragraph logic, especially in the "added pressures" section
Proofread for grammar/mechanics
Strengthen rebuttals with specific reasoning, not just assertion

You have a compelling argument and clear personal investment in this topic—the revision work now is about ensuring your evidence and logic are as strong as your conviction.

Feedback Report: AIDS/Condom Distribution Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a genuinely controversial policy question with a clear thesis and a logical structure: you introduce the problem, present the proposal, then work through three major objections one by one, refuting each. This "objection-and-rebuttal" structure is a strong choice for argumentative writing, and your paragraphing largely follows it well. However, the essay has one immediate and serious problem, plus several issues of argumentation, evidence use, and style that need attention before this draft is ready.

The Missing Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)

The bracketed placeholders () appear roughly a dozen times throughout the essay, precisely where you should be delivering your most persuasive statistics, quotations, and expert testimony. As it stands, a reader cannot evaluate your argument at all—the CDC statistic that opens the essay, Dr. Imellizzeri's objection, Fernandez's response, the Johns Hopkins study results, the Epstein quotations, the Time magazine citations, and Rygor's closing words are all absent. This is not a minor formatting issue; it is the substance of your argument. Before revising anything else, fill these in with the actual sourced material, properly quoted and cited. Once they're in place, re-read the essay to check that each piece of evidence actually does the job you're currently asking it to do (see below).

Argument Structure and Logic

Your three-part refutation (promiscuity objection → abstinence objection → condom reliability objection) is a sensible organizing principle. A few structural suggestions:

The promiscuity/psychologist paragraph is underdeveloped. You introduce Dr. Imellizzeri's objection and Fernandez's reply, but then the paragraph ends. Once the quotations are filled in, make sure you explicitly analyze *why* Fernandez's response is convincing rather than just reporting it. Right now this paragraph is more summary than argument.

The abstinence paragraph contains a logical leap. You write that half of high school girls are sexually active and 16% have had four or more partners, then conclude "this shows that the idea of abstinence is a mere fantasy." This is a strong claim from limited data. Consider softening to something like "this suggests abstinence-only messaging has had limited success" or bolster the claim with additional data (once inserted) so the leap feels earned rather than asserted.

Watch for circular reinforcement. In the condom-reliability paragraph, you assert your conclusion ("condoms should be readily available") twice in slightly different words within four sentences. Trim this redundancy and let the statistic (1 in 65 breakage rate) do more of the persuasive work through analysis—explain why this rate is low enough to justify the policy, rather than simply repeating the recommendation.

Use of Evidence

Once your citations are restored, apply these principles:

Attribute and analyze, don't just insert. Several sentences currently read as "Source says X. This shows Y," with little space between quotation and interpretation. Strong argumentative writing lingers on evidence—explain *how* a statistic supports your claim, especially when a skeptical reader might push back (eg, correlation vs. causation in the Johns Hopkins study).

Address the counter-evidence fairly. You mention that opponents "argue there is evidence to prove abstinence is realistic" but then pivot away without seriously engaging that evidence. A stronger essay would state the strongest form of the opposing evidence and explain specifically why your evidence outweighs it, rather than implying it's simply wrong.

Style and Mechanics

Spelling/naming errors: "Dr Irene Imellizzeri," "John Hopkins Medical School" (should be Johns Hopkins), "refutals" (should be "refutations" or "rebuttals"), "form" for "from," "than" for "then" ("If condoms are our only means... than anyone..."). Proofread carefully for these.

Punctuation with titles: Article and report titles should be italicized or placed in quotation marks consistently (you do this correctly for Epstein's article—maintain that consistency elsewhere).

Sentence variety: Several paragraphs rely on a formula of "Source states X. This shows Y." Vary your sentence openings and integrate quotations more smoothly into your own sentences (eg, "As Epstein observes, students who...").

Conclusion

Your final paragraph is honest and effective in acknowledging the limits of current evidence ("there is no evidence available... because the plan has only been in effect for such a short time"), which strengthens your credibility. The closing quotation from Robert Rygor is a strong emotional appeal to end on—make sure it's substantial enough to carry that weight once inserted, and consider adding one final sentence of your own after the quotation so the essay doesn't end entirely on someone else's words.

Priority for Revision

Insert and cite all missing evidence accurately.
Strengthen the analysis connecting evidence to claims, especially in the psychologist/promiscuity paragraph.
Proofread for the errors noted above.
Trim repetitive restatements of your thesis in the later paragraphs.

This is a solid argumentative skeleton—your task now is to give it flesh.

Feedback on "Prayer in Public Schools" Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a genuinely contentious topic with a clear thesis and a logical structure that moves through counterarguments systematically. Your organizational strategy—addressing opposing claims one by one—shows sound argumentative planning. However, the essay currently has significant gaps that undermine its persuasive power, most notably the missing content indicated by the  placeholders, which suggests this draft needs substantial completion before it can be evaluated as a finished piece. Beyond that structural issue, there are several areas where your reasoning and use of evidence could be strengthened.

Strengths

Your thesis is clear and appears early: the pro-prayer argument relies on "shaky speculation, fallacies, and fanaticism," while the anti-prayer position emphasizes "fairness and equality." This gives readers a roadmap. You also use some effective rhetorical devices—the "thimble full of water" analogy in paragraph two vividly illustrates the disproportion between prayer and complex social problems like gang involvement. Your acknowledgment of confounding variables (broken homes, television violence) in paragraph three shows an awareness of correlation-versus-causation issues, which strengthens your critique of the opposing side's data claims.

Areas Needing Revision

Missing Evidence (Critical Issue)
The  placeholders throughout the essay leave crucial claims unsupported. You cannot effectively refute an argument you haven't actually stated. Before revising further, you must fill these in with specific, accurately quoted claims from actual prayer-in-schools advocates. Vague summaries won't do—use direct quotations with proper citations so readers can verify you're representing the opposing view fairly. Right now, readers must take your rebuttals on faith, which is ironic given your topic.

Uneven Treatment of Your Own Evidence
You critique pro-prayer supporters for lacking rigorous data, but your own paragraph three does something similar: you assert that segregation, lynching, and gender oppression existed *during* the era of school prayer, implying causation or at least discrediting the "good old days" argument. But this comparison has the same causal-fallacy problem you're accusing your opponents of committing. If you're going to hold their evidence to a high standard, hold your own to the same standard, or explicitly frame this as "if their causal logic is invalid, so is this parallel example."

Strawmanning Risk
In paragraph four, you write "By blanketing all students in America as Christians, a large part of the religious population evaporates." Make sure Senator Gunn's actual quoted statement supports this characterization once you insert it. Without the original quotation, this section risks appearing as a strawman argument. When you fill in the placeholder, ensure your rebuttal responds precisely to what was said, not to an exaggerated version of it.

Repetition of Ideas
Paragraphs five and six essentially reiterate the same core point: that public prayer imposes on students who hold different beliefs. While repetition can reinforce a thesis, here it reads as redundant rather than cumulative. Consider merging these paragraphs or ensuring each one contributes a *distinct* angle—perhaps one focuses on younger children's inability to consent/dissent, while another focuses on the legal precedent of the Establishment Clause specifically.

Grammatical and Mechanical Issues
"The concept of God is not discourage" should be "is not discouraged."
"encounter any determent" should likely be "detriment," and the phrasing is awkward—consider "should not face any consequence" or similar.
"Because America's vast religious diversity and the lack of apparent good..." is a sentence fragment missing a verb (eg, "Because America has vast religious diversity and..."). Proofread your final paragraph carefully, as concluding paragraphs deserve extra polish since they're often what readers remember.

Strengthen the Legal Argument
You mention "constitutional law" in your introduction but never develop this legally grounded objection with specificity. A brief reference to *Engel v. Vitale* (1962) or the Establishment Clause of the First Amendment would lend academic weight and show you're engaging with the actual legal history behind this debate, not just moral or social reasoning.

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all placeholders with accurately sourced, quoted material before doing anything else.
Add one legal citation to ground your argument in constitutional precedent, not just ethical reasoning.
Consolidate paragraphs 5 and 6 or differentiate their arguments more clearly.
Revisit your own use of historical evidence in paragraph three to ensure it doesn't fall into the same causal fallacy you criticize.
Proofread thoroughly, especially the concluding paragraph, for grammar and sentence completeness.

With these revisions—especially completing the evidentiary gaps—this essay could become a much more rigorous and persuasive piece. The bones of a strong argument are here; it now needs the specific evidence and polish to support that structure fully.

Feedback Report: AIDS Discrimination Essay

Overall Impression

However, the essay currently reads more like a draft than a finished piece—there are missing citations, some significant logical slips, and moments where emotional appeal substitutes for argument. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Opening Claims Need Support

Your first sentence—"AIDS is the most common disease of our world today"—is both unsupported and factually questionable. This is a risky way to open an argumentative essay, since a skeptical reader will doubt your credibility from the first line. Consider replacing this with a claim you can actually document, or simply cut it and begin with your stronger point about how AIDS is transmitted.

Missing Quotations

The essay is full of  placeholders where quotations should appear. Before you submit this, every one of these needs to be filled in with the actual quoted material and a proper citation (author, publication, date). Right now, a reader cannot evaluate your evidence at all, which undermines the entire evidentiary structure of your argument. Make sure you're using a consistent citation style (MLA is standard for this kind of essay) and that in-text citations point to a Works Cited page.

A Critical Logical Error

In the paragraph addressing the health-risk argument, you write: "This is more than enough proof that HIV, and later AIDS, is not, and cannot be transmitted through casual contact. Therefore, co-workers, patients, friends, family, or anyone else in close contact with the victim is in danger of contracting the disease from everyday occurrences, or contact."

This sentence says the *opposite* of what you mean. If it cannot be transmitted through casual contact, then people are not in danger. This is likely a drafting slip, but it appears in one of your most important paragraphs—the one refuting the employers' central argument—so it needs to be fixed immediately. Read your body paragraphs aloud during revision to catch errors like this.

A Self-Undermining Concession

In your final substantive paragraph, you write that the government's classification of AIDS as a disability "is not true, but unfortunately it's the only way to legally keep employers and insurance companies from willfully discriminating." This sentence concedes ground you don't need to give up, and it weakens your argument right before the conclusion. If you believe the ADA's framing is inaccurate, you need to explain why and offer an alternative—otherwise, simply drop this qualification and let the legal protection stand as evidence in your favor.

Characterizing the Opposition

You describe the opposing argument as "weak" and its supporters' position as "a fallacy" before you've actually presented or examined that argument in the essay's body. This is an assertion, not a demonstration. Give the opposition's reasoning a fair, clear restatement (health risk, financial risk to insurers) *before* you dismantle it—you actually do this later in the essay, so consider moving your framing language to align with where the real refutation occurs, rather than asserting victory in the introduction.

Repetitive Phrasing

Phrases like "One can clearly conclude from the evidence" appear multiple times almost verbatim. Vary your transitions and avoid telling the reader what to conclude—show them through the evidence and let the conclusion follow naturally.

Proofreading

There are several typos and inconsistencies to clean up:
"Wookley" vs. "Woodley"
"Norwood" vs. "Underwood" (you cite the same author two different ways)
"A AIDS" should be "AIDS"
"fays" should be "days"
"hospice car" should be "hospice care"

A careful proofreading pass will catch most of these.

Strengthen the Cancer Comparison

Your comparison between AIDS patients and cancer patients is one of the most effective emotional appeals in the essay. Consider developing it further—perhaps with a brief statistic or example—rather than leaving it as a rhetorical question. This would give your pathos appeal more substance to match your legal arguments.

Suggested Revision Priorities

Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Fix the "in danger" logical reversal—this is urgent.
Remove or rework the self-undermining ADA concession.
Proofread for names, spelling, and repeated phrasing.
Reconsider your opening claim and strengthen your framing of the opposition.

You have real substance here—strong case studies and a clear moral stance. The revision work is mostly about precision: making sure every sentence says exactly what you mean and every claim is backed by a properly cited source.

Overall Impression

You also do real work that many argumentative essays skip—you name the two main counterarguments (family breakup and cost) early on. That's a strong foundation. The revision now needs to focus on three things: developing your evidence, tightening your organization, and cleaning up mechanics.

The Missing Evidence Is the Biggest Problem

Throughout the draft, you've left placeholders () where quotations should go. I understand these may be gaps you intended to fill later, but as it stands, the essay's persuasive power depends entirely on words that aren't there. Right now, a reader has to take your interpretations on faith ("stories such as these certainly put a hold on the idea of family preservation") without ever seeing the stories themselves. When you insert the actual quotations, don't just drop them in—introduce them, and then explain what they mean for your argument. A good pattern is: (1) set up the context, (2) give the quotation, (3) unpack it in your own words, (4) connect it explicitly back to your thesis. Right now steps 3 and 4 are often missing even where quotes exist (eg, the Zmora paragraph).

Thesis and Roadmap

Your thesis—"orphanages should be reinstituted"—is sound, but it's buried at the end of a long, meandering introduction that spends more time describing the opposition than previewing your own argument. Consider tightening the intro so the reader knows by the second or third sentence what debate you're entering and what your position is. You might also add a brief roadmap sentence (eg, "This essay will show that concerns about family separation and cost are outweighed by evidence of orphanages' historical success and the failures of foster care") to help guide readers through your four main sections.

Paragraph Organization

The second paragraph (historical background) is your weakest structurally. It jumps between the "dehumanization" critique, the 1960s "social control" theory, and Zmora's counter-study without clear transitions or a topic sentence that tells the reader why this history matters to your argument. Consider splitting this into two paragraphs: one that fairly presents the historical critique, and one that presents the revisionist evidence (Zmora, Samuel Ready School) as a rebuttal. Make the rebuttal relationship explicit with a transition like "However, more recent scholarship complicates this narrative."

Similarly, the foster care paragraph tries to do too much at once—it covers homelessness statistics, foster care's psychological limits, feelings of neglect, and testimonials, all in one block. Breaking this into two paragraphs (one on foster care's documented failures, one on orphanages as a comparative alternative) would let each point breathe.

You promise in the introduction to address both the "breakup of families" and "too costly" objections, and you devote a full paragraph to the former. But the cost argument gets only a single, thin paragraph near the end, and it doesn't engage with any actual figures or opposing claims about expense. Since you flagged this as one of the two central objections, readers will expect a more substantial rebuttal—ideally with real numbers (Gingrich-era welfare figures, comparative costs of foster care vs. orphanages, etc).

The Conclusion Needs to Do More Work

Right now the conclusion mostly restates the plan (Cooper's three criteria) and asserts urgency ("something needs to be done, and soon") without synthesizing your argument or addressing the strongest objection one more time. Consider ending with a sentence that returns to your opening image (Oliver Twist/Little Orphan Annie) to show how far the argument has moved from that stereotype—this would give the essay a satisfying sense of closure.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

A few recurring errors to fix in revision:
Apostrophes/possessives: "publics attention" → "public's attention"; "it's own" → "its own."
Spelling/word choice: "no where" → "nowhere"; "and alternative" → "an alternative"; "difficult to content with" → "difficult to contend with."
Capitalization after quotations: "with foster care, there is also..." needs a capital W after the period.
Run-on/comma splices: eg, "Orphanages, like many other institutions, have been around for hundreds of years. Stated by..." — reads awkwardly; revise so the attribution introduces the quote grammatically ("As Nicholas Scoppetta... states...").

Priority for Revision

Insert and *analyze* your quotations.
Split the overloaded paragraphs (history, foster care).
Strengthen the cost counterargument.
Proofread for the mechanical errors listed above.

You have a clear, arguable position and good instincts for using expert testimony—now the essay needs the connective tissue (explanation, transitions, and a fuller counterargument) to make that testimony persuasive.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay engages with an interesting and worthwhile question—whether business is fundamentally about relationships and trust rather than profit alone—and you bring in a reasonable range of sources to support this claim. However, the essay in its current form has significant structural, logical, and mechanical issues that need attention before it can be considered polished academic writing.

Missing Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)

The most pressing problem is that nearly every piece of supporting evidence is represented by a placeholder () rather than actual quoted or paraphrased material. Sentences like "According to Fortune, " or "The case states that " leave your reader unable to evaluate whether your evidence actually supports your claims. Before revising anything else, you must go back to your sources and insert the specific language, statistics, or case details you intend to cite. Right now, the essay is essentially a skeleton of an argument—the connective tissue (your own analysis) is present, but the bones (evidence) are missing. This should be your first priority in revision.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your thesis—that business ethics is fundamentally about relationships, respect, and trust rather than profit alone—is a defensible and interesting claim, but it needs to be stated more precisely and early, ideally as a single, clear sentence at the end of your introduction. Currently, the introduction moves through several related but distinct ideas (what business is, what ethics is, how they intersect, and the role of trust) without committing to a specific, arguable claim. Try condensing this into one sentence: something like, "This essay argues that ethical business practice ultimately depends on maintaining trust-based relationships among producers, consumers, and employees, rather than pursuing profit as an isolated goal."

Additionally, your definition of ethics ("Ethics are the goals and actions people ought to perform to do what is good or right for human beings") is somewhat circular and could be sharpened by citing a specific philosophical or business-ethics source.

The Counterargument Problem

The B.F. Goodrich Co. example is introduced as a *counterargument* ("To contrast my claim...") but your analysis of it actually *supports* your thesis (the company built a relationship first and profited later). This is a significant logical inconsistency. If Goodrich's strategy aligns with your argument about relationships mattering more than immediate profit, it cannot function as a counterexample. Either (a) find a genuine counterargument—a case where prioritizing relationships failed or where profit-first thinking succeeded ethically—or (b) reframe Goodrich as further supporting evidence rather than a contrasting case. A strong essay needs an authentic counterargument that you then rebut; simply mislabeling supporting evidence as opposition weakens your credibility.

Repetition and Tone

Several passages repeat the same idea in slightly different words without advancing the argument, particularly around the claim that some business people "are just out for themselves" and "don't care about relationships as long as they get their money." This phrase (or close variants) appears at least three times. Academic writing benefits from variety and precision—each paragraph should add a *new* dimension to the argument rather than restating the same point. Consider consolidating these repetitions into one strong, well-developed paragraph.

Additionally, the tone occasionally becomes moralistic or informal ("This is what irritates business...", "they are just greedy and out for self satisfaction"). Academic argument is more persuasive when it relies on evidence and reasoning rather than emotionally charged characterizations of "greedy" business people. Aim for analytical distance even when making a normative claim.

Paragraph Structure and Transitions

Each paragraph tends to introduce a new example (Fortune magazine, Sam Walton, advertising slogans, Norton's article, Goodrich) without clearly linking back to a developing thread of argument. Consider using topic sentences that explicitly connect each example to your central claim, and transitional phrases that show how each new example builds on—rather than simply follows—the previous one. Right now the essay reads as a list of examples rather than a cumulative argument.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

"Ethics are" should be "Ethics is" (treating "ethics" as a singular field of study) — though usage varies, be consistent.
"just make it seem more intriguing" and similar phrases could be tightened for concision.
Watch for run-on constructions, eg, "the producers are lying or making their product seem better than it really is just to make money in sales and a greater profit even though they are lying to their consumers by making their product seem more intriguing" — this sentence repeats "lying" and "intriguing" ideas twice; break it into two clearer sentences.
The conclusion's claim that "Money can not buy you friends, health or happiness" is a nice rhetorical flourish but reads as a cliché; consider replacing it with a more specific, evidence-based closing statement that ties back to your three examples (Fortune data, Walton, Goodrich).

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all missing citations with actual source material.
Rewrite your thesis as one clear, arguable sentence.
Either revise or replace the Goodrich "counterargument."
Cut repetitive phrasing and consolidate overlapping points.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs to show cumulative argument-building.

With the evidence in place and the logical inconsistency resolved, this essay has real potential—your instinct that business ethics hinges on trust and relationships, not just profit, is a strong and defensible claim.
