Feedback on Draft: Prayer in Public Schools Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting rhetorical comparison between two arguments, and your organizational structure—introducing each author's claim, then evaluating supporting evidence—is a sound approach to argument analysis. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas before it will be persuasive: missing textual evidence, conceptual clarity about what counts as "fact" versus "opinion," grammatical polish, and a more balanced, analytical tone.

Missing Quotations

This is the most urgent issue. Your essay contains numerous  placeholders where quotations should appear. As it stands, a reader cannot evaluate your claims because the evidence you're discussing is literally absent. Every time you say "he states, ," you are asking your reader to trust your interpretation without seeing the primary source. Before revising anything else, insert the actual quotations from Shriver and Methvin's texts, and make sure each one is properly introduced (with context) and followed by *your analysis* of why it succeeds or fails.

Conceptual Confusion: "Facts" vs. "Opinions"

Your thesis rests on the claim that Shriver uses "facts" while Methvin relies on "opinions." But looking at your own description of the essay's content, both authors are making value-laden claims about religious liberty, theology, and constitutional interpretation—this is inherently a debate of interpretation, not empirical fact. When Shriver quotes the Bible or reasons through theological points, that is not "fact" in the same sense as statistical data; it's a different *rhetorical strategy* (appeal to shared values, use of authoritative text) than Methvin's appeal to survey respondents. I'd encourage you to reframe your analysis in terms of rhetorical strategy and effectiveness rather than a simplistic fact/opinion binary, which currently makes your argument seem more black-and-white than the material warrants.

Fairness and Balance

Right now, the essay reads as though you decided Shriver was correct before you began the analysis, and then found reasons to support that conclusion. For instance, you criticize Methvin's use of an "authority figure" as a fallacy of unqualified authority, but you don't tell us *why* the person is unqualified—you simply assert it. Meanwhile, you praise Shriver's Bible quotation as "supporting his reasoning," without asking whether quoting scripture to argue for excluding prayer from schools might itself be a rhetorical move worth scrutinizing (using religious authority to argue against religious expression). A stronger essay would apply your evaluative criteria (clarity of claim, fallacies, use of evidence, engagement with opposition) with the same rigor to both authors, and let the evidence—not your framing—lead the reader to a conclusion.

The Historical Framing in Your Introduction

Your opening paragraph makes several large historical claims: that "the founders learned this lesson through war and consequence," that "people... learned through war" that church and state don't mix, and that the union of church and state "ultimately results" in persecution. These are common claims, but they need at least brief support or citation, since a lot of historical work is being asked to do a great deal in a few sentences. Consider trimming this section and moving more quickly to your actual textual analysis, which is where your essay's real strength lies.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are a number of sentence-level errors that need cleanup:
"religion... continues to be influential factor" → needs "an"
"people and learned through war" → likely "have learned"
"This ruling ha become" → "has"
"A research for his argument he conducted" → this sentence needs to be restructured entirely; it's currently unclear
"praying has should not be allowed" → remove "has"
"momen tum" → "momentum" (spacing error)
"it is not necessary to bring it into public places" — check whether this accurately paraphrases Shriver, or whether it's your inference
"In the case of Shriver and Methvin, Shriver delivers..." — slightly awkward parallel structure

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, paragraph by paragraph, to catch these small errors, and running a careful proofreading pass separate from your revision for content.

Repetition

Phrases like "supporting his claim," "strong claim," and "provides factual information" recur many times without much variation. Once you've established your evaluative criteria early on, you don't need to restate them in nearly identical language in every paragraph. Vary your vocabulary and, where possible, let the quotations and your analysis of them do more of the work, rather than repeatedly asserting "this is strong" or "this is weak."

Suggested Next Steps

Insert and properly introduce all missing quotations.
Reconsider the "fact vs. opinion" framework—shift toward rhetorical strategy and effectiveness.
Apply your critical criteria evenly to both authors.
Tighten the introduction; support or cut sweeping historical claims.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spacing, and syntax errors.
Reduce repetitive phrasing.

With these revisions, you'll have a much more rigorous, credible piece of rhetorical analysis.

Your essay tackles an interesting and genuinely debatable issue—media access to executions—and you've done solid research to ground your argument in a real case. Below are suggestions organized by priority to help you strengthen the essay.

This is a strong overstatement that opponents could easily attack—surely there are things the public wants to know that don't constitute a *right* to know (eg, private medical records, ongoing investigations). Logical inconsistency: Your final paragraphs argue that filming should be banned for safety reasons, but reporters with "notebooks and pencils" should be allowed. This is a reasonable and nuanced position, but it sits awkwardly with your introduction, which frames the issue as simply "media should have access to report on executions." Consider revising your thesis to state this more precise, two-part position from the start: (1) media presence should be guaranteed, but (2) recording devices may be restricted for legitimate security reasons. This would make your essay's structure feel more purposeful, since you're currently building toward this nuanced conclusion without signaling it early on.

Sentence-Level Issues

"However, there access should be restricted" → should be "their access"
"One such case which receive national attention" → "received"
"the warden, Vasquez, had made to effort to justify" → likely means "made no effort"
"inmate are more tense" → "inmates"
Several sentences are quite long and could be split for clarity, particularly in the paragraph describing the lawsuit (the sentence listing five organizations, for example, could be shortened or moved to a footnote-style aside).

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all bracketed quotations with actual source material or properly paraphrased content, with citations.
Revise your thesis to reflect the more nuanced position you actually argue for (access yes, filming no).
Cut or condense the case-history sections that don't directly serve your argument.
Expand your engagement with the counterargument—explain *why* access should be protected despite legitimate concerns about media motives.
Proofread carefully for the grammatical errors noted above.

Once revised, this essay has the potential to make a compelling, well-supported case. The core argument—that access and recording are separable issues—is a sophisticated position; make sure your structure and thesis reflect that sophistication from the start.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic with genuine personal stakes, and your willingness to share your own story gives this essay a voice that many argumentative papers lack. That said, the essay currently reads more like a personal narrative with an argument attached than a fully developed argumentative essay. Below are areas to focus on for revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Your thesis appears at the end of the first paragraph: schools need to teach "morals and values" around sexual relationships. This is a reasonable claim, but it's muddled by an unclear stance on *what* those morals should be. You advocate for abstinence-until-marriage values *and* condom use in the same breath—these are often seen as competing philosophies in sex education debates. Decide which argument you're making:
Are you arguing for abstinence-based education?
Are you arguing for comprehensive sex education that includes both abstinence and safe-sex practices?

Right now the essay tries to do both, which weakens your position. Pick a clear stance and make sure every paragraph supports it.

Missing Evidence

You have several placeholder markers () where statistics or research should go. These are currently empty, which means your key claims—about STD rates, divorce statistics, and study results on sex education—are unsupported. This is the most urgent issue to fix. Before revising further, locate real sources:
Look for peer-reviewed studies or reputable organizations (CDC, Guttmacher Institute, Kaiser Family Foundation) on sex education outcomes.
Cite specific statistics with proper attribution, not just "one article researched."
Make sure the numbers you use actually support your argument (eg, does comprehensive sex education correlate with *lower* teen pregnancy rates? This would strengthen your case if framed correctly).

Personal Narrative vs. Argument

Your opening story is compelling, but consider its function. Right now, the essay implies that *because* you didn't get comprehensive sex education, you ended up in a difficult marriage—but the causal link isn't explicitly argued. You also editorialize within the narrative ("Casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading, animalistic...") before you've built any argumentative support for that claim. This judgment feels inserted rather than earned.

Suggestion: Use the narrative to illustrate a *specific* gap (eg, lack of parental communication, absence of guidance on decision-making) and connect it more precisely to your thesis. Save value-laden judgments ("degrading," "animalistic") for later, after you've built a case—or reconsider whether such loaded language belongs in an academic essay at all, since it may alienate readers who don't share your religious/moral framework.

Structural and Paragraph-Level Issues

Several paragraphs raise a claim without developing it fully:
The paragraph on "studies show" sex education is effective is only two sentences after the missing statistic—expand this significantly. This should be one of your strongest paragraphs, not a placeholder.
The counterargument paragraph ("There are some that may argue...") is underdeveloped. You gesture at a rebuttal (media saturation) but don't fully engage with *why* parents believe school-based sex-ed is unethical (eg, concerns about parental authority, religious values, age-appropriateness). A stronger essay would state the opposing view fairly before dismantling it.
The paragraph on churches is one sentence with a missing citation—either cut it or develop it with actual evidence.

Tone and Word Choice

Watch for language that shifts from analytical to moralizing:
"Casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading, animalistic" — this is an assertion, not an argument. Consider whether you want to rely on emotionally loaded terms or build a more evidence-based case.
"live happily ever after" and "We were wrong" are conversational but appropriate in the narrative section; make sure this tone doesn't bleed into your more analytical paragraphs.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to address in editing:
Apostrophes: "bird's and the bee's" should be "birds and the bees"; "parental guidance regarding sexual relationships for youths'" has a stray apostrophe.
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "Casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading, animalistic, and runs the risk of disease" needs revision (also "its" should be "it's").
Capitalization: "my Father" should be lowercase unless used as a direct address; "Him" (referring to your son) should be lowercase.
Consistent tense: You shift between past narrative and present-tense argument; make sure transitions between these modes are smooth and intentional.

Conclusion

Your ending returns nicely to the personal narrative, which creates a sense of closure. However, it doesn't explicitly restate your argument or call for action. Consider adding a sentence that ties your personal success in raising your son back to the broader policy claim: schools should adopt similar strategies.

Priority Revision Steps
Fill in your missing evidence with real, cited statistics.
Clarify your specific policy position (abstinence-only vs. comprehensive sex ed).
Develop the counterargument more fairly and thoroughly.
Proofread for grammar/mechanics issues listed above.

You have a strong foundation and a genuine motivating story—now it's a matter of building the scaffolding of evidence and argument around it.

You assert that welfare "eroded" family values and caused a rise in crime, but this causal claim needs more support. Is this correlation, or do you have evidence of causation? Readers sympathetic to alternative explanations (structural unemployment, wage stagnation, systemic racism, deindustrialization) will want to see you engage with these possibilities, even briefly, to strengthen your argument's credibility. You don't need to abandon your thesis, but acknowledging complexity will make your case more persuasive to skeptical readers.

Right now the transition from the Hoover history to your personal story to the Gingrich critique feels a bit like separate essays stitched together. The Spring Meadows Example

Minor Mechanical Issues
"It's" should be "its" throughout when used as a possessive (eg, "it's purpose," "it's traditions," "it's position"). This is a consistent error worth fixing with a search-and-replace.
"air programs" in paragraph one appears to be a typo—likely "welfare programs."
"food rent" should probably be "food, rent."

Revise the Gingrich section to critique policy rather than the person.
Add a sentence acknowledging alternative explanations for welfare dependency/crime correlation.
Proofread for it's/its errors.
Consider expanding the Spring Meadows example with specific outcomes or statistics if available.

Your argument has real emotional and logical force—these revisions will help ensure the evidence and structure are as strong as your conviction.

Feedback on Your Euthanasia Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult ethical issue, and you've organized it with a clear thesis and three supporting arguments. However, there are significant issues with evidence, logic, and clarity that need attention before this essay is fully persuasive. Let me walk through the main areas for revision.

Evidence and Factual Accuracy

This is your most pressing concern. Several factual claims need verification or correction:

The "monkey euthanasia" example is vague and unverifiable as presented. Who are these monkeys? What laboratory? What year? Without a citation, this reads as an unsubstantiated anecdote rather than evidence. If this is a real case, cite it properly; if not, replace it with documented evidence.
"Since the ruling on euthanasia took place in 1988" — This claim needs a specific citation. What ruling? Euthanasia's legal status in the U.S. has evolved through multiple cases and state laws (eg, Oregon's Death with Dignity Act in 1997), not a single 1988 ruling. This factual vagueness undermines your credibility.
The placeholder brackets  in your final section are a major problem — it appears you intended to insert quotations but never completed this step. These gaps make your third argument's evidence incomplete and confusing to readers. Fill these in with actual, properly cited quotations before submitting.

Logical Structure

Several of your arguments contain reasoning gaps:

The animal analogy (Argument 1): Comparing public reaction to animal euthanasia with human euthanasia is a weak analogy. Animals cannot consent or communicate suffering the way humans can, and the ethical stakes differ significantly. This comparison needs more justification, or you should choose a more directly relevant example.

The abortion analogy (Argument 2): You acknowledge abortion and euthanasia are "separate and unique" issues, which weakens your own argument before you make it. If they're fundamentally different controversies, why should violence surrounding one predict violence surrounding the other? Strengthen this connection by identifying *why* the ethical structures are similar, not just that both "involve ending a life."

Circular reasoning in Argument 2: You state euthanasia *might* cause future violence, then conclude the law "should be modified" based on this possibility. Speculation about future events is a weak foundation for policy argument. Consider whether you have present-day evidence of harm instead.

Clarity and Mechanics

There are recurring grammatical issues that affect readability:

"there own lives" → "their own lives"
"few people who are thinking... and are also of sane mind" — this sentence's logic is unclear; do you mean *few* people considering suicide are of sane mind? Revise for clarity.
"a live is worth living" → "a life is worth living"
"shaky judgments" and "full of wholes" (should be "holes") — proofread carefully for homophones and typos.
Several sentences are run-ons or lack clear subject-verb agreement (eg, "the doctor and the family has little to say").

I recommend a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading your essay aloud to catch awkward phrasing.

Argumentative Consistency

Your introduction claims euthanasia should not be "practiced as freely as it is," suggesting you support *restriction* rather than *elimination*. Yet your conclusion asks whether it's "worth losing lives... to follow a few others to lose their lives to euthanasia," which sounds like you're arguing for elimination. Decide clearly: are you arguing for tighter regulation (more family involvement, competency evaluations) or for banning the practice? Your strongest material (the point about family involvement and patient competency) supports *regulation*, so consider aligning your thesis and conclusion with this more defensible, moderate position.

Suggestions for Revision

Verify or replace weak evidence, especially the monkey example and the 1988 ruling claim.
Complete all placeholder quotations — these are currently missing and central to your third argument.
Strengthen the logical connections between your analogies (animals, abortion) and euthanasia specifically.
Clarify your actual policy position — regulation vs. elimination — and ensure your thesis and conclusion match.
Proofread thoroughly for grammar and word errors.

Your strongest section is the third argument, where you engage directly with the mechanics of the law and offer a concrete policy suggestion (family intervention rights). Consider expanding this section and using it as the anchor for a more focused, moderate thesis. This would give your essay a clearer, more defensible argumentative core.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely compelling ethical question, and the anecdote about Amy is an effective hook that personalizes an otherwise abstract policy debate. You've also clearly done research and are attempting to use a sophisticated analytical framework (distinguishing "value" arguments from "consequence" arguments). Before revising anything else, fill in every quotation with the actual source material, properly cited (author, publication, page if available).

Define these terms explicitly the first time you use them so a general reader can follow your reasoning. Several paragraphs drift from their stated purpose. For example, the paragraph on Owensboro/Rainey Bethea begins by promising a "specific instance," but then shifts into general claims about violence and Sherman's separate argument. You state their positions but don't explain how each supports or complicates your thesis. Since this comes near the end of the essay, it weakens your overall argument at exactly the point where you should be reinforcing it. "usual routing" → "usual routine"
"in the be drooms" → "in the bedrooms"
Random lowercase "pat Clark" → "Pat Clark"
"effect their ratings" → "affect their ratings"
Repetitive phrasing: "is not a valid argument because only one aspect is being considered and is therefore not a valid argument" repeats itself redundantly—cut the repetition.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Abortion and Morality

Thank you for tackling such a difficult and high-stakes topic. You've clearly done research and have a strong personal conviction, which comes through in your writing. However, the essay needs significant revision before it will be persuasive to a skeptical academic reader. Below are the main areas to address.

Missing Evidence
The most urgent issue is that many of your quotations and statistics appear as blank placeholders (). A reader cannot evaluate your argument without seeing the actual evidence from Callahan, Grisez, Liley, Dr. Hanley, and the Hippocratic Oath. Since your entire argument rests on "biological proof" and expert testimony, these gaps are fatal to your credibility as it stands. Before revising anything else, insert the complete quotations, provide full citations (author, title, page number), and make sure each source is introduced with enough context (who is this person, why should we trust them?).

One-Sided Argument Despite Claiming Balance
You open by describing both pro-choice and pro-life positions fairly, which suggests you intend a balanced analysis. However, the essay quickly abandons pro-choice reasoning altogether and never seriously engages with it again. A strong argumentative essay must represent the opposing view accurately and then explain *why* it fails—not simply assert your own position more forcefully. Right now, the "woman's right to privacy/bodily autonomy" argument is dismissed in a single sentence ("the fetus is not part of the mother's body") without addressing the actual philosophical and legal reasoning behind bodily autonomy (eg, even if the fetus is a separate human life, does that automatically obligate the mother to sustain it?). Engaging with this stronger version of the opposing argument—and refuting it—will make your essay far more convincing.

Logical Structure and Fallacies
Several claims are asserted as self-evident rather than argued:
The move from "the fetus is alive and human" to "abortion is murder" skips a crucial step. Legally and philosophically, "killing" and "murder" are not automatically equivalent—murder implies an *unjustified* killing. You need to argue why abortion is unjustified, not just that it ends a human life.
The claim that 98% of abortions are not medical necessities needs a citation; as written, it functions as an unsupported assertion used to dismiss opposing cases entirely.
The rhetorical questions ("Can one really justify...?" "Is it not then morally wrong...?") function as loaded questions rather than argument. Loaded/rhetorical questions can feel manipulative to readers who don't already agree with you. Convert these into stated claims with supporting reasoning.

Tone and Word Choice
Words like "senseless killing," "turn their backs," and "sworn... oath" build emotional intensity but can alienate readers who don't already share your view. Academic argument is more persuasive when it relies on calm, precise reasoning rather than charged language. Consider revising to a more measured, analytical tone—your evidence is stronger than your rhetoric needs to be.

Organization
Your paragraph structure is logical (biological proof of life → proof of separateness → conclusion of murder → doctor's ethical responsibility → summary of debate), which is a good skeleton. To improve it:
Add clear topic sentences that state the claim of each paragraph before diving into evidence.
Use transitions that show *logical* relationships, not just sequence ("Furthermore," "In addition") — explain *why* one point follows from another.
The final paragraph introduces a new claim (Callahan's assertion about the nature of the debate) that shifts the essay's focus from "abortion is immoral" to "this debate is intractable." This is a significant tonal and argumentative shift that needs to be set up earlier, or removed.

The Conclusion
The essay ends abruptly with "You can't legislate morality," which is a striking claim but contradicts your entire thesis (you've spent the essay legislating exactly this kind of moral judgment). If you want to end on this note, you need to reconcile it with your argument—perhaps by clarifying that you believe moral truth exists independent of law, or that law *should* reflect moral truth even if enforcement is imperfect. As written, this final line undercuts your own position rather than reinforcing it.

Next Steps
Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Add a paragraph that seriously engages the strongest pro-choice argument (bodily autonomy) and rebuts it directly.
Revise rhetorical questions into argued claims.
Tone down emotionally charged language.
Reconsider your conclusion so it aligns with, rather than undermines, your thesis.

You have the bones of a serious philosophical argument here—now it needs the evidence, logical connective tissue, and rhetorical control to make it persuasive to a wider audience. I'd be happy to discuss revisions further.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0036.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: The Cost of Preserving Life

Overall Impression

You've tackled an ambitious and genuinely provocative topic—challenging the assumption that prolonging life is always an unqualified good. This is the kind of question that makes for compelling argumentative writing, and your willingness to take an unpopular position shows intellectual courage. However, the essay currently has significant problems with logical reasoning, evidence, and tone that undermine your credibility with readers. Below I outline the main areas for revision.

Missing Citations and Evidence

You have several bracketed placeholders () where quotations or paraphrases should appear. These gaps are currently the essay's most visible weakness—they suggest the essay was submitted before revision was complete. Before anything else, you need to fill these in with properly integrated, cited material. Beyond fixing the placeholders, ask yourself: does each piece of evidence actually support the specific claim you're making, or does it just relate loosely to the topic? For example, the Heimer/Bosk material on genetic counseling needs to connect more explicitly to your argument about symptom treatment vs. cure, rather than functioning as a separate mini-essay on parental choice.

The Evolutionary Argument Needs Serious Rethinking

This is the essay's biggest logical vulnerability. You argue that modern medicine is degrading the human gene pool by allowing people with genetic diseases like PKD to survive and reproduce, when "survival of the fittest" would otherwise eliminate these traits. This argument has several problems:

It misapplies evolutionary theory. Natural selection describes what happens in the absence of intervention; it isn't a moral standard for how a society *should* treat its members. Citing Darwin doesn't establish that we *ought* to let genetic diseases run their unmitigated course—that's a naturalistic fallacy (deriving "ought" from "is").
It has troubling implications you don't address. The logical endpoint of this argument is a form of eugenics—the idea that certain people should not survive or reproduce because their genes are "undesirable." You don't need to abandon your broader point about resource allocation, but you should recognize how this reasoning reads to a critical audience and either qualify it heavily or reconsider the argument altogether.
It conflates disease severity with reproductive fitness in modern contexts. Reproductive success today isn't primarily governed by disease survival in the way it was for pre-modern populations; social, economic, and personal factors dominate. This weakens the direct causal claim you're making.

I'd strongly encourage you to either drop this evolutionary framing or substantially qualify it, perhaps by shifting the focus toward resource allocation and quality-of-life questions rather than genetic "fitness."

Tone Toward the Parent in the Bosk Quotation

Calling a parent's decision "selfish" and stating you "see no honor to life" in their choice is a strong moral judgment made without adequate context (especially given the missing quotation). Readers don't yet know what the parent said or the circumstances. Even once you supply the quote, consider that decisions about genetic risk, pregnancy, and disability are deeply personal and ethically complex. A more persuasive approach would examine *why* someone might make that choice and engage with the difficulty of the decision, rather than dismissing it outright. This would also make your suggestion—that such parents should "adopt instead"—land as a reasoned position rather than a moral accusation.

Organizational and Argumentative Clarity

The essay moves through several loosely connected case studies (PKD, genetic counseling, antibiotics, AIDS, Hantavirus) without a clear throughline stating *what claim each example proves*. Consider adding a explicit roadmap in your introduction: are you arguing (a) that resources are misallocated, (b) that medicine harms the species genetically, (c) that human overuse of antibiotics is creating superbugs, or (d) that our psychological relationship to death is unhealthy? Right now the essay gestures at all four without fully committing to how they relate. A clearer thesis statement—one that names your central claim precisely—would help readers track your argument.

The antibiotic-resistance section, for instance, is a strong, well-explained example of a real problem, but it's about *human behavior* (not finishing prescriptions) rather than about medicine's philosophical commitment to preserving life "at any cost." Consider whether this section supports your central thesis or belongs in a separate argument about patient responsibility.

Rhetorical Questions and Direct Address

You use direct address ("you," "your mother told you") effectively to make the antibiotic example vivid, but this conversational tone sits awkwardly next to the more clinical, high-stakes claims about PKD and genetic policy. Consider evening out your register throughout.

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all citation gaps with fully integrated quotations.
Reconsider or heavily qualify the evolutionary/eugenics argument.
Revise the parent-shaming language into a more even-handed ethical discussion.
Write a sharper thesis statement and use it to test whether each section belongs.
Standardize your tone throughout.

This essay has a real argument buried in it—about how we allocate resources and define the value of life—but it needs to be separated from the more ethically fraught genetic-fitness claims to be persuasive.

Feedback Report: Assisted Suicide and Euthanasia Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a genuinely difficult ethical issue and shows real effort to present multiple perspectives fairly. Your instinct to evaluate the *strengths and weaknesses* of each side (rather than simply picking one) is intellectually mature. However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of positions than an argument, and several structural and mechanical issues are undermining its persuasive power. Below are the main areas to revise.

Clarify Your Purpose and Thesis

Right now it's unclear what your essay is actually arguing. Is your thesis that *both sides have merit and society needs a "median"*? If so, state this explicitly and early, and make sure every paragraph works toward that specific claim. Currently the introduction ends with a vague statement that both sides "have definite strengths and weaknesses in the reasoning of their arguments"—this is descriptive, not argumentative. Ask yourself: what is the *so what*? What do you want the reader to conclude or do differently after reading this? A stronger thesis might commit to a specific "median" position (eg, legal safeguards, restricting eligibility, mandatory waiting periods) rather than leaving the compromise unnamed.

Fix the Imbalance Between Sides

You devote three full body paragraphs to opponents' arguments (suicide normalization, better alternatives exist, erosion of trust in doctors) but only one, much shorter paragraph to supporters. This imbalance makes the essay feel like it's arguing *against* Kevorkian despite your claimed even-handedness. If your goal is genuine balanced analysis, supporters' arguments need equal development—for instance, autonomy/self-determination, quality of life versus quantity, and the argument that pain relief is itself a form of medical care. Right now supporters are represented almost entirely through an appeal to Kevorkian's sincerity and a comparison to MLK, which is a weak analogy (civil disobedience for civil rights is not analogous to ending another person's life) and needs far more critical scrutiny than you give it.

Missing Quotations

The placeholders () for Hendin, Saunders, Garcia, Reich, and Kevorkian himself are the backbone of your evidence, and without them a reader cannot evaluate your analysis at all. This is the single most urgent fix. When you insert the actual quotations, make sure to:
Introduce each quote with context (who the person is, why they're credible)
Explain the quote's meaning before analyzing it
Explicitly connect the quote back to your stated point about strength/weakness

Right now several paragraphs assert "this is significant" or "this strongly supports the case" without actually walking through the logic connecting the quote to the claim.

Deepen the Analysis of Strengths/Weaknesses

Your recurring structure—naming a strength, then a weakness—is a good analytical framework, but the weaknesses you identify are often thin. For example: "the reasoning seems a bit steep, like the idea of suicide becoming as popular as fast food" is a good instinct (slippery-slope critique) but underdeveloped. Push further: *why* is this a slippery slope fallacy? Is there empirical evidence from jurisdictions where assisted suicide is legal (Oregon, the Netherlands, Belgium) that either supports or undermines this fear? Real-world evidence would strengthen every section of this essay considerably.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are numerous errors that need correcting in revision:
Capitalization: "when his case was first released" should begin with a capital letter; "Kevorkian" is inconsistently capitalized in places like "a suicide."
Sentence fragments: "While opponents believe that Kevorkian is trying to play God..." is a fragment—join it to the previous sentence.
Typos: "i faith" (should be "in faith"), "af raid" (should be "afraid"), "i the care" (should be "in the care"), "neglection" (should be "negligence" or "failure").
Comma splices: "This is important because this would let people be presented with other options, which could allow them to lead their normal lives and prolong the years that they have left." Consider breaking into two sentences for clarity.
Word choice: "no weaknesses because placing our complete faith in a fellow human being takes a lot of courage" doesn't quite follow logically—courage on the patient's part doesn't make the *argument* weakness-free.

I recommend a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch fragments and missing words.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion restates that both sides have merit but doesn't advance a concrete recommendation. If your thesis is about finding a "median," name what that median looks like—even briefly. End with something the reader can act on or think further about, rather than simply reiterating that the debate is important.

Priority Revision Order
Insert and properly integrate all missing quotations
Develop the supporters' section to match the depth given to opponents
Sharpen your thesis into an actual argumentative claim
Proofread for grammar and mechanics

This essay has a solid analytical skeleton—now it needs balanced evidence and a clearer argumentative spine.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting angle on the corporal punishment debate—rather than arguing for or against the practice itself, you critique the *rhetorical effectiveness* of the opposition's arguments. This is a sophisticated meta-argumentative move, and your thesis is clear: opponents fail due to "ambiguous terminology and a wavering position." Below are suggestions to strengthen the essay.

Strengths

Your opening narrative vignette is engaging and effectively humanizes the abstract issue before you pivot to analysis. The rhetorical questions in your introduction and conclusion create a nice frame for the essay, and your thesis statement is admirably concise and specific.

Areas for Revision

Missing Evidence (Critical Issue)
The placeholder  appears four times where you intended to insert direct quotations from Jambor, Johns and MacNaughton, and Diamantes. This is the most urgent fix—your entire argument rests on demonstrating *inconsistency* and *ambiguity* in these definitions, but without the actual quoted material, readers cannot evaluate your claims. Before revising anything else, insert the specific language from your sources and then explicitly analyze *how* the definitions differ or contradict each other. Don't just quote—unpack the discrepancy for the reader.

Development of the "Wavering Position" Argument
Your second main argument (inconsistency in taking a stance) is thinner than your first. The paragraph about Diamantes/Heitzman needs the actual contradictory quotes and clear analysis connecting them. Similarly, the paragraph on "vacillating on consequences" is vague—you assert that authors are indecisive but don't provide concrete examples. Strengthen this section with specific citations showing the contradiction in action.

Structural and Logical Issues
The paragraph beginning "How is it, then, that anyone would choose to believe..." shifts abruptly into recommendations for opponents to improve their argument. This is useful content, but it currently interrupts your critical analysis. Consider whether this belongs in your conclusion or as a distinct "counterargument/solution" section with clearer transitions.
The claim about "many factors" affecting reader perception (personal experience with paddling, personal definitions) is a good point, but it complicates your thesis. If readers' *prior beliefs* determine their reaction regardless of argument quality, doesn't this undermine your claim that better argumentation would change minds? Address this tension directly rather than letting it sit unexamined.

Historical Statistic Needs Context
The 1988 statistic about states banning corporal punishment is useful but oddly placed near your conclusion, feels disconnected from your rhetorical critique, and is now quite dated. Consider either updating this with current data or moving it earlier to establish stakes, or cutting it if it doesn't serve your central argument about rhetorical effectiveness.

Tone and Framing
The recurring "Tommy" motif and direct-address questions ("Were you a tommy?") are stylistically bold but risk feeling gimmicky by the third repetition. Consider using this device once more sparingly—perhaps only in the introduction and conclusion—to keep it fresh and impactful rather than repetitive.

Line-Level Edits

"took he seat" → "took his seat"
"unteeteringly" is not a standard word; consider "unwaveringly" or "without wavering"
"several of the authors fail to make any attempt at defining" — this sentence is a comma splice / fragment; revise to "The authors who define the term do not apply the same definition, and several fail to attempt a definition at all."
"subjected the paddle" → "subjected to the paddle"
Watch for shifts in verb tense, particularly in the historical/statistical paragraph

Overall Assessment

Your critique of definitional ambiguity is a strong, defensible claim once supported with actual textual evidence. Your argument would benefit from:
Filling in all missing quotations immediately
Providing explicit analysis of *why* each quoted definition or claim is contradictory or vague (don't assume readers will see it themselves)
Tightening the structure so your two main claims (ambiguous terminology, wavering position) receive parallel, comparably developed treatment
Clarifying the relationship between your critique and your brief suggestions for improvement—are these separate sections, or does the second flow naturally from the first?

With the source material properly integrated, this essay has real potential to make a compelling case about argumentative rigor in a genuinely fraught policy debate.

Feedback on Your Essay: Censorship and Television

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic, and you've clearly done research to support your position, incorporating multiple sources like Aletha Huston's study, Walter Berns' article, and Ingrid Groller's survey. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas to strengthen its argumentative power and clarity. Below are key areas for improvement.

Clarify Your Rhetorical Stance

The most confusing aspect of this essay is your relationship to the argument you're presenting. You repeatedly refer to "the advocates for censorship" and "the proponents" in third person, as if you are summarizing someone else's argument rather than making your own case. Phrases like "the advocates effectively prove" and "by the advocates depicting this show, they diminish..." create distance between you and your thesis. 

Is this your argument, or are you analyzing someone else's rhetoric? If this is your own position, write in first person or simply assert your claims directly: "This case study demonstrates..." rather than "the advocates use this case study to demonstrate..." This shift will make your essay feel more confident and less like a report on other people's arguments.

Strengthen Your Thesis Statement

Your introduction needs a clear, single-sentence thesis that states your position unambiguously. Currently, the opening paragraph describes examples of television content and defines censorship, but doesn't crystallize into a strong claim until buried phrases like "we need censorship" appear. Consider opening with something like: "The decline of television censorship over the past several decades has produced measurable harm to children's behavior and desensitized American audiences to violence and sexual content; therefore, stricter censorship standards must be restored."

Fix Grammatical and Mechanical Errors

There are numerous errors that need correction:
"advocates for censorship know that an increase in censorships is needed" (subject-verb and word choice issues)
"diminish" is spelled "deminish," "caniving" should be "conniving," "peak" should be "pique," "bout" should be "about," "your" vs. "you're" confusion
"Psychologist, Aletha Huston" doesn't need a comma after "Psychologist"
"if censorship does not increase the networks will continue" needs a comma after "increase"
Missing citations indicated by "" need to be filled in with actual quotes/titles

I'd recommend reading the essay aloud or using grammar-checking software, then doing a careful proofreading pass focused solely on mechanics.

Address the Counterargument More Substantively

Your treatment of the opposing view (that parents should simply turn off the TV) is a good instinct—strong essays acknowledge counterarguments. However, this section feels underdeveloped and appears late in the essay (paragraph 6) rather than being woven throughout. Consider:
Introducing this counterargument earlier, perhaps in your second paragraph, so readers know from the start what tensions you're navigating
Expanding your rebuttal with more evidence, not just logical assertions about parental negligence
Also addressing the First Amendment argument (Jack Valenti's point) more directly—you mention it but don't fully counter it. Why should content regulation not violate free speech principles? This is a crucial gap.

Improve Paragraph Organization and Transitions

Several paragraphs contain repetitive phrasing ("the advocates for censorship realize...") that makes the essay feel circular rather than progressive. Each paragraph should advance a *new* point building toward your conclusion. Consider outlining your essay's structure:
Thesis
Evidence children are influenced by media (Huston study, Power Rangers example)
Evidence of sexual content's impact (Grace Under Fire, NYPD Blue)
Counterargument and rebuttal
Evidence of societal desensitization (Mapplethorpe, Florida jury case)
Public opinion evidence (Groller survey)
Conclusion

Restructuring with this kind of clear progression—rather than returning repeatedly to similar claims—will make your argument feel more cumulative and persuasive.

Complete Your Citations

There are multiple "" placeholders throughout the essay where specific show titles, quotes, or examples should appear. These are essential—readers need concrete evidence, and vague references undermine your credibility. Make sure every source is fully cited (author, publication, date) so readers can verify your claims.

Reconsider the Conclusion

Your conclusion continues the "advocates" framing and introduces a slightly different claim ("we won't have the same civilized society") that isn't fully supported earlier in the essay. Make sure your conclusion synthesizes only the points you've actually argued, and restate your thesis with the confidence of your own voice, not as a report on someone else's position.

Overall Assessment

You have valuable evidence and a clear sense of what you want to argue. Your primary tasks now are (1) claiming ownership of your argument through first-person or direct assertive language, (2) tightening your logical structure so each paragraph builds on the last, (3) more robustly countering opposing views, and (4) polishing grammar and completing citations. With these revisions, this could be a compelling and well-supported argumentative essay.

Feedback on Draft: Genetic Research Argument Essay

This draft shows real engagement with a complex, timely issue, and you've clearly done research into multiple perspectives (Sinsheimer, Maddox, Annas, Aldhous). However, the essay currently has a significant structural problem that undermines its persuasive power: it describes *how one should write* an argument against genetic research rather than *making* that argument. Below are the key issues to address in revision.

Shift from Meta-Commentary to Direct Argument

Throughout the essay, you use phrasing like "One possible way of presenting the most effective argument opposing genetic research... is by doing three doings," "This reference to the Nazis deals with... This obvious atrocity would be effective in an argument," and "Citing his example of the sickle-cell gene would be excellent for this argument." This is instructional/analytical language about argument construction, not argumentation itself. It reads like a set of notes for an essay rather than the essay.

Revision strategy: Commit to the position. Instead of "This reference to the Nazis would be effective," write something like: "Sinsheimer's invocation of Nazi eugenics reminds us that humanity has already shown itself capable of catastrophic abuse of genetic control." Drop the hedging about what "would convince a reader" and simply try to convince the reader. Every time you catch yourself writing "would be effective" or "could strengthen the argument," rewrite the sentence to actually do that work.

Missing Evidence

The essay contains multiple placeholder brackets () where quotations should appear from Sinsheimer, Maddox, Annas, and Aldhous. These are the evidentiary backbone of your argument, and without them, readers cannot evaluate your claims. In the final draft, every one of these needs to be filled in with the actual quotation, properly cited, and followed by your own analysis of how it supports your thesis. Right now the essay is structurally sound as an outline but is not yet a complete essay.

Clarify and Commit to a Thesis

Your introduction sets up genetic research as leading to "profound discovery" with positive framing (curing Huntington's, eliminating disease genes), but the body of the essay pivots to oppose genetic research. This is fine as a rhetorical move (acknowledging the appeal before countering it), but you need an explicit thesis statement that stakes out your position clearly—something like: "Despite its promise, the human genome project poses ethical risks serious enough that genetic research should proceed with extreme caution, if at all." Right now your closest thing to a thesis is buried in the second paragraph as a description of essay-writing strategy ("One possible way of presenting the most effective argument...").

Strengthen the Refutation Sections

Your refutation of pro-research arguments (disease screening, knowledge for its own sake) has good bones—the sickle-cell/malaria point is a strong example—but the paragraphs need to *argue* rather than *summarize what Maddox says and how it could be used*. For example: "What Maddox is saying is that by eliminating potentially harmful genes, the benefits of those genes may be eliminated also" tells us what Maddox argues but doesn't yet make it your argument with your own elaboration and stakes.

Address Counter-Refutations

A stronger essay would anticipate objections to your own points. For instance, on privacy: could genetic information be protected by the same legal frameworks that (imperfectly) protect medical records now? On eugenics: is comparing routine genetic screening to Nazi genocide a fair analogy, or does it risk overstatement? Engaging with these counterpoints—even briefly—will make your argument more credible rather than less.

Editing and Mechanics

"Mendal" should be "Mendel."
"doing three doings" is awkward; revise to something like "taking three steps" or restructure the sentence.
"One consequences deals with privacy" — subject-verb agreement error.
"The question is if genetic information be kept private in " is a fragment/grammatical error—rework this sentence once the quote is inserted.
Several sentences run long and lose clarity (eg, the opening sentence of paragraph 3 has multiple clauses that could be split for readability).
Watch for comma splices and missing articles throughout ("This new knowledge of genetics is leading to a much more profound discovery" is fine, but similar constructions elsewhere need double-checking).

Strengthen the Conclusion

The conclusion currently restates your organizational strategy ("It would show how genetic research... has been harmful in the past, how it could have harmful consequences...") rather than delivering a final, forceful statement of your position. Revise to synthesize your strongest points and leave the reader with a clear call to action or judgment, rather than a summary of your outline.

Overall

You have strong source material and a clear sense of structure. The main task now is to transform this from a plan for an argument into the argument itself—filling in evidence, committing to claims, and writing with conviction rather than describing what a convincing essay would contain. I'd encourage you to revise paragraph by paragraph, asking after each one: "Am I making this argument, or am I just describing how it could be made?"

Feedback on Your Essay: Rhetorical Analysis of Binge Drinking Coverage

Overall Impression

You've chosen a strong, timely topic and a clear comparative framework (context and attitude), and your opening scenario is vivid and effective. The essay demonstrates real engagement with the idea that audience shapes rhetorical choices. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: citation of sources, depth of analysis, organizational efficiency, and sentence-level clarity. Below are specific areas to address.

Missing Citations and Quotations

This is the most significant issue. Several places in your essay contain placeholder brackets () where quotations or statistics should appear—for example, "such as " and "the authors ask ." Without these specifics, your reader cannot evaluate your claims about tone or diction. Since your entire argument rests on close reading of the two articles' language, you must include the actual wording you're analyzing, properly cited (author, title, publication date, page number). Additionally, please include full bibliographic information for both source articles early in the essay—right now we don't know who wrote either piece or when it was published.

Depth of Analysis

Many paragraphs restate the same claim—that JAMA is objective/technical and Newsweek is emotional/sensational—without pushing the analysis further each time. For instance, the "Audience" section and the "Social Context" section largely repeat the same point about JAMA's thoroughness versus Newsweek's selectivity. Consider consolidating these observations and using the space you save to analyze *how* specific word choices, sentence structures, or rhetorical strategies create these effects. For example, when you mention Newsweek's "startling statistics in bold type," analyze why bold type functions rhetorically—what visual/cognitive effect does it produce on a reader skimming a magazine, as opposed to a researcher reading a data table?

Also be cautious with unsupported psychological claims, such as "people are for some reason drawn to look at the dark side of issues" and "the general public enjoys reading material that upsets or angers them." These are debatable assertions presented as fact. Either support them with reasoning/evidence or qualify them as your own interpretation.

Organization and Structure

Your five-part structure (introduction, general problem/statistics, audience, physical context, social context, attitude, conclusion) is logical, but the "Audience" section and the "Physical/Social Context" sections overlap considerably. Consider merging your general "Audience" paragraph into your introduction of the "Context" section, since audience *is* the reason for the contextual differences you go on to describe. This would tighten the essay and eliminate repetition.

Watch for transitional sentences that announce future content without adding substance—eg, "Also, how the author uses rhetoric can decide the reaction of an audience, and we will look at that." Try integrating this signal into a topic sentence that also makes a claim, rather than simply promising to discuss something later.

Your conclusion largely repeats the thesis. Use this space instead to synthesize: what does this comparison reveal about media rhetoric and audience more broadly? What are the implications for how readers should approach information about social issues?

Style and Mechanics

There are several recurring grammatical issues to address in revision:

Apostrophes/possessives: "todays," "writers assumptions," "reader's" vs. "readers'" need consistent correction.
Word confusion: "have an affect" should be "have an effect."
Pronoun consistency: You shift between third person ("the writer," "readers") and first-person plural ("we will look at that," "as we have seen"). Choose one voice—academic writing typically favors third person or occasional "I" for your own analysis, but "we" can feel inconsistent if not used deliberately throughout.
Run-on/comma splice issues: Sentences like "This scenario could probably fit the activities outside any dorm..." are fine, but others, such as "They include statistics... and do not just look at those who binge drink," could be tightened for concision.
Vague pronoun references: Phrases like "these people" or "this technique" sometimes lack a clear antecedent—double check each instance.

Strengthening the Thesis

Your thesis—"The writers for Newsweek and JAMA utilize context and attitude to describe their two different viewpoints…which are brought on by the differences in audience"—is a good start, but consider sharpening it to preview *what conclusion* this comparison leads to. Right now it promises description; a stronger thesis would also assert why this matters (eg, "revealing how media outlets shape public understanding of social issues for commercial rather than informational purposes").

Suggested Next Steps

Insert all missing quotations and citations.
Cut redundant paragraphs; merge audience discussion into context analysis.
Deepen analysis of specific rhetorical devices (diction, syntax, visual design).
Revise the conclusion to offer a broader insight, not just a summary.
Proofread carefully for possessives, pronoun consistency, and sentence boundaries.

You have a genuinely interesting comparison here—with revision focused on evidence and depth, this essay can become a much sharper piece of rhetorical analysis.

Feedback on "Abstinence-Only vs. Comprehensive Sex Education" Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done research into this debate and have assembled a range of evidence—studies, quotes, and specific incidents—to work with. However, the essay currently has significant structural and rhetorical problems that prevent your argument from coming through clearly. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Missing Quotations

The most immediate issue is that several key quotations are missing (marked with ), including the opening quote that is supposed to establish the entire controversy. Since your argument depends heavily on *what specific people said*, a reader cannot currently follow or evaluate your reasoning. Before revising anything else, make sure every quotation is filled in and properly cited (author, source, date).

Unclear Thesis and Stance

You need to decide—and state clearly in your introduction—what kind of essay this is. Are you:
neutrally analyzing the *rhetoric* used by both sides in this debate, or
arguing that comprehensive sex education is the better approach?

Right now the essay tries to do both and ends up doing neither well. The body paragraphs are framed as reporting what "opponents" say about "proponents," but your word choices consistently editorialize (see below), and your conclusion abruptly shifts into your own argument ("it is crucial to give correct and comprehensive views"). Choose one approach, state it explicitly in a thesis sentence, and then be consistent about it throughout.

Loaded Language Undermines Objectivity

If your goal is to analyze both sides fairly (or even to argue a position while seeming credible), avoid language that pre-judges the material you haven't yet shown the reader. Phrases like "enraged," "illogical reasoning," "misinformation," "facile reasoning," and "Proponents' support has turned into their own fallacies and misfortunes" tell the reader what conclusion to draw before you've presented the evidence. Let the evidence do that work, or clearly attribute these judgments as the *opponents'* characterization rather than stating them as fact.

Attribution Confusion

Throughout the essay, it's often unclear whether a claim is:
your own analysis,
something "opponents" say, or
something "proponents" say.

For example, in the first paragraph, sentences move between reporting Flores's claim, describing what opponents believe, and stating outcomes as fact ("the rates... would decrease") without clear signals of whose view this is. Use consistent attribution phrases ("Opponents argue that...", "Proponents claim...", "In my view...") so the reader always knows who is speaking.

Organization

Your body paragraphs are organized by *type* of criticism (illogical reasoning, misuse of studies, scare tactics), which is a reasonable structure. However, each paragraph would benefit from:
A clear topic sentence stating the specific critique before diving into examples.
A concluding sentence that ties the example back to your overall thesis, rather than simply restating the opponents' complaint.

Also, watch for paragraphs that end with unsupported generalizations (eg, "The proponents case does not meet the reality of children's full needs")—this needs to follow logically from the evidence given, not just be asserted.

Conclusion

The conclusion introduces new questions ("Should we teach prevention methods in addition to abstinence?") that should really be raised in your introduction. It also makes a sweeping causal claim—"teens will continue to be sexually active"—that isn't adequately supported by the evidence presented. Revise the conclusion so it synthesizes your actual thesis and evidence rather than raising new issues.

Sentence-Level Issues

A few things to clean up in your final draft:
"birthcontrol" → "birth control"
"beared this scrutiny" → "borne this scrutiny" (or "withstood")
"prevelant" → "prevalent"
"teens will not being informed" → "teens will not be informed"
The sentence "since teens know neither of those ideas is true and since, why should teens even listen..." has a grammar/logic break—revise for clarity.
Watch subject-verb agreement (eg, "Proponents' support has turned into their own fallacies" reads awkwardly—consider "Proponents' arguments have turned into liabilities").

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all missing quotations and cite sources properly.
Write a clear thesis statement that specifies your purpose (analysis vs. argument).
Revise word choice to either maintain objectivity or clearly mark opinion as your own.
Add topic sentences to each paragraph that state the specific point being made.
Revise the conclusion to align with your thesis rather than introducing new questions.

With these revisions, you'll have a much clearer, more persuasive, and more credible essay. You clearly have strong research—now it needs a stronger argumentative frame to support it.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Rights of Biological vs. Adoptive Parents

Overall Impression

This essay demonstrates sophisticated analytical thinking. Rather than simply arguing for one side of the biological/adoptive parent debate, you've taken a more complex rhetorical stance: analyzing *how well* each side argues its case using values, consequences, and evidence. This is an ambitious and interesting approach. However, the essay's effectiveness is significantly undermined by several structural and technical issues that need attention before this analytical framework can fully succeed.

Major Issues

Missing Content (Critical)
The most urgent problem is the presence of multiple  placeholders throughout the essay—in paragraphs discussing the Schmidt case, the National Council for Adoption statistic, and the source citation for Ingrassia and Springen's article. These gaps make it impossible to fully evaluate your argument, and they will seriously undermine your credibility with a reader. Before any other revision, ensure all citations, statistics, and quotations are properly inserted. A paper submitted with these placeholders visible will likely be read as unfinished.

Unclear Thesis and Purpose
Your thesis—that neither side argues effectively because they rely on weak values, consequences, and don't attempt to shift public opinion—is intellectually interesting, but it creates a confusing double task for the reader. We are simultaneously trying to understand the *content* of the adoption debate and evaluate the *rhetorical quality* of each side's arguments. Consider being more explicit in your introduction about this meta-argumentative approach so readers know what lens to apply from the start. You might also clarify: what would a *strong* argument on either side actually look like? Without this baseline, "weak" becomes a vague judgment.

Repetitive Structural Pattern
Nearly every paragraph follows the same formula: state the claim, present the example, then note "yet this is weak because..." This becomes predictable and mechanical by the essay's midpoint. Consider varying your paragraph structures—perhaps beginning some paragraphs with the counterargument, or combining shorter, similar points into more synthesized analysis. Also consider whether every single argument on both sides needs equal treatment, or whether some deserve more depth while others could be mentioned more briefly.

Vague and Repetitive Language
Phrases like "weak values," "weak consequences," and "oversimplification" appear so frequently that they lose analytical force. Ask yourself: what specifically makes a value "weak"? Is it because it's stated without support? Because it doesn't address counterarguments? Because it relies on emotion rather than principle? Distinguishing between different *types* of argumentative weakness would sharpen your critique considerably.

Sentence-Level and Clarity Issues

Opening paragraph: The rhetorical question "Will they keep the child... or give another couple that same chance?" is a false binary that doesn't quite match your nuanced thesis. Consider revising to introduce complexity from the start.

Pronoun/antecedent errors: "the fathers weren't even aware of their children's existence. Therefore, they have even more rights because they never signed the adoption papers"—this logic needs clarification. Not signing papers due to lack of knowledge is legally different from a deliberate choice; explain this connection more precisely.

Word choice: "one *could* argue" should be "one *could* argue" (typo: "on could argue" appears in the Jessica DeBoer paragraph)—proofread carefully for such slips.

Vague pronoun reference: In several places, "these proponents" or "this argument" refers back several sentences, creating ambiguity about which side is being discussed. Given how complex your dual-sided analysis already is, precision here is essential.

Strengths to Preserve

Your engagement with specific, real cases (Baby Richard, Baby Jessica) gives the essay concrete grounding.
The final paragraphs on oversimplification and false dilemma are genuinely insightful—you correctly identify that both sides treat dissimilar cases as identical, which is a sophisticated point.
Your conclusion appropriately resists false resolution, acknowledging the genuine difficulty of the ethical question rather than manufacturing an artificial answer.

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all missing citations and data before making further edits.
Write a clearer analytical thesis that explicitly frames your meta-argumentative approach.
Vary paragraph structure to avoid the "claim-example-critique" formula repeating identically each time.
Define your evaluative criteria early on: what exactly makes an argument's values or consequences "strong" versus "weak"?
Consider consolidating some of the shorter, similar-argument paragraphs (eg, the extreme-example paragraphs) to avoid redundancy and allow more space for your strongest insights.
Proofread for pronoun clarity, ensuring readers always know which "side" of the debate you're referring to.

This is a genuinely ambitious essay that engages with the complexity of a difficult ethical issue rather than settling for an easy answer. With attention to these structural and technical issues, your analytical insight will come through much more clearly.

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling topic and you clearly have real research and personal experience to draw on. However, the essay's biggest obstacle right now is structural and mechanical: the numerous "" placeholders suggest that citations, quotations, or transitional sentences were never filled in. Until these gaps are closed, it's impossible to fully evaluate your argument, because much of your evidence and source attribution is literally missing from the text. My feedback below assumes you'll complete these citations, but I also want to flag some deeper issues in argument and organization that will remain even after the placeholders are filled.

Your introduction is vague about what you're actually arguing. You say the authors show that "revamping the academic code and establishing strict honor codes are the two strongest methods," and that fixing their "faults" will increase effectiveness. This suggests you'll argue for improving *both* solutions. But your conclusion instead declares that honor codes are simply "the best way" and that academic code revamping's loopholes are "too strong" to be salvaged. This is a real shift in position, and the essay doesn't acknowledge or justify it.

Revision suggestion: Decide now, before you revise, exactly what you're claiming. If your real position is "honor codes are superior to code revamping," say so directly in your introduction, and structure the body paragraphs as a comparison building toward that conclusion—rather than presenting both solutions as equally strong and only revealing your preference at the end.

Source Use and Attribution

This is the essay's most urgent technical problem. You refer to "the authors," "Mr. Levine of Harvard," and "Hiley Ward" without ever telling us who these authors are, what they wrote, or where. Every claim about UVA's honor code, the "XF" grade, and court-based punishments needs a clear citation (author, source, and ideally a direct quotation or paraphrase introduced with a signal phrase like "According to X..."). Right now the reader cannot distinguish between:

Facts drawn from your sources
Your own analysis of those facts
Your personal anecdotes and opinions

For example, in the honor code paragraph, you move from the UVA description straight into "As a student, I believe..." with no transition marking the shift from evidence to opinion. Signal these shifts explicitly ("This example suggests that..." / "In my own experience...").

Paragraph Development

Several paragraphs introduce an idea and then abandon it before it's fully explained:

The Levine/Harvard sentence ("Mr. Levine of Harvard believes that ") is incomplete, so the peer-pressure counterargument against honor codes is never actually made.
The final body paragraph on court-based punishment asserts that these methods are "unrealistic and irrational" but never explains *why*. What specifically makes lawsuits an inappropriate tool for academic cheating? This needs at least two or three sentences of reasoning.
The faculty-involvement paragraph lists two strategies (support programs, prevention education) but doesn't connect them back to your central argument about honor codes vs. code revamping. Is faculty involvement a third solution, a supplement to one of the other two, or a separate issue? Right now it feels like a detour.

Revision suggestion: For each solution you discuss, follow a consistent pattern: (1) state the solution, (2) give evidence for its strength, (3) give evidence for its weakness, (4) explicitly evaluate—does the weakness outweigh the strength or not, and why? Right now steps 3 and 4 are often missing or underdeveloped.

Clarity and Sentence-Level Issues

A few sentences need tightening:
"ensure and honest academic environment" — likely a typo for "ensure an honest academic environment."
"Some faculty have been to reluctant" — should be "too reluctant."
"the loopholes involved with revamping the academic codes are too strong" is a slightly odd phrase—loopholes are usually described as *significant* or *severe*, not "strong."

Also, watch for repetitive sentence openings ("The authors show...," "The argument for this solution is strong...") which make the essay feel mechanical rather than analytical.

Priority Revisions

Fill in every missing citation with a proper source and quotation/paraphrase.
Clarify your thesis so it matches your conclusion from the start.
Add explicit reasoning wherever you assert something is a "weakness" or "unrealistic"—show your thinking, don't just label it.
Distinguish clearly between source-based evidence and personal anecdote using transitions.
Reconsider whether the faculty-involvement paragraph belongs in this argument, or whether it needs to be tied more directly to your main claim about honor codes.

You have strong raw material here—real research, real examples, real personal investment in the topic. The next draft should focus on making your reasoning visible to the reader at every step.

Feedback Report: Fourth Amendment / O.J. Simpson Essay

Overall Impression

You've taken on a genuinely interesting rhetorical question—how two sides in a high-profile case construct competing ethical arguments about the same set of facts—and your analysis of *how* each side persuades (through emotional appeals, appeals to authority, and character attacks) shows real critical thinking. The middle two sections, where you break down the proposition's and opposition's rhetorical strategies, are the strongest part of the essay. However, the essay needs work in four areas: clarifying its purpose, tightening its structure, completing its evidence, and cleaning up factual and mechanical errors.

Clarify Your Purpose and Thesis

Right now the essay isn't sure what kind of paper it is. Is this:
A rhetorical analysis of how two sides argue an ethical dilemma, or
An argumentative essay taking a position on whether the evidence should have been admitted?

Your opening paragraph moves toward legal/ethical argument ("this is ethical because..."), but by the time you reach your body paragraphs, you're clearly doing rhetorical analysis (examining "emotional statements," appeals to authority, character attacks). Your conclusion then shifts again, treating the admissibility question as unresolved and offering a policy suggestion (get warrants) that doesn't follow from your rhetorical analysis.

Revision suggestion: Decide explicitly that this is a rhetorical analysis essay, and rewrite your thesis to state this clearly in paragraph one: something like, "Both sides in the debate over the admissibility of evidence from Simpson's home construct ethically persuasive—but rhetorically manipulative—arguments, the proposition through emotional identification with the detectives and appeals to judicial authority, the opposition through character assassination and manufactured skepticism." Structural Issues

The essay currently opens with a mix of legal background, ethical positions, and a factual narrative of the murders (paragraph 2) before arriving at your analytical work. Evidence and Quotations

Several places in your draft contain placeholder brackets  where quotations should appear. The victim's name is Ronald Goldman, not "Ronald Goldwin"—this appears both in the intro and needs correcting throughout.
"Los Angelas" should be "Los Angeles" (appears multiple times).
Check capitalization after periods ("one way the opposition tries..." should begin with a capital letter).
The word "portentous" is used oddly in "confronted by a series of portentous questions"—portentous means ominous or foreboding; you likely want something like "pressing" or "urgent" questions. Double-check this word choice against your source.
Watch comma splices and run-on sentences, eg, in the paragraph about Fuhrman's history—several ideas are strung together with commas where periods or conjunctions would improve clarity.

Analytical Depth

Your analysis of *how* the opposition "hijacks" Judge Ito's quote to make it seem supportive of their position is genuinely sharp—this is exactly the kind of rhetorical move you should be highlighting throughout. Similarly, your point about the "double standard" appeal to sympathy for the detectives is insightful. Push this kind of close reading further: rather than just describing what each side does, explain *why* it works rhetorically (eg, appeals to authority carry weight because readers assume expertise; character attacks work because of pre-existing distrust from the Rodney King case, which you smartly note).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fix the missing quotations (urgent).

Remove or better integrate the conclusion's new claim about warrants.
Correct factual/spelling errors (Goldman, Los Angeles).

With these revisions, this essay could move from a solid draft to a genuinely compelling piece of rhetorical analysis. Your instinct for spotting persuasive strategy is strong—now make sure your structure and evidence support that insight consistently.
