Feedback Report: Draft on Rational Suicide

Overall Impression

You've chosen an ambitious and difficult topic, and your central insight—that opponents of suicide often assert strong moral claims without empirical support—is a genuinely interesting critical angle. However, the essay struggles to fully realize this insight because of problems with thesis clarity, structural repetition, and the logic of your own counter-arguments. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Scope

Your opening paragraph is confusing. The claim that suicide "means the end of all problems" followed immediately by "though those problems are usually solved easily" is contradictory and undercuts your credibility before you've made an argument. Cut this or clarify what you mean.

More importantly, your actual thesis doesn't emerge until well into the essay: that opponents' arguments, while intellectually plausible, are unsupported by evidence. State this clearly and early. Also clarify your scope decision: you say you'll examine only the opponents' side "to analyze specific values and consequences," but this rationale is vague. Why does focusing on one side let you analyze values/consequences better? A sentence or two justifying this choice would strengthen your introduction considerably.

Structural Repetition

Nearly every body paragraph follows an identical pattern: introduce an author, paraphrase a quotation, restate what the author "wants to convey" or "intends to show," and conclude that the claim lacks evidence. This formula becomes predictable by the third or fourth repetition, and it flattens the distinctions between your sources—Rauscher, Forbes, and Ingram and Ellis end up sounding interchangeable. Consider varying your approach: sometimes lead with your own analytical point and use the source as support; sometimes put two authors in direct conversation with each other rather than treating them one at a time. Also, since your evaluative refrain ("this is a good argument, but there's no evidence") repeats almost verbatim in every paragraph, look for ways to make each iteration feel like a new discovery rather than a formula.

The Evidence Problem

Your critique—that these authors assert psychological and sociological claims (grief, shame, ostracism, the "cry for help") without data—is a legitimate and interesting rhetorical observation. But note the placeholder brackets () throughout the draft: as it stands, we cannot actually assess whether your paraphrases are fair or whether evidence really is missing, since the quotations themselves aren't there. Filling these in is essential—not just for completeness, but because your entire argument depends on accurately representing what these authors *did* and *didn't* claim.

There's also a deeper issue: when you rebut these authors, you frequently substitute your own unsupported speculation for their unsupported speculation. For example, you counter Rauscher's selfishness argument by suggesting a cancer patient "may be thinking about his family" when contemplating suicide—but this is speculation, not evidence, and it's not clear why your speculation should carry more weight than his. If your standard is "claims need evidence," you must hold your own rebuttals to that standard too, or explicitly acknowledge that you're offering a competing plausible narrative rather than proof. Otherwise, the essay's central critique loses its force.

Logical Consistency

Your discussion of the slippery-slope fallacy in the AIDS/assisted-suicide section is a nice moment of formal logical analysis and shows real critical thinking—expand on this kind of specific, named-fallacy critique elsewhere if you can identify comparable logical gaps (eg, is there a hasty generalization in the "selfishness" argument? An appeal to emotion in the religious argument?). This would diversify your critique beyond the repeated "no evidence" refrain.

Paragraph Cohesion

The short paragraph introducing AIDS and religion feels disconnected—it appears suddenly, makes general claims about HIV transmission, and then pivots to a new source without clearly linking back to your argument about evidence. Either integrate this material more smoothly into the surrounding paragraphs or give it a clearer transactional purpose.

Conclusion

Your ending shifts tone rather abruptly into moral reflection ("suicide will never cease to exist and the best we can do is try to understand it"). This is a reasonable place to end, but it should be prepared for earlier—perhaps by revisiting your scope decision from the introduction and explaining why examining evidence (or its absence) serves the larger goal of understanding suicide "objectively."

Style and Mechanics

Watch for: repeated phrasing ("in one way or another" appears twice in the first paragraph alone), the typo "so lution," and occasional missing words ("When there little safety"). A careful proofreading pass will catch these.

Priority for Revision

Fill in all quotations and verify your paraphrases against them.
Sharpen and relocate your thesis to the introduction.
Vary paragraph structure to avoid formulaic repetition.
Hold your own counter-claims to the same evidentiary standard you demand of your sources.

Overall Impression

This theoretical lens is your essay's strongest asset—it shows real analytical ambition. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: grammar and mechanics, argument structure, and development of ideas. Below I address each.

This is the most urgent area for revision. Errors are frequent enough that they interfere with your credibility and clarity:

Subject-verb agreement: "the worst problem concerning are young people" should be "concerning our young people" or similar; "the attempts are even more phenomenal" is fine, but "there classifications" should be "three classifications" (a likely typo, but check carefully).
Its vs. it's: You consistently write "its" for "it's" (eg, "Being that its a very sensitive... issue"). Proofread specifically for this.

"Appropiatly," "contraversal," "programed": Run a spell-check; these are distracting typos.

Action step: Read your essay aloud, sentence by sentence. Awkward phrasing often becomes obvious when spoken. Consider using a grammar-checking tool as a first pass, then manually verify each flagged issue.

Structural and Organizational Issues

Your essay currently reads as three loosely connected sections: (1) statistics establishing the problem, (2) Durkheim's typology, (3) Marx's conflict theory, and (4) a brief synthesis around depression. To strengthen this:

Sharpen your thesis: Your introduction states suicide is "the worst problem," but you never defend this comparative claim against drugs or crime—you simply assert it. Either build a real argument for why suicide deserves more attention, or reframe your thesis to focus on what you actually do well: explaining *why* adolescent suicide occurs through sociological theory.
Transitions are abrupt: You move from statistics to "During the years of this rise..." without explaining *why* theory matters or what question your theoretical discussion will answer. Add a sentence explicitly framing the shift: "To understand what drives these numbers, we can turn to sociological theory."
The Marx section is underdeveloped: Your connection between Marxian dialectics and adolescent suicide is asserted rather than argued. The link between "dialectics" and "suicidal families" needs a clearer logical bridge—right now the reasoning is implicit and hard to follow. Spell out *how* dialectical tension in society produces the family dysfunction that leads to suicide.
The conclusion is too brief: Your final paragraph ("maybe one has to look backwards...") is a nice rhetorical flourish, but it doesn't synthesize your argument. After introducing depression as a "common ground," you should explain *what this synthesis means*—does it suggest that theory ultimately point toward the same practical interventions? Give the reader a real takeaway.

Development and Evidence

The <R> and  placeholders: I assume these mark citations you intend to insert. Make sure every empirical claim (all your statistics) and every theoretical claim attributed to Durkheim or Marx has a specific, correctly formatted citation. Given how citation-dependent this essay is, accuracy here is essential for credibility.
Case study integration: Your quotations from Rabkin's book are promising but underused. When you write "a young girl... stated that she'd look at everyone around her and feel different and crazy," follow this with analysis: *how* does this illustrate egoistic suicide specifically? Don't just place evidence next to a claim—explain the connection explicitly.
The depression paragraph needs more rigor: Claiming "there are no theories... that argue adolescent suicide isn't a direct result of depression" is a strong, sweeping claim. Either support it with sourcing or soften the language ("Most researchers agree...").
Altruistic suicide: You dismiss this category quickly. Consider: is there truly *no* adolescent altruistic suicide (eg, suicide pacts, ideological/religious motivations, gang-related self-sacrifice)? Even briefly acknowledging complexity here would strengthen your credibility.

Suggested Next Steps

First revision pass: Focus purely on grammar/mechanics—read aloud, use spell-check, fix subject-verb agreement.
Second revision pass: Strengthen transitions between sections; make your comparative claim (worst problem) either provable or reframe the thesis.
Third revision pass: Deepen the Marx section and case-study analysis—don't just present evidence, *interpret* it for your reader.
Final pass: Verify all citations are complete and correctly placed.

You have real analytical instincts here—the choice to use sociological theory to explain a pressing social problem is a smart one. With careful revision, this essay's argument will come through much more clearly.

Feedback Report: Water Pollution Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an ambitious task—connecting water pollution to two sociological theories (Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory)—and shows genuine engagement with the material. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of facts and theory summaries than a unified argument. The most pressing issue is structural: your thesis is not clearly stated, and the connective tissue between your factual content and your theoretical analysis needs strengthening.

Missing Citations

The most urgent technical problem is the unfilled placeholders throughout the essay (` <R>`). These appear in critical spots—your thesis statement, your definition of Rational Choice Theory, your claims about the Clean Water Act, and your conclusion. As it stands, the essay literally lacks its own thesis and key definitions. Before revising anything else, you need to fill these in with actual source material and citations. Until you do, it's difficult to evaluate whether your argument holds together, since some of the most important sentences are blank.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your introduction states that the paper focuses on "water pollution and its relationship to the Exchange Theory and the Rational Choice Theory," but this is a description of topic, not an argument. What are you claiming *about* that relationship? Possible theses might be:
"Water pollution persists because both companies and individuals lack awareness of the costs and rewards involved—a problem that Rational Choice and Exchange theories help explain and potentially solve."
"Sociological theory reveals that changing behavior around water pollution requires making costs and consequences visible and personal."

Whatever you choose, state it explicitly and make sure every paragraph supports it. Right now, the middle sections (pollutant categories, point/non-point sources) function as background information, but you don't explicitly return to connect this taxonomy back to the theories later. Consider adding a sentence at the end of that section explicitly bridging to your theoretical framework: eg, "Understanding these sources is essential because both theories depend on actors recognizing costs they may not currently perceive."

Use of the Two Theories

Your explanations of Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory are the strongest parts of the essay, but they suffer from three problems:

Repetition: You define Rational Choice Theory as being about personal beliefs, then repeat this claim nearly verbatim three or four times ("Rational Choice Theory states that individuals are driven by personal beliefs... Personal beliefs are propounded... The Rational Choice Theory is directed by personal beliefs..."). Trim this down to one clear definition, then move forward with application rather than redefinition.
Insufficient distinction: You note that the two theories are "similar" in being personalized, but you don't clearly explain how they *differ*. Rational Choice is about conscious, belief-driven decisions; Exchange Theory (per your own description) "has little interest in consciousness" and instead emphasizes conditioned reinforcement. This is actually a rich contrast—use it! Are you arguing companies act rationally (weighing costs/benefits deliberately) or are they simply following ingrained patterns of reward (Exchange Theory)? Right now your essay applies both theories to almost identical evidence (statistics on illness, beach closures), which blurs the distinction you set up.
Underdeveloped conclusion: The Milwaukee anecdote at the end is compelling, but it appears without being tied back to either theory. Does this incident illustrate a failure of rational choice (companies/agencies not weighing costs correctly) or a matter of reinforcement (no consequence experienced until illness struck)? Make this connection explicit rather than leaving it as a closing appeal to emotion.

Organization

Consider this restructuring:
Introduction with explicit thesis
Brief background on water scarcity/importance (currently paragraph 2)
Types and sources of pollution (currently paragraphs 3–4) — trim this section; it currently reads as a list and doesn't need to be as long, since it's background rather than argument
Rational Choice Theory applied to companies/policy
Exchange Theory applied to individual behavior
Conclusion that synthesizes both theories and explicitly answers your thesis question

Style and Mechanics

Several recurring issues weaken your prose:

Apostrophe errors: "its'" appears repeatedly; the correct form is "its" (possessive, no apostrophe) or "it's" (contraction).
Homophone/word-choice slips: "then" for "than" ("costs more... then it does"); "war pollution" for "water pollution."
Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "This is significant because water is something that we need in order to survive and without water one cannot function. Therefore, ones' behavior..." Break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Vague pronoun reference: "If they are, then... do companies want to improve conditions" — clarify antecedents throughout.
Gendered pronoun inconsistency: You shift to "she" for a generic actor in the Exchange Theory section; make sure this is intentional and consistent, or use "they."

Priority Revisions

Fill in all citation placeholders with real evidence and proper attribution.
Write an explicit, arguable thesis statement.
Cut repetitive theory definitions; sharpen the contrast between the two theories.
Explicitly link background sections (pollutant types, sources) to your argument.
Proofread for apostrophe and word-choice errors.

With these revisions, you'll have a tighter, more persuasive essay that fulfills its ambitious framework.

Feedback Report on Homelessness Essay

Overall Assessment

This essay demonstrates substantial research and genuine engagement with the topic of homelessness, particularly in its historical overview and application of sociological theory. However, the essay currently reads more as an informative survey than a focused argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you need to sharpen your thesis, improve structural cohesion, and ensure every section explicitly serves your central argument.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction contains a promising claim: that advocates get too caught up in *quantifying* homelessness rather than acknowledging its fundamental injustice. This is a compelling angle—but you abandon it almost immediately. The essay then becomes a historical survey followed by a theoretical survey, and neither section returns to defend or develop this initial claim. 

Revision suggestion: Decide what you actually want to argue. Is it that:
(a) the *moral* dimension of homelessness is being lost in debates over statistics, or
(b) conflict theory offers the most useful framework for understanding and solving homelessness, or
(c) something else entirely?

Right now the essay tries to do too much—it's a history lesson, a theory lesson, and an argument, but these three strands don't clearly reinforce one another. Choose one central claim and let every paragraph work toward proving it.

Structure

The essay's organization mirrors what looks like class notes or a textbook outline: history → definitional problems → causes → theories → conclusion. This is a logical structure for a *report*, but an argumentative essay needs a different shape—one where paragraphs build a case rather than simply presenting information sequentially.

Specific issues:
The historical section (paragraphs 2–5) is the longest part of the essay but never connects explicitly back to your thesis. What is this history *proving*? You need topic sentences that state the historical section's argumentative purpose, and a concluding sentence for that section that ties it back to your claim.
The conflict theory section is thorough but essentially repeats itself across multiple paragraphs (property as power, conflict between groups, control over deviance labeling). Consider consolidating these into 2 well-organized paragraphs rather than 5 loosely related ones.
The final paragraph introduces a new claim—that micro-level analysis is preferable—that hasn't been set up anywhere earlier in the essay. This should either be foreshadowed in your introduction or removed.

Citations and Evidence

You have a placeholder  in paragraph 2 where a quotation should appear—make sure to fill this in before submission. More broadly, this essay relies heavily on a single source (Fantasia and Isserman) and general sociological claims without citation. For an argumentative essay of this scope, you should:
Cite specific statistics about homeless populations, even while making the argument that statistics are overemphasized (this would actually strengthen your point).
Attribute theoretical claims about conflict theory and symbolic interactionism to specific theorists or texts (you cite Cooley once, but Marx, Simmel, or contemporary conflict theorists should be named for their specific claims).
Support claims like "real estate agencies have conspired against the poor" with actual evidence—this is a strong assertion that needs backing.

Clarity and Precision

Several sentences contain vague or imprecise phrasing that weakens your argument:
"There is an agreement between all opposing viewpoints in government" — Whose viewpoints? Which agreement, specifically?
"The consent unrest socked endures" — this sentence is unclear/possibly contains a typo and needs revision.
"This proves the symbolic interactionist's theory" — one example does not constitute proof; consider "illustrates" or "supports" instead.

Grammar and Mechanics

"lobs" should be "jobs" (paragraph 4)
"Contact theorists" should be "Conflict theorists" (paragraph 12)
"mayor cause" should be "major cause" (paragraph 20)
"establishement" should be "establishment"
Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences, particularly in the theoretical sections where ideas pile up quickly (eg, "Change is a term most often associated with conflict theorists because they see society as constantly in chaos and undergoing change.")

Strongest Elements

Consider expanding this section and using it as a model for how the rest of the essay could integrate theory with concrete implications.

Priority Revisions

Add topic and transition sentences throughout that connect each section back to that thesis.
Cut redundant paragraphs in the conflict theory section.
Fill in the missing citation and add supporting evidence for unsupported claims.

With a sharper thesis and tighter structural connections, the strong research you've already done will have much more persuasive power.

Feedback on Welfare Reform Essay

Your essay demonstrates impressive research and a genuine effort to present multiple theoretical perspectives on a complex policy issue. You've clearly invested significant time gathering statistics and historical context. However, the essay needs substantial revision in several key areas to strengthen its argumentative power and coherence.

Thesis and Argumentative Structure

The most pressing issue is that your essay lacks a clear, identifiable thesis. You spend considerable space presenting Spencer's Social Darwinism, then Merton's Matthew Effect, but your own position only emerges in the final two paragraphs—and even there, it remains somewhat vague ("hopefully certain reforms can be made"). For an argumentative essay, readers need to know your claim early on and see how each section builds toward defending it.

Revision suggestion: Add a clear thesis statement in your introduction that stakes out your position (eg, "While welfare abolitionists rightly identify problems with dependency, Merton's theory better explains recipient behavior, and thus welfare reform—not abolition—is the ethical and practical solution"). Then ensure every subsequent section explicitly serves this claim.

Balance and Fairness in Presenting Theories

You present Spencer's theory persuasively and at length, complete with supporting evidence (Aletha Townsend, GAO statistics, the Talent-Faircloth Bill). Then you introduce Merton's theory as the counterargument. This structure is logical, but by dedicating so much space to Spencer before revealing your own sympathies, you risk leaving readers uncertain about your stance until very late. Consider either:
Signaling your position earlier while still giving Spencer's view a fair hearing, or
Explicitly framing the Spencer section as "the case FOR abolition" so readers understand it's not your final position

Missing Textual Elements

I notice numerous `<R>` and  placeholders throughout the draft—these appear to be citation markers and quotations you haven't yet filled in. This is critical to address before final submission. Direct quotations from Ann Clark, Bill Bennett, Toby Herr, and others are essential to your evidence base; without them, claims like "she quit school because..." are incomplete and confusing. Additionally, proper citations are required for academic integrity, especially given how heavily statistic-dependent this essay is.

Evidence Evaluation

You've gathered excellent statistics, but much of the essay reads as a *list* of facts rather than *analysis* of them. For instance:

> "Twenty-five to forty percent of welfare recipients have learning disabilities, and sixteen percent have substance abuse problems..."

This paragraph and others like it pile up statistics without much interpretive commentary. Ask yourself after each fact: *So what? How does this support Merton over Spencer, or vice versa?* Strengthening the analytical connective tissue between facts and argument will elevate this from a report to a persuasive essay.

Logical Consistency

There's a tension worth resolving: you use Spencer to argue that failed reform programs (like Project Match) prove reform is futile, but you personally reject this conclusion. Make sure you explicitly rebut this move—for example, by arguing that program *failures* stem from poor design or underfunding rather than from an inherent flaw in the *concept* of reform. Right now, the rebuttal is implied rather than argued.

Similarly, your own conclusion contains a potentially self-undermining moment:

> "it is also not fair that some welfare recipients candidly admit that they could work more if they really wanted to work more"

This sentence unintentionally lends credibility to Spencer's argument, right after you've spent paragraphs building the Merton-based counter-case. If you want to acknowledge this complexity, do so more deliberately—perhaps by conceding that *some* recipients fit the "unwilling" category while arguing that *structural* barriers explain the majority, per Holzer's and Herr's findings.

Style and Mechanics

Watch for minor errors: "in to" should be "into"; "deferent men" should be "different men"; "he no useful place" should be "has no useful place."
The rhetorical questions in paragraph 9 are effective for framing the debate—consider using this technique again in your conclusion to bring the essay full circle.
Some transitions between sections feel abrupt (eg, moving from the poverty statistics paragraph directly into "Thus, with so many Americans..."). Consider transitional sentences that explicitly connect historical background to your analytical framework.

Priority Revisions

Fill in all placeholder citations and quotations
State your thesis explicitly and early
Add analytical commentary connecting statistics to your argument
Strengthen your rebuttal of Spencer's "failed programs" argument
Proofread for the mechanical errors noted above

You have strong raw material here—the historical background, theoretical frameworks, and evidence are substantial. The task now is to sharpen the argumentative spine so readers can follow *your* reasoning from start to finish, rather than simply learning about two competing sociological theories.

Feedback Report: Draft Essay on Divorce

Overall Impression

This is a well-researched, ambitious piece that demonstrates real engagement with sociological theory. You clearly understand symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, and exchange theory, and you apply each competently to the phenomenon of divorce. However, as it stands, the essay reads more like an informative survey of sociological perspectives than an argumentative essay. To move it toward genuine argument, several structural and rhetorical issues need attention.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

The most significant issue is the absence of a clear, arguable thesis. Your introduction establishes that divorce rates are high and stabilizing, but it doesn't tell the reader what you will *argue*. Are you claiming that one theoretical framework explains divorce better than the others? That divorce results from multiple interacting causes? That certain interventions are more promising than others? Currently, you present four theories sequentially without ranking, synthesizing, or critiquing them—this is description, not argument.

Revision suggestion: Add a thesis statement in your introduction that stakes a claim—for example, that no single theory fully explains divorce, and only by integrating structural (functionalist), relational (exchange), and cultural (symbolic interactionist) factors can we understand—and address—the problem. This would justify surveying multiple theories while still giving the essay an argumentative spine.

Structure and Organization

The essay's organization mirrors a textbook chapter: theory 1, theory 2, theory 3, theory 4, followed by "implications" and a section on post-divorce issues, followed by "solutions" mapped back onto each theory. This parallel structure is logical but mechanical. Each section stands alone; there is little cross-referencing between theories, and no paragraph explicitly compares or evaluates them against one another.

Suggestion: Consider adding transitional analysis between sections—sentences that explicitly connect theories ("While symbolic interactionists focus on changing meanings, functionalists locate the same phenomenon in structural change—suggesting these are complementary rather than competing explanations"). This will transform a list into an argument.

The "solutions" section, while a nice attempt at synthesis, is underdeveloped and somewhat generic ("couples need to have a secure relationship"). These recommendations don't clearly derive from the theories you've just explained—strengthen the logical connection between diagnosis and prescription.

Use of Evidence and Sources

You cite statistics and named scholars (Ogburn, Burgess and Locke, Spanier and Thompson), which is good practice, but the placeholder `<R>` markers throughout suggest that your citation formatting is incomplete. Before submitting, ensure every source is properly cited according to your assigned style (APA, MLA, etc) with full bibliographic information in a reference list.

Additionally, some claims are asserted without evidentiary support—for instance, the claim about medieval views of children, or the generalization about African American and white divorce rates. Since this essay leans heavily on empirical claims, each should be traceable to a specific source, not offered as common knowledge.

Clarity and Precision of Language

Several sentences are vague or imprecise:
"Society has become impersonal and complex" — this needs unpacking or a citation.
"As long as there is conflict in society, there will be conflict in marriages" is a broad generalization that doesn't follow logically from conflict theory as you've described it.
The line "widespread personal troubles indicate a social problem" states a sociological principle (echoing C. Wright Mills) without citing it—if this is drawn from a specific theorist, credit them explicitly, as this is a foundational claim for your whole essay.

Grammar and Mechanics

The draft contains numerous small errors that should be corrected in revision:
"coning" → "coming"
"wilt" → "will"
"involved" → "evolved" (in "how it has involved through history")
"own" → "on" ("With then own job")
"than" → "then" (used inconsistently throughout)
"fob" → "job"
"Piety" → likely "Society" or similar (unclear intended word)
Subject-verb agreement: "the following economic issues were points of conflict: amount of money one had..." — awkward list structure needs parallel grammatical form.
"decided" → "decide"

A careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud, will catch most of these.

Conclusion

Your conclusion largely restates the essay's content rather than synthesizing it into a final insight or call to action. Consider ending with a sharper, more specific claim: what should readers (or policymakers, or couples) take away from this multi-theoretical analysis? Right now the ending gestures broadly at "hope" and "education" without specifying what kind of intervention your analysis actually supports.

Priority Revisions

Add an explicit, arguable thesis in the introduction.
Build connective analysis between theoretical sections rather than presenting them in isolation.
Strengthen the link between diagnosis (theories) and prescription (solutions).
Fix citations and proofread carefully for grammar/spelling.
Sharpen the conclusion into a specific, argued takeaway.

With these revisions, the strong research base you've already built can support a genuinely persuasive essay rather than a well-organized summary.

Feedback Report: "Running the Score Up" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging, debatable topic and you clearly know the subject matter well—the Florida/Carolina example shows genuine command of the material. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: thesis clarity, argument structure, and evidence/logic. Below are specific suggestions organized by priority.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your core claim seems to be: *coaches who "run up the score" are not morally culpable because systemic pressures (rankings, Heisman politics, bowl money, job security, fan expectations) make high scores unavoidable or even necessary.* This is a strong, arguable thesis—but it never appears as a single, clear statement. Readers have to piece it together from scattered sentences ("who could blame him," "aren't guilty of anything," "It is what secures their job").

Revision suggestion: State this thesis explicitly in your introduction, and make sure every paragraph explicitly returns to it. Currently, your paragraphs read as a list of loosely related reasons rather than a cumulative case.

Organization

The essay's structure is additive rather than logical—each paragraph introduces a new reason but doesn't build on previous ones or acknowledge counterarguments. Consider organizing your reasons by strength, moving from weakest to strongest, and adding transitions that show *relationships* between reasons (eg, "Beyond the pressure of rankings, coaches also face pressure from award politics like the Heisman").

Also, several of your reasons work against each other. You argue coaches are "merciful" by benching starters early, but then argue they *must* leave star players in long enough to rack up Heisman-worthy stats. These two claims need to be reconciled or explicitly separated (eg, "in most games coaches show mercy, but in a few high-stakes games, other pressures take precedence").

Evidence and Logical Reasoning

Strongest section: The Spurrier/Florida-Carolina narrative is vivid and specific—keep this, but tighten it and clarify *why* it supports your thesis (right now it reads more like a complaint that Florida still lost the ranking, which slightly undercuts your point).
Weakest claims: The Heisman Trophy paragraph and the "fans love blowouts" paragraph assert things ("all colleges want this pay-cut," "fans like to see" score-running) without any supporting evidence—statistics, quotes from coaches or athletic directors, attendance data, etc Even one citation per major claim would substantially strengthen your credibility.
Questionable logic: The claim that passing is "unfair" because it's "quicker and more destructive" than running needs more support—right now it sounds like an assertion rather than an argument. Consider explaining *why* fairness should be measured by play-calling speed rather than final score.
The  placeholder in paragraph 3 was left unfinished ("You could give them the ball and say ")—this needs to be filled in or the sentence reworked, as it currently breaks the reader's trust in the draft's completeness.

Counterarguments

You never seriously engage with the opposing view: that running up the score is unsportsmanlike regardless of institutional pressures. Even a brief paragraph acknowledging *why* the NCAA and critics object—then explaining why those objections are outweighed—would make your argument feel more persuasive and less one-sided.

Style and Tone

Phrases like "getting their butts beat bad" are too casual for academic argument. Consider "Carolina was losing badly" or similar.
Avoid rhetorical filler like "No one knows" and "who could blame him"—these read as shrugs rather than argued points.
The conclusion largely repeats the introduction's questions without synthesizing your reasons into a final, forceful statement. Use the conclusion to briefly recap your *strongest* two or three points and end with a memorable claim about what the NCAA (or fans, or coaches) should do differently.

Grammar and Mechanics

You have a number of recurring errors worth addressing in your next draft:
Comma splices: eg, "Carolina was already losing by four touchdowns. Florida did score with two seconds left" (some of these are separated correctly, but many run together with commas—review comma-splice rules).
Homophones/word confusion: "there" vs. "their" ("their fans"), "to" vs. "too" ("do it to" should be "do it too"), "its" vs. "it's" ("along with it's ranking" should be "its").
Apostrophe misuse: "Bobby Bowden,'" has a stray apostrophe.
Subject-verb agreement: "If a player gets the award, the college he plays for will receive lots of money" is fine, but check similar constructions throughout for consistency.

Priority for Revision

Write an explicit, one-sentence thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorder paragraphs so reasons build toward your strongest point.
Add at least one piece of outside evidence (statistic, quote, article) to support your weaker claims.
Add a counterargument paragraph.
Proofread for comma splices and homophone errors.

This essay has real potential—your voice is confident and your central idea is worth defending. Tightening the structure and adding evidence will make it far more persuasive.

Feedback on "The Confederate Battle Flag" Essay

Overall Impression
You've chosen a genuinely difficult, high-stakes topic, and your personal investment in the subject comes through clearly. The essay has an identifiable position and moves through several distinct points. However, the argument currently undermines itself in a significant way, and it would benefit from stronger evidence, tighter organization, and more precise historical claims. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Resolve the Central Contradiction
This is the most urgent issue. Your thesis states that "the battle flag should not be flying over the South Carolina State Capitol" — a clear, strong claim. But the body of your essay doesn't argue that *no* Confederate flag should fly there; it argues that a *different* Confederate flag (the "Stars and Bars") should replace it, because that flag represents "heritage" rather than "hate."

This creates a problem: the First National Flag ("Stars and Bars") was also the flag of a government explicitly formed to preserve slavery. Historians and primary sources (eg, the Confederate states' own declarations of secession) make this connection directly. If your argument is that the battle flag is illegitimate *because* it symbolizes a fight to preserve slavery, that same logic applies to any Confederate flag, since the Confederacy itself existed for that purpose. As it stands, a skeptical reader will feel you've swapped one problematic symbol for another rather than making a principled case.

Revision options:
Narrow your claim to focus specifically on the battle flag's post-1964 political use (a strong, defensible argument), and drop the call to raise a "real" Confederate flag instead.
Or, if you want to keep the heritage argument, you need to more carefully define what "heritage" means apart from the Confederacy itself — perhaps regional culture, language, food, music — rather than another Confederate flag, which will always carry the same associations.

Strengthen and Cite Your Historical Claims
Your strongest, most persuasive point is the 1964 claim: that the battle flag was raised in connection with resistance to the civil rights movement rather than as a Civil War memorial. This is a great argument *if supported*. Currently, you state it as if it's common knowledge ("These people are wrong"), but you don't cite where this information comes from. Adding even one specific citation (a historian, a state archive, a news article on the flag's history) would transform this from an assertion into evidence, and it's compelling enough to be developed further — perhaps into its own more detailed paragraph with more supporting detail (who proposed raising the flag, what was said about it at the time, etc).

Reconsider the "Southerners Are Misunderstood" Paragraph
The paragraph about outsiders "not understanding" Southern life risks reading as evasive, since it shifts focus from the flag's meaning to other people's ignorance. Consider either cutting this paragraph or refocusing it on how the flag *actively prevents* outsiders from engaging with Southern culture on its own terms — which would tie it back to your thesis rather than sounding like a deflection.

Organization
Your paragraphs each contain a reasonable idea, but the essay as a whole reads more like a list of separate points than a cumulative argument. Consider:
Adding clearer topic sentences that explicitly connect back to your thesis ("This history matters because...").
Grouping the "definition of the two flags" paragraphs together before moving into your historical/political argument, so readers aren't asked to hold two flag descriptions in mind before you get to your strongest point.
Ending with a conclusion paragraph that clearly restates your (revised, non-contradictory) thesis and synthesizes your points, rather than introducing a new proposal (the explanatory plaque) for the first time at the very end.

Style and Mechanics
"succeeded from the union" should be "seceded from the union."
Some sentences repeat ideas almost verbatim (eg, "the battle flag stands for nothing but racism and hatred" appears twice in close proximity). Vary phrasing and let each sentence add something new.
Watch for informal phrasing ("I agree that... I agree because...") — vary sentence openings for a more academic register.
Define your flag descriptions more precisely and, if possible, include an image or citation, since readers unfamiliar with vexillology may struggle to visualize "bars" versus "stripes."

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Fix the logical inconsistency between your thesis and your call to raise a different Confederate flag.
Add citations/sources for historical claims, especially the 1964 point.
Reorganize paragraphs so each builds toward a single, consistent thesis.
Proofread for word choice ("seceded") and repetitive phrasing.

You have the makings of a compelling, well-reasoned essay — the 1964 historical argument alone is a strong foundation. Sharpening the logic and adding source support will make the essay much more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled an ambitious, values-laden topic and organized your argument around three clear consequences (loss of respect for women, illegitimate families, STDs). The structure is logical on its surface, but the essay needs significant revision in four areas: argumentative rigor, use of evidence, tone, and mechanics.

Argument and Logic

Your central claim—that premarital sex *causes* these social ills—relies heavily on correlation presented as causation. For instance, you cite statistics about STD rates and multiple partners, then conclude that monogamy would solve the problem. But this conflates premarital sex with promiscuity; a monogamous unmarried couple and a promiscuous married one are treated as if they don't exist. Sharpen your claims: are you arguing against premarital sex itself, or against a broader pattern of sexual irresponsibility? Right now the essay slides between the two, which weakens your logic.

Similarly, in the pregnancy/family section, you note that "over 90% of people believe the family is the most important institution," then infer that "the idea of what family is has changed" and that this is producing dysfunctional children. This is a significant leap—you'd need direct evidence connecting family structure changes to the crime/education statistics you assert, not just juxtaposition of separate facts. Right now, the reasoning reads as *cherry-picked stats made to fit a pre-decided conclusion*, rather than a conclusion built from evidence. Revise by explicitly stating the logical connection between each piece of evidence and your claim, and consider addressing counterarguments (eg, could other factors—poverty, lack of education—better explain these outcomes?).

Use of Evidence

You're citing sources (Reiss, Gardner, Janus, Schur) but:
Citations are inconsistent: some have page numbers, some don't; format varies. Standardize according to whatever style (MLA/APA) your instructor requires.
Some statistics are used oddly. The claim that "38% of single men and 45% of single women would like to become parents even if they never marry" doesn't clearly support your point about dysfunction—it shows desire for parenthood, not family breakdown. Make sure each statistic is doing the specific work you claim it's doing.
The 99.91% STD statistic appears fabricated or at least unsupported—where does this number come from? Any number this precise needs a clear source, or it will damage your credibility.
Consider whether your sources are current and reputable enough for an academic argument on a sensitive topic.

Tone and Word Choice

This is the area most in need of attention. Phrases like "wedding night consummations have become post-game shows which only review past highlights," the extended riff on beer commercials and "bra cup sizes than IQs," and the sweeping generalization about "the oldest profession" undercut your credibility as a serious, fair-minded writer. Academic argument benefits from *restraint*—let your evidence carry the rhetorical weight rather than editorializing with loaded, informal language. Ask yourself: would a reader who disagrees with you feel you've engaged their perspective fairly, or feel mocked? Right now, the tone risks alienating any reader not already persuaded, which limits your argument's persuasive reach.

The essay would also benefit from acknowledging complexity. You gesture toward it ("It is true that sex is not the only thing involved in marriage...") but quickly retreat to sweeping claims. Steelman the opposing view—why might reasonable people disagree with you?—before returning to your position. This will make your argument feel more credible, not weaker.

Structure and Transitions

The four-part structure (history → STDs → pregnancy/family → social respect) is sound, but transitions between sections are abrupt. Each new paragraph should explicitly link back to your thesis and to the previous paragraph's point. Right now, the sections read as separate mini-essays stapled together.

Your conclusion asserts a solution ("return to moral and Christian values") that hasn't been argued for in the body of the essay—it appears suddenly. If this is your proposed solution, you need at least a paragraph developing *why* this specific solution follows from your evidence, and *how* it would address the specific harms you've described.

Mechanics

There are frequent errors that need proofreading:
affects/effects confusion throughout
their/there errors ("had there bad side affects," "give up there children")
"lost" for "loss," "least" for "at least," "din-finishing" (likely "diminishing")
Missing words and garbled phrases ("less structured relies," "most of sex")

A careful proofreading pass—reading aloud, or using a grammar checker—will catch most of these.

Priority for Revision

Tighten the logical connections between evidence and claims (avoid correlation/causation slippage).
Cut inflammatory or dismissive language; adopt a more measured academic register.
Verify and properly cite all statistics.
Add explicit transitions and address counterarguments.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word confusion.

You have a genuine argument here with real supporting research—the next revision should focus on making that argument *fair, precise, and rigorously supported* rather than broadly asserted.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Women in Combat

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely complex and contested policy question, and you clearly have a personal investment in the topic that comes through in your voice. However, the essay currently reads more as a personal reflection than an academic argument. To strengthen it significantly, you'll need to address issues of evidence, structure, logical consistency, and precision of language. Below are the key areas for revision, organized by priority.

Evidence and Support (Highest Priority)

This is the essay's most significant weakness. You make several factual claims that require substantiation but provide none:

"the standards for a particular routine are sometimes lowered to accommodate the physical weakness"
"the physical demands of combat would not be feasible for a woman without changing the way these demands are handled"

These are empirical claims about military policy and physical performance standards. Without citations to military studies, policy documents, or research on physical standards, these remain assertions rather than argument. An academic essay on this topic requires you to engage with actual data—physical fitness test comparisons, studies on gender and combat performance, or military policy documents on standards. Without this, readers have no way to evaluate whether your central premise is accurate.

Revision suggestion: Research specific instances where standards have been altered, or find data on physical requirement differentials. If you cannot find support for a claim, consider whether the claim should be softened or removed.

Logical Consistency

There's a tension in your argument that needs resolving. You argue that women should be recognized as "intentionally, purposefully created differently" and that we shouldn't try to "fix" these differences—yet your emotional argument ("I love having feminine characteristics...") suggests a somewhat essentialist view of gender that doesn't account for individual variation. Not all women share identical physical or emotional profiles, and not all men do either. Your argument would be stronger if you addressed the obvious counterargument: what about individual women who do meet combat physical standards without modification? Currently, your essay treats "women" as a monolithic category, which weakens the logical foundation.

Revision suggestion: Consider whether your argument is really about *average* differences and policy implications at scale, rather than claims about all women. Acknowledging individual exceptions while arguing for policy based on aggregate data would be more defensible.

Structure and Repetition

There is a substantial duplicated passage in paragraph two—the sentence about "intellectual capabilities" and "standards being lowered" appears twice in nearly identical form. This looks like a drafting error that needs correcting.

More broadly, the essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Consider restructuring around:
Introduction with clear thesis
The policy/standards argument (with evidence)
The physical difference argument (with evidence)
The emotional difference argument (this one is your weakest, as "emotional makeup" is not clearly defined or supported)
Conclusion addressing counterarguments

Tone and Word Choice

Some phrasing undermines your credibility with academic audiences:

"the weaker sex" — this phrase will read as dismissive to many readers and doesn't strengthen your argument. Even if you believe physical differences exist, this phrasing sounds more like editorializing than analysis.
"Our society and the feminists that support this equality" — this framing sets up feminists as a monolithic opposing force, which is a generalization that weakens your credibility. Many feminists hold nuanced views on this exact issue; engaging with specific arguments (rather than a broad label) would strengthen your position.

The Emotional Argument Needs Development

Your claims about women's "emotional makeup" are the least developed part of the essay. What specifically do you mean? Are you referring to psychological research on stress response, empathy, risk assessment? As written, this section relies heavily on assertion ("The emotional makeup of a woman is quite different from a man's") without definition or evidence. This is also the section most vulnerable to charges of stereotyping, so it requires the most care.

Consider the Counterargument More Fully

A stronger essay would directly address the strongest opposing arguments: What do proponents of women in combat roles argue? Do they cite examples of women succeeding in combat roles historically or in allied militaries? Engaging seriously with the best version of the opposing view (rather than characterizing it as "society being obsessed") will make your own argument more persuasive.

Summary of Action Items

Remove the duplicated paragraph section
Find and integrate actual evidence/sources for empirical claims
Address the individual-variation counterargument
Revise language ("weaker sex") for more academic tone
Develop or narrow the emotional-makeup argument
Engage with specific counterarguments rather than general characterizations of "feminists"

With revision focused on evidence and precision, this essay has strong bones—a clear personal stake and an organized progression from policy concerns to physical differences to emotional considerations. The next draft should focus on transforming personal conviction into substantiated argument.

Feedback on Your Essay: Gender Role Stereotyping in American Culture

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and complex question, and you've clearly done research to support your points. The Farrell, Masters, Friedan, and Poilit references show good engagement with relevant scholarship. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas before it will be persuasive and polished. Below are the main issues to address.

Clarify and Align Your Thesis with Your Conclusion

This is the most pressing issue. Your thesis states that culture should "maintain gender identities, but only to a certain extent." Yet your conclusion argues the opposite: that people should be free to be "who they want... to be" regardless of traditional definitions, and that individuals deserve "freedom of self-actualization." These two positions are in tension, and the essay never explains what the "certain extent" of maintained gender identity actually is. Readers finish the essay unsure what you're actually arguing.

Revision suggestion: Decide which claim you actually believe. If you think some traditional gender socialization has value (eg, teaching children responsibility or nurturing skills) but rigid enforcement is harmful, say so explicitly and specify *which* traits are worth preserving and *which* should be discarded. Right now, the body paragraphs function almost entirely as a critique of gender stereotyping's harms, with almost no sustained defense of the "should maintain identities" half of your thesis. Either strengthen that side of the argument or revise the thesis to match the argument you're actually making.

Strengthen Paragraph Structure and Topic Sentences

Several paragraphs mix advantages and disadvantages without clear signposting, which makes it hard to follow your logic. For instance, the paragraph on male stereotypes pivots from "advantageous in creating role models" to "puts a lot of pressure on men" without a transitional sentence marking that shift. Similarly, the paragraph on financial stability (para. 8) feels disconnected from the surrounding argument—it introduces a new scenario (father's death) without linking it back to your thesis about maintaining or discarding gender roles. Consider either integrating this point more explicitly into your argument about economic reasons (which your thesis promises but never fully delivers) or cutting it if it's a tangent.

Suggestion: Add clear topic sentences that state the main claim of each paragraph, and use transitions ("However," "In contrast," "This suggests that") to guide readers through shifts between advantages and disadvantages.

Fix Citation Problems

There are several placeholder markers in your draft ( and `<R>`) that were never filled in—for example, after "as they think" and after "unfeminine." These leave sentences incomplete and unsupported. Before submitting, you must:
Fill in every citation with the actual source information.
Use a consistent citation format (author, year, page number, depending on your assigned style—likely MLA or APA).
Make sure every claim attributed to a named scholar (Farrell, Masters, Friedan, Poilit) has a specific, cited source, not just a general reference to "her research."

Right now, the essay reads as though a draft was submitted before revision was complete.

Address the "Economic" Argument More Fully

Your introduction promises "psychological, sociological, and economic reasons" for your position, but the economic dimension is underdeveloped—it appears only briefly in the final body paragraph about a widow needing job skills. Consider dedicating a full paragraph to economic reasons (eg, dual-income households, workforce participation rates, the economic vulnerability of women without job skills) to fulfill this promise and balance the essay's structure.

Proofread Carefully

The essay contains numerous typographical and spelling errors that undermine its credibility: "genre identities" (should be "gender"), "femine," "famimlies," "mascularity," "enstranged," "abnomal," "estranged," "afterall," "Ieadig-.to," "judge" (should be "judged"), "woman*s," and inconsistent capitalization. Read the essay aloud, or use a grammar-checking tool, and correct these systematically. Precision in language matters especially in an essay about the precision of social categories.

Revisit Word Choice for Neutrality

Phrases like "these so-called images" and "afterall for such a country that fights for freedom of liberty" are effective rhetorically, but make sure your tone remains consistently academic. Avoid casual phrasing ("get bored," "a lot of pressure") in favor of more precise, formal alternatives ("experience dissatisfaction," "considerable psychological strain").

Summary of Priorities
Resolve the contradiction between thesis and conclusion.
Develop the economic argument more fully.
Improve paragraph transitions and topic sentences.
Complete all citations properly.
Proofread thoroughly for spelling and mechanics.

With these revisions, your essay's genuine insight into gender stereotyping's psychological and sociological costs will come through much more clearly and persuasively.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a topic you clearly have direct experience with, and your passion comes through in the specific details about USC's visitation policy and quiet hours. This lived experience is a strength—it gives your argument authenticity. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas to make it more persuasive and academically polished. Here is feedback organized by priority.

Thesis and Focus

Your central claim is buried and inconsistent. The introduction suggests your argument is about college failing to help students mature because of excessive rules, but the essay increasingly narrows to *specific* complaints about visitation and quiet hours. Decide: is your thesis "residence hall rules prevent maturation" or "residence hall rules are unfairly applied and discriminatory"? These are related but distinct claims requiring different evidence. Revise your introduction to state one clear, arguable thesis, and make sure every paragraph supports it.

Structure and Organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Right now, paragraphs shift between emotional appeals, policy description, and discrimination claims without clear transitions. Consider this structure:

Introduction with clear thesis
Visitation policy as an obstacle to independence
Visitation policy as discriminatory (gender/sexuality)
Quiet hours as an additional restriction
Conclusion that ties back to your maturation argument

Currently, the discrimination argument (heterosexual vs. same-sex couples) is one of your strongest and most original points, but it gets lost in the middle of a paragraph about gender and rape. Give it its own paragraph with a clear topic sentence.

Development of Ideas

Several claims need more support or more careful reasoning:

The rape-warning argument is provocative but underdeveloped. You assert that men are raped "if not by other males by females," but provide no evidence (statistics, sources, examples) to support the claim that this is common enough to warrant equal signage. This weakens an otherwise interesting point about unequal treatment.
The discrimination example (lesbian resident vs. heterosexual resident) is excellent and concrete—expand on this. Could policies simply require sign-in for *all* overnight guests regardless of gender? Offering a potential solution would strengthen your argument.
The "roommate contract" paragraph feels like a tangent. You raise it to counter a potential objection, but the follow-through is unclear—are you saying the contract adequately protects roommates, making stricter rules unnecessary? State this connection explicitly.

Handling Counterarguments

You gesture toward counterarguments ("I understand you may be thinking...") but tend to dismiss them quickly rather than engaging seriously. For example, you acknowledge that visitation policies protect residents' safety, then immediately wave this away ("nine times out of ten... disturbance is the last thing on their minds"). Strengthen your argument by taking the counterargument seriously: Why do administrators believe these rules are necessary? What evidence would you need to prove they're unnecessary? Engaging genuinely with the opposing view makes your own position more credible.

Tone and Audience

Phrases like "That's ridiculous!" and rhetorical questions ("What is too loud?") create an informal, conversational tone appropriate for a blog post but not for academic argument. Consider revising these moments into more analytical statements. For instance, instead of "That's ridiculous!" you might write: "This policy fails to account for guests traveling long distances, undermining its stated purpose of protecting residents' well-being."

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are frequent errors that need correction:
Subject-verb agreement: "guest have," "policies restrict visitors," "the rules and regulations aren't fair to males and heterosexual residents" (this sentence's logic is also unclear—do you mean the rules are unfair *because* they assume all males are threats?)
Word confusion: "parenting rolls" should be "parenting roles"; "Atleast" should be "At least"
Comma splices and run-ons appear throughout, especially in the visitation paragraph
"Men are rape also" should be "Men are raped also"

I recommend reading your essay aloud to catch these errors, or using a grammar-checking tool as a first pass before a final human review.

Overall Recommendation

Your strongest material is the discrimination argument regarding same-sex versus heterosexual visitation policies—this is specific, logical, and compelling. I'd suggest making this the centerpiece of your revision, with the maturation argument serving as a frame in your introduction and conclusion. Cut or significantly shorten the rape-warning discussion unless you can find supporting evidence, as it currently distracts from your more persuasive points.

Focus your next draft on tightening the thesis, developing your best argument (discrimination) more fully, and revising your tone to sound more analytical than conversational.

Feedback Report: College Football Playoff Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, well-defined argumentative topic, and your use of specific historical case studies (1992-93, 1993-94, and 1994 seasons) gives your essay concrete evidence rather than vague generalizations. This is a real strength—you clearly did your research and understand the material. However, the essay currently reads more like three detailed case narratives followed by a brief argument than a tightly structured persuasive essay. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Structural Balance (Highest Priority)

Your three case studies take up roughly 70% of the essay, while your actual proposal and its benefits (revenue, excitement, fairness) receive comparatively little development. Since your thesis promises three specific benefits of a playoff system, each deserves substantive treatment—not just a paragraph or two at the end.

Suggestion: Consider trimming the case studies. You don't need to narrate every score and ranking; instead, extract the *pattern* each case demonstrates (eg, "Case 1 shows how the polls can't account for undefeated teams from weaker conferences") and use that as evidence within your argument, rather than as standalone narratives. This would free up space to develop your solution and rebuttal sections more fully.

Underdeveloped Counterarguments

You mention that people oppose playoffs because of concerns about bowl traditions, season length, and revenue loss—but you dismiss these in a single paragraph without real engagement. A stronger essay would:
Explain *why* these concerns have merit (eg, how much money specific bowls generate, or data on player injury/academic strain from longer seasons)
Then systematically rebut them with evidence, not just assertion

Right now, Bowden's reluctance about season length is mentioned but never actually addressed. This is a missed opportunity, since it's a legitimate objection to your specific plan.

The Proposal Itself Needs More Rigor

Your 16-team playoff proposal is introduced quite late and briefly. Readers will have practical questions:
How exactly are the 16 teams selected? By whom?
How does this resolve the *conference tie-in* problem you spent three case studies illustrating (eg, Big Eight teams must go to the Orange Bowl)?
What happens to teams like Texas A&M or Penn State under your new system, specifically?

Walking through how your proposed system would have resolved one of your three case studies (pick the clearest one) would powerfully demonstrate your solution's effectiveness and tie the essay together.

Revenue and Excitement Claims Need Support

You assert that a playoff would "generate more revenue" and create "unbelievable" excitement, but offer no evidence—no comparisons to March Madness ratings/revenue, no economic reasoning, no data. These claims are stated as fact but not argued. Since generating revenue is one of your three named benefits, it deserves at least a paragraph with real support, not two sentences of assertion.

Citation and Mechanical Issues

Your citations are inconsistent and unclear (eg, "(18)," "(C3)," "(C6)")—these appear to be page numbers, but without a Works Cited page or in-text attribution to publications, readers can't identify or verify sources. Add full citations.
There are recurring typos and grammatical slips: "1993-93" logic errors, "the University of Alabama... didn't have was a history," "they're in and the teams they played," "implied this rule" (likely meant "applied"), and repeated word "going to going to." A careful proofreading pass is needed.
Quotations are garbled with stray "11" characters (likely OCR/formatting errors from quotation marks)—these should be cleaned up throughout.

Tone and Register

Phrases like "absolutely ridiculous," "whipped up on," and "got the better luck of the draw" are casual and effective for engaging readers, but a few (eg, "chunky soup") pulled from a quote without context are confusing. Make sure quoted material is fully explained so readers unfamiliar with the reference understand the point being made.

Priority Revision Plan

Cut narrative detail in the three case studies by about a third; keep only evidence that directly supports your argument.
Expand the "benefits" section (revenue, excitement, fairness) with real evidence/data for each.
Add a dedicated counterargument section that seriously engages and rebuts opposing views.
Detail your playoff proposal's mechanics and demonstrate how it resolves one specific case from your essay.
Proofread for typos, fix citations, and clean up quotation formatting.

You have strong instincts for using real cases as evidence—now focus on giving equal weight to your own solution and anticipated objections so the essay feels like a complete argument, not just a problem statement.

Feedback on Your Go-Kart Safety Essay

Overall Impression

You clearly have valuable firsthand experience, and your passion for this topic comes through—you genuinely want readers to understand why these rules matter. Your specific anecdotes (getting hit by a go-kart, seeing children hurt) give the essay authenticity and emotional weight that many argumentative essays lack. This is a strong foundation to build on. However, the essay needs work in organization, clarity of thesis, and proofreading to reach its full potential.

Strengths to Preserve

Personal authority: Your experience at Frankie's Fun Park gives you credibility that most writers on this topic wouldn't have.
Concrete examples: The image of being hit at 25 mph, or a car breaking down and getting hit again, makes abstract "safety" concerns vivid and real.
Logical structure: You move through the rules in a sensible order (seatbelts → gas/brake → bumping → accidents → returning to pit), which mirrors the actual sequence of a ride.

Areas for Revision

Sharpen Your Thesis
Your opening paragraph buries the thesis. "There are many reasons why you should follow the rules" is vague. Consider a clearer, more specific claim: *"Following go-kart safety rules prevents injuries, protects children, and preserves the equipment that makes the ride possible for everyone."* This gives readers a roadmap and gives you three clear categories to organize around (which you're already doing implicitly).

Reconsider Your Opening
The first sentence ("I don't know how to express how important...") undercuts your authority right away. You *do* know how to express it—you're about to write an entire essay demonstrating that. Try opening with a striking image or statistic instead: "Twenty-five miles per hour doesn't sound fast—until a go-kart going that speed slams into your arm." This hooks readers immediately with your strongest personal anecdote.

Tighten Repetitive Phrasing
Several sentences restate the same idea without adding new information:
"Having all these rules are for the safety of everyone" (also a subject-verb agreement error: "rules... are")
"I hope everyone understands the importance of having all these rules" (redundant with the sentence before it)

In your conclusion especially, consolidate these repeated points into one strong closing statement rather than circling back multiple times.

Organize Around Categories, Not Just a List of Rules
Right now, the essay reads as "rule 1, rule 2, rule 3..." Consider explicitly grouping your rules under the three reasons you mention in your intro: *safety of self, safety of others (especially children), and protection of equipment.* This would let you cut some repetition and make each paragraph's purpose clearer. For example, your bumping paragraph could open with "Bumping endangers both riders and equipment" rather than just describing what bumping is.

Address the Audience's Perspective
You assume readers already agree rules matter, but part of an argumentative essay should acknowledge counterarguments. You mention that "most people ride in a group with their friends and bump each other for fun"—this is a great start! Push further: acknowledge *why* people bend rules (thrill-seeking, competitiveness, feeling invincible) and then explain why that reasoning fails, using your examples as evidence.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

Please proofread carefully for these recurring errors:
Subject-verb agreement: "Having all these rules are" → "is"; "the only reason...are" → "is"
Homophone/spelling errors: "their" should be "there" ("bumpers are not their for people"); "announcements" is misspelled as "annoucements"
Awkward phrasing: "shouldn't be taking off" → "shouldn't be taken off"; "riding the rails" needs a comma or restructuring for clarity
Comma splices: "I was even lucky that when I got hit, I wasn't seriously hurt, I just bruised my arm real bad" — this needs to be broken into two sentences or restructured with a semicolon.
Informal register: "you got to remember" should be "you must remember" or "you have to remember" for a more polished, formal tone appropriate to argumentative writing.

Next Steps

Rewrite your introduction with a clear, specific thesis and a stronger hook.
Reorganize body paragraphs around your three main reasons (safety of self, safety of others, protection of property) rather than a rule-by-rule list.
Proofread line-by-line for grammar, especially subject-verb agreement and homophones.
Add at least one paragraph addressing why people are tempted to break rules, then refute that reasoning directly.
Tighten the conclusion so it reinforces your thesis without repeating the same idea three times.

You have compelling material here—the revision work is mostly about structure and precision, not about needing new content. Good luck!

Feedback Report: Susan Smith Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a complex and emotionally charged topic, and you clearly have a personal, sincere investment in your argument. However, the essay currently reads more as a collection of related but loosely connected claims rather than a unified argument. To strengthen this piece, you need to clarify your central thesis, tighten your reasoning, and address a significant factual error. Below are specific areas for revision.

Factual Accuracy (Critical Issue)

You state that "the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty" — this is incorrect and needs correction. Susan Smith was tried in state court in South Carolina; a jury, not the Supreme Court, decided her sentence. This error undermines your credibility with readers who know the case, and it suggests you may not have thoroughly verified your sources. Please fact-check all case details (dates, legal proceedings, terminology) before revising.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Your introduction promises an opinion on "the justness of the sentence," but the essay pursues three different arguments that are never fully reconciled:

Smith's traumatic history explains (or excuses) her actions
Life in prison is actually a *worse* punishment than death (guilt, fear, etc)
Religious/moral reasons why the death penalty is wrong (forgiveness, God's judgment)

Each of these could be a strong essay on its own, but combined without clear transitions, they compete for the reader's attention. Choose one central claim and organize all your evidence to support it. For example, if your thesis is "life imprisonment was the more just sentence," then the psychological background should support *why* she deserves this specific sentence (diminished culpability), the emotional suffering section should support *why* it's sufficiently punitive, and the religious material should support *why the state especially should not take this decision into its own hands*. Right now these sections read as parallel tracks rather than a converging argument.

Logical Tensions to Resolve

A few claims work against each other and need reconciling:
You argue Smith's mental state made her incapable of "rational choices," yet earlier you say "it is obvious to me" that her reasoning about the affair/boys was clear (if disturbed) reasoning. Is she rational-but-troubled, or incapable of rational thought? Pick one and be consistent.
You argue death "would have been quick, easy, and more economical" — implying life in prison is a *harsher* punishment — but then argue no one deserves the death penalty on moral/religious grounds. If life in prison is worse, doesn't that undercut your claim that avoiding execution is more humane? Consider whether you want to argue "life is a worse punishment" or "the death penalty is morally wrong," since these pull in different directions.

Handle the Religious Argument Carefully

The turn to Christian theology in the final third is a significant shift in argumentative register. This is a legitimate rhetorical strategy, but in an academic essay, you should:
Explicitly frame this as one perspective among others ("From a Christian perspective...") rather than presenting religious doctrine as self-evidently authoritative for all readers.
Fix the placeholder text — you have  where Bible quotations should appear. These must be properly cited (book, chapter, verse) before submission.
Consider how a secular or differently-religious reader might respond, and address that potential counterargument, even briefly. This will make your reasoning more persuasive to a broader audience.

Emotional Appeals vs. Evidence

Your paragraph describing Smith's guilt (the "hideous picture," the "small voices") is vivid and effective as writing, but it is speculative — you're imagining her interior psychological state without evidence. Distinguish clearly between what you *know* (case facts, psychiatric testimony) and what you're *imagining* for rhetorical effect. You might reframe this material as "One might imagine that Smith will be haunted by..." to signal it's interpretive.

Proofreading Needed

"recieve" → receive
"prision" → prison (appears twice)
"endowed with logical mental coherence" — awkward phrasing; simplify to something like "was not guided by rational thinking"

Suggested Next Steps

Decide on ONE central claim.
Cut or subordinate material that doesn't directly support that claim.
Fix the factual error about the Supreme Court.
Fill in your Bible citations.
Proofread carefully for spelling and mechanics.

You have compelling raw material here — particularly the psychological background and the emotional stakes of the case. With sharper focus, this can become a much more persuasive piece.
