Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely debatable topic and organized your essay around three clear reasons (crime reduction, responsibility, and health). This structure is a strength—your reader can follow the skeleton of your argument. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: source credibility and citation, logical development of claims, and sentence-level proofreading. Below I'll walk through each.

Source Credibility (Most Urgent Issue)

This is the biggest problem in the essay. Several of your citations are not legitimate academic sources:

"(Rick Finley@rfinleygmail.vt.edu)" appears to be an email address, not a citable source. Who is this person? What is their expertise? An email address alone tells your reader nothing about credibility.
"(WWW.mis.duke.edu/...)" is a URL, not a proper citation. Web addresses should be cited according to a style guide (MLA/APA), with the author, organization, or article title identified in the text.
"Netscape.Young Drinkers Homepage" and "nttp.www.kfu.com/nsayer" (note the typo "nttp") raise serious red flags. These do not sound like sources a college paper should rely on for factual claims about federal law or drinking statistics.

Action items: Replace these with verifiable, reputable sources—government reports (NIH, CDC, NHTSA), peer-reviewed journal articles, or well-established organizations. Use consistent, correct in-text citation format (author's last name, page number) and include a Works Cited/References page. Right now, your argument's credibility is undermined because a skeptical reader will question every claim once they see the shaky sourcing.

Logical Gaps and Unsupported Claims

Several key assertions need stronger reasoning or evidence:

"Once these rights are given back, young adults will act responsibly." This is asserted, not argued. Why would removing a restriction *increase* responsibility? You gesture at a "forbidden fruit" psychological argument later, but you need to make this causal link explicit and support it with evidence (eg, comparative data from countries with lower drinking ages).

"If eighteen-year-olds can be drafted... they should be allowed to enjoy all adult privileges." This is a common argument, but it's a rights-based claim, separate from your causal claims about crime/health. Consider addressing the counterargument: opponents will point out that other privileges (renting a car, some insurance rates) also have age minimums above 18, so the "adult privileges" argument isn't airtight. Acknowledging and responding to this counterargument would strengthen your credibility.

The binge-drinking causal chain (scarcity → binge drinking → lowering age fixes this) needs actual evidence. You assert that alcohol is "scarce" for 18-20 year-olds and that scarcity causes binge drinking, but you don't cite data connecting drinking age to binge-drinking rates specifically. This is your weakest paragraph logically—it relies on assumption rather than demonstrated correlation.

Consider addressing the strongest counterargument directly: What about drunk-driving fatality data since 1984? The National Minimum Drinking Age Act was passed partly because fatalities *decreased* after the age was raised. Ignoring this data (which the "other side" will surely raise) weakens your essay. Address it and explain why you still believe lowering the age is better policy.

Repetition and Structure

The essay reintroduces the same point in slightly different words across paragraphs, especially around "responsibility" and "society's view of alcohol." For example, the idea that eighteen-year-olds will act more responsibly once given rights appears at least three times. Trim these repetitions and use the space instead for evidence and counterargument.

Your conclusion largely restates the intro without adding synthesis or a forward-looking statement (eg, a call to action, a policy recommendation, or acknowledgment of remaining challenges). Strengthening the conclusion will leave a more decisive final impression.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are numerous errors that need cleanup in your next draft:

Apostrophe misuse: "eighteen-year old's" should be "eighteen-year-olds" (no apostrophe for plural nouns). This happens many times throughout.
Homophone/word errors: "would effect the United States" → "would affect"; "would cut back on reform programs" — check verb choice; "amoung" → "among."
Capitalization: sentences beginning with lowercase letters ("on most college campuses...").
Vague pronoun references: "Giving youths back their privileges" — did 18-20 year-olds ever collectively have this "privilege" nationally, or only in some states before 1984? Be precise.

I recommend a careful proofreading pass focused solely on these mechanical issues after you've revised content.

Priority for Revision

Fix/replace all questionable sources and citation format.
Strengthen the causal logic connecting "lowering the age" to each of your three outcomes.
Add at least one paragraph addressing the strongest counterargument (drunk-driving fatality statistics).
Trim repetition; strengthen the conclusion.
Proofread for grammar/mechanics.

You have a workable argumentative structure here—the revision work now is about *evidence* and *rigor*, not reorganization. Good luck with your next draft!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling topic with real-world stakes, and your proposal—mandatory swimming instruction in P.E.—is specific and actionable. The narrative opening is a strong rhetorical choice that pulls readers in emotionally before you pivot to argumentation. However, the essay needs revision in three key areas: structural clarity, evidentiary support, and mechanical polish.

Strengths to Build On

Your opening narrative is vivid and effective—putting readers in the position of a drowning child creates urgency that abstract statistics couldn't achieve. Your solution is also practical: you've thought through implementation details (contracting with local pools, using certified instructors, seasonal employment benefits) that many argumentative essays skip. This shows real engagement with the problem, not just the crisis.

Areas Needing Revision

Evidence and Credibility
This is your essay's biggest weakness. You make several empirical claims without support:
"Many school systems have pools that are under-used"
"In as little as four weeks a swim instructor can teach someone"
The implied frequency of drowning deaths in "another drowning at a local pool"

For an argumentative essay to be persuasive to a skeptical reader, you need data: How many childhood drownings occur annually? What percentage are preventable through swim education? Do any school districts already have swim requirements, and what were the outcomes? Even one or two credible statistics would transform this from an appeal to emotion into a persuasive argument grounded in evidence.

Structural Organization
Your paragraphs sometimes contain multiple ideas that could be separated for clarity. For instance, paragraph 3 introduces the proposal, mentions curriculum requirements, states your thesis, *and* claims the topic is "rarely heard in the education community"—that's a lot of ground covered quickly. Consider:
Dedicating one paragraph solely to establishing the problem's scope (with evidence)
A separate paragraph for your specific proposal
Separate paragraphs for each major implementation strategy (using school pools vs. partnering with local pools vs. staffing solutions)

Your closing paragraph also feels abrupt. After such a detailed proposal, a single-sentence conclusion doesn't do justice to your argument. Consider addressing potential counterarguments here (cost concerns, liability issues, students who already know how to swim) even briefly, which would show you've anticipated objections.

Addressing Counterarguments
Right now, your essay only presents the case *for* your proposal. Stronger academic arguments acknowledge complexity. What about:
Costs to school districts already facing budget constraints?
Liability/insurance issues with swim programs?
Students with disabilities or medical conditions that complicate swimming?
Cultural or religious objections some families might raise regarding required swim attire?

You don't need to spend paragraphs on this, but even a brief "some might argue X, but..." would strengthen your credibility as someone who has thought through this proposal seriously.

Tone Consistency
Your opening narrative uses second-person, casual, immersive storytelling ("You figure that you will be fine..."). This works well for the hook, but the transition to your argument sections shifts into a more direct, expository voice, then occasionally drifts back into casual rhetorical questions ("How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone's life?"). This particular line, while memorable, feels slightly flippant given the essay's serious subject matter. Consider whether it serves your argument or undercuts your ethos.

Mechanical Issues to Address

You have several typos and errors that need correction in your next draft:
"panicing" → panicking
"drownding" → drowning (appears multiple times)
"cericulum" → curriculum
"instuctor/instuctors" → instructor/instructors
"wuold" → would
"thaech" → teach
"employent" → employment
"simlpe" → simple
"remidied" → remedied
Inconsistent capitalization (eg, "News" doesn't need capitalization)
Missing periods and inconsistent paragraph indentation

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these systematically. Errors like these can distract readers and undermine their confidence in your argument, even when the ideas themselves are sound.

Priority Revision Steps

Add at least 2-3 concrete statistics or sources about drowning rates and existing swim education gaps
Expand your conclusion to synthesize your argument and address at least one counterargument
Proofread carefully for the spelling errors listed above
Reconsider the dodgeball line—cut it or revise for tone consistency

Your instinct for structuring a persuasive appeal is good—you have logos (practical solutions), pathos (the opening narrative), and even a bit of ethos (addressing implementation feasibility). Strengthening the evidentiary base and tightening the mechanics will make this a much more polished, convincing piece.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and debatable topic, and your essay follows a clear structural logic: each body paragraph addresses a different area of life (research/psychology, marriage, single fatherhood, single parenting generally) where rigid gender identities cause harm. Your topic sentences are strong, and your paragraphs are unified around single, coherent ideas. However, the essay is currently underdeveloped in terms of evidence, and there are some structural and mechanical issues that need attention before this draft is ready for submission.

Missing/Incomplete Evidence

This is the most urgent issue. You reference several authorities—Masters, Johnson, and Kolodny; Betty Friedan; Warren Farrell—but the actual quotations and specific claims are missing (marked with placeholders like  and `<R>`). As it stands, a reader cannot evaluate whether your sources actually support your argument. You need to:

Insert the actual quotations or paraphrases from each source.
Provide proper in-text citations (author, year, page number, depending on your required citation style).
Make sure each quotation is followed by your own analysis explaining *how* it supports your claim. Right now the quotations are doing all the work; you need to interpret them for the reader.

Without this evidence, the essay currently reads as a series of assertions rather than a supported argument. This should be your top priority in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Framing

Your thesis—that society should "abolish distinct gender identities" and recognize that masculine and feminine traits can appear in either gender—is clear. However, consider sharpening it. "Abolish" is a strong word; make sure your body paragraphs consistently argue for abolition rather than simply *flexibility* or *coexistence* of traits. At several points (eg, the single-parent paragraph), your evidence actually supports the idea that individuals *adopt* traits from both traditional categories as needed, which is a slightly different claim than "abolishing" the categories altogether. Consider clarifying: are you arguing that the categories themselves are meaningless, or that individuals should be free to exhibit any traits regardless of category? These are related but distinct claims, and tightening this distinction will make your argument more precise.

Paragraph Development and Logic

Your paragraphs generally have a clear topic and follow through, but a few could be strengthened:

The Friedan paragraph asserts that abolishing distinct gender identity would eliminate psychological harm to women, but this causal leap needs more support. Why would removing the category itself solve the problem, rather than simply expanding what is considered acceptable within it? Address this counterargument or complication directly.
The Farrell paragraph relies on a single narrative example (Ralph). One anecdote is useful as illustration, but you should acknowledge its limitations as evidence, or supplement it with additional research-based support.
The single-parent paragraph is your most concrete and persuasive one because it relies on observable social reality rather than psychological theory. Consider moving it earlier, or using it as a model for how to ground your more abstract points in this paragraph's tone.

Counterarguments

Right now, the essay does not acknowledge any opposing view. A stronger essay would briefly address why some might defend distinct gender identities (eg, arguments from tradition, biology, or child development) and then explain why your position still holds. This will make your argument more persuasive and show critical engagement with the topic.

Style and Mechanics

Several sentences begin with lowercase letters after a period ("if these traits were abolished..."; "it would be ridiculous..."). These appear to be typos from run-on sentences—proofread carefully for sentence boundaries and capitalization.
Watch for repeated phrasing: "distinct gender identity/identities" appears dozens of times. Vary your language (eg, "traditional gender roles," "rigid categories," "stereotyped traits") to avoid monotony.
"Dependent" is misspelled as "dependant" in one instance—minor, but worth catching in proofreading.
The conclusion largely restates prior points without adding synthesis or a forward-looking statement (eg, what should replace these categories, or what specific changes you'd advocate). Consider ending with a stronger call to action or a broader implication.

Next Steps

Fill in all missing quotations and citations, with proper attribution and analysis.
Clarify whether your argument is for eliminating gender categories or loosening them.
Add at least one counterargument and rebuttal.
Proofread for sentence-boundary and capitalization errors.
Strengthen the conclusion with a synthesis or forward-looking claim.

You have a solid organizational skeleton—the work now is filling it in with real evidence and tightening the precision of your claims.

Your essay tackles an interesting and relevant topic, and you clearly have knowledge of how talk shows operate—your description of the emotional buildup and audience dynamics shows genuine engagement with the subject. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas to strengthen its argument and improve its clarity. Below are my main areas of feedback.

Unclear Thesis and Argumentative Position

This is the most pressing issue. As an *argumentative* essay, your paper needs a clear claim that you defend throughout. Currently, you present both sides (talk shows are harmless entertainment vs. talk shows are vulgar and dangerous) but never commit to a position. Your final paragraph shifts to a different claim entirely—that responsibility for talk-show tragedies lies with guests and hosts—which feels disconnected from the entertainment-value discussion that dominates the rest of the essay.

Revise by asking: What do *you* actually think? Possible theses could be:
"While talk shows can provide genuine emotional support and information, their profit-driven emphasis on conflict makes them ethically irresponsible."
"Talk shows are unfairly maligned; despite their theatrics, they serve an important social function."

Choose one position and organize your paragraphs to build toward it.

Structural and Organizational Issues

Right now the essay moves: (1) intro presenting two sides → (2) case for entertainment/help value → (3) description of manufactured conflict → (4) return to positive function → (5) murder anecdote → (6) responsibility argument. This structure doesn't build a case; it circles between "talk shows are good" and "talk shows are bad" without resolution.

Suggested restructuring:
Intro: State your thesis clearly.
Body 1: Present the case *for* talk shows (help, information, entertainment).
Body 2: Present the case *against* (manufactured conflict, exploitation, danger—use your murder example here, as it's your strongest piece of evidence).
Body 3: Your own analysis/synthesis—where do you come down, and why?
Conclusion: Return to thesis, perhaps with a call to action (eg, increased screening protocols, viewer media literacy, etc)

This is your most compelling piece of evidence, but it's currently underdeveloped and unverified. You write "according to the media"—which source? Vague, uncited claims about real deaths undermine your credibility. This anecdote could anchor your entire argument if developed with concrete details and clear analysis of its implications.

You claim shows "provide helpful information about health, financial matters, family counseling" but then immediately pivot to describing manufactured conflict and secret-digging. These two characterizations of talk shows sit uneasily together—explain the connection or acknowledge the tension.

Your final paragraph's argument about guest responsibility doesn't follow from what preceded it. If you want to end on responsibility/ethics, foreshadow this earlier.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are frequent errors that need correction:
Spelling: "audeinces" (audiences), "agressively" (aggressively), "thier" (their), "heath" (health), "fueding" (feuding), "nieghbor" (neighbor), "thay" (they)
Comma splices/misplaced periods: "trash t.v.. is vulgar. and inappropriate" — punctuation here creates fragments

Random spacing/formatting issues: Extra spaces and line breaks appear inconsistently (eg, around "one gruesome setback")

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these errors systematically.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely debatable topic, and you clearly have a personal stake in the argument, which comes through in your passionate final paragraph. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: proofreading/mechanics, logical development, and evidence. Let me address each.

Proofreading and Mechanics

This draft has numerous errors that undermine your credibility as a writer. Before anything else, you need to proofread carefully:

Spelling errors: "Every since" → "Ever since"; "Independance" → "Independence"; "dieing" → "dying"; "too young" (you wrote "two young"); "gonvernment" → "government"
Word confusion: "affects" should be "effects" (a common but important distinction—"effects" is the noun you want here)
Capitalization: "civil war" should be "Civil War" (proper noun)
A critical contradiction: Your final paragraph states "Drinking should be prohibited to kids eighteen or older"—this directly contradicts your thesis! I believe you meant "permitted" or "allowed." This kind of error is exactly why proofreading matters; a reader could think you've reversed your entire argument at the end.
The concluding sentence is also garbled: "the military should give convince the gonvernment" needs to be rewritten cleanly—perhaps "the military should convince the government."

Read your essay aloud before submitting—this is often the fastest way to catch these issues.

Logical Development

Your essay makes several claims that need stronger reasoning or acknowledgment of complexity:

The military service argument (paragraph 2) is your strongest point—the inconsistency between fighting/dying for the country and not being trusted to drink is a real tension worth exploring. However, you could strengthen this by noting that many rights (voting, signing contracts, marriage without parental consent) *do* transfer at eighteen, so alcohol is somewhat anomalous. Consider researching *why* the drinking age was raised to 21 in 1984 (it was tied to federal highway funding and specifically aimed at reducing drunk driving fatalities among young drivers)—engaging with this counterargument would make your essay more persuasive.

The diploma argument (paragraph 3) is confusing and underdeveloped. You suggest tying drinking rights to high school graduation, but then complicate it further by suggesting exceptions. This creates a policy proposal within your essay that distracts from your main thesis. Either develop this idea fully (how would this be enforced? Is educational attainment really the right measure of "maturity"?) or cut it, since it currently raises more questions than it answers.

The crime/drunk driving argument (paragraph 4) has a logical gap. You argue that legalizing drinking at eighteen would reduce drunk driving because kids wouldn't need to "hide" their drinking. But this doesn't necessarily follow—having legal access to alcohol doesn't automatically mean people won't drink and drive. In fact, you could strengthen this section by considering *whether* legal drinking might make people more likely to drink and drive because it becomes more normalized and accessible, then explain why you think the benefits outweigh this risk.

Evidence and Support

This is the area needing the most attention. Right now, your essay relies entirely on assertion and personal reasoning ("I think," "kids are getting in too much trouble") without any concrete evidence. To strengthen your argument significantly:

Cite statistics on drunk driving rates, both before and after the 1984 law change, and in comparison to other countries with lower drinking ages
Research countries with lower drinking ages (much of Europe, for instance) and compare relevant outcomes
Find examples of specific policy proposals (like the "GRAD Act" or other actual legislative efforts to lower the drinking age) to show this isn't just a personal opinion but part of a larger public debate
Consider citing military statistics: How many 18-20 year olds serve? How many have died in recent conflicts? This would make your strongest argument even more powerful.

Structure

Your five-paragraph structure is a reasonable start, but each paragraph would benefit from a clearer topic sentence that previews its specific argument, and smoother transitions between paragraphs. The essay currently reads as a list of separate reasons rather than a cumulative, building case.

Overall Suggestion

Focus your revision on: (1) thorough proofreading, (2) fixing that critical contradiction in your conclusion, (3) adding real evidence/statistics to support at least two of your main points, and (4) acknowledging and responding to the strongest counterargument (the connection to drunk driving fatalities that motivated the original law). This will transform your essay from an opinion piece into a substantive, evidence-based argument.

Feedback on Your Essay on Gender Identity and Roles

Overall Impression

You are grappling with a genuinely interesting and complex question—how children's early exposure to gender stereotypes affects their adult happiness—and you've done real work pulling together four different sources (Pollitt, Farrell, Friedan, and Gray) to support your argument. That synthesis is the essay's strongest feature. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: the clarity and consistency of your thesis, the organization and integration of evidence, and proofreading/mechanics.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim seems to be that children should be taught a "core" of gender identity while remaining open to change, and that current media and cultural messages instead impose rigid, harmful stereotypes. This is a reasonable and interesting thesis, but it gets muddled as the essay progresses. At times you argue that gender differences should be celebrated ("This difference is what makes life exciting"), and at other times you argue that the stereotypes creating those differences are the *problem*. You need to clarify: are you arguing that innate differences are good and should be nurtured, or that socially constructed roles are harmful and should be dismantled, or some more nuanced synthesis of both? Right now the essay reads as if it wants to have it both ways without explaining how they fit together. A stronger thesis would explicitly state this synthesis in the introduction—something like: "Children should be taught that some gender differences are natural and worth celebrating, but the *rigid, moralized* stereotypes imposed by media and culture prevent adults from living authentically." Make this distinction early and return to it consistently.

Organization

The essay's structure follows your sources somewhat mechanically (Pollitt, then Farrell/Friedan, then Gray) rather than following an argument. Consider organizing around *claims* rather than *sources*—for example, one section on how children absorb harmful messages, one on the adult consequences, and one on a proposed solution. Right now, each source gets its own paragraph almost like a book report, which weakens the sense that you're building a single argument.

The short paragraph beginning "Pollitt points out..." is only one sentence and appears to be a stray note rather than a developed paragraph—it should be integrated into the surrounding text, not left as an isolated fragment.

Your transitions between paragraphs are abrupt. Phrases like "Now that our children are off to a wonderful start they are ready for adulthood" are clever but function more as sarcasm than as a logical bridge. Make sure each new paragraph explicitly states how it builds on the previous point.

Use of Evidence

You cite your sources but rarely explain *why* the quotation or example matters to your argument. For instance, you mention that "Masters and Johnson say children begin to recognize gender roles by age three," but you don't explain what this fact does for your argument—does it suggest urgency? Vulnerability? Say so explicitly. Similarly, when you cite Pollitt on PBS bias or Gray on the "cave" metaphor, follow the citation with a sentence of analysis: What does this example prove? How does it support your thesis?

Also, be careful with your citation format—page numbers appear in parentheses without a signal phrase explaining which text they're from (eg, "(548)" should be tied clearly to Masters and Johnson in the same sentence, and you should follow whatever citation style your instructor requires, likely MLA, consistently throughout).

Style and Mechanics

This essay needs careful proofreading. Some issues to fix:

Names: You spell "Pollitt" inconsistently (Pollit, Pollitt), and misspell Friedan ("Fredian," "Friedon"), Farrell ("Ferrell"), and Gray ("Grey," and possibly the wrong first name—double check "John Gray" vs. "John Grey"). Also "Venus" is spelled "Venue."
Typos/word errors: "relazed" (relaxed?), "ammend" (amend), "sponse" (spouse), "scarry" (scary), "piont" (point), "metophor" (metaphor), "rolds" (roles), "Califorian" (Californian).
Word choice: "exogenic pressures" is unclear—did you mean "external" pressures? Clarify or replace jargon that isn't functioning precisely.
Sentence-level clarity: Some sentences are grammatically tangled, eg, "To achieve a well adjusted, gender relazed, adulthood society must ammend the method and the message we are giving our children" needs restructuring for clarity and correct punctuation (no comma needed before "adulthood," and "relaxed" should modify "adulthood," not stand alone).

I strongly recommend reading the essay aloud before your next draft—many of these errors will become audible.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your introduction to state a single, clear, synthesized thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around ideas/claims rather than one-source-per-paragraph.
After every quotation or citation, add at least one sentence of analysis connecting it explicitly to your thesis.
Proofread carefully for spelling, especially proper names.
Reconsider the tone in places (sarcasm can work, but overuse weakens academic credibility).

You have a strong foundation of ideas and sources—the next draft should focus on tightening the logical thread that connects them.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely complex and controversial topic, and you clearly have passionate views on drug legalization. You've gathered several distinct arguments (health regulation, crime reduction, jail overcrowding, disease prevention, civil liberties), which shows you've thought about this issue from multiple angles. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of organization, argumentation, and clarity to make your case persuasively. Below are the key areas to address.

Thesis and Focus

Your thesis—that legalizing marijuana would improve society—gets lost. The introduction mentions marijuana specifically, but paragraphs shift between discussing marijuana, "drugs" generally, and even alcohol/cigarettes comparisons. Revise your thesis to clearly state your specific claim, and then make sure every paragraph explicitly ties back to that claim. If you want to argue for broader drug legalization (not just marijuana), say so upfront and defend that stronger claim; if you mean primarily marijuana, keep the focus there and use other substances only as points of comparison.

Paragraph Structure and Topic Sentences

Many paragraphs lack clear topic sentences and drift between ideas without transitions. For example, the paragraph on age limits moves from law obedience to underage smoking punishments to fake IDs without clear logical connections. Each paragraph should open with a topic sentence that states its main claim, followed by evidence and analysis that supports that specific point. Right now, ideas are listed rather than developed.

Logical Development and Evidence

Several arguments are asserted without support:
"A majority of drugs relate to or have deadly effects" — cite a source.
"Most violent crimes stimulate from selling illicit drugs" — this is a significant claim; provide statistics or a citation.
"Marijuana is probably one of the least harmful drugs" — support this with evidence, not just opinion.

You have one citation (Wibotsky), which is good, but it appears with a typo and is dropped in without context—we don't know who this person is or why their opinion matters. Introduce your source properly ("As legal scholar [Name] argues...") and integrate more research throughout to strengthen your claims. Right now the essay reads as personal opinion; evidence will make it academic argument.

Addressing Counterarguments

You raise the health-risk objection early, which is good practice, but you don't fully answer it—you pivot quickly to comparing marijuana with cigarettes rather than addressing addiction, health costs, or long-term societal effects directly. A stronger essay would acknowledge the strongest opposing arguments and directly refute them with evidence, rather than sidestepping them by comparison alone.

Similarly, your point about age limits seems to undercut your own argument—if kids can't be prevented from getting alcohol/cigarettes now, doesn't that suggest legalization *increases* access problems rather than solving them? This section needs clearer reasoning about how *your* policy proposal addresses this concern, rather than just describing the current problem.

Consistency of Argument

There's a contradiction worth resolving: you argue drugs should be legal because "people have the right to hurt themselves," but you also suggest lowering the drinking/smoking age might *reduce* underage use. These are two different justifications (rights-based vs. practical-effects-based) and should be organized as distinct arguments, each with its own paragraph, rather than blended together.

Also, be careful with claims like "intravenously used drugs would not likely lead to the spread of AIDS" — this needs stronger qualification and evidence, since providing needles doesn't guarantee people won't share them.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are numerous errors that need proofreading:
Sentence fragments: "Issues such as permanent damage to the brain and other parts of the anatomy" (attach to previous sentence).
Subject-verb agreement: "The side effects of marijuana is" → "are"
Word choice errors: "lease harmful" → "least harmful"; "hann" → "harm"; "injuries" → "injures"; "use" → "us" (in the quotation)
Run-on ideas: "People are going to use drugs regardless of what anyone say" → subject-verb agreement issue ("says")

Read your essay aloud or use grammar-checking software to catch these errors, as they undermine your credibility as a writer.

Conclusion

Your conclusion raises good rhetorical questions but doesn't synthesize your specific arguments. Consider briefly summarizing your strongest 2-3 points before ending with your call to action or final provocative question.

Overall Priority for Revision

Narrow and clarify your thesis (marijuana specifically, or drugs broadly?)
Reorganize into clear paragraphs, each with one main argument and topic sentence
Add evidence/citations to support factual claims
Directly address counterarguments rather than sidestepping them
Proofread carefully for grammar and word-choice errors

You have compelling ideas here—the challenge now is organizing and supporting them with the rigor this topic deserves. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, relatable topic and anchored it with a concrete anecdote that immediately draws readers in. Your argument—that professors should demonstrate English proficiency before teaching—is clear and defensible. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: proofreading, structural organization, evidence, and deeper engagement with counterarguments.

Strengths to Build On

Your opening anecdote is your strongest asset. It's specific, personal, and illustrates the real consequences of the problem (a student potentially delayed from graduating). Use this narrative momentum throughout the essay rather than letting it become "backstory" that you leave behind.

Areas for Revision

Proofreading and Formatting
This draft contains numerous typos, stray punctuation marks, and formatting artifacts (eg, "b t U," "a-,@ spoke," "squander through," "1, university"). These appear to be leftover errors from copying/pasting or OCR scanning. Before your next revision, do a careful line-by-line edit. These errors distract readers from your argument and undermine your credibility—especially ironic in an essay about the importance of clear communication.

Strengthen Your Thesis and Roadmap
Your thesis appears in paragraph 3: professors should prove English proficiency before teaching. Consider moving a clearer, more specific version of this thesis to your first paragraph, and briefly preview your supporting reasons (eg, professors' core duty to communicate, the financial/tuition argument, and your proposed policy solution). This gives readers a roadmap and makes your essay feel more unified rather than anecdote-then-argument.

Develop Your Evidence Beyond One Anecdote
Right now, your entire case rests on your friend's experience. While vivid, one story is anecdotal evidence, not proof of a widespread pattern. Strengthen your argument by:
Researching whether USC (or universities generally) have any existing policies on language proficiency for international faculty
Citing any studies on student outcomes with non-native English-speaking instructors
Including even one or two other examples (a classmate's experience, a news article, etc)

This will move your essay from "one student's frustration" to "a documented institutional problem."

Address the Counterargument More Substantively
You raise the teaching assistant substitution idea, which is good, but you dismiss it quickly. A stronger essay would seriously engage with why the university *does* hire faculty based on research credentials rather than teaching ability—and then explain why teaching should take priority for undergraduate courses specifically. You might also consider:
Are there valid reasons universities prioritize research output when hiring?
Could there be a distinction between research-focused roles and teaching-focused roles?
Is your solution (mandatory English proficiency programs) actually practical? What would this cost the university? Would it deter qualified researchers from applying?

Anticipating these objections and responding to them will make your argument more persuasive to skeptical readers, including administrators.

Reconsider the Store/Product Analogy
Your comparison of tuition to buying a product ("when a person buys an item from a store, he pays and receives the item he paid for") is a common analogy, but it's somewhat reductive. Education isn't a simple transaction like buying a toaster—it involves effort from both parties, and "results" aren't guaranteed the way a product's function is. Consider either complicating this analogy (acknowledging its limits) or replacing it with a stronger comparison, such as other professional services where competency requirements exist (eg, doctors must pass licensing exams to practice, regardless of their research credentials).

Tighten Repetitive Passages
Several paragraphs repeat the same point in slightly different words (eg, multiple sentences reiterating "professors need to communicate clearly"). Look for places where you can consolidate ideas and cut redundancy. This will make room for the additional evidence and counterargument analysis suggested above.

Clarify Your Proposed Solution
Your solution—requiring Ph.D. hires to pass an English proficiency program similar to TA requirements—is reasonable, but underdeveloped. What would this program look like? Would it test speaking ability specifically (since your friend's professor understood the material but couldn't verbally explain it)? Would existing faculty be grandfathered in, or only new hires? Adding these specifics will make your policy recommendation more concrete and actionable.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Proofread thoroughly for typos and formatting errors
Sharpen and relocate your thesis to the introduction
Add at least one more piece of evidence beyond the single anecdote
Develop a counterargument section addressing why universities prioritize research credentials
Clarify and flesh out your proposed policy solution

This essay has a strong emotional core and a fair, defensible position. With revision focused on evidence, counterargument, and clarity, it can become a much more persuasive piece of writing.

Feedback on "Business is Business" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, emotionally resonant topic, and your passion for the subject is evident throughout. The essay has the raw material for a strong argument about corporate loyalty versus civic loyalty in professional sports. However, the piece currently reads more as an impassioned rant than a structured argumentative essay. Below are specific areas to strengthen.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis is buried and unclear. The opening paragraph ends with "one may assume that the NFL and its tax subsidized teams no longer owe any loyalty to the fans"—this is a reasonable claim, but it's phrased tentatively ("one may assume") rather than asserted as your actual position. Since your entire essay argues that Modell acted in bad faith, state this directly and early: something like, "Art Modell's decision to relocate the Browns reveals that NFL owners, despite receiving enormous public subsidies, feel no genuine obligation to the cities and fans who sustain them."

Structure and Organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Currently, you move between (1) other team relocations, (2) Cleveland's civic investments, (3) the emotional betrayal, and (4) the financial details of Modell's deal—but the connections between these sections aren't always signaled. Consider:

Paragraph 2 (other relocations) feels disconnected from your main argument about Cleveland specifically. Either tie it explicitly back to your thesis (eg, "This pattern of abandonment suggests a league-wide disregard for fan loyalty") or trim it.
The Gateway Project paragraph is effective evidence of civic investment, but you should explicitly state *why* this matters to your argument: Cleveland proved its commitment to its teams, making Modell's departure a betrayal, not a rational business necessity.

Evidence and Development

Your financial details about the Baltimore deal are your strongest evidence—concrete numbers make the "greed" argument persuasive. Push this further: what did Cleveland offer in comparison? You mention the sin tax and stadium renovation, but a direct side-by-side comparison of the two offers would make your case more compelling and analytical rather than purely emotional.

Also, verify your facts. The Houston Oilers moved to Nashville and became the Tennessee Titans, not the "Tennessee Cherokees." This kind of factual error undermines your credibility on a topic where you're asking readers to trust your account of events. Double-check all team names, dates, and figures.

Counterargument

You gesture toward opposing views ("Some try to blame the city of Cleveland...Others say the city needs to suck it up") but dismiss them too quickly without genuine engagement. A stronger essay would spend a few sentences taking these counterarguments seriously—why might reasonable people see this as fair business practice?—before explaining why your view still prevails. This will make your ultimate argument more persuasive, not weaker.

Tone and Word Choice

Some phrasing undermines your credibility as an academic writer:
"Take it like a man" and "gave Cleveland the one finger salute" are colloquial to the point of being flippant in an argumentative essay.
Calling Modell "a liar" and stating he acted because "he is greedy, pure and simple" are conclusions you should *earn* through evidence rather than assert as flat statements. Let the financial details and the broken promise ("I will never take the team out of Cleveland") do that work for you; you can then draw the conclusion more measuredly.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address in revision:
Its/it's: "the cities most beloved team" should be "city's," and "it's tax subsidized teams" should be "its."
Run-on/comma splices: eg, "Others say that the city of Cleveland needs to suck it up and take it like a man" is fine, but many sentences elsewhere fuse independent clauses without proper punctuation.
"Inwhich" should be two words: "in which."
Quotation punctuation: "Bob Costas of NBC's sports called it," The Renaissance of Cleveland."—the comma and quotation marks are misplaced; it should read: Bob Costas of NBC Sports called it "The Renaissance of Cleveland."
Tense consistency: You slip between past and present tense when discussing events that, from your writing position, are still unfolding (eg, the January vote). Decide on a consistent tense for events that haven't yet occurred versus those that have.
Proofread for typos: "Rains" (Rams), "scream rapid" (likely "rabidly"), and missing words ("they that when people talk Cleveland").

Priority for Revision

Clarify and strengthen your thesis statement.
Verify all facts (team names especially).
Add genuine engagement with counterarguments.
Revise tone to sound more analytical, less colloquial.
Proofread carefully for grammar and punctuation.

You have a strong emotional core and good use of financial evidence—tightening the structure and polishing mechanics will make this a much more persuasive piece.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Trap of Others' Advice"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and relatable topic, and your two central examples—Friedan's dissatisfied housewives and Ralph's story—offer strong material for exploring how conformity to others' expectations can derail personal fulfillment. However, the essay would benefit from a clearer central claim, tighter organization, and more careful use of textual evidence.

Thesis and Focus

Your opening paragraph gestures toward an argument about people—especially young people—being "ruled by the advice of others," but this claim is broad and abstract. By the second paragraph, the essay narrows sharply to a specific case: the conflict between career and family for women and men. This is a legitimate and interesting angle, but the transition needs to be made explicit. Consider revising your introduction to state directly: *"This essay will argue that societal expectations about career and family roles have trapped both men and women into choices that betray their deeper wishes, using Friedan's account of housewives and Farrell's account of Ralph as evidence."* A precise thesis like this will help you (and your reader) stay focused throughout.

Organization

The essay moves logically from Friedan's era to Ralph's story to hypothetical alternatives, which is a sound structure. However, several paragraphs feel like extended summaries rather than analysis. For instance, the long paragraph on the "career woman who worked from 9 to 5" reads as description; try to fold analysis into it—*why* does this pattern illustrate the danger of following others' advice? Make your interpretive point after each piece of evidence, not just before it.

Additionally, the essay would be stronger if you flagged your argumentative moves with clearer topic sentences. Right now, paragraphs often begin with narrative ("Ralph decided early on...") rather than with the claim the paragraph is meant to support. Consider revising topic sentences so each one names the point you're proving, eg, "Ralph's story shows that following others' advice can cost a person not only personal happiness but also the relationships he was trying to protect."

Use of Evidence and Quotations

You've left placeholder marks () where quotations should go from Friedan and Farrell. Be sure to fill these in with the actual quoted language and proper citation before submission—an essay that leans this heavily on named sources needs the sources' own words to feel credible. When you do insert quotations, integrate them smoothly into your sentences rather than dropping them in with a dangling attribution ("wrote Friedan" placed oddly before the quotation, for example). A cleaner model: *Friedan described this dissatisfaction as "the problem that has no name," in which women "wondered if they were going crazy" despite their comfortable lives.*

Argument Development

Your hypothetical "what if" paragraphs (What if Ralph had pursued baseball or social-work law? What if a woman delayed her career?) are some of the most persuasive parts of the essay because they force readers to imagine alternatives. Push these further: What evidence or reasoning supports your claim that Ralph "might have earned more love and respect" as a social-work lawyer? Right now these claims are asserted rather than argued.

The essay would also be strengthened by acknowledging a counterargument. For instance, some readers might object that career ambition and family devotion aren't always mutually exclusive, or that societal expectations sometimes exist for good practical reasons (financial security, social stability). Addressing this directly—even briefly—would make your argument more persuasive rather than one-sided.

Finally, the conclusion's call for a "middle road" is a reasonable synthesis, but it arrives abruptly and remains vague. What would this middle road look like in practice? You gesture toward it earlier (a woman delaying career, remote work), so consider returning to these examples in your conclusion to make the resolution feel earned rather than tacked on.

Style and Mechanics

There are a few errors worth correcting:
"erected of them" should likely be "expected of them"
"reouired" → "required"
"tine-in" → "tied in" or "tie-in"
"fear women" → "few women"
Watch for inconsistent claims: you state "most men helped around the house" but this undercuts your broader argument that women bore a disproportionate burden. Either qualify this statement or remove it.
Some sentences are quite long and could be split for clarity (eg, the sentence beginning "Men of my acquaintance have been required to travel...").

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen and foreground your thesis early on.
Fill in missing quotations with proper citations.
Add analytical topic sentences to guide the reader.
Develop your hypothetical scenarios with more supporting reasoning.
Address at least one counterargument.
Proofread for the typos noted above.

With these revisions, this essay has strong potential to become a compelling, well-supported argument about the costs of living by others' expectations.

Overall Impression

For example: "Legal equality between the sexes does not require us to eliminate cultural spaces—like entertainment, marketing, and athletics—that celebrate physical difference, and doing so may actually cause more harm than good."

Each example needs a topic sentence that ties it explicitly back to your thesis, and you need transitional sentences that show *why* this example follows the last one. Right now the essay reads as a list rather than a developing argument.

The athletic competition paragraph makes a fair point about physical differences in sports, but it doesn't connect clearly to your broader argument about culture, marketing, and entertainment. Consider whether this example belongs, or how to bridge it more explicitly.

Tone and Audience Awareness

The essay would benefit from more awareness of counterarguments. Feminist critiques of Hooters, strip clubs, and beauty standards exist for specific, substantive reasons (economic exploitation, unequal standards for men and women, objectification). Engaging with these more seriously—rather than dismissing them—would make your argument more persuasive to skeptical readers, and would demonstrate deeper critical thinking.

Some phrasing is casual to the point of undermining your credibility ("Maybe open one called buttocks for women"). Reconsider whether jokes or provocative asides serve your argument or distract from it.

Grammar and Mechanics

This essay needs careful proofreading. Common errors include:

their/there/they're confusion throughout ("their money," not "there money"; "they're" for contractions)
sense/since ("since" should be "sense" in several places)
resent/recent
hear/here
trask (likely meant "trash")
scene/seen
warring/wearing
Calvin Cline should be "Calvin Klein"
Comma splices and run-on sentences (eg, "The differences here are the underlining driving force...")
"underlining" should likely be "underlying"

I recommend running a careful read-aloud pass, or using grammar-checking software, before your next draft—these errors are frequent enough to distract readers from your ideas.

Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into one clear, arguable claim.

You have an interesting, even daring argument here—the revision work now is about discipline and precision rather than generating new ideas.

Feedback on Your Welfare Reform Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, genuinely contested policy issue and taken a clear position: that a workable welfare reform combines elements from both parties' proposals. This is a reasonable and even sophisticated thesis—it resists the temptation to pick a "side" and instead argues for synthesis. However, the essay's execution doesn't yet match the ambition of that thesis. Below are the main areas to work on.

Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Your thesis names four specific components (time limits, mandatory training, reduced funding for certain programs, and eliminating incentives for more children), which is good—specific claims are easier to defend than vague ones. But the essay's body doesn't consistently argue for these points; it mostly *describes* what each party proposes and *reports* statistics and anecdotes. Argumentative writing needs you to explain *why* your proposed combination is better than either party's plan alone, and to anticipate objections. For example: Why would mandatory training succeed where voluntary programs have failed? What happens to families if AFDC is cut before training programs are fully in place? A stronger draft would spend more time on this reasoning and less on summarizing sources.

Over-reliance on Quotation

This is the most pressing issue. Nearly every sentence contains a quotation, sometimes strung together with minimal connective analysis. This makes the essay read more like a research summary than an argument in your own voice. Ask yourself, for each quotation: *Do I need the source's exact words, or can I paraphrase and cite it instead?* Save direct quotation for language that is especially vivid, authoritative, or difficult to paraphrase without losing meaning (the Whitman quote about women rehearsing "good-bye" and "thank you" is a good example of a quote worth keeping). Numbers and factual claims (billions of dollars, numbers of recipients) can usually be paraphrased in your own sentence structure with a citation.

Citation and Quotation Mechanics

There are several recurring technical errors that need correcting:
Misspelling "Silverstein" as "Sliverstein" and "Whitman" inconsistently capitalized/spelled ("whitman").
Inconsistent and sometimes incorrect punctuation around quotations—commas and periods placed outside quotation marks (eg, "programs," should have the comma inside the closing quotation mark, not after).
Strange typographical symbols appearing where apostrophes or quotation marks should be (eg, "programs is the answer (Silverstein 13-15)11" and "the government," — these look like OCR or formatting errors, but proofread carefully to make sure your quotation marks and punctuation are consistent and correctly placed).
Missing page numbers or inconsistent citation format—make sure every source has consistent in-text citation format per whatever style guide (MLA appears to be intended) you're using.
A full Works Cited page is not included here, but make sure one exists and matches your in-text citations exactly (Silverstein, Economist, Whitman, Del Valle).

A careful proofreading pass focused solely on quotation punctuation and spelling of proper nouns will elevate the essay's professionalism significantly.

Evidence and Logic

Some causal claims need more support. For instance, you write that eliminating AFDC would end "the cash incentive for mothers to have babies," implying that welfare *causes* higher birth rates. This is a contested claim in the welfare debate—you cite Del Valle's statistic that recipients have more children on average than non-recipients, but correlation isn't necessarily causation. A stronger essay would acknowledge this is a debated point rather than presenting it as settled fact. Similarly, the anecdote about Princess Rowell is compelling but functions as a single case study—consider explicitly framing it as an illustrative example rather than proof of a broader trend.

Paragraph Organization and Transitions

Paragraphs are organized by topic (time limits, training, funding cuts) but transitions between them are abrupt ("Another solution..." "Another program..."). Since your thesis promises a *synthesis* of ideas, consider organizing paragraphs to explicitly build a combined proposal step by step, showing how each piece supports the others (eg, "Training programs alone are not enough without corresponding time limits, because...").

Conclusion

Your conclusion asserts that "a simple compromise" would solve the problem, but this feels like an assertion rather than a demonstrated conclusion. After a paper built mostly on source summary, the conclusion should synthesize *your own reasoning* about why this particular combination of policies is more effective than either party's plan alone—this is your chance to show original analytical thinking.

Priority Revisions
Reduce direct quotations by half; paraphrase and analyze more.
Proofread quotation marks, punctuation, and author name spellings.
Add explicit reasoning connecting your four proposed policies into a coherent, defended plan.
Acknowledge counterarguments or complicating evidence (eg, debates about welfare and birth rates).

With revision focused on these points, this essay has real potential to become a persuasive, well-supported argument rather than a research report.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Dormitory Curfew Policy

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic you clearly care about, and your personal investment comes through in the specific anecdotes you share. Readers can tell this policy affects your daily life in real ways. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of grievances than a structured argument. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, reorganize your points, address counterarguments, and clean up numerous mechanical errors that currently distract from your message.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim shifts throughout the essay. At times you argue for overnight visitation specifically; at other points you argue against having *any* curfew; near the end you suggest a compromise (weekend-only visitation). Readers need one clear, consistent claim stated early on. Consider: Are you arguing that curfews should be abolished entirely, or that overnight visitation policies should be applied consistently across all dorms? These are related but distinct arguments, and conflating them weakens your persuasive power.

Suggestion: Write a single thesis sentence stating your primary claim, then organize each paragraph around one supporting reason for that claim. Your safety argument, your maturity argument, and your fairness/consistency argument are all promising—but they need to work together rather than compete.

Organization

Right now, the essay moves associatively—one memory leads to another—rather than building a logical case. For instance, the anecdote about your loud dormmates at 2 a.m. actually seems to argue *against* unrestricted late-night activity, since you yourself found it disrespectful. This story complicates your argument rather than supporting it, so you'll need to either cut it or explicitly address why noise disruption is a separate issue from visitation rights.

Suggestion: Try outlining your essay with these components:
Introduction with clear thesis
Body paragraph: inconsistency of policy (co-ed dorms already allow overnight guests)
Body paragraph: maturity/autonomy argument
Body paragraph: safety argument (walking alone at night)
Counterargument and rebuttal
Conclusion with a concrete proposed policy change

Strengthen Your Evidence

Your anecdotes are your strongest material—especially the story about your cousin in the military. This is genuinely moving and makes a compelling case for exceptions to visitation rules for family members. Lean into this story more; it's more persuasive than general assertions about "wanting freedom."

However, some evidence undermines your argument. The claim that intoxicated students are dangerous when returning to their rooms alone actually seems to argue that curfews should be *stricter* or that policies should ensure people aren't walking alone—not that curfews should be eliminated. Reconsider whether this point serves your thesis or contradicts it.

Address Counterarguments

Right now, you don't acknowledge why the university might have these policies in the first place (safety concerns, liability, parental expectations, preventing exactly the kind of noise disruption you describe in the lobby). A stronger essay would name the opposing view and then explain why your proposal still makes sense despite those concerns. This will make you seem more credible and less like you're only venting.

Suggestion: Add a paragraph like: "Some administrators argue that curfews protect student safety. However, [your rebuttal—perhaps that curfews don't prevent unsafe late-night walks, they simply relocate them]."

Tone

Phrases like "Freshman does not mean mistreat" and "we deserve a bit of freedom" read as informal and somewhat adolescent for an academic argument, which may undercut your case for being treated as an adult. Aim for a tone that models the maturity you're arguing you possess. Avoid rhetorical questions like "Why?" as standalone sentences—expand them into full reflective statements.

Mechanics and Proofreading

There are frequent word-choice errors that likely came from dictation or scanning software (eg, "he" for "be," "sigu.her" for "sign her," "bad" for "had," "he" for "be" again in "if some dorms... A could"). These errors are serious enough to distract readers from your argument. Please proofread carefully, ideally reading the essay aloud, and consider using a grammar-check tool as a first pass before a close human review.

Also work on:
Consistent punctuation (many sentences run together or lack proper periods)
Removing unnecessary quotation marks (eg, "special" dorms could be phrased more directly)
Fixing verb tense consistency (mixing past tense narrative with present tense argument)

Next Steps

Revise with a single clear thesis in mind, cut or reframe anecdotes that don't support that thesis, add at least one counterargument/rebuttal, and do a careful mechanical edit. You have real material here—especially your cousin's story—so the revision should focus on organizing and clarifying rather than generating new content.

Your essay tackles a worthwhile and relatable topic, and your passion for this subject—clearly rooted in personal experience—comes through in several places, especially in your discussion of your high school teacher. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected reflections than a tightly argued piece. To strengthen this work, you'll need to focus on structure, argumentation, and precision. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Focus

Your essay makes several distinct claims: teachers deserve verbal recognition, teachers deserve material rewards, teachers deserve higher pay, and teachers deserve the same respect as doctors. Each of these is a reasonable claim, but together they create a diffuse argument. Ask yourself: what is the single central claim you want readers to accept?

I'd suggest revising your thesis to something like: "Teachers deserve greater recognition—through words, gestures, and fair pay—because of the profound and often unacknowledged impact they have on students' lives." This gives you a unifying thread while still allowing you to discuss multiple *forms* of recognition as supporting points rather than competing arguments.

Organization

Right now, your paragraphs sometimes repeat points made earlier (the elephant pin anecdote after already establishing your personal experience; the "why aren't teachers rewarded" question appearing twice in different forms). Consider organizing around this structure:

Introduction: State thesis clearly.
Body 1: Teachers have significant personal/emotional impact (your strongest material—the "teacher as mother" idea, your personal anecdotes).
Body 2: Society undervalues the difficulty of teaching (the misconception paragraph, the doctor comparison).
Body 3: Concrete recommendations—who should reward teachers and how (students, parents, schools; verbal vs. material; pay).
Conclusion: Return to the "list of people who helped you" image—this is a strong, evocative closing image and could work well distilled into your conclusion.

Development of Ideas

Several claims need more support or explanation:

"High school teachers deserve the most recognition because students in high school seem to be the hardest to deal with" — This is asserted twice but never substantiated. Why are high schoolers harder to teach? Consider addressing adolescent psychology, academic pressure, or behavioral issues briefly.
"Teachers have a mother instinct" — This is a lovely, personal observation, but it risks essentializing all teachers (and ignores male teachers, or teachers whose caregiving doesn't look "maternal"). Consider reframing this as *"many teachers take on a nurturing, almost parental role"* to broaden its applicability.
The doctor comparison — This is one of your most promising arguments but needs more development. What specifically do doctors and teachers share? Both require years of specialized training, both make consequential decisions affecting long-term wellbeing, both are undervalued relative to their impact. Spell this out.
The "Teacher of the Year" redefinition — This is a genuinely interesting idea (redefining an existing award concept), but it appears suddenly and isn't tied back into your broader argument. Consider using it as evidence that recognition *can* be small and daily, not just an annual formal award.

Counterarguments

Your essay would be stronger if it acknowledged the other side. For instance: some might argue that teaching salaries reflect budget constraints tied to public funding rather than a "misconception" about the job's difficulty. Briefly addressing this (even to disagree with it) would show a more sophisticated argument and preempt reader skepticism.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There's a placeholder  in the paragraph about your favorite teacher—make sure to fill this in with the actual words you used.
Watch for sentence fragments used as complete sentences: *"A material gift or a kind word of expression."* This should be attached to the previous sentence or revised into a complete sentence.
Some phrasing needs tightening: *"it teachers that have helped made these figures"* should read *"it is teachers who have helped make these figures [who they are]."*
Pronoun-antecedent issues: *"stay after school for as long as a student needed her to help him"*—consider consistent pronoun use (eg, "them" or "him or her") rather than switching between "her" and "him."
Comma splices appear frequently (eg, "These teachers really care and.try to work with their students"—also note the stray period between "and" and "try").

Voice and Personal Anecdotes

Your personal examples are the most compelling part of this essay—the safety pin, the elephant pins, staying after school. Don't be afraid to lead with these stories rather than embedding them mid-paragraph after abstract claims. Show, then generalize, rather than the reverse.

Next Steps

Draft a single-sentence, unifying thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around 3 core arguments (rather than a chronological stream of thoughts).
Expand your doctor comparison and high-school-difficulty claims with real evidence or clearer reasoning.
Proofread carefully for fragments, comma splices, and the missing quotation.

You have real insight and genuine feeling in this piece—the revision process should focus on giving that insight a clearer, more persuasive shape.

Feedback on "Violence on Television" Essay

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an important and timely topic, and you've clearly done research into the legislative and cultural debate around TV violence. However, the essay's biggest weakness is that it doesn't seem to know what it's arguing. Let's work through the issues so you can strengthen the next draft.

Establish a Clear, Arguable Thesis

Right now, the essay reads more like a summary of sources than an argument. Your introduction suggests that TV violence is harmful ("can sometimes cause the child to become violent"), but by the end, you're arguing that violence is a legitimate storytelling tool and that it's "up to the viewer to decide." These are contradictory positions, and the reader is left unsure what you actually believe.

Revision strategy: Decide on one clear claim. For example:
*"Although television violence serves legitimate narrative purposes, current regulatory efforts like the V-chip are insufficient to protect children from its harmful effects."*
*"Legislative attempts to curb TV violence, such as the V-chip and Senate report card, represent government overreach into creative and parental territory."*

Either position is defensible, but you need to pick one and organize every paragraph around supporting it.

Improve Structure and Paragraph Unity

The essay currently moves through topics (V-chip → Senate bills → NAB's objection → adult appeal of violence → cartoon violence → conclusion) without clear logical connections. Each paragraph should have a topic sentence that ties back to your thesis, and you should use transitions to show *why* one point follows another.

For instance, the paragraph about the NAB's criticism of "federal censorship bureaucracy" is presented right after a paragraph listing five different bills—but you never explain whether you agree or disagree with the NAB, or how this fits your argument. Are you sympathetic to broadcasters, or skeptical of their claims? Tell the reader.

Strengthen Your Use of Evidence

You cite Hepburn, Stern, and Jensen, but you mostly summarize these sources rather than analyzing them. Ask yourself: what does this evidence prove, and how does it support my thesis? For example:

> "The most violent cartoons are 'Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles,' 'Spider Man,' and some 'Bugs Bunny' episodes with Elmer Fudd."

This is a good factual detail, but you follow it with "that is strictly up to the viewer to decide"—which undercuts the point of citing the statistic in the first place. If violence is simply a matter of viewer preference, why does the level of violence in children's cartoons matter? Resolve this tension explicitly.

Also, double-check your citations: you list "1995-1995" for the Jensen study, which is likely a typo (probably 1995-1996). Small errors like this can make readers question your accuracy elsewhere. Make sure you have a Works Cited page with full bibliographic information, since in-text citations alone aren't sufficient.

Address Logical Gaps

Several claims need more support or complication:

"Many children watch violent tv shows because the action thrills them and can sometimes cause the child to become violent." This is a strong causal claim (violence on TV → violent children) that needs actual evidence, not just assertion. Is there a study you can cite?
"The cause for television violence is to add texture to the show and not to poison children's minds." This is presented as fact but is actually a debatable interpretive claim. Acknowledge that intent (adding "texture") and effect (potential harm to children) can both be true simultaneously—your own essay suggests this tension without resolving it.
Rhetorical questions like "how many parents actually watch television with their teenager?" are effective for raising an issue, but you need to follow up. What are you suggesting—that parental oversight, not government regulation, is the real solution? Say so explicitly and develop the point.

Clarify Your Conclusion

Your final paragraph offers two claims that don't quite connect: violence is meant to "add texture," and children will eventually confront violence "rationally or not." Neither of these directly answers the question your essay raises: *should something be done about TV violence, and if so, what?* A strong conclusion should return to your thesis and explain what you want the reader to conclude or do differently.

Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis and put it at the end of your introduction.
Reread each paragraph and ask, "Does this support my thesis?" If not, revise or cut it.
Add analysis after each piece of evidence explaining its significance.
Fix the citation typo and ensure your Works Cited page is complete.
Revise the conclusion to directly answer the question your thesis poses.

With a clearer argumentative stance, this essay has strong potential—you've clearly identified rich, relevant source material. The next draft just needs a more consistent argumentative throughline.

Feedback on "Government Funding for the Arts" Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely interesting and debatable topic, and your essay shows real enthusiasm for the subject—particularly evident in your personal aside about painting as "escape and relaxation." You've organized your argument into distinct categories (anthropological, communicative, community-building, financial, and therapeutic), which gives readers a clear roadmap. However, the essay would benefit significantly from stronger evidence, tighter logic, and more disciplined focus. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Argument and Logic

Your opening move—suggesting that defense spending cuts are "obviously" appropriate while arts funding should be protected—needs more support. Right now, this comparison feels asserted rather than argued. Why is defense different from the arts in terms of its claim on public funds? You don't need to resolve this fully, but acknowledging the complexity would strengthen your credibility.

Similarly, your historical example of Louis XIV–XVI and Napoleon is a double-edged sword. You cite them to show art's power to "communicate values," but these are examples of art used for propaganda and personal glorification by monarchs—not exactly a ringing endorsement of art's democratic, community-building potential that you argue for elsewhere. Consider either developing this tension explicitly or choosing a different example that more directly supports your thesis about camaraderie and shared communication.

Your financial argument also contains an unaddressed tension: if museums "attract tourists" and "encourage art sales" that benefit dealers and artists, this actually suggests the arts can be self-sustaining or profitable. You need to explain more precisely *why* government subsidy is necessary if the arts already generate revenue. Is it about access for underfunded institutions? Startup costs? Public goods that private markets underprovide? This is where a stronger economic argument could go, but as written, the paragraph works against your own thesis.

Use of Evidence

This is the area most in need of revision. Throughout the essay, you rely on vague appeals to authority: "psychologists and psychoanalysts have told us," "as researchers have found," "continual research has been done." These phrases signal that evidence exists without actually providing it. For an argumentative essay, you need specific studies, statistics, or named authorities. For example:

Which researchers studied art and "clearer thinking," and what did they find?
What specific data exists on museum-driven tourism revenue?
Which prison art programs have been evaluated, and what were the reported outcomes (recidivism rates, behavioral changes)?

Without this specificity, your claims risk sounding like assumptions rather than substantiated arguments. Even one or two concrete, cited examples per section would dramatically strengthen your credibility.

Focus and Digression

The paragraph distinguishing "art" from "pornography" is a significant digression that doesn't serve your central argument about government funding. It raises a complex definitional question (what counts as art?) that you don't have space to resolve, and it risks derailing readers from your main thesis. Unless you're specifically addressing an anticipated counterargument (eg, "opponents say public funds might support obscene material"), consider cutting this section or substantially shortening it to a single sentence acknowledging the issue exists.

Counterarguments

Your essay would be considerably stronger if it directly engaged with opposing views. Common objections to arts funding include: opportunity costs (money could fund healthcare, education, etc), questions about who benefits (do elite museums serve the general public?), and free-market arguments (should the arts survive on private patronage alone?). Addressing at least one of these seriously—and then countering it—would demonstrate critical thinking and make your argument more persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Style and Mechanics

A few smaller issues to address:

The opening metaphor ("looms on the horizon like an ominous storm cloud") is a bit clichéd; consider a more original opening.
Watch for typos ("pschosanalysts") and awkward phrasings ("it leaves many to think which area").
The word "necessarily" is misused in "Necessarily, it's interesting to note..."—consider "Notably" or simply removing it.
You default to "he/his" when discussing humanity in general ("man has seen a need," "the artist... his or her"); consider consistent gender-neutral language throughout.
Some paragraph transitions are weak (eg, "Following from the idea of sharing" doesn't clearly link back to prior content). Consider explicit topic sentences that connect each paragraph to your overarching thesis.

Suggestions for Revision

Cut or drastically shorten the pornography discussion.
Add 2-3 specific, cited sources or studies to bolster your strongest claims.
Clarify the financial argument—address why subsidy is needed despite revenue generation.
Add a paragraph addressing at least one counterargument.
Proofread carefully for typos and awkward phrasing.
Strengthen your conclusion by directly restating your thesis and perhaps proposing a concrete policy recommendation.

You have a solid conceptual framework here—now it needs the evidentiary backbone to make it truly persuasive. Good luck with your revision!
