Feedback Report: Salary Cap Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic with real contemporary relevance, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You clearly care about issues of fairness, and your personal anecdote about the rundown stadium adds a nice touch of concrete detail. However, this essay needs significant revision to strengthen its argumentative structure, logical consistency, and use of evidence. Below are the key areas to focus on as you revise.

Thesis and Organization

Your thesis appears in the first paragraph—you list three reasons for a salary cap—but the essay doesn't consistently follow this structure. The bulk of your essay (paragraphs 2-4) actually argues something different: that football players are overpaid and undeserving relative to doctors, and that this money should go to charity instead. This is a compelling argument, but it's not really an argument *for a salary cap*—it's an argument that players make too much money, period. A salary cap doesn't necessarily reduce *total* team spending; it just distributes money differently among players. You need to clarify: are you arguing for lower player salaries overall, or for a more equitable distribution among players? These are different claims requiring different evidence.

Revision suggestion: Return to your three original reasons and make sure each body paragraph clearly maps onto one of them. Right now, the doctor/football player comparison, the charity discussion, and the OJ Simpson example all feel like separate essays loosely stitched together.

Logical Gaps

Several of your points need stronger reasoning to connect cause and effect:

The OJ Simpson example is a serious accusation (that he was acquitted because of his fame) presented without any support or connection to the salary cap argument. How would a salary cap have prevented this? This paragraph would be stronger if cut entirely, or if you clarified the actual connection between wealth/fame and legal outcomes.

The stadium paragraph conflates two different issues: player salaries and *organizational* spending priorities. A salary cap on players wouldn't necessarily result in better bathrooms—that money might just go to team owners' profits instead. You need a clearer causal chain here.

The Deion Sanders/free agency section is actually your strongest and most relevant argument, since it directly addresses competitive imbalance—but it arrives quite late in the essay. Consider moving this up, since it most directly supports your stated thesis.

Evidence and Specificity

Right now, most of your claims rely on assertion rather than evidence. Consider:
What is the actual average salary of an NFL player versus a doctor? Citing real figures would strengthen your argument considerably.
Is there any historical or economic explanation for *why* money entered football in the 1950s? This claim needs a source or at least more context.
"There is no person on Earth that could possibly use the amount of money" is a strong claim—can you find any data on athlete salaries, endorsement deals, or spending habits to support it?

Academic argument is more persuasive when grounded in verifiable facts, not just moral appeals.

Tone and Fairness

Some phrasing risks alienating readers who might otherwise be sympathetic to your argument:
Describing football as something that requires only the ability to "run and catch balls" oversimplifies the athleticism, strategy, and physical risk involved in professional sports. This weakens your credibility, since it's easily challenged.
"Greedy football players" is an emotionally loaded phrase. A more persuasive essay would critique the *system* that allows/encourages high salaries rather than attacking players' character directly.

Revision suggestion: Try rephrasing points to critique structures and incentives (eg, "the current salary system incentivizes...") rather than making individual moral judgments about players' worth as people.

Sentence-Level Issues

Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences, particularly in paragraph 2 ("A student can graduate from college as a doctor of science and still not be as important...").
"ant other team" should be "any other team."
"wee" should be "we."
Several sentences use "so" as a sentence-starter for rhetorical effect—this is fine occasionally, but overuse weakens formal tone.
Consider varying your paragraph transitions; several paragraphs begin with a rhetorical question, which becomes repetitive as a structural device.

Priority Revisions

If you have time to focus on only a few things, prioritize:
Clarify your central claim—is this about salary caps specifically, or about reducing athlete pay generally?
Cut or substantially rework the OJ Simpson paragraph, which doesn't support your thesis and introduces an unsupported legal claim.
Move the free-agency/Deion Sanders paragraph earlier, as it's your most directly relevant evidence.
Add concrete data (salary figures, historical context) to strengthen credibility.

You have a genuine argumentative instinct and strong personal conviction—channeling that into a more tightly organized, evidence-based structure will make this essay considerably more persuasive.

Feedback on Gun Control Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, contentious topic and assembled substantial research to support your position. Your essay demonstrates genuine engagement with the sources and a clear thesis: that gun control is both constitutional and necessary to reduce violence. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of researched points than a cohesive, polished argument. Below are specific areas for revision.

Structure and Organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Currently, it moves somewhat unpredictably between constitutional analysis, statistics, rebuttals to pro-gun arguments, and policy proposals. Consider reorganizing around a clear structure:

Introduction with thesis (your current opening paragraph is strong—keep this)
Constitutional argument (Second Amendment analysis)
Evidence of the problem (statistics, the Brady example)
Rebuttal of counterarguments (the "slippery slope" argument, NRA claims)
Proposed solutions (licensing, registration, waiting periods)
Conclusion

Right now, similar content appears in multiple places—for instance, you discuss federal legislation and licensing programs somewhat repetitively across several paragraphs. Consolidate these into one well-developed section.

Use of Evidence

Your statistics (12,000 murders, 200,000 injuries, etc) are powerful, but they need proper introduction and follow-up analysis. Don't just state numbers—explain what they mean for your argument. After citing that 16,000 people use firearms for suicide, for example, connect this explicitly to your waiting-period argument: does easy access increase impulsive suicide, similar to your point about impulsive murder?

The Jim Brady anecdote is compelling but oddly placed and confusingly written (see citation issues below). Consider opening a body paragraph with this narrative to hook readers before transitioning to your analytical point about background checks.

Critical Citation and Attribution Problems

This is the most urgent issue to address:

"lnl981,JimBrady,W'hiteHousePresssecretary..." — This appears to be a garbled OCR or copy-paste error. You need to rewrite this sentence entirely: something like "In 1981, James Brady, White House Press Secretary, was shot during the assassination attempt on President Ronald Reagan."

Inconsistent citation of "Cozic" vs. "Cosec" — You cite this source as "Cozic" in most places but "Cosec" in others (eg, "(Cosec 198-9)," "(Cosec 200)"). Verify the correct spelling and use it consistently throughout.

Cole vs. Cozic confusion — You cite "(Cole 198-9)" and "(Cozic 198)" for what seem to be different pieces of information, but also "(Cole 298-9)" earlier. Double-check your notes to ensure you're attributing each claim to the correct source and page number.

Missing quotation marks and unclear attribution: In the paragraph beginning "The NRA debates," you write "the NRA debates 11 the 2nd Amendment guarantees..." — this needs a proper opening quotation mark and should read "argues," not "debates" (organizations don't "debate" a claim; they argue for one).

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

Several sentences need revision:

"federal laws restrict private ownership of sawed of shotguns" → "sawed-off shotguns"
"Statisticians, however, point out that persuasiveness of a statistic lies in its ability to be factually verified" — awkward phrasing; consider: "Statisticians note that a statistic's persuasiveness depends on its verifiability."
"definatly" → "definitely" (proofread carefully for spelling)
"riffles" → "rifles"
"require gun buyers to prevent identification" → likely should be "present identification"
The sentence "Not every individual who wants to own a gun should be trusted with it" is a good point but feels disconnected from surrounding sentences—smooth the transition.

Argumentative Development

Your rebuttal of the "slippery slope" argument (para. 6) is one of your strongest moments—you directly acknowledge and respond to a counterargument, which strengthens your credibility. Do more of this. For instance, after presenting the NRA's position on the Second Amendment, spend a sentence or two explaining *why* their interpretation fails, rather than just quoting the Supreme Court's contrary view.

Similarly, your point about statistical verifiability (pro-control evidence vs. pro-gun claims) is sophisticated but underdeveloped. Explain *why* claims about guns "preventing" crimes are harder to verify—is it because prevented crimes leave no evidence? This reasoning would strengthen your argument considerably.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph shifts to a more rhetorical, patriotic register ("Founding Fathers foresaw a great nation of peace...") that doesn't quite match the analytical tone of the rest of the essay. This can work as a concluding flourish, but make sure it follows logically from your evidence rather than feeling like a new appeal. Consider explicitly summarizing your three strongest points before this closing gesture.

Priority Revisions

Fix all citation inconsistencies and verify source names/page numbers
Rewrite the garbled Jim Brady sentence
Reorganize to eliminate redundancy between paragraphs on legislation/licensing
Add analytical follow-through after each major statistic
Proofread thoroughly for spelling and grammar

With these revisions, you'll have a much stronger, more credible essay. Good luck!

Feedback on Your Abortion Essay

Thank you for tackling such a difficult and personal topic. You clearly have strong convictions, and the personal anecdote about your cousin adds an emotional dimension to your argument. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: structure, logical consistency, argumentative strategy, and tone. Below are the key areas to focus on as you revise.

Logical Consistency

This is the most pressing issue. Your essay establishes a firm position early on—that abortion is essentially murder and those who have it should be jailed—but then you carve out two significant exceptions (rape and life-threatening pregnancy). This creates a contradiction that undermines your argument. If abortion is the "murder" of an "innocent, defenseless, helpless child," as you state in your conclusion, how does the circumstance of conception change the moral status of that child? A rigorous argument would need to address this tension directly rather than simply asserting both positions. Consider: are you arguing that abortion is *always* wrong, or that it should be *legally restricted* with specific exceptions? These are different claims requiring different support.

The Cousin Anecdote Undermines Your Thesis

This is a significant structural problem. Your most vivid, detailed piece of evidence—the story about your cousin—actually illustrates the *dangers* of restricting a young woman's reproductive choices (her mother forced the decision, the procedure was seemingly performed poorly, and she suffered lasting harm). Yet you conclude from this story that abortion needs *stricter* government regulation. Ask yourself: what does this anecdote actually demonstrate? It seems to show harm caused by lack of consent and possibly inadequate medical care—not harm caused by abortion being too accessible. Revise this section so your evidence actually supports your claim, or reconsider what claim the evidence is pointing you toward.

Contraception Statistics Don't Support Your Argument

You spend a full paragraph detailing contraceptive effectiveness rates, concluding that "a person has no excuse for wanting an abortion after consensual sex." But even your own numbers acknowledge failure rates—condoms are not 100% effective, and even combined methods carry some risk. If contraception fails despite responsible use, is the resulting pregnancy really evidence of irresponsibility deserving punishment? This section needs to either be cut significantly or reframed to acknowledge that contraceptive failure is a foreseeable reality, not a hypothetical.

Tone and Word Choice

Several phrases undercut the seriousness of your topic and could alienate readers you're trying to persuade:
"if a person decides to play the game" and "not be in the game" (referring to sex) feels flippant given the gravity of the subject.
"horseplay" to describe sexual activity trivializes the topic.
"his vicious sperm" and "this fiend" in the rape passage, while emotionally understandable, read as sensationalized rather than persuasive.

Aim for language that reflects the seriousness of both pregnancy and sexual violence. Precision and restraint will make your argument more credible.

Extreme Proposals Need Support

Your suggestion that women who have abortions should be "put in jail for murder" is a serious legal and moral claim that needs far more development. Are you calling for the same sentencing as for killing a born person? How would this apply retroactively? This proposal, as stated, may distract readers from your broader point rather than strengthening it. Consider whether this claim serves your essay's purpose or whether a more measured legal argument would be more persuasive.

Structural and Organizational Issues

Introduction: State your thesis more precisely. Right now it's broad ("strict rules") but the essay reveals a much more specific position with several caveats. Preview these caveats in your intro so readers aren't surprised later.
Adoption paragraph: Your point about adoption as an alternative to abortion is a good one, but it's underdeveloped—address potential counterarguments (eg, health risks of full-term pregnancy, emotional cost of pregnancy and adoption).
Conclusion: This is a good place to restate your refined thesis, but currently it doesn't acknowledge your rape/health exceptions, making the ending feel disconnected from the middle of the essay.

Sentence-Level Issues

There are several grammatical errors to address in revision (eg, "how are financially unable," "I does think," "her family," subject-verb agreement issues, and comma splices). A careful proofreading pass focused solely on grammar will help your argument read as more polished and authoritative.

Suggested Next Steps

Decide precisely what your thesis is (total prohibition? regulation with exceptions?) and make sure every paragraph supports that specific claim.
Revisit the cousin story—either change what you're arguing it proves, or use different evidence.
Cut or reframe the contraceptive statistics section.
Revise language for tone and seriousness.
Proofread carefully for grammar and clarity.

You have real passion for this topic—channel it into a tighter, more internally consistent argument, and this essay will be much stronger.

Feedback Report: "Sex in the Media" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a timely and engaging topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You've chosen concrete, recognizable examples (specific TV shows, songs, and ad campaigns) that ground your argument in real cultural touchpoints. However, the essay would benefit from a sharper central claim, more logical connections between paragraphs, and revision for grammar and mechanics. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your title and opening line say sex is "overrated" in the media, but as the essay develops, your actual concern seems to be something different: that sex is *overexposed* and *irresponsibly portrayed* (ie, shown without consequences like condom use or STD risk). "Overrated" suggests something is overvalued or given too much credit, which isn't quite what you're arguing. Consider revising your thesis to state directly what you mean—something like: *"The media's saturation with sex, stripped of any discussion of consequences or responsibility, contributes to unrealistic and unsafe attitudes among teenagers."* This gives your essay a clearer throughline and helps you avoid drifting between "there's too much sex" and "the sex shown is irresponsible," which are related but distinct claims.

Organization and Logical Development

Your paragraphs are organized topically (TV, radio, magazines, then consequences), which is a reasonable structure. However, the transitions between paragraphs are mechanical ("Television and radio are not the only places...") rather than analytical. Try to end each paragraph with a sentence that explains *why* the example matters to your argument, rather than simply announcing the next topic.

The biggest logical gap in the essay is the leap from "media shows a lot of sex" to "teenagers get pregnant and contract STDs because of media." This is a serious claim that needs support beyond personal observation. Right now, the connection is asserted rather than argued. Consider:
Acknowledging other contributing factors (lack of sex education, family communication, peer pressure) so your argument doesn't seem to place all blame on media.
Citing at least one statistic or study connecting media exposure to teen sexual behavior. Even one credible source would significantly strengthen your credibility.

Your conclusion introduces new ideas (the "understood sex" concept, the challenge to media producers) rather than synthesizing what you've already argued. Consider moving the "understood sex" idea earlier, perhaps as a proposed solution in its own paragraph, and use your conclusion to reinforce your central claim and its stakes.

Use of Evidence

Your examples from television, music, and advertising are your strongest asset—they're specific and vivid. To strengthen them further:
You left a placeholder () where the Gravity cologne slogan should go. Fill this in before submitting.
Double-check factual details: the artist's name is R. Kelly, not "R. Kellye."
Consider adding one example of "responsible" media portrayal (if one exists) for contrast, which would make your critique feel more balanced and less like a blanket condemnation.

Style and Mechanics

The essay contains several errors that need correction in revision:
Word errors: "bluntly saids" → "bluntly says"; "soak and wet" → "soaked and wet"; "precautions measures" → "precautionary measures" or just "precautions."
Apostrophes: "shampoo is used to wash your hair not your whole body" is fine, but watch missing apostrophes elsewhere, eg, "model's head" is correct, but check similar possessives throughout.
Sentence fragments and run-ons: Several sentences are fragments ("Instead, they show a model lying down with a sheet wrapped around her body. Nothing but a sheet.") — this can be effective for emphasis if used sparingly and intentionally, but you use this technique often enough that it starts to feel like a pattern of incomplete sentences rather than a stylistic choice.
Repetition: The "You can't..." series near the end is rhetorically powerful, but the parallel structure breaks down slightly ("You can talk and advertise more..." shifts from "can't" to "can," which is a good turn, but make sure the transition is clearly marked, eg, "But you *can*...").
Tone shifts: Phrases like "Now if I'm not mistaken" and "Its funny how..." are conversational and can work in an argumentative essay, but use them consistently—right now the tone moves between casual and formal in ways that feel unintentional.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to precisely name your claim (overexposure + lack of responsibility, not "overrated").
Add at least one outside source or statistic to support the media-behavior connection.
Fill in the missing slogan quote.
Proofread carefully for the specific errors noted above.
Reorganize the conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new material.

You have a strong foundation with vivid, relatable examples—sharpening your central claim and tightening the logic connecting your evidence to your conclusions will make this a much more persuasive essay.

Feedback on "The Psychology of Fashion" Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial reading on the psychology of clothing, and you bring in a nice range of sources—Ryan, Flugel, Davis, even a Herrick poem. However, the essay's biggest weakness is that it reads as a summary of research rather than an argument. As you revise, your central task should be to decide: what is the one claim I am trying to persuade my reader of? Right now, the essay touches on identity, adolescents, consumer motivation, the elderly, and modesty—five distinct topics that could each be its own essay—but never commits to a thesis that ties them together or stakes out a debatable position.

Thesis and Argument

Look at your opening paragraph. It offers observations ("clothing reveals mood," "clothing invites judgment") but no arguable claim. An argumentative essay needs a thesis that someone could disagree with. For example, you might argue: *"Because clothing choices are driven primarily by the need for social acceptance rather than individual self-expression, fashion functions less as a form of personal freedom than as a tool of social conformity."* That kind of claim would give every subsequent paragraph a job to do—supporting, complicating, or qualifying that idea—rather than simply reporting information from Ryan or Flugel.

Right now, each paragraph functions as a mini-report on what one source says, and the transitions between paragraphs (adolescents → consumer motivation → the elderly → modesty) are abrupt because nothing is pulling the material toward a single point. Revise by asking, after each paragraph, "How does this prove my thesis?" If you can't answer, either cut the paragraph or reframe it.

Use of Evidence

You rely heavily on quotation and paraphrase, sometimes stringing several source-based sentences together with little of your own analysis in between (see the paragraph on Ryan, pages 82–91). Strong argumentative writing uses sources as *support* for your own claims, not as the claims themselves. After each quotation or paraphrase, add a sentence of your own that explains *why* this evidence matters to your argument. For instance, after the Hollingworth quotation about "delinquency" tied to clothing cravings, don't just move to dress codes—tell us what this reveals about the power of peer approval, and connect it explicitly back to your thesis.

The Herrick poem is a nice touch, but it's dropped into the essay with no interpretation. Tell your reader what the poem illustrates about your argument (eg, that "disorder" in dress can be read as authenticity rather than carelessness) before moving on.

The personal example ("if I were a person who dresses the way society wants me to dress...") is a good instinct—analysis benefits from concrete illustration—but it's phrased hypothetically and vaguely. Consider replacing it with a real, specific example (from observation, media, or personal experience) that you analyze in more depth.

Organization

Consider restructuring around your thesis rather than around life stages (adolescents, elderly) and topics (motivation, modesty) in sequence. If your argument is about conformity vs. self-expression, you could organize paragraphs by *type of evidence* (psychological studies, historical/literary example, sociological data on age groups) all working toward the same claim, rather than by age group. Also, the modesty section (Flugel's five-part list) feels like a digression—it's interesting but disconnected from what precedes and follows it. Either connect it explicitly to your central claim or cut it.

Conclusion

Your conclusion mostly restates ideas already covered rather than synthesizing them into a final insight. Try ending with a clear statement of what your evidence has shown and why it matters—what should the reader now understand about fashion and psychology that they didn't before?

Style, Tone, and Mechanics

A few smaller issues to address in revision:

Register: The opening line's list ("a slut, a pimp") is jarring and informal for academic writing. Consider more neutral language ("promiscuous," "aggressive," or simply cutting this and choosing more precise, professional diction).
Subject-verb agreement: "she probably feel sexy," "what a person wears can be a good indicator on how that person feel"—these need correction throughout ("feels").
Capitalization/sentence starts: "one doesn't know what style..." should begin with a capital letter; check for sentence fragments and run-ons throughout.
Typos: "fell self-pity" should likely be "feel self-pity."
Citation consistency: You alternate between naming the author and citing only a page number. Make sure each in-text citation clearly indicates which source it comes from, especially when you switch sources within a paragraph.

Priority for Revision

Draft a specific, debatable thesis.
Cut or subordinate material that doesn't serve that thesis.
Add your own analytical sentences after each piece of evidence.
Proofread carefully for grammar and register.

You have good raw material here—now it needs a spine.

Feedback Report: Abortion Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely important, debate-worthy topic, and your position—that the decision should rest with the pregnant woman—is clear by the end of the essay. However, the essay currently reads more like a rough draft than a polished argument. The central issue is that your reasoning is often *asserted* rather than *demonstrated*, and several passages are grammatically incomplete or logically disconnected. Below are the main areas to revise.

Thesis and Introduction

Your introduction spends time establishing that abortion is controversial, but this is common knowledge and doesn't need extensive setup. Consider cutting the vague historical claim ("These debates began over two decades ago") unless you can support it with a source—right now it reads as an unverified guess. Instead, use your introduction to state your thesis early and precisely: *the moral authority to decide belongs to the woman because she alone bears the consequences.* This claim doesn't fully appear until paragraph two; move it forward so your reader knows immediately what you're arguing.

Paragraph Structure and Logical Gaps

Your third and fourth paragraphs contain the most serious problems. The sentence "For example some anti-abortionist, think that even woman women who have abortions. Rape is something that the woman has no control over..." is grammatically broken and, more importantly, logically incomplete. It appears you meant to say that anti-abortionists don't make exceptions for rape victims, but the sentence never actually states the anti-abortion position before you rebut it. A reader needs to see the opposing argument clearly stated before your rebuttal makes sense. Try restructuring as:

> "Anti-abortionists often argue that abortion is morally wrong under any circumstance, even in cases of rape. However, rape is something a woman has no control over, and she should not be forced to carry a pregnancy that resulted from a violent crime."

This paragraph would also benefit from a real example or statistic—how many abortions result from rape or incest? Even one credible figure would strengthen your rebuttal considerably.

The "Slippery Nazi Slope" Argument

This section is your strongest, because you actually define a term (genocide) and apply logical reasoning to dismantle a comparison. Good instinct. Two suggestions:

Name the argument accurately. The rhetorical device you're describing is generally called "reductio ad Hitlerum" or simply a "slippery slope" argument that invokes Nazi comparisons. "Slippery Nazi Slope" is not a term you'll find in ethics or rhetoric scholarship, so using it without explanation may confuse a reader or grader who knows the field. Either define your coined term explicitly or use the standard terminology.
Cite an actual source. Right now it's unclear who makes this argument. Quoting or paraphrasing a specific pro-life commentator or organization would make your rebuttal feel like it's addressing a real position rather than a strawman.

Repetition Without New Support

Your concluding paragraph largely restates your thesis ("The decision of abortion should be left entirely up to the woman") without adding new reasoning. The claims about "higher death rates from self-induced abortions" and psychological damage to unwanted children are significant and could be powerful—but they need evidence. As written, they are unsupported assertions that could easily be dismissed by a skeptical reader. Even one statistic (eg, historical data on illegal/self-induced abortion mortality rates pre-*Roe v. Wade*) would transform this from a rhetorical flourish into a substantive point.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are frequent comma splices, missing words, and homophone errors that distract from your argument:

"The two names for this categories are either pro-choiceor pro-life" → fix spacing and subject-verb agreement ("these categories").
"proffers" appears to be a typo for "pro-lifers."
"This shows that, genocide is a massive killing..." — this sentence is circular; you define genocide, then say "this shows genocide is a massive killing," which just repeats the definition rather than showing anything new.
"Although if you analyze many of these arguments they are not very substantial." — sentence fragment; "Although" should be removed or the clause should be attached to the next sentence.

I'd strongly recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, before your next draft—many of these errors are the kind that become obvious when heard rather than silently read.

Next Steps

Clarify your thesis and move it earlier.
Rewrite the rape/anti-abortionist paragraph so the opposing argument is stated before it's rebutted.
Add at least two concrete pieces of evidence (statistics, quotations, or named sources) to support your strongest claims.
Proofread carefully for comma splices, fragments, and word confusion (woman/women, there/their).
Ensure your conclusion synthesizes rather than repeats your argument.

You have a clear moral stance and one genuinely strong paragraph (the genocide analysis). Building the rest of the essay to that same standard of clarity and evidence will make this a much more persuasive piece.

Feedback Report: Television and Children's Exposure to Sexuality

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely important and timely topic—the mismatch between adult television programming and children's viewing habits—and you clearly care about this issue, drawing on real experience with your own children. This personal investment gives your writing authenticity. However, the essay needs significant revision in organization, argumentation, and mechanics to make its case persuasively to an academic audience.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim—that children are exposed to and negatively influenced by adult content on television because parents are busy and children's programming is scarce—is worth making, but it isn't stated clearly early on. Consider opening with a concise thesis statement that previews your specific claims (eg, about the shortage of children's programming, the developmental unreadiness of children to process adult content, and the need for industry change). Right now, the essay unfolds associatively, one idea leading to the next, rather than following a planned structure. Each paragraph should have a clear topic sentence that ties back to this thesis.

Evidence and Statistics

The "ninety-eight percent" and "ninety percent" statistics are the backbone of your argument, but they appear without any citation or source. Academic readers will not accept these numbers on faith. Where did you find this information? If it's an estimate based on your own viewing, say so explicitly and soften the language ("it seems that the vast majority..."). If you have a source, cite it. Unsupported statistics undermine your credibility rather than strengthen it.

Your hour-by-hour breakdown of a typical afternoon's programming is a strong, concrete piece of evidence—this is exactly the kind of specific detail that makes an argument persuasive. Consider expanding this technique elsewhere. However, this section also reveals a logical gap: you assume that because certain shows are "not for children," children are necessarily watching them unsupervised in large numbers. You should either provide evidence that children do watch these programs or reframe this as a risk/possibility rather than a certainty.

Personal Examples

Your anecdotes about your own daughters (the kissing/marriage assumption, banning *The Simpsons*) are the most compelling parts of the essay because they are vivid and specific. Use more of these, and integrate them more purposefully—explain explicitly what each example proves about your thesis rather than letting them stand somewhat isolated.

Counterarguments

The essay would be considerably stronger if it acknowledged opposing views. For instance: Don't parents bear primary responsibility for supervising their children's viewing, rather than the television industry? Isn't the V-chip or parental controls a partial solution? By raising and responding to such objections, you'll demonstrate a more sophisticated grasp of the issue and preempt the objections a skeptical reader will raise.

Organization

The essay currently reads as a series of related but loosely connected paragraphs rather than a tightly built argument. I'd suggest reorganizing along these lines:
Introduction with clear thesis
The problem: scarcity of children's programming (with your statistics and schedule breakdown)
Why this matters developmentally: children aren't equipped to process what they see (Ricki Lake/pregnancy example, marriage example)
The role of cartoons and "safe-seeming" content that isn't actually safe (Simpsons example)
The special complexity of topics like homosexuality
Recommendation/solution (more children's programming in after-school hours)
Conclusion restating the stakes

Right now, similar ideas (parents' busyness, the "role model" effect, the developmental argument) repeat across multiple paragraphs rather than being consolidated. Look for redundancy and either cut repeated points or use repetition strategically to build to a climax.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are numerous errors that need correction in revision:
Odd formatting artifacts: "11 Big people shows"", "11The Simpsons"" — these appear to be OCR or typing errors where quotation marks became "11." Check your original document.
Comma splices and run-on sentences (eg, "Only adults do not usually show sexual relations, violence and profanity in front of their children. Instead the television bluntly shows them" — the logic connecting these two sentences needs an explicit transition).
Inconsistent verb tense and subject-verb agreement ("Opra talk show is on" should be "The Oprah talk show is on" or similar; "mom is cooking" vs. "mom is in the kitchen cooking").
Spelling: "Ricki Lake," "Opra" (Oprah), "suppose to" (supposed to), "its" vs. "it's" confusion throughout.
Vague pronoun references: "That's not always a problem" — what does "that" refer to exactly?

I strongly recommend reading the essay aloud slowly, which will help you catch run-ons, missing words, and awkward phrasing.

Tone

Your personal conviction comes through strongly, which is good, but phrases like "I am not sure" or "I feel" undercut your authority in places where you actually have a well-reasoned point to make. Reserve the first person for genuine personal anecdotes, and use more assertive, analytical language when making your argument.

Priority Revisions

Write a clear thesis statement and reorganize paragraphs around it.
Cite or qualify your statistics.
Add at least one counterargument and response.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors.

With revision, this essay has real potential—your evidence and personal examples are genuinely persuasive once the structural and mechanical issues are addressed.

Feedback on Your Recycling Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research and cover an impressive range of recyclable materials—plastics, paper, glass, refrigerators, batteries, motor oil, printer cartridges, and household toxics. Your commitment to the topic is evident, and you provide many specific, memorable facts (the Twin Towers comparison, the light bulb statistic, the mercury limits). However, the essay currently reads more like an informational report organized by category than an argumentative essay with a clear thesis being defended. To strengthen this piece, focus on the issues below.

Clarify and Sharpen Your Thesis

Your introduction states that recycling "needs to become commonplace uniformly," but the essay doesn't consistently argue *why* or *how*—it mostly describes *what* can be recycled. Consider revising your thesis to make a more specific, debatable claim. For example: "Individual consumers, not just corporations, must take responsibility for sorting and recycling household waste, because voluntary industry efforts alone will not solve the landfill crisis." This gives you a claim to defend rather than a topic to survey.

Restructure Around Argument, Not Categories

Right now, each paragraph is essentially "Here is a material; here is how to recycle it." This is useful information, but it's list-like rather than argumentative. Consider reorganizing around *reasons* rather than *materials*. For instance:
Reason 1: Recycling prevents environmental harm (use your Freon, mercury, and motor oil examples here)
Reason 2: Recycling conserves resources and energy (glass, paper, plastic examples)
Reason 3: Landfill space is running out (your Twin Towers and "80% full in 15 years" statistics)

This would let your strong evidence serve a persuasive purpose rather than just cataloging facts.

Address Counterarguments

An argumentative essay is strengthened by acknowledging opposing views. You could address objections such as: recycling programs are inconvenient, recycling costs money, or "single-stream" recycling doesn't work as well as advertised. Even a brief paragraph anticipating skepticism (eg, "Some argue recycling takes too much time...") followed by your rebuttal would make the essay feel like a genuine argument rather than a fact sheet.

Fix Citation and Source Issues

Your source "Recycler's Bill of Rights" is cited as "(Recycler's 1)," "(Recycler's 2)," etc—make sure this matches a proper Works Cited entry, and that the source is credible and clearly identified (Who published this? A recycling company? An advocacy group?).
The Superbag Corporation is a bag manufacturer with a financial interest in promoting plastic bags over paper. Using their claim that "plastic bags are much better for the environment than paper bags" as your own conclusion is a conflict-of-interest problem. You should either find independent verification of this claim or explicitly flag the source's bias and address it.
Double-check all quotations for accuracy. Several quotes contain typos or garbled text (eg, "fun" for "full," "@" symbol, "fun" for "full," "fight bulb" for "light bulb," "m" for "in"). These appear to be OCR or transcription errors, but they undermine credibility and must be corrected before submission.

Improve Paragraph Transitions and Topic Sentences

Many paragraphs launch directly into a fact about a material without a topic sentence connecting it to your argument. For example, the glass/steel paragraph begins mid-thought ("...for saving") suggesting a dropped sentence or edit error—please proofread carefully for missing text. Each paragraph should open by stating *why* this material matters to your overall argument, not just *what* it is.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a nice analogy (recycling should be as routine as getting gas), but it also introduces new information (the "3.3 cubic yards" statistic) that would be better placed earlier in the body. Conclusions should synthesize your argument, not introduce new evidence. Consider moving the landfill statistics to your body paragraphs and using the conclusion purely to reinforce your thesis and call readers to action.

Sentence-Level Proofreading

There are several mechanical issues to address in revision:
Comma splices and missing periods (eg, "This includes but is not limited to; plastic bags...")
Inconsistent capitalization ("Rechargeable" mid-sentence)
Apostrophe/quotation mark errors throughout (probably from formatting/copy-paste issues)
Run-on sentences that could be split for clarity

A careful proofreading pass—ideally reading the essay aloud—will catch most of these.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to make an arguable claim.
Reorganize by argument/reason, not by material type.
Add a counterargument paragraph.
Fix the Superbag source bias issue and clean up all citations.
Correct transcription errors in quotes and statistics.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new evidence.

You have excellent raw material here—the facts are compelling and well-chosen. The next revision should focus on turning this strong research base into a persuasive, unified argument.

Feedback on Your Essay: Color Stereotypes

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting topic with real potential, and you clearly have good observational skills—the examples from commercials and baby products show you're paying attention to your cultural environment. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of observations than an *argumentative* essay. To strengthen this piece, you need a clearer thesis, more analytical depth, and tighter organization. Let me walk through specific areas for revision.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your essay describes a phenomenon (pink = feminine, blue = masculine) but doesn't fully argue anything about it. Ask yourself: What is the claim you want readers to accept? Possible directions include:

Gendered color-coding is a *culturally constructed* stereotype, not a natural or inevitable association (historically, pink was even associated with boys in the early 20th century—this could be a powerful piece of evidence).
This stereotype reinforces unequal gender expectations, particularly disadvantaging males who face stricter policing of their color choices than females do.
Advertising doesn't just reflect this stereotype—it actively reinforces and profits from it.

Your final paragraph gestures toward an interesting asymmetry (girls can wear "masculine" colors more easily than boys can wear "feminine" ones), but this insight arrives too late and isn't developed. This could actually be your strongest argumentative point—consider making it the essay's central thesis rather than a closing observation.

Organization

Currently the essay moves: general claim → commercials (adult products) → babies → commercials (baby products) → boys → conclusion. This is reasonably logical, but each section needs a clearer connecting thread back to your main argument. Consider:

Opening: Replace the rhetorical questions with a clear statement of your argument.
Body paragraphs: Organize around *ideas* rather than just categories (eg, one paragraph on how advertising reinforces the stereotype, one on how it's imposed on children who have no say in it, one on the double standard in adulthood).
Conclusion: Expand this significantly. Right now it introduces new, interesting territory (the asymmetry between how tolerantly boys' vs. girls' color choices are treated) without exploring it. This deserves its own full paragraph with analysis, not just a summary sentence.

Analysis Needs Development

Many of your examples are stated but not analyzed. For instance:
*Why* does it matter that Secret's packaging uses pink and soft blue? What is the commercial implying by using "strong enough for a man" language alongside stereotypically feminine visuals? This is actually a rich contradiction worth unpacking—the product claims to cross gender lines, but the packaging retreats to gender stereotypes.
The Huggies example is good, but push further: *why* would a diaper company bother color-coding diapers for infants who have no awareness of color symbolism? This point could support an argument that the stereotype serves marketing purposes more than any inherent gender truth.

Try adding a "so what?" sentence after each example—tell the reader why the example matters to your argument, not just what it shows.

Evidence and Credibility

Your evidence is anecdotal and drawn from personal observation, which is fine for some support, but the essay would be much stronger with:
Historical context (eg, the surprising fact that pink/blue gender associations are relatively recent, dating mainly to mid-20th century marketing)
At least one or two outside sources—scholarly or journalistic—discussing the psychology or history of color and gender
Consider citing specific studies on color perception and gender socialization if your assignment allows research

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Capitalization after periods: You have several sentences that begin with lowercase letters following a period (eg, "because it is a very soft color," "on the Secret deodorant commercial," "once a baby has developed"). Proofread carefully for this.
Informal language: "(jam referring to babies)" appears to be a typo for "I am referring to babies"—but even corrected, this parenthetical aside feels too informal for academic writing. Consider integrating this clarification into your sentence structure instead.
Comma splices and run-ons: Some sentences string together too many ideas with commas. Break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Wordiness: Look for places where you can tighten prose (eg, "Always maxi pads have white, pink, and light blue colors on the package. Again soft colors were used" could combine into one sentence).

Next Steps

Draft a clear, one-sentence thesis stating your argument.
Reorganize body paragraphs around ideas rather than product categories.
Add analytical sentences after each example.
Expand your conclusion—especially the boys/girls double standard—into full-fledged argumentative material.
Proofread for capitalization and sentence-boundary errors.

You have compelling raw material here. With a sharper argumentative focus and deeper analysis, this essay can move from *describing* a stereotype to *making a case* about its cultural significance.

Feedback on Drug Legalization Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely important and debatable topic, and you've identified several distinct benefits of drug legalization—economic, criminal justice, and public health—which gives your argument good potential range. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas before it will be persuasive to an academic audience.

Structural and Development Issues

The most pressing problem is the number of placeholder gaps marked with  throughout your body paragraphs. These appear in almost every paragraph and represent missing evidence, examples, or logical connections. Right now, your paragraphs often state a claim and then jump straight to a conclusion without the supporting reasoning that would make the claim convincing. For instance, in your first body paragraph, you assert that legalization would decrease drug prices and reduce trade, but the mechanism connecting these ideas is missing. Before this essay is ready for submission, every one of these gaps needs to be filled with specific evidence, statistics, or logical explanation.

Paragraph Coherence

Several paragraphs contain claims that don't fully connect to one another. In the crime paragraph, you move from lower prices, to crime rates, to "the cities," to young people being drawn into drug dealing—but the logical thread between these ideas isn't always clear. Consider using topic sentences that state one clear claim per paragraph, then build your evidence to support that single claim, rather than covering multiple related-but-distinct ideas in one paragraph. The "responsible for their own debts" paragraph is particularly thin—it's only two sentences before pivoting to taxation, which is really a separate economic argument deserving its own space.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

There are several sentence-level errors worth addressing:

"They have come to reality" should likely be "come to realize" or "come to terms with reality"
The sentence beginning "Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers" is grammatically incomplete—it seems a clause was dropped. This needs to be rewritten so the comparison between heroin and alcohol is clear.
"because of their socially and economically margin" is unclear—did you mean "marginalization"? This sentence needs revision for clarity.
Watch for comma splices, such as in "They have come to reality and decided that ,drug prohibition has not worked..." (the comma placement here is also incorrect).
"beneficiary" should be "beneficial" in the taxation sentence.

Use of Evidence

You cite Duke once early on, which is a good start, but the essay would be much stronger with more consistent, specific evidence throughout—statistics on incarceration rates, historical examples (Prohibition is a natural comparison you don't currently make), or data on drug-related deaths. The public health paragraph is your most developed section in terms of reasoning, but even here, claims like "deaths and diseases caused by such drug use would be much lower" need sourced statistics to be credible. Academic argumentative writing requires you to show your sources, not just assert conclusions.

Addressing Counterarguments

One significant weakness is the complete absence of counterargument. A strong persuasive essay anticipates objections—what would opponents of legalization say? Wouldn't drug use increase if drugs were legal and cheaper? What about the public health costs of increased use, even if per-incident harm decreases? Engaging with and refuting at least one counterargument would make your position much more credible and show that you've thought through the complexity of the issue, rather than presenting only one side.

Introduction and Conclusion

Your introduction sets up the topic but relies on some vague framing ("all that has not benefited the United States at all" is imprecise—benefited in what way, according to whom?). Consider opening with a more specific hook: a statistic, a historical reference to Prohibition, or a concrete scenario. Your conclusion effectively summarizes your main points, which is good practice, but it would be stronger if it also gestured toward what should happen next—a policy recommendation or call to action beyond "something will have to be done."

Priority Revisions

Fill every  gap with specific, cited evidence or clear logical reasoning
Add a counterargument paragraph with rebuttal
Revise unclear/incomplete sentences, especially the heroin/alcohol comparison
Strengthen paragraph unity—one main idea per paragraph, clearly developed
Increase citation density—more sources would strengthen credibility significantly

You have a solid foundation and a clear position—now the work is in building out the evidentiary support and logical connections that will make readers who disagree with you take your argument seriously.

Feedback on "Gun Control" Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled an important and complex issue, and your use of local, personal examples (especially the story about your Uncle Edward) gives this essay emotional weight that many argumentative papers lack. Your passion for the topic is clear. However, the essay needs significant revision in three key areas: thesis clarity, argumentative structure, and use of evidence. Below are specific suggestions organized by priority.

Strengthen and Clarify Your Thesis

Your thesis buried in the first paragraph—that gun control would "reduce crime, save lives, and lessen the fears of innocent ones"—is a reasonable claim, but it needs to be sharper and more specific. Right now, "gun control" remains vague throughout the essay. Ask yourself: What *specific* policies are you advocating? Universal background checks? Waiting periods? An outright ban on civilian ownership (as you suggest in your conclusion)?

This matters because your conclusion reveals a much more radical position (only police and military should carry guns) than your body paragraphs actually argue for. Your evidence deals with murders, robberies, and impulsive shootings—none of which are used to support the specific policy you land on. Decide early what you're arguing for, and make sure every piece of evidence builds toward that same specific claim.

Address the Counterargument More Substantively

You mention the NRA's opposition in one sentence and dismiss it with "this opposing organization doesn't realize that gun-related crimes are steadily rising"—but this doesn't engage with *why* people oppose gun control (Second Amendment rights, self-defense, the argument that criminals will obtain guns regardless of law). A strong argumentative essay doesn't just mention the opposition—it fairly represents their strongest points and then explains why your position is still more persuasive. Consider adding a full paragraph that does this work. This will make your argument feel more credible and less one-sided.

Sharpen the Logical Connections Between Evidence and Claims

This is your biggest area for growth. Several of your examples don't actually demonstrate what you claim they demonstrate:

The Toby Sincino story: You use this to argue for stricter gun control, but the essay doesn't explain *how* gun control laws would have prevented a 15-year-old from accessing a gun. Did he steal it? Buy it? This gap weakens your point—you need to connect the specific failure (easy access) to the specific solution (what law would have closed that gap?).

The bank robbery: This example actually seems to argue for *more security/policing*, not gun control specifically—notice that your own quoted citizens ask for "more police protection" and "more security," not stricter gun laws. You should address this tension directly rather than let the quotes work against your point.

Uncle Edward's death: This is a powerful, personal story, but it also illustrates an *impulsive* shooting between two people who knew each other—which raises the question of whether background checks or waiting periods would have prevented it. Be honest with your reader about which examples support which specific policies, rather than treating all "gun violence" as evidence for one solution.

Revision strategy: For each piece of evidence, add a sentence that explicitly states: "This shows that [specific policy] is needed because [specific mechanism]." If you can't complete that sentence convincingly, reconsider whether that example belongs in this essay.

Citation and Attribution Issues

Your citations need cleanup for both clarity and academic credibility:

"Multimedia Resources-CD-Rom" should be replaced with a proper citation (author, title, publisher, date) if possible.
The hate crime statistics are confusing—robbery and murder as percentages of "hate crimes" seems like a mismatched statistic for your argument, which is about general gun violence, not hate crimes specifically. Double-check that this statistic actually says what you think it says.
Quotation marks are inconsistent (some appear as apostrophes: 'Because of...). Proofread carefully for this formatting issue throughout.

Editing and Proofreading

There are numerous typos to fix in your next draft: "govemment," "techers," "memebers," "misue," "acquaintace," "Resoureces." I'd recommend reading the essay aloud slowly, or using a text-to-speech tool, to catch errors like these that spell-check might miss (since some are real words, just wrong ones).

Priority for Revision

If you only have time to fix a few things, focus on:
Narrow your thesis to one specific, defensible policy position.
Realign your evidence so each example clearly supports that specific policy.
Add a genuine counterargument paragraph.

Your personal stories are the strongest part of this essay—don't lose that voice as you tighten the argument. You have real material here; it just needs a clearer analytical frame around it.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0011.4.txt =====
Feedback on Capital Punishment Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a clear, debatable topic and organized your essay in a logical, easy-to-follow structure: you introduce the debate, then systematically refute three common pro-death-penalty arguments (cost, retribution, deterrence) before concluding. This three-part refutation structure is a real strength—it gives your essay a clear architecture that a reader can follow. However, the essay would benefit significantly from revision in three main areas: tone and rhetorical credibility, evidentiary support, and mechanics/proofreading.

Tone and Argumentative Credibility

Your passion for this issue comes through clearly, but in academic argumentation, that passion needs to be channeled through *evidence and reasoning* rather than *loaded language*. Phrases like "this obscene form of punishment," "ridiculous," and "midevil philosophy" function as ad hominem attacks on the *opposing position itself* rather than genuine engagement with it. This can actually weaken your credibility with a skeptical reader, because it suggests you're dismissing the other side rather than seriously refuting it. Academic argument is most persuasive when it treats the opposing view charitably and then shows, through logic and evidence, why it fails.

Similarly, direct address to the reader ("Let me throw a couple of statistics at you," "Correct me if I'm wrong") creates a conversational, almost confrontational tone that feels more suited to a spoken debate than a written argumentative essay. Consider revising these moments to maintain a more measured, analytical voice—one that still conveys conviction but relies on the strength of your reasoning rather than rhetorical flourish.

Evidence and Support

This is the area needing the most attention. You reference "statistics" and "studies" multiple times, but you never cite a source. For example:
"it costs five times the amount than it would to imprison somebody from age eighteen to eighty" — Where does this figure come from?
"They have done studies in states where the death penalty has been employed" — Which states? Which studies? When?

Simply telling readers you gathered these statistics "doing a paper last year" is not sufficient sourcing for academic writing. Without citations, your strongest evidence appears anecdotal and unverifiable, which undermines the very statistics-driven approach you promise in your introduction ("I will show you...with statistics"). For your revision, track down the actual sources (eg, Death Penalty Information Center, specific criminology studies) and cite them properly. This will transform your essay from an opinion piece into a well-supported argument.

Additionally, your retribution paragraph relies entirely on logical/moral reasoning with no evidence at all, which is fine as a philosophical argument, but consider whether citing relevant legal or ethical scholarship (eg, discussions of retributive vs. restorative justice) could strengthen this section to match the evidentiary rigor of your other two points.

Addressing Counterarguments

You do a good job anticipating one counterargument (eliminating appeals to reduce costs) and responding to it. Consider doing this more consistently throughout—for instance, in your deterrence section, is there any credible research suggesting the death penalty *does* deter crime, and if so, why do you find it less persuasive than the studies you cite? Engaging with the strongest version of the opposing argument (not just the weakest) will make your refutation more convincing.

Mechanics and Proofreading

The essay contains frequent spelling and typographical errors that distract from your argument and suggest insufficient proofreading: "untill," "it's" (for "its"), "Ounce" (for "Once"), "inprison," "recieves," "midevil," "el,,,actly" (for "exactly"), "prols and con's," "murdeers," "deference" (you likely mean "deterrence"), "statels" (states), "detramental." Please read through the essay slowly aloud, or use spell-check plus a manual review, to catch these. Errors like this, though minor individually, accumulate and can make a reader question the overall care taken with the argument.

There are also several run-on or awkwardly constructed sentences (eg, "How this statement can be valid is due to the reasons that...") that would benefit from being broken into clearer, more direct sentences.

Suggestions for Revision

Find and cite real sources for your cost and deterrence statistics.
Tone down inflammatory language; let evidence do the persuasive work.
Strengthen the retribution section with at least one scholarly or philosophical reference.
Proofread carefully, ideally reading the essay aloud or having a peer review it.
Consider acknowledging the strongest counterargument before refuting it, to show balanced engagement.

You have a solid argumentative skeleton here—with more rigorous sourcing and a more measured tone, this essay could be quite persuasive. Keep working on it!

Feedback on Euthanasia Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult and important topic, and it's clear you've done research into the legal history (the Kevorkian case) and philosophical/religious debates (May's four forces, the Hippocratic Oath). However, the essay currently reads more like an information report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, tighten your organization, and make sure your evidence is actually working to support a clear claim.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction poses a question ("Should it be legalized...or should they be bound by their Hippocratic Oath?") but never commits to an answer until the very last paragraph—and even there, the position is stated rather than argued. This is the most important issue to fix. Decide early: are you arguing *for* physician-assisted suicide, *against* it, or for some qualified middle position (eg, doctors shouldn't initiate it but may ethically cooperate)? Your conclusion suggests the last option, which is actually a nuanced and interesting stance—but it appears suddenly, without being built up to. Revise your introduction so the reader knows from paragraph one what you will argue, and then make sure every subsequent paragraph is doing work toward proving that claim, rather than just summarizing background information.

Organization

Right now the essay moves through topics in a way that feels additive rather than argumentative: types of euthanasia, then May's forces, then Kevorkian's history, then the Hippocratic Oath, then Fletcher's critique, then your conclusion. Each section is informative on its own, but the connections between them aren't made explicit. After the Kevorkian narrative, for instance, tell us *why* this case matters to your argument—is it evidence that the law is inadequate? That courts are sympathetic to assisted suicide? That physicians are willing to defy the medical establishment? Right now it reads as a history lesson.

Similarly, your paragraph on May's four forces only really develops the first two forces in detail; the third and fourth are mentioned but not explained with the same care. Either develop all four with equal attention or explain why some matter more for your argument than others.

Consider organizing around your *claims* rather than around your *sources*. For example: one paragraph could argue "the law is currently unclear and inconsistent," using Kevorkian as evidence; another could argue "the Hippocratic Oath does not clearly prohibit assisted death," using Fletcher's critique as evidence; a third could argue "the decision should rest with the patient and cooperating physician, not the doctor unilaterally," which is your conclusion's claim.

Use of Evidence and Quotations

There are several bracketed placeholders () throughout the essay where quotations should appear—from May's essay, Judge Breck's ruling, the Hippocratic Oath itself, and Fletcher's argument. These are the most important pieces of evidence in your essay, and currently they're missing. Fill these in with the actual language, and then—crucially—*analyze* the quotations rather than just dropping them in. For instance, after quoting the Oath, explain specifically which phrase physicians cite against assisted suicide and why. After quoting Fletcher, explain what the "two contradictory promises" are and why this matters to your argument. Right now these sections gesture at analysis without actually completing it.

Clarity and Word Choice

Reconsider your opening framing. Describing patients who choose euthanasia as looking for "an easy way out" is a loaded phrase that undercuts the sympathy your later paragraphs try to establish. If your ultimate position is that patients deserve autonomy over end-of-life decisions, your language throughout should reflect respect for that choice rather than subtly characterizing it as a shortcut or weakness.

Mechanics

A revision pass should address:
Apostrophe errors: "patient's" should often be "patients" (plural, not possessive)
Typos: "Christains" → "Christians"; "Re unmanageable" appears to be a stray fragment
Inconsistent pronoun use: mixing "one's" and "his/her" within the same passages
Spacing/compound word issues: "break throughs" → "breakthroughs"
Some sentences are overly long and convoluted (eg, the sentence about "medical conduct to end the patient's life"); consider breaking these into shorter, clearer statements

Suggested Revision Plan

Rewrite the introduction to state your actual thesis clearly.
Reorganize body paragraphs around claims that build toward that thesis, using your existing research as supporting evidence rather than as the organizing structure itself.
Fill in and analyze all missing quotations.
Revise the conclusion so it doesn't feel like a new argument dropped in at the end, but rather the logical culmination of what precedes it—perhaps by adding a paragraph that anticipates and responds to the doctor's-authority question before your final paragraph.
Proofread carefully for the mechanical issues noted above.

You have strong raw material here—historical detail, philosophical tension, and a genuinely interesting nuanced conclusion. The revision task is really about making your argument the spine of the essay, with your research serving that argument rather than standing on its own.

Feedback on "The Wild Card" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely, well-defined topic and structured your essay around a clear three-part thesis (more teams, a new "race," more competition). You clearly know the subject matter well, and your enthusiasm for the sport comes through. The essay's main weakness is that it spends far more space *explaining* the wild card system than *arguing* for it. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Balance Explanation and Argument

The first three paragraphs (roughly 40% of the essay) are devoted to describing how divisions were realigned and how home-field advantage is determined. This is useful background, but it isn't argument—it doesn't yet support your thesis that the wild card is *good* for baseball. Consider cutting this section by half and moving directly into your three supporting points sooner. The home-field-advantage discussion, in particular, doesn't connect back to your thesis at all; either tie it explicitly to one of your three claims (eg, how the rotating system affects competitive balance) or remove it.

Strengthen the Logic of Each Body Paragraph

"More teams" paragraph: This is your strongest section—concrete, well-supported with named players. Consider trimming the list of stars (eleven names is a lot) and instead analyze two or three in more depth. Also explain *why* star power matters to baseball's health (TV ratings, revenue, fan interest) rather than relying on the analogy to advertising, which feels like an aside.

"New race" paragraph: The playoff-scenario list is compelling evidence, but you could better explain the causal link: without the wild card, would fans in Boston, Cincinnati, or New York have had any reason to watch after their teams fell out of the division race? Make that counterfactual explicit.

"More competition" paragraph: This is the weakest link. You use the Rockies' 1-4 record and 19 runs as evidence of competitiveness, but a losing record with lopsided results doesn't clearly prove "more competition"—readers may see this as contradicting your point. Also, your claim that a team must "win two games instead of one" to become champion applies to *all* teams now, not just wild card teams, so the reasoning needs adjustment. Consider using head-to-head closeness (games decided by one run, extra innings, etc) rather than win totals to make the point more convincing.

Add a Counterargument

Right now the essay presents only supporting evidence. A brief paragraph acknowledging an objection—for instance, traditionalists' complaint that the wild card dilutes the meaning of the pennant race, or that it rewards a team for *not* winning its division—and then rebutting it would make your argument feel more persuasive and less one-sided.

Clean Up Sourcing and Citations

You quote and cite sources (Major League Baseball, "The Wildcard...," "Final 1995 Wild Card Standings"), which is good practice, but the citations are inconsistent and lack full information. Make sure each source has a complete entry on a Works Cited page, and check that in-text citations follow a consistent format (author or shortened title, and page/paragraph number if available). This will make your evidence more credible and easier to verify.

Proofread Carefully

There are several small errors that a careful read-through would catch:
"National Leagued Championship Series" → "National League"
"wild car spot" → "wild card spot"
"receives from the playoffs" reads awkwardly—try "excluded from the playoffs" or "denied playoff berths"
The concluding sentence claims baseball "remained behind football and basketball," but the final sentence says it lost popularity to "football and baseball"—this is almost certainly a typo (should be *basketball*) and is worth fixing since it appears in your conclusion.
Watch subject-verb agreement in a few places (eg, "Major League Baseball has set up the following rules to determine who plays who in the Division Series" — consider tightening this sentence, which currently trails off awkwardly before the block quote).

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively uses the "without the wild card" repetition for rhetorical effect—this is a nice technique. To make it even stronger, tie each "without the wild card" statement more explicitly back to one of your three original claims, so the conclusion reads as a synthesis rather than a list of new examples (the Mariners-Yankees game, for instance, is introduced here for the first time).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Cut background explanation by roughly half; get to your argument faster.
Rework the "more competition" paragraph with clearer, more consistent evidence.
Add a short counterargument/rebuttal paragraph.
Standardize and complete your citations.
Proofread for the typos noted above.

You have strong command of the facts and a genuine argumentative structure already in place—these revisions will sharpen the analytical edge of what is otherwise a well-informed essay.

Feedback Report: Media, Privacy, and the "Right to Know"

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely important and timely topic—the tension between media freedom and personal privacy—and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. The personal frame (the phone call from "The State") is an effective hook, and you've assembled a wide range of examples spanning decades. However, the essay currently reads more like a passionate rant than a structured argument. To strengthen it significantly, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, organize your evidence more purposefully, and tighten your prose. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your essay touches on several related but distinct claims: media sensationalism lowers journalistic quality; media coverage endangers military operations; media invades privacy; media discourages good candidates from public service; and media prioritizes profit over integrity. These are all connected, but you never quite state *which* claim is your central argument, or how the others support it.

Revision suggestion: Write a single thesis sentence that captures your main claim—for example: "Modern media's obsessive pursuit of the 'right to know' has eroded both public safety and personal privacy, prioritizing profit over responsible journalism." Then organize your paragraphs so each one visibly supports that specific claim, rather than drifting into a new complaint.

Organization

Currently, the essay moves chronologically through examples (personal anecdote → Grenada → Surgeon General nominee → Spectator incident → O.J. Simpson → Colin Powell → general privacy complaints → dystopian prediction) without clear transitions explaining *why* one example follows another or how they build on each other. This creates a "list" feeling rather than a developing argument.

Revision suggestion: Consider grouping your examples by *type* of harm:
National security harm (Grenada, Desert Storm, Bosnia)
Harm to individuals' careers/reputations (Surgeon General nominee, USC professor, O.J. trial participants, Colin Powell)
Harm to ordinary privacy (the hamburger/bedtime examples, Reagan/Ford)

Within each group, use topic sentences that state the shared point before diving into examples. This will make your logic visible to readers.

Depth of Evidence

Many of your examples are asserted rather than developed. For instance, the Surgeon General anecdote (likely referring to Henry Foster) states the outcome but doesn't explain *how* the media coverage was excessive or unfair compared to legitimate scrutiny of a public health official. Similarly, the "Spectator" incident about the USC professor is vague—readers don't know what was actually written or why it was unjustified.

Revision suggestion: Pick 3-4 of your strongest examples and develop them more fully: What exactly did the media report? Why was it excessive, inaccurate, or unnecessary? What harm resulted? Cutting some examples (perhaps Ford's golf game, which feels like an aside) will give you room to develop the stronger ones.

Counterargument

You occasionally gesture toward the opposing view ("I suppose this might come under the people's 'right to know'"; "Perhaps, if everybody were alike, there would be no 'news'") but you don't seriously engage it. A stronger essay would state the strongest version of the "right to know" argument and then explain specifically why it doesn't justify the harms you describe.

Revision suggestion: Add a paragraph addressing the legitimate argument for press freedom and government/public accountability, then pivot to explain where you believe the line should be drawn.

Tone and Rhetorical Choices

Some rhetorical questions and exclamations ("Was this a rejection or a crucifixion?"; "Watch out, future warriors of this country") are effective and memorable. Others—like the extended riff on hamburgers and running—feel like padding and undercut the seriousness of your argument, especially next to the Grenada example. Academic argument benefits from consistent register; deciding which moments deserve rhetorical flourish (and using them sparingly) will make those moments more powerful.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are numerous typos and errors that need correction in revision: "successfiil," "fives" (lives), "gwne" (game), "hwnburgers," "dodt," "ad that" (all that), "0. J. Simpson," "inunune," "Then," used as a sentence fragment starter, and inconsistent quotation mark usage around "right to know." A careful proofreading pass is essential before this is submission-ready.

Conclusion

Your ending calls for journalistic integrity and personal liberty, which is a fitting close, but the "cameras in every home" prediction feels like a slippery-slope leap without evidence. Consider grounding your conclusion more firmly in the examples you've already given, perhaps circling back to your opening anecdote about "The State" to bring the essay full circle.

Priority Revisions

Write an explicit thesis statement.
Reorganize examples into thematic clusters.
Deepen 3-4 key examples; cut weaker ones.
Add substantive counterargument engagement.
Proofread thoroughly for typos and mechanics.

You have real conviction and useful examples here—the next draft should focus on channeling that energy into a tighter, more persuasive structure.
