Feedback Report: Marijuana Legalization Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely debatable and relevant topic, and your personal voice comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more as a personal narrative with scattered opinions than as a structured argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, build a more logical case, and support your claims with credible evidence rather than anecdote and assertion. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Focus

Your thesis appears in the first sentence ("it should be legalized"), which is good—state your claim early. However, the essay then wanders through personal history, conspiracy theories about government corruption, and claims about marijuana's medical benefits without clearly organizing these into a coherent argument. Ask yourself: what are the *strongest three reasons* marijuana should be legalized? Structure your essay around those reasons, with each paragraph developing one clearly.

Logical Fallacies to Address

Several sections rely on reasoning that won't hold up under scrutiny:

Ad hominem/whataboutism: The claim that politicians "use drugs themselves" doesn't logically support legalization—it's an attack on individuals' hypocrisy, not an argument about policy.
Appeal to authority (celebrity): Citing the President, Notorious B.I.G., and Snoop Dogg as evidence that marijuana isn't harmful is a weak argument. Fame and drug use don't prove safety or policy merit. Cut this section or reframe it—perhaps discuss cultural normalization instead of using it as "proof."
Anecdotal evidence: "My friends," "a Jamaican man we called Black Moses," and "former classmates who lit up before a test" are not substitutes for research. These moments are good color for a personal essay but weaken an argumentative one. Real evidence—studies, statistics, medical research, legal outcomes in states/countries that have legalized marijuana—will make your case far more persuasive.
Unsupported conspiracy claims: The idea that officials profit from keeping marijuana illegal is a serious accusation. If you want to include this argument, you need actual sourcing (news investigations, policy analysis) rather than presenting it as self-evident.

Evidence and Credibility

Right now, your strongest potential arguments—medical benefits (eye conditions, eg, glaucoma), low addiction potential, minimal long-term side effects—are stated as unsupported facts ("Did you know...?"). These are exactly the claims that *should* be developed with citations from medical journals, studies, or health organizations. Doing so would transform vague assertions into a credible case. I'd encourage you to research:
Comparative harm studies of marijuana vs. legal substances (alcohol, tobacco)
Data from states/countries that have legalized marijuana (tax revenue, crime rates, health outcomes)
Medical literature on therapeutic uses

Tone and Audience

The conversational, personal tone works well for a memoir or op-ed but undercuts your credibility in a formal argumentative essay. Phrases like "talking out of his head," "mega bucks," and rhetorical questions ("Do you ever notice...?") feel more like a spoken monologue than a persuasive essay aimed at a skeptical reader. Consider: who is your audience? If you're trying to convince someone who currently disagrees with you, you'll need a more measured, evidence-driven tone that anticipates and addresses counterarguments (eg, addiction concerns, gateway drug theory, impact on developing brains, driving impairment).

Organization

The essay currently follows a loose chronological structure (childhood memory → learning about parents → getting older → conversation with Black Moses → present-day observations). This works for personal narrative but doesn't build a persuasive case. Consider reorganizing around claims, not memories:
Paragraph 1: Introduction + thesis
Paragraph 2: Argument 1 (eg, medical benefits) with evidence
Paragraph 3: Argument 2 (eg, economic/criminal justice arguments) with evidence
Paragraph 4: Counterargument and rebuttal
Conclusion

Your personal anecdotes (parents smoking in the 70s, the Jamaican man, "Just Say No" education) can still appear, but as *illustrations* within a paragraph making a specific claim, not as the paragraph's main content.

Mechanics

Watch for:
Comma splices and run-on sentences ("I always told myself that even though they did it back in the 70's it still wasn't right.")
Homophone errors: "their" vs. "there" (used incorrectly multiple times), "it's" vs. "its"
Inconsistent capitalization ("european," "Advil")
The essay ends abruptly with a quotation—consider adding a sentence or two that ties the quote back to your argument rather than letting it stand alone as the conclusion.

Next Steps

For your revision, I'd recommend choosing your two or three strongest, most defensible arguments, finding credible sources to support each, and restructuring the essay so each paragraph builds a specific claim rather than continuing the narrative. This will shift the essay from a persuasive personal reflection into a rigorous argumentative piece.

Feedback on Your Essay: Shield Laws and Journalist-Source Confidentiality

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research on this topic, and the case examples you've gathered (Morris, Decker, Swasy, Woodward/Bernstein, Stuart, Serrano) are genuinely interesting and relevant. However, the essay currently reads more like an annotated collection of examples than a cohesive argument. My main recommendation is to significantly strengthen the argumentative framework that should be organizing all this evidence.

Structural Issues

Thesis and Argument Development: Your essay lacks a clear, stated thesis early on. Readers don't know until nearly the end what your actual claim is (that shield laws should be protected/strengthened). State this position explicitly in your introduction, and make sure every example you include is clearly working to support that specific claim—not just providing background information.

Balance Between Narrative and Analysis: Right now, the essay is roughly 80% historical/factual narration and 20% argument. Flip this ratio. For each case you present (Morris, Decker, Swasy, Stuart, Serrano), you need to explicitly connect it back to your thesis: *What does this case prove? How does it support your position that shield laws are necessary?* Currently, you present the facts and move on without drawing out the implications.

Organization: Consider reorganizing around your argument's logic rather than chronology or case-by-case listing. For example:
Why source confidentiality matters (the trust argument)
Evidence that shield laws are inconsistently applied/enforced (Decker, Ohio, Serrano cases)
Evidence of what's lost when sources aren't protected (Watergate as counterfactual—what if Deep Throat had been exposed?)
Response to counterarguments (why might courts want to compel disclosure? Address this directly)
Your policy recommendation

Specific Content Concerns

Missing Counterargument: You never seriously address *why* courts sometimes compel disclosure—the need for fair trials, the defendant's right to confront evidence, or law enforcement's legitimate interests (as in the Decker case, where a court order was allegedly violated). Acknowledging and responding to these competing interests would make your argument far more persuasive. Right now, judges seem to appear only as adversaries "twisting the law," which weakens your credibility as a careful thinker.

The Doctor-Patient Analogy: This is a strong rhetorical move, but you introduce it and drop it. Develop it further, or engage with its limits (eg, doctor-patient privilege has specific legal exceptions too—how does that compare?).

The Decker Case: This example seems to complicate your argument more than support it, since the reporter was found to have possibly enabled a violation of a court confidentiality order protecting a psychiatric evaluation. You should address this tension directly rather than simply reporting the outcome. Why should shield laws protect a reporter in this circumstance? This is where your argument needs to do real work.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Quotation formatting is inconsistent and sometimes garbled (eg, "�a journalist's sources were stripped bare..." and stray punctuation marks throughout, like ",internal policy debates'"). Proofread carefully for quotation marks, especially where curly quotes or special characters have corrupted during formatting.
The extended block quote of the 1896 Maryland law is quite long for the point it's making. Consider trimming to the most relevant portions and paraphrasing the rest.
Citation format should be checked against whatever style guide you're using (MLA/APA/Chicago)—right now you're using parenthetical author-page citations, which is fine, but make sure this is consistent and matches assignment requirements.
Statistics need follow-through: You mention "4,500 subpoenas" in 1989 and that the number has "definitely increased"—but you don't cite a source for the increase or give any concrete follow-up figures. Either find data or soften the claim.
Watch for typos and formatting artifacts ("Twila Decker,," "reporter,, Alecia Swasy,,")—these suggest the essay needs a final proofreading pass.

Conclusion

Your conclusion is emotionally resonant but underdeveloped as argument—it's mostly assertion ("People have a right to know the whole truth") without synthesis of the evidence you've presented. Use this space to explicitly tie your cases back together and restate why, given the evidence, shield laws deserve stronger protection.

Priority Revisions

Write a clear thesis statement and place it in your introduction.
Add analytical commentary after each case example explaining its relevance to your argument.
Address the counterargument seriously, especially using the Decker case as a testing ground for the limits of shield laws.
Proofread for quotation and punctuation errors throughout.
Strengthen the conclusion to synthesize rather than merely restate your position.

You have excellent raw material here—the examples are vivid and well-chosen. The next draft should focus on making your voice and argument the throughline, with the cases serving as evidence rather than the main content.

Feedback on "Women at the Citadel" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely debatable topic and you clearly have a personal stake in the argument, which comes through in your passionate tone. The essay also does something many student writers skip: it directly engages counterarguments (tradition, physical strength, distraction) rather than ignoring them. That's a real strength. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: structure and thesis clarity, logical development of arguments, and grammar/mechanics. Below I address each.

Thesis and Introduction

Your introduction doesn't tell the reader what *you* think until the second paragraph, and even then your position is somewhat buried. Readers need a clear, direct thesis statement by the end of paragraph one—something like: "Because the Citadel is publicly funded and offers educational advantages unavailable elsewhere, it should be required to admit qualified women, regardless of tradition or stereotypes about physical ability." Right now, the opening sentences ("there has been one question that appeared in the courts") read as filler. Cut straight to the issue and your stance.

Logical Development

This is the area needing the most attention.

The slavery/housewife comparison (paragraph 2) is a risky move. Comparing the exclusion of women from a military college to slavery can feel like an overstatement that weakens your credibility rather than strengthening it. Consider instead citing specific, comparable examples of formerly all-male institutions (West Point, VMI) that successfully integrated women—this is more directly relevant and harder to dismiss.

The "men are just like women" paragraph contains a contradiction that undermines your point. You write that some women couldn't handle the Citadel, then immediately argue "everyone deserves the chance to try." These aren't contradictory ideas, but as written they read as if you're conceding the opponent's point before defending your own. Revise this section so the logical sequence is clear: (1) physical ability varies by individual, not by gender; (2) therefore the Citadel should evaluate individuals rather than exclude an entire gender; (3) some men and some women will fail to meet standards, and that's fine—the standard, not the gender, should determine admission.

The public funding paragraph makes your strongest legal/logical point, but it gets lost in a confusing hypothetical about Hispanic or Polish students that doesn't quite work (this scenario describes something closer to *segregation into separate schools*, not exclusion from one). Simplify: state the legal principle (public institutions can't discriminate by gender), state that the Citadel is publicly funded, and let the logic stand without the tangent. This paragraph could be much more powerful if tightened.

The "distraction" paragraph is well-argued and probably your best paragraph—it uses a real-world comparison (coed universities, coed workplaces) to refute the opposing claim. Keep this approach and consider expanding it, since it currently ends the essay somewhat abruptly. Note also: you likely mean "inferior," not "less superior," in the final lines—right now the sentence says the opposite of what you intend.

Structure

The essay lacks a conclusion. It ends on the distraction argument without returning to your central claim or explaining why this issue matters (eg, what's at stake for individual women, for the Citadel, for the principle of equal access to public education). Add a concluding paragraph that restates your thesis in stronger terms and leaves the reader with a sense of the argument's larger significance.

Each paragraph could also benefit from a clearer topic sentence stating the specific counterargument you're about to address, followed by your rebuttal. Right now transitions between paragraphs are abrupt.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are frequent errors that will distract careful readers and undercut your credibility on a formal argument. I recommend a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud. Specific patterns to watch for:

Spelling: appearred, alao, desciniinate, pubfically, poliish, eqaul, learening
Subject-verb agreement: "That questions is," "There has been many objections," "Men and women will not be and are not separated"
Word confusion: "women" vs. "woman" (singular/plural), "superior" vs. "inferior," "excellence" (adjective needed: "excellent")
Sentence fragments/run-ons: "Everyone does deserve the chance to try." (fine, but preceded by a comma splice)
Vague pronoun reference: "what makes it any different" — different from what, exactly? Be explicit.

Priority for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement for the introduction.
Resolve the internal contradiction in the "women are just like men" paragraph.
Simplify the public-funding paragraph and cut the confusing hypothetical.
Add a real conclusion.
Proofread carefully for spelling and agreement errors—read each sentence in isolation to catch these.

You have the makings of a strong, persuasive essay here. The ideas are solid; they simply need clearer organization and cleaner execution to fully convince your reader.

Feedback Report: Stay-at-Home Wives and Marital Equality

Overall Impression

You've chosen a thoughtful, relevant topic and clearly have a personal stake in the argument. Your passion comes through, and the essay contains the seeds of a strong, well-organized argument. However, the essay currently reads as a first draft that needs revision for clarity, organization, and precision. Below are the main areas to address.

Thesis and Focus

Your opening sentence is confusing rather than provocative. Starting with "If a husband is considerate enough to 'allow' his wife..." followed immediately by "Not in this day and age" reads as a fragment reacting to an unstated claim. Readers need context before you push back against an idea. Consider rewriting the opening so the ironic or sarcastic tone is unmistakable—perhaps by clearly attributing this view to "some people" or "traditional thinking" before you refute it.

Your actual thesis is buried in the second and third sentences. Pull it forward and sharpen it into one clear claim: something like, "A stay-at-home wife's contributions to a marriage are equal in value to her husband's income, and therefore she deserves equal ownership of marital property and equal respect—regardless of whether she earns a paycheck." State this explicitly near the end of your introduction so readers know exactly what you're proving.

Organization

The essay's structure is not yet serving your argument. Several problems stand out:

Repetition: You make the "the husband should have known what he was agreeing to before marriage" point twice—once early and once again in the paragraph beginning "As stated earlier." Consolidate this into a single, well-developed paragraph rather than repeating it.
Unclear topic sentences: Several paragraphs open with vague transitions ("In this sense then...") rather than a clear claim that previews the paragraph's content. Revise topic sentences so each one states the specific point that paragraph will prove.
Missing anecdote: The television dialogue example contains several  placeholders where the actual quotations should be. Right now, readers cannot follow the exchange between father and child, which weakens what could be your most vivid piece of evidence. Fill these in before submitting.
Personal example needs sharper framing: The paragraph about your own mother is well-written and concrete, but it actually shows a *working* mother doing double duty, not a stay-at-home wife. As written, it risks confusing your argument rather than supporting it. Clarify explicitly that you're using this as a contrast—showing how much harder her life would be without staying home—so the connection to your thesis is obvious.

Development of Ideas

Your comparison of a wife's housework to a paid housekeeper's wages ($4.25/hour) is a good instinct—it makes an abstract argument concrete—but it needs more development. Explain *why* this comparison matters: if her labor has a calculable market value, doesn't that prove her work is not merely "help" but an economic contribution equal to her husband's paycheck? Draw that conclusion explicitly rather than leaving it implied.

Similarly, your point about putting both spouses' names on legal documents (mortgages, car titles) is a strong, practical piece of your argument. Consider moving this earlier and expanding it, since it offers a concrete solution rather than just an abstract principle.

Style and Mechanics

Several recurring issues need attention:

Comma splices and run-ons: Many sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "He then went on to say... He viewed the car as his because he took care of it, She then took the argument..."). Read your sentences aloud to catch these.
Apostrophe and quotation mark inconsistency: You alternate between single and double quotation marks, and there are stray punctuation errors (",his'," ",stay home wife""). Standardize to double quotation marks throughout, and proofread each instance carefully.
Word confusion: "loose" should be "lose" ("the wife could possibly *lose* the house").
Sentence fragments: Some sentences lack a main clause or trail off awkwardly ("Where as if she were able to stay home...").
Wordy phrasing: Trim repetitive phrases like "as stated earlier" or "in this sense then" that add length without adding meaning.

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your introduction with a clear, explicit thesis statement.
Reorganize paragraphs to eliminate repeated points; give each paragraph one clear job.
Fill in the missing dialogue in your TV example.
Reframe the personal anecdote so its purpose is explicit.
Proofread carefully for punctuation, word choice, and sentence boundaries.

You have real insight and a genuine argument here—tightening the structure and polishing the prose will let your ideas come through much more powerfully.

Feedback Report: Marijuana Legalization Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely complex and relevant topic, and you clearly did some research. However, the essay suffers from a significant contradiction between its stated purpose and its actual execution, along with several issues in argumentation, evidence use, and organization that need attention before this could be considered a successful piece of persuasive or informative writing.

Major Issue: Purpose and Thesis Mismatch

This is the most important problem to address. You explicitly state in your introduction that "the purpose of this passage is not to convince you to legalize marijuana... rather, it's purpose is to inform you on all the facts." Yet by the conclusion, you're asking readers to see "there can only be one logical answer"—which is clearly an argument *for* legalization. This is a fundamental inconsistency.

You need to choose one of two paths:
Commit to persuasion: Revise your introduction to state clearly that you're arguing for legalization, then structure your evidence to build that case explicitly.
Commit to genuine neutrality: If you truly want to inform rather than persuade, you must present counterarguments with equal weight—the case *against* legalization deserves serious treatment, not just acknowledgment that "there's nothing wrong" with opposing it.

As written, the essay reads as one-sided advocacy dressed in the language of neutrality, which undermines your credibility with readers who value intellectual honesty.

Evidence and Source Issues

Several claims need stronger support or correction:

Unverified/misquoted statistics: The "1988 DEA" quote about marijuana being "one of the safest, therapeutically active substances" is actually from an administrative law judge's recommendation (Francis Young), which the DEA itself rejected. This is a crucial distinction—citing it as the DEA's conclusion is factually incorrect and would seriously damage your credibility with an informed reader. Verify all quotes against primary sources.

"NO ONE has ever died from marijuana": This absolute claim is difficult to substantiate and reads as hyperbole rather than fact. Consider more precise language, such as "no confirmed deaths from marijuana overdose have been recorded" if you can find a credible source for this narrower claim.

The hemp/paper statistic: This appears without context—how does hemp paper production relate to marijuana legalization specifically? The connection needs explanation.

Citation format: "Pgychology and You, by McMahon and Romano" (note the typo) needs a proper citation with page number, edition, and publication year. Throughout the essay, statistics lack sources (Where does the 400,000 arrests figure come from? The 150,000 alcohol deaths?).

Logical Gaps

The transition from "marijuana is less harmful than alcohol" to "therefore it should be legalized" needs more development. This is essentially a comparative harm argument, but you should address why relative harm alone justifies legalization (versus, say, harsher alcohol regulation instead).

The crime argument is compelling but underdeveloped. You claim legalization would "elevate much of the crime"—this word choice is confusing (did you mean "eliminate"?). Also, this argument would benefit from evidence from jurisdictions that have already legalized marijuana (Colorado, Washington, etc, if this essay predates broader legalization, or historical parallels).

The Prohibition comparison in your conclusion is promising but arrives too late and isn't developed. This could be a powerful organizing framework if introduced earlier and explored throughout.

Organizational Suggestions

Your five-part structure (history, physical effects, mental effects, comparison to alcohol, benefits of legalization) is logical, but consider:

Strengthening topic sentences that explicitly connect each section to your overall argument
Adding a section addressing the strongest counterarguments (addiction potential, gateway drug theory, effects on developing brains, workplace/driving safety concerns) — even briefly, to demonstrate you've considered opposing views
Expanding your conclusion beyond restating the alcohol comparison; synthesize your strongest points

Style and Mechanics

Watch for typos: "Pgychology," "govermnent," "goverwnent," "eanount," "Food and Drug Administration" (FDA doesn't prescribe drugs—the FDA approves them; doctors prescribe)
The FDA doesn't "prescribe" medications—clarify this relationship (FDA approves, physicians prescribe)
Some sentences run together awkwardly (eg, "The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States" needs a verb)
Consider varying sentence openings—many paragraphs begin with similar constructions ("The next step," "After the physical effects")

Revision Priorities

Resolve the purpose contradiction (informative vs. persuasive)
Verify and properly cite all factual claims, especially the DEA quote
Add substantive counterarguments if maintaining informative framing
Proofread carefully for typos and grammatical errors
Strengthen logical connections between evidence and claims

This essay has a solid foundation of interesting information, but needs more rigorous fact-checking and a clearer, more honest relationship between its stated goals and actual content.

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and timeless topic—the persistence of gender-based double standards—and you clearly have passionate, genuine convictions about this issue. There's real potential here, especially in your historical framing and your personal connection to the topic in the concluding paragraphs. However, the essay currently needs significant revision in three key areas: structural organization, argumentative focus, and mechanical polish. Below, I'll walk through specific issues and suggest concrete strategies for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your essay lacks a clear, arguable thesis statement. The opening line ("Society has a double standard for women in our culture") is a claim, but it's stated as fact rather than as something you'll *prove*. As you move through the essay, you cover historical background, biblical justification, media representation, childhood socialization, and a bill of rights—each is interesting, but they read as a list of related observations rather than a unified argument building toward a specific point.

Revision strategy: Ask yourself: what is the *one* insight about double standards you want readers to walk away with? Is it that double standards are rooted in outdated religious/cultural justifications? That media perpetuates them today? That personal experience proves they persist despite feminist progress? Choose one angle and let every paragraph serve that specific claim.

Structural and Organizational Issues

The essay moves chronologically (past → present) but within that structure, ideas often jump around without clear transitions. For example, the paragraph about the Bible's Adam and Eve narrative appears somewhat disconnected from the paragraph before and after it. Similarly, the "Everywoman's Bill of Rights" list appears abruptly without being integrated into your argument—it's simply dropped in.

Revision strategy: After drafting, try outlining what you already have. For each paragraph, write one sentence summarizing its main point. This will reveal where paragraphs are doing too much (the paragraph on media/appearance, for instance, covers historical ideals, current ideals, TV tropes, *and* the psychology of settling for the wrong partner—that's four ideas in one paragraph). Split these into focused paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence.

Missing Content

There is a bracketed placeholder——left in the text before your discussion of a quote ("This quote clearly describes..."). This is a significant oversight; readers currently see a reference to a quote that doesn't exist in the text. Make sure to insert the actual quotation before revising further, since your interpretation depends on it.

Development and Evidence

Your best paragraphs are the ones with concrete, vivid examples—like the description of daughters being "grilled" about dates while sons roamed freely, and the analysis of fairy tales like *Cinderella* and *Sleeping Beauty*. These sections work because they show rather than tell. 

However, other claims are asserted without support: "women tend to do better than their male counterparts" needs evidence; the claim about media and "beautiful and thin" ideals could be strengthened with specific examples (a particular ad campaign, magazine cover, or TV show) rather than general statements.

Revision strategy: For every generalization, ask "How do I know this?" and add a specific example, statistic, or citation.

Tone and Register

Some phrases—"fine and dandy," "uppity," "clamour to be with this one woman"—work well for informal, engaging tone, but the essay swings between casual voice and more formal academic gestures (like citing Bloom). Decide on a consistent register. If this is meant to be a personal/persuasive essay, lean into your strong voice (evident in your concluding paragraphs) throughout. If it's meant to be more formal academic argument, reduce colloquialisms and strengthen citations.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are frequent errors that need careful proofreading:
Missing words: "which was a movement for the equality of warm" (should be "women"); "Women wanted... Men felt that belonged in the home" (subject missing—"women belonged")
Word confusion: "warm" appears repeatedly where "women" is intended (likely an autocorrect/typing error)—search and fix throughout
Subject-verb agreement and pronoun issues: "females... it was nearly impossible for them"
Comma splices and run-on sentences, especially in the concluding paragraphs
Homophone errors: "horn" for "home," "hard tire" for "hard time," "particulary" for "particularly"

Revision strategy: Read your essay aloud slowly. This will help you catch missing words and awkward phrasing that spell-check won't flag. Also, do a specific search for "warm" and replace with "women" throughout.

Citation

You cite Bloom (24) for the Bill of Rights, but there's no works cited page, and no other sources are cited despite discussing feminism, media, and biblical interpretation. Academic essays need consistent citation practices—identify your sources for historical claims (the "early nineteen hundreds" feminist movement) and add a bibliography.

Priority Action Items for Revision

Fill in the missing quote and revise that paragraph.
Write a clear, specific thesis statement and revise your introduction to set up that argument.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one focused idea; break up overloaded paragraphs.
Fix "warm/women" and other typos throughout via careful proofreading.
Add specific evidence (examples, sources) for generalized claims.
Create a Works Cited page and ensure all sources are properly cited.

You have strong instincts for using concrete examples (fairy tales, dating double standards) and a genuine personal stake in this topic—use these strengths as the foundation for a tighter, more focused revision.

Feedback on "Censorship of Literary Works" Draft

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and still-relevant topic, and your passion for intellectual freedom comes through clearly. You also did solid research, pulling in specific court cases and statistics that many student writers wouldn't bother to find. The essay's biggest opportunities for improvement are in thesis clarity, paragraph-to-paragraph logic, and sentence-level editing. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Central Claim

The essay opens with a series of rhetorical questions but never converts them into a clear, arguable thesis statement. Readers should know by the end of your introduction exactly what position you're defending—something like: *"No institution—school board, parent group, or censorship organization—has the moral or constitutional authority to override an author's First Amendment right to be read."* Right now, the thesis has to be inferred from the conclusion. Move a version of your strongest closing ideas (about authors' voices and the impossibility of shielding children from reality) up into your introduction.

Reconsider the Historical Opening

The Gutenberg/Martin Luther reference is interesting, but it's introduced and then dropped—you never return to it or explain why 15th- and 16th-century censorship matters to your 20th-century American argument. Either develop this connection (eg, "censorship has always followed the spread of new ideas, and America is no exception") or cut it and move directly into your American case studies, which are your strongest material.

Strengthen the Logic Between Examples

Your case studies are the heart of the essay, but you need to guide readers through what each one *proves*. Right now they read as a list rather than a building argument:

Salvail v. Nashua supports your thesis nicely—the board lost.
Cary v. Board of Education, however, actually *complicates* your argument: the teachers' First Amendment rights were violated, but the court still allowed the ban because of a prior contractual agreement. This is a genuinely interesting complication (constitutional rights vs. contractual waiver), but you present it and move on without analysis. Ask yourself: what does this case suggest about the limits of the First Amendment defense? Address it directly instead of just noting "nothing could be done."
The Mesa Library example is your weakest case, since the book was *not* banned. Either cut it or use it explicitly to show that even unsuccessful censorship attempts chill free expression and cost public institutions time and resources.

Clarify and Cite Your Sources

Your PAW statistics are impressive, but readers need to trust them. Include a full, correctly formatted citation (author, title, date, publisher) rather than just naming the source in-text. The same goes for the Vonnegut quotation near the end—where does it come from? A speech? An interview? Cite it. This is especially important in an argumentative essay, where credibility depends on verifiable evidence.

Revisit the Conclusion

Your conclusion has real energy, and the line about children seeing sex and violence in the world regardless of banned books is a strong point—consider moving it earlier and using it as a supporting argument, not just a closing flourish. The final paragraph, however, drifts into a new example (Maya Angelou) that would be better integrated into your body paragraphs, where you're already discussing censored authors. Save your conclusion for synthesis, not new evidence.

Editing for Clarity and Correctness

The essay needs a careful proofreading pass. Some specific issues to fix:

Spelling: "Gutenburg" → Gutenberg; "fireedom" → freedom; "bum" → burn; "curriculwn" → curriculum; "baimed" → banned
Punctuation: several comma splices and stray commas (eg, "However,'"); missing or misplaced apostrophes ("students'," "Board's of Education," "it's" vs. "its")
Word choice: "the go as far as to bum them" is unclear—revise for clarity; "restrain" should be "restraint"; "causal walk" should be "casual walk"; "dishonesty" doesn't quite fit—consider "disservice" or "injustice"
Awkward phrasing: "try to stop the world for turning" is a nice image but needs a cleaner sentence structure; "What right do we...have to say" needs a question mark, not a period
Capitalization: "high students" should likely be "high school students"

I'd recommend reading the essay aloud, which will help you catch many of these issues naturally.

Summary

You have the raw materials for a strong essay: real evidence, real cases, and a genuine point of view. Your next draft should focus on (1) stating a clear thesis early, (2) using each case study to build a cumulative argument rather than listing examples, (3) grappling honestly with the case that complicates your position, and (4) a thorough proofreading pass. With these revisions, this essay could move from a promising draft to a genuinely persuasive piece of writing.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Confederate Flag Controversy

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research for this essay, gathering an impressive range of quotations from multiple sources—politicians, ordinary citizens, historical figures, and news polls. This is commendable groundwork. However, the essay currently reads more like an annotated collection of sources than an argumentative essay. Your primary task now is to transform this research into a coherent argument with a clear thesis that you develop and defend throughout.

Major Issues to Address

Unclear Thesis and Argumentative Position

An argumentative essay requires a clear claim that you defend with evidence. Currently, your essay presents many *other people's* arguments (Lunford, Felfling, Moseley-Braun, Campbell, the various survey respondents) but your own position only emerges in the final two paragraphs. Ask yourself: What is *my* argument? Is it that the flag should remain as a symbol of heritage? That removing it would be historically dishonest? That compromise is possible? State this thesis explicitly in your introduction, and make sure every subsequent paragraph works to support it.

Contradictory Evidence Without Analysis

You present strong arguments on both sides—Lunford and Cavera defending the flag as heritage, Moseley-Braun and Stephens's historical speech revealing its foundation in racist ideology—without reconciling them or explaining why one view should prevail. Simply presenting Stephens's shocking 1861 speech (which explicitly grounds the Confederacy in white supremacy) immediately before your concluding paragraphs asking readers to accept the flag as "heritage" creates a serious logical tension. You must directly address this: How do you respond to Stephens's own words about the flag's founding purpose? If you don't engage with this counterevidence, your conclusion feels unsupported.

The "Bandwagon" of Quotations

Long strings of quotations (particularly the USA survey respondents) list opinions rather than analyze them. For each quotation, ask: *Why am I including this? What does it prove? How does it relate to my thesis?* Consider condensing multiple similar viewpoints into a single sentence of summary, and reserve direct quotation for the most powerful or representative statements. This will also help you meet a reasonable length while strengthening focus.

Weak Conclusion

Your final paragraph asserts a position ("there is no reason to fight over something established so many years ago") but doesn't logically follow from the evidence you've presented—especially given Stephens's speech. The claim that "if we let these people have their way it will only lead to bigger controversy" is vague: Who are "these people"? What is their "way"? What controversy? This needs to be specific and connected to your argument's logic, not simply asserted.

Structural Suggestions

Introduction: Add a clear thesis statement after your historical context.
Body paragraphs: Organize around *ideas*, not sources. For example: one paragraph on "heritage" arguments, one on "racism" arguments, one addressing historical evidence (Stephens), one on public opinion data. Within each, use your quotations as *evidence* for a claim you state first.
Counterargument: Explicitly acknowledge the strongest opposing view (likely Stephens's speech or Moseley-Braun's statement) and explain why your position still holds despite this evidence, or revise your position accordingly.
Conclusion: Return to your thesis and show how the evidence supports it specifically.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Several typos need correction: "Carles Lunford" (Charles?), "grand father" (grandfather), "fory percent" (forty), "an the Capital grounds" (on the Capitol grounds), "siad," "todl," "unacceptabe," "Imprimatur" (fine, but check spelling), "Africa-Americans" (African-Americans), "an" should be "and" in "defended an explained."
Watch for run-on/fragment issues: "There is always going to be controversy for since we live in this world. Whether it is over a flag or anything else." The second sentence is a fragment; combine or revise.
Citation format is inconsistent—make sure all citations follow one system (it looks like you're using an author-page format, but double-check against your assigned style guide, likely MLA given the subject matter).
"Capital" vs. "Capitol": these are different words (Capitol = building; capital = city or money). Check throughout.

Next Steps

Write out your thesis in one clear sentence.
Reorganize your paragraphs around ideas rather than individual sources.
Directly grapple with Stephens's speech—this is your most powerful counter-evidence and cannot be left dangling.
Trim redundant quotations from the USA survey; select 2-3 representative voices instead of six.
Proofread carefully for the typos noted above.

You have strong research materials here—now the work is to shape them into an argument that is *yours*.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely, engaging topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. Your essay identifies a real problem (frivolous lawsuits and their systemic costs) and offers concrete solutions. However, the essay needs significant revision in three main areas: evidence and credibility, organization, and proofreading. Below, I'll address each area with specific suggestions.

Evidence and Credibility

This is your most pressing issue. Your argument relies heavily on anecdotes and general impressions ("Seems like everybody is suing everybody these days," "I heard of a case last week") rather than verifiable evidence. For an academic argumentative essay, you need:

Citations for specific claims. When you mention inmate lawsuits about ice cream flavors or underwear, these need sources. Are these documented cases, or urban legends? Unverified anecdotes (especially ones introduced with "I heard of a case") undermine your credibility rather than support it.
Statistics on caseloads and costs. You claim lawsuits cost "millions" in tax dollars and that trials take "three to five years"—but you don't cite where these figures come from. Look for data from court administration offices, insurance industry reports, or legal scholarship.
Acknowledgment of counterarguments. Right now, your essay treats all lawsuits as either legitimate or frivolous with no gray area. A stronger essay would acknowledge that lawsuits also serve important functions (holding negligent parties accountable, protecting civil rights) and then explain why the problem of frivolous suits still outweighs these benefits, or how your proposed reforms would preserve legitimate access to courts while filtering out abuse.

Action step: For your revision, identify every factual claim and either find a source or qualify the claim (eg, "some studies suggest" rather than presenting it as established fact).

Organization and Argument Structure

Your essay currently reads as a list of grievances rather than a structured argument. Consider this framework:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly and specifically. Right now, your thesis is implied but not stated directly. Try something like: "Reforming civil litigation procedures—by empowering judges to dismiss frivolous suits and penalizing bad-faith claimants—would reduce costs to taxpayers, insurance holders, and the broader justice system."
Body paragraphs: Each paragraph currently covers too much ground. For example, your second paragraph jumps from inmate lawsuits to staged accidents to insurance company behavior. Consider dedicating one paragraph to each category of frivolous litigation (inmate suits, staged accidents, workplace/malpractice suits) with clear topic sentences.
The causal chain paragraph (about civil case backlogs affecting criminal courts) is actually quite effective and shows sophisticated reasoning. Consider expanding this and using it as a model for developing your other points with similar logical rigor.
Conclusion/Solutions: Your final paragraph offers good policy proposals, but each one needs more development. How exactly would judges determine a case is "frivolous" without violating due process? What criteria would apply? This is where a thoughtful reader will look for nuance.

Tone and Word Choice

Words like "ridiculous," "frivolous" (used many times), and "begging" create an emotional, informal tone that weakens your argument's persuasive power in an academic context. Legal and policy writing benefits from measured language even when discussing frustrating topics. Consider:

Replacing "ridiculous cases" with more precise terms like "cases lacking legal merit" or "claims without substantial evidentiary support."
Cutting rhetorical questions ("Are all of these lawsuits necessary?") in favor of direct assertions, which read as more authoritative.

Proofreading

This draft contains numerous typos and OCR-style errors (eg, "ft~tes" for "inmates," "wn t~g" for likely "want to talk," "bmtes" for "inmates," "ne Hundreds" for "The hundreds"). While I recognize some of this may be a transcription issue, please:

Read your essay aloud before submission—this catches errors your eyes skip over.
Use spell-check, but don't rely on it exclusively, since it won't catch all context errors.
Consider having a peer read a printed copy of your final draft.

Priority Revision Checklist

Find and cite real sources for your key claims (or remove unverifiable anecdotes).
Restructure body paragraphs around single, focused topics.
Add a counterargument and address it directly.
Revise your language to sound more measured and analytical.
Proofread carefully for typos and clarity.

You have a strong instinct for identifying a systemic problem and connecting it to broader consequences (the criminal-court backlog argument is your strongest moment). With stronger evidence and tighter organization, this essay could be genuinely persuasive. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further during office hours.

Feedback Report: Draft on the 1995 Government Shutdown

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a compelling historical event—the 1995 government shutdown—and attempts to frame it through Thomas Jefferson's warning about partisanship. The topic is timely even now, and you clearly did research into the specific numbers and dates involved. However, the essay currently reads more like a chronological report than an argumentative essay. To meet the assignment's goals, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, tighten your structure, and make sure every paragraph is working to prove your central claim rather than simply narrating events.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening quotation from Jefferson is a strong hook, but the essay doesn't return to it consistently enough to feel like a true throughline. State your thesis explicitly and early—something like: "The 1995 government shutdown proves Jefferson's warning that partisan division would eventually paralyze the American government." Right now, the closest you get to a thesis is buried in paragraph four ("I agree with Thomas Jefferson that political parties eliminate stability..."), but by then you've already spent three paragraphs on background information. Move your argument to the introduction, and make sure each subsequent paragraph explicitly ties back to it (eg, "This stubbornness illustrates exactly the danger Jefferson predicted...").

Also, be careful with your claim that "no one" won and "no one" is right. This is a reasonable observation, but it slightly undercuts your thesis that *partisanship itself* is the villain. Sharpen this: are you arguing partisanship is inherently bad, or that in this specific case both parties failed the public? These are related but distinct claims—choose one and develop it consistently.

Structure and Organization

The essay's biggest structural issue is that it delays its argument in favor of lengthy historical exposition (the fiscal year, the 1962 shutdown, specific job-loss statistics by military base). This information is useful, but it currently overwhelms your analysis. Consider this reorganization:

Introduction: Jefferson quote + explicit thesis
Background: Brief explanation of fiscal year/budget process (condense significantly)
The 1995 shutdown: Key facts, but trim the granular statistics (500 from Fort Jackson, 400 from Charleston, etc) unless they serve a specific argumentative purpose
Analysis: Why did this happen? Connect directly to Jefferson's warning
Consequences: Economic impact on ordinary Americans
Conclusion: Return to Jefferson; assess whether his 200-year-old warning was vindicated

Currently, some of your strongest analytical material (the pros/cons of divided power, the Jefferson connection) appears in paragraph four, gets dropped, and doesn't return until near the end. Consider moving this analytical paragraph closer to your conclusion, after readers have the full picture of what happened.

Use of Evidence

Your factual details (dates, job-loss numbers, the veto sequence) show good research, but data alone doesn't make an argument—you need to explain their significance. For example, after listing the shutdown-affected institutions (Smithsonian, Grand Canyon, food stamps, veterans' benefits), tell readers *why* this list matters: it shows the shutdown's reach extended far beyond government employees into ordinary citizens' lives, which supports your point about "unfairly making Americans pay."

Similarly, the comparison of the two shutdowns (1962 vs. 1995) is a missed opportunity—why did one last two days and the other six? Does the difference say something about growing partisanship over three decades? This would strengthen your Jefferson connection considerably.

Style and Tone

The "two kids fighting on a playground" metaphor is vivid and effective—more moments like this would help your voice come through. However, some phrases sound like a news report rather than an argumentative essay ("Well, it goes back to who has the most power"). Consider using more analytical transitions like "This reveals..." or "This pattern demonstrates..." to signal that you're interpreting evidence, not just reporting it.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are numerous errors that need correction in revision:
Inconsistent capitalization ("govermnent," "Should," "TheShutdown")
Comma splices and run-ons (the "1, the November deadline had passed" sentence needs rewriting)
Apostrophe errors ("presidents cabinet" should be "president's cabinet"; "Congress'" vs. "Congress's")
Sentence fragments ("Starting with the release of all nonessential personnel...")
The quotation marks around "in" (should be "'m seven years" — likely a typo for "in")

I recommend reading the essay aloud slowly to catch these; many are the kind of error that's easy to miss on-screen but obvious when heard.

Priority Revisions

Write a clear, explicit thesis statement and place it in your introduction
Cut redundant statistical detail; keep only what supports your argument
Move your Jefferson analysis to bookend the essay more clearly
Proofread carefully for the mechanical errors listed above
Add analytical transitions connecting evidence to your central claim

You have real potential here—an interesting historical parallel and some genuinely sharp observations (the playground metaphor, the "who won/who lost" framing). Now it's about disciplining the material to serve your argument.

Feedback on Your Abortion Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult, high-stakes topic, and you clearly have a position and some good instincts about the kinds of evidence that matter (statistics, legal precedent, real-world consequences). However, the essay needs substantial revision in four areas: clarity of thesis and organization, development of ideas, use of credible evidence, and proofreading. Right now the essay reads like a rough draft or an outline of talking points rather than a developed argument. Below are specific, actionable suggestions.

Thesis and Roadmap

Your thesis appears in the first paragraph, but the sentence "Hapeo povertyg personal choice and illegal abortions are examples why people should maintain their right to decide" is garbled and unreadable—likely a typing error, but as it stands it destroys the reader's ability to know what your essay will argue. Fix this sentence first, since everything else depends on it. A clear thesis should preview your main supporting reasons in a logical order (eg, "Abortion should remain legal because it protects survivors of sexual assault, prevents cycles of poverty, is a constitutionally protected right, and reduces public health risks."). Right now your paragraphs jump between rape, poverty, family size, legal precedent, government overreach, and cost to taxpayers without a clear sense of which reasons are primary and which are secondary.

Paragraph Development and Logic

Several paragraphs raise an idea but don't fully develop or support it:

Rape paragraph: The statistic (1 in 4 women) is compelling, but you move quickly into rhetorical questions ("Would be able to love him or her?") without following through with analysis. You need to explicitly connect this specific case back to your broader claim.

Poverty paragraphs: The claim that "children born into impoverished environments remain in that socioeconomic level their entire life" is asserted without a source, and it's a sweeping generalization. Similarly, the claim about family size (five children vs. two) needs a citation. More importantly, this argument risks sounding like you're suggesting abortion as a tool to control the poor population, which is an ethically fraught implication you should address or reframe—readers may find this argument alienating rather than persuasive.

Roe v. Wade: This is a crucial piece of evidence, but the case name is misspelled ("Rowe versus Way" should be *Roe v. Wade*), and you don't explain *what* the ruling established (the constitutional right to privacy under the 14th Amendment) or its current legal status. Given that Roe was overturned by *Dobbs v. Jackson* (2022), you should update this section to reflect current law—otherwise your argument will seem outdated or uninformed to any reader familiar with the topic.

Government/religious control paragraph: This is a strong point (bodily autonomy, slippery-slope concerns about rights), but it's only one sentence long. Expand it—this could be one of your most persuasive arguments if developed with examples or reasoning about precedent-setting.

Illegal abortion / coat hanger paragraph: Good use of consequence-based reasoning, but back it up with historical data (eg, statistics from before Roe v. Wade or from countries with abortion bans today).

Cost to taxpayers paragraph: The $125,000 figure needs a citation. Also, "infant mortality rates would sky rocket... because there would be a higher rate of ignorant and uncaring parents" is a weak, unsupported causal claim and comes across as judgmental toward parents rather than analytical. Revise this reasoning or drop it.

Evidence and Citations

None of your statistics (1 in 4 women, average family sizes, $125,000 cost) are cited. In an argumentative essay, especially on a topic this contested, uncited statistics will undermine your credibility. Find and cite reputable sources (CDC, Guttmacher Institute, RAINN, etc) for every number you use.

Tone

Aim for a more measured, analytical tone rather than rhetorical questions and casual phrasing ("society," "no one should be able to," "this includes religious people"). Avoid characterizing opposing views dismissively (eg, implying religious people are simply trying to control others); instead, acknowledge the strongest counterarguments and explain why your position still holds.

Proofreading

The essay has frequent typos and errors: "Rowe versus Way," "sky racket alsop," "eightteen," "force to give birth," missing words, and inconsistent capitalization/hyphenation. Read your essay aloud before submitting, or use a grammar-checking tool, to catch these errors—they distract readers from your argument and suggest the essay wasn't proofread.

Next Steps

Rewrite the thesis so it's grammatically clear and previews your main points.
Choose your 3–4 strongest arguments and cut or merge weaker ones.
Add citations for every statistic.
Update the Roe v. Wade section to reflect current legal reality.
Proofread carefully, ideally after letting the essay sit for a day.

You have the seeds of a solid argument here—now it needs structure, evidence, and precision.
